“This” was a roll of peppermint lozenges. Emily clutched it and fled, overcome with shame at being seen by Cousin Jimmy in her nightgown. She hated peppermints and never ate them, but the fact of Cousin Jimmy Murray’s kindness in giving them to her sent a thrill of delight to her heart. And he called her “small pussy,” too﻿—she liked that. She had thought nobody would ever call her nice pet names again. Father had had so many of them for her﻿—“sweetheart” and “darling” and “Emily-child” and “dear wee kidlet” and “honey” and “elfkin.” He had a pet name for every mood and she had loved them all. As for Cousin Jimmy, he was nice. Whatever part of him was missing it wasn’t his heart. She felt so grateful to him that after she was safely in her bed again she forced herself to eat one of the lozenges, though it took all her grit to worry it down.
The funeral was held that forenoon. For once the lonesome little house in the hollow was filled. The coffin was taken into the parlour and the Murrays as mourners sat stiffly and decorously all round it, Emily among them, pale and prim in her black dress. She sat between Aunt Elizabeth and Uncle Wallace and dared not move a muscle. No other Starr was present. Her father had no near living relatives. The Maywood people came and looked at his dead face with a freedom and insolent curiosity they would never have presumed on in life. Emily hated to have them looking at her father like that. They had no right﻿—they hadn’t been friendly to him while he was alive﻿—they had said harsh things of him﻿—Ellen Greene had sometimes repeated them. Every glance that fell on him hurt Emily; but she sat still and gave no outward sign.

===Chinese===

“再见，最亲最爱的，”她哽咽着低语。
她抹掉模糊双眼的泪水，把露丝姨妈的靠枕放回原处，从此永远遮住了父亲的脸。随后她溜出门去，一心只想赶紧回到自己房里。在门口，她差点撞倒吉米表哥（Cousin Jimmy）——他裹着一件肥大的格子晨衣，坐在门旁一把椅子上，怀里搂着迈克。
“嘘——！”他低声说，拍拍她的肩膀。“我听见你下楼，就跟了下来。我明白你要做什么。我一直坐在这儿，万一他们有谁追你来，好把她们挡回去。拿着这个，快回床上去，小猫崽。”
“这个”是一卷薄荷糖锭。艾米丽一把攥住，逃了——被吉米表哥撞见自己穿着睡衣，她羞得没处钻。她讨厌薄荷糖，从来不吃的；可是吉米·默里（Jimmy Murray）表哥这份好心，却叫一股欢喜的暖流直涌到她心底。何况他还叫她“小猫崽”呢——她喜欢这个。她原以为再也不会有人叫她好听的昵称了。爸爸从前给她起过那么多——“甜心”啦，“宝贝”啦，“艾米丽孩子”啦，“亲爱的小家伙”啦，“蜜糖”啦，“小精灵”啦。他每一种心绪都配着一个昵称，她个个都爱。至于吉米表哥，他是个好人。不管他身上缺了什么，缺的决不是心肝。她对他感激极了，所以等重新安全地缩回被窝以后，她硬逼着自己吃下一颗薄荷糖锭——虽说要把它嚼碎咽下，几乎得用尽她全副的狠劲。
葬礼就在那天上午举行。谷中那座孤单的小屋破天荒挤满了人。灵柩抬进客厅，默里家的人作为吊丧的亲族，端端正正、规规矩矩围坐在四周，艾米丽也夹在其间，一身黑衣，苍白而拘谨。她坐在伊丽莎白姨妈和华莱士舅舅（Uncle Wallace）中间，一动也不敢动。场中再没有一个斯塔尔家的人。她父亲没有健在的近亲。梅伍德（Maywood）的人都来了，用一种他生前他们决计不敢放肆的随便和无礼的好奇，端详他的遗容。艾米丽恨他们这样打量她父亲。他们没有这个权利——他活着的时候他们不曾对他好过——他们说 过他的难听话——艾伦·格林有时还学给她听过。每一道落在他身上的目光都刺得艾米丽疼；可她端坐不动，外表没有一丝流露。

===Original===

Aunt Ruth said afterwards that she had never seen a child so absolutely devoid of all natural feeling.
When the service was over the Murrays rose and marched around the coffin for a dutiful look of farewell. Aunt Elizabeth took Emily’s hand and tried to draw her along with them but Emily pulled it back and shook her head. She had said her goodbye already. Aunt Elizabeth seemed for a moment to be on the point of insisting; then she grimly swept onward, alone, looking every inch a Murray. No scene must be made at a funeral.
Douglas Starr was to be taken to Charlottetown for burial beside his wife. The Murrays were all going but Emily was not to go. She watched the funeral procession as it wound up the long, grassy hill, through the light grey rain that was beginning to fall. Emily was glad it was raining; many a time she had heard Ellen Greene say that happy was the corpse the rain fell on; and it was easier to see Father go away in that soft, kind, grey mist than through sparkling, laughing sunshine.
“Well, I must say the funeral went off fine,” said Ellen Greene at her shoulder. “Everything’s been done regardless. If your father was looking down from heaven at it, Emily, I’m sure he’d be pleased.”
“He isn’t in heaven,” said Emily.
“Good gracious! Of all the children!” Ellen could say no more.
“He isn’t there *yet*. He’s only on the way. He said he’d wait around and go slow until I died, too, so that I could catch up with him. I hope I’ll die soon.”
“That’s a wicked, wicked thing to wish,” rebuked Ellen.
When the last buggy had disappeared Emily went back to the sitting-room, got a book out of the bookcase, and buried herself in the wing-chair. The women who were tidying up were glad she was quiet and out of the way.
“It’s well she can read,” said Mrs. Hubbard gloomily. “Some little girls couldn’t be so composed﻿—Jennie Hood just screamed and shrieked after they carried her mother out﻿—the Hoods are all such a *feeling* people.”
Emily was not reading. She was thinking. She knew the Murrays would be back in the afternoon; and she knew her fate would probably be settled then. “We’ll talk the matter over when we come back,” she had heard Uncle Wallace saying that morning after breakfast Some instinct told her just what “the matter” was; and she would have given one of her pointed ears to hear the discussion with the other.

===Chinese===

露丝姨妈后来说，她这辈子没见过一个孩子这样彻头彻尾地没有天伦之情。
仪式完毕，默里家的人起身，绕着灵柩鱼贯而行，尽一尽告别瞻仰的礼数。伊丽莎白姨妈拉起艾米丽的手，想带她一同走，艾米丽却把手抽了回来，摇摇头。她的别早已道过了。伊丽莎白姨妈一时似乎就要发作；随即她冷冷地昂然独自前行，从头到脚无一寸不是默里家的做派——葬礼上万万失态不得。
道格拉斯·斯塔尔（Douglas Starr）要运往夏洛特顿（Charlottetown），安葬在他妻子身旁。默里家的人全去，艾米丽却不去。她望着送葬的行列蜿蜒爬上长长的青草山坡，走进渐渐落下的浅灰色雨幕里。艾米丽庆幸天下着雨；她不知多少回听艾伦·格林说过，雨落在身上下葬的死者是有福的；何况，父亲是在那样轻柔、善意的灰雾里离去，总比在金光闪闪、笑语盈盈的太阳底下容易目送。
“嗬，我得说，这丧事办得真体面，”艾伦·格林在她肩后说，“样样都不惜工本。要是你爸爸从天上往下看着这一切，艾米丽，我管保他心里满意。”
“他不在天上，”艾米丽说。
“老天爷！瞧瞧这孩子！”艾伦再没话说了。
“他还没有到呢。他只在半路上。他说过他要慢慢走，一路等着我，等我死了，好让我追上他。我巴望自己快点儿死。”
“盼这样的心愿，罪过，真是罪过，”艾伦责备道。
最后一辆马车看不见了，艾米丽回到起居室，从书柜里抽出一本书，把自己埋进那把高背椅里。收拾屋子的妇人们巴不得她安安静静不碍事。
“亏得她还能看书，”哈伯德太太（Mrs. Hubbard）愁眉苦脸地说，“有的小姑娘可就沉不住气了——詹妮·胡德（Jennie Hood）他们把她母亲抬出门以后，哭喊得死去活来——胡德家的人家，个个都是那么重感情。”
艾米丽并没有看书。她在想心事。她知道默里家的人下午就要回来；她知道自己的命运多半就在那时候定夺。“等我们回来再商量这件事，”早饭后她听见华莱士舅舅这么说过。某种本能告诉她，“这件事”指的正是她；她甘愿割下自己一只尖尖的耳朵去换，好用另一只耳朵听听那场商议。

===Original===

But she knew very well she would be sent out of the way. So she was not surprised when Ellen came to her in the twilight and said:
“You’d better go upstairs, Emily. Your aunts and uncles are coming in here to talk over the business.”
“Can’t I help you get supper?” asked Emily, who thought that if she were going and coming around the kitchen she might catch a word or two.
“No. You’d be more bother than help. March, now.”
Ellen waddled out to the kitchen, without waiting to see if Emily marched. Emily got up reluctantly. How could she sleep tonight if she did not know what was going to happen to her? And she felt quite sure she would not be told till morning, if then.
Her eyes fell on the oblong table in the centre of the room. Its cloth was of generous proportions, falling in heavy folds to the floor. There was a flash of black stockings across the rug, a sudden disturbance of drapery, and then﻿—silence. Emily, on the floor under the table, arranged her legs comfortably and sat triumphant. She would hear what was decided and nobody would be any the wiser.
She had never been told that it was not considered strictly honourable to eavesdrop, no occasion for such instruction ever having arisen in her life with her father; and she considered that it was a bit of pure luck that she had thought of hiding under the table. She could even see dimly through the cloth. Her heart beat so loudly in her excitement that she was afraid they would hear it; there was no other sound save the soft, faraway singing of frogs through the rain, that sounded through the open window.
In they came; down they sat around the room; Emily held her breath; for a few minutes nobody spoke, though Aunt Eva sighed long and heavily. Then Uncle Wallace cleared his throat and said,
“Well, what is to be done with the child?”
Nobody was in a hurry to answer. Emily thought they would *never* speak. Finally Aunt Eva said with a whine,
“She’s such a difficult child﻿—so odd. *I* can’t understand her at all.”
“I think,” said Aunt Laura timidly, “that she has what one might call an artistic temperament.”
“She’s a spoiled child,” said Aunt Ruth very decidedly. “There’s work ahead to straighten out *her* manners, if you ask me.”
(The little listener under the table turned her head and shot a scornful glance at Aunt Ruth through the tablecloth. “*I* think that your own manners have a slight curve.”

===Chinese===

可是她心里明白得很，自己准会被支开。所以黄昏时分艾伦过来对她说“你最好上楼去，艾米丽。你姨妈舅舅们要进屋来谈事情”的时候，她毫不意外。
“我不能帮你预备晚饭吗？”艾米丽问——她盘算着，若是在厨房里出出进进，兴许能捞着一句半句。
“不能。你净帮倒忙。快走。”
艾伦摇摇摆摆地出去到厨房，也不等看艾米丽走不走。艾米丽不情愿地站起身。要是不知道自己会有什么遭遇，她今夜怎么睡得着？而且她十拿九稳，他们不到明早是不会告诉她的——说不定到了明早也不。
她的目光落在屋子正中那张长方桌上。桌布大得出奇，沉甸甸的褶裥一直拖到地板。长袜的黑影在地毯上一闪而过，帘幔间一阵急促的窸窣，然后——悄无声息。桌子底下的艾米丽舒舒服服摆好两条腿，得意洋洋地坐着。他们将商量出个什么结果，她全听得见，而谁也不会起半点疑心。
从来没有人教过她偷听是算不得光明正大的——她跟着爸爸过日子，压根儿没有过需要这样教她的场合；她还只当是自己灵机一动想起躲进桌子底下，纯是一桩好运气。她甚至能隔着桌布影影绰绰看见。她激动得心咚咚直响，生怕被他们听了去；四下再没有别的声息，只有雨里远远的蛙鸣软软地、飘飘地穿过敞开的窗子。
他们进来了；围着屋子坐下；艾米丽屏住了呼吸；有好几分钟谁也不开口，只有伊娃姨妈（Aunt Eva）长长地、沉沉地叹气。随后华莱士舅舅清了清嗓子，说，
“那么，这孩子该怎么办？”
谁也不抢着回答。艾米丽只当他们永远不开口了。末了伊娃姨妈哭腔哭调地说，
“她这么个别扭的孩子——这么古怪。我压根儿一点儿也弄不懂她。”
“我想，”劳拉姨妈怯生生地说，“她有一种不妨叫作艺术家气质的东西。”
“她是个被惯坏的孩子，”露丝姨妈说得斩钉截铁，“要依我说，得下些功夫，好好扳一扳她的规矩。”
（桌子底下的小听客转过头去，隔着桌布朝露丝姨妈射去轻蔑的一瞥。“我看哪，你自己的规矩才有点儿歪呢。”

===Original===

Emily did not dare even to murmur the word under her breath, but she shaped them with her mouth; this was a great relief and satisfaction.)

“她这孩子，看着确实单薄，”华莱士舅舅承认道。

“——咱们中间总得有人给她一个家。朱丽叶（Juliet）的女儿，断不能任凭外人发落。”

Personally, I feel that Eva’s health is not equal to the care and training of a child﻿—”
“Of *such* a child,” said Aunt Eva.
(Emily stuck her tongue out at Aunt Eva.)

(Something frozen in Emily’s heart melted at that moment. She was pitifully pleased over being called “poor little soul” so tenderly.)
“I do not think you need pity her overmuch, Laura,” said Uncle Wallace decidedly. “It is evident that she has very little feeling. I have not seen her shed a tear since we came here.”
“Did you notice that she would not even take a last look at her father?” said Aunt Elizabeth.
Cousin Jimmy suddenly whistled at the ceiling.
“She feels so much that she has to hide it,” said Aunt Laura.
Uncle Wallace snorted.
“Don’t you think *we* might take her, Elizabeth?” Laura went on timidly.
Aunt Elizabeth stirred restlessly.
“I don’t suppose she’d be contented at New Moon, with three old people like us.”
(“I would﻿—I would!” thought Emily.)
“Ruth, what about you?” said Uncle Wallace. “You’re all alone in that big house. It would be a good thing for you to have some company.”
“I don’t like her,” said Aunt Ruth sharply. “She is as sly as a snake.”
(“I’m *not*!” thought Emily.)
“With wise and careful training many of her faults may be cured,” said Uncle Wallace, pompously.
(“I don’t *want* them cured!” Emily was getting angrier and angrier all the time under the table. “I like *my* faults better than I do *your*﻿—*your*﻿—” she fumbled mentally for a word﻿—then triumphantly recalled a phrase of her father’s﻿—“your *abominable* virtues!”)
“I doubt it,” said Aunt Ruth, in a biting tone. “What’s bred in the bone comes out in the flesh. As for Douglas Starr, I think that it was perfectly disgraceful for him to die and leave that child without a cent.”
“Did he do it on purpose?” asked Cousin Jimmy blandly. It was the first time he had spoken.
“He was a miserable failure,” snapped Aunt Ruth.
“He wasn’t﻿—he wasn’t!” screamed Emily, suddenly sticking her head out under the tablecloth, between the end legs of the table.
For a moment the Murrays sat as silent and motionless as if her outburst had turned them to stone. Then Aunt Ruth rose, stalked to the table, and lifted the cloth, behind which Emily had retired in dismay, realising what she had done.
“Get up and come out of that, Em’ly Starr!” said Aunt Ruth.
“Em’ly Starr” got up and came out.

（“我才不呢！”艾米丽想。）

（“我才不要治好呢！”桌子底下的艾米丽火气越蹿越高。“我喜欢我的毛病，胜过喜欢你们的——你们的——”她在脑子里摸索一个词儿——随即得意地想起爸爸说过的一句话——“你们那些可憎的美德！”）
“我不信，”露丝姨妈语气尖刻，“骨子里带来的东西，肉上总要露出来。至于道格拉斯·斯塔尔，我说，他死的时候一个铜板也不给这孩子留下，简直是丢尽了人。”

“他不是——他不是！”艾米丽尖叫一声，猛地从桌子底下探出头来，正卡在桌子端头的两条桌腿中间。
一时间，默里家的人生得又哑又僵，仿佛她这一嗓子把他们震成了石头。随后露丝姨妈站起身，大步走到桌前，掀起了桌布——艾米丽明白自己闯了祸，早在桌布后面缩成一团。
“给我起来，从那儿出来，艾米丽·斯塔尔！”露丝姨妈说。
“艾米丽·斯塔尔”就起来，从那儿出来了。

===Original===

She was not specially frightened﻿—she was too angry to be that. Her eyes had gone black and her cheeks crimson.
“What a little beauty﻿—what a regular little beauty!” said Cousin Jimmy. But nobody heard him. Aunt Ruth had the floor.
“You shameless little eavesdropper!” she said. “There’s the Starr blood coming out﻿—a Murray would never have done such a thing. You ought to be whipped!”
“Father wasn’t a failure!” cried Emily, choking with anger. “You had no right to call him a failure. Nobody who was loved as much as he was could be a failure. I don’t believe anybody *ever* loved you. So it’s *you* that’s a failure. And I’m *not* going to die of consumption.”
“Do you realize what a shameful thing you’ve been guilty of?” demanded Aunt Ruth, cold with anger.
“I wanted to hear what was going to become of me,” cried Emily. “I didn’t know it was such a dreadful thing to do﻿—I didn’t know you were going to say such horrid things about me.”
“Listeners never hear any good of themselves,” said Aunt Elizabeth impressively. “Your mother would *never* have done that, Emily.”
The bravado all went out of poor Emily. She felt guilty and miserable﻿—oh, so miserable. She hadn’t known﻿—but it seemed she had committed a terrible sin.
“Go upstairs,” said Aunt Ruth.
Emily went, without a protest. But before going she looked around the room.
“While I was under the table,” she said, “I made a face at Uncle Wallace and stuck my tongue out at Aunt Eva.”
She said it sorrowfully, desiring to make a clean breast of her transgressions; but so easily do we misunderstand each other that the Murrays actually thought that she was indulging in a piece of gratuitous impertinence. When the door had closed behind her they all﻿—except Aunt Laura and Cousin Jimmy﻿—shook their heads and groaned.
Emily went upstairs in a state of bitter humiliation. She felt that she had done something that gave the Murrays the right to despise her, and they thought it was the Starr coming out in her﻿—and she had not even found out what her fate was to be.
She looked dismally at little Emily-in-the-glass.
“I didn’t know﻿—I didn’t know,” she whispered. “But I’ll know after this,” she added with sudden vim, “and I’ll never, *never* do it again.”
For a moment she thought she would throw herself on her bed and cry. She *couldn’t* bear all the pain and shame that were burning in her heart.

===Chinese===

她并不怎么害怕——她的火气太大，顾不上怕。她的眼睛黑沉沉的，两颊烧得通红。
“多俊的一个小美人儿——多地道的一个小美人儿！”吉米表哥说。可是谁也没听见他说话。这时说话的是露丝姨妈。
“你这个不知羞耻的小偷听贼！”她说，“瞧见没有，斯塔尔家的血露出来了吧——默里家的人可干不出这种事来。就该挨一顿鞭子！”
“爸爸才不是废物！”艾米丽喊道，气得嗓子都哽住了。“你没有权利叫他废物。被那么多的人爱着的人，不可能是废物。我看来压根儿就没有一个人爱过你。所以废物的是你。还有，我也不会死于肺痨。”
“你可知道自己闯下了多丢人的祸？”露丝姨妈质问道，气得浑身冰凉。
“我不过想知道我往后怎么办，”艾米丽喊道，“我不知道这是这么了不得的坏事——我不知道你们要这样编排我的坏话。”
“偷听的人，从来听不见自己的好话，”伊丽莎白姨妈一字一板地说，“你母亲是决不会做这种事的，艾米丽。”
可怜的艾米丽那点虚张声势的胆气全泄光了。她又愧又苦——哦，苦透了。她原本不知道——可照这么一说，她仿佛犯下了弥天大罪。
“上楼去，”露丝姨妈说。
艾米丽去了，没有一句分辩。可是在走之前，她把屋子环视了一周。
“我躲在桌子底下的时候，”她说，“朝华莱士舅舅做过一个鬼脸，还朝伊娃姨妈吐过舌头。”
她是伤心地说出来，一心想把过错从实招个干净；可是人与人之间多么容易彼此误会，默里家的人反倒以为她是存心再无礼上一回。她身后的门一关上，他们——除了劳拉姨妈和吉米表哥——个个摇头叹气。
艾米丽上楼去，一路羞苦交加。她觉得自己做了件什么事，给了默里家的人小看她的权利；他们又认定这是斯塔尔家的血在她身上现了形——而她连自己究竟会有什么命运都还没打听出来呢。
她愁眉苦脸地望着小小的镜中艾米丽（Emily-in-the-glass）。
“我不知道——我不知道，”她低声说，“可这回我知道了，”她忽地又来了劲头，添上一句，“我再不——再也不干了。”
有一会儿她想扑到床上痛哭一场。她心里烧着的那份痛苦和羞辱，她实在受不住。

===Original===

Then her eyes fell on the old yellow account book on her little table. A minute later Emily was curled up on her bed, Turk-fashion, writing eagerly in the old book with her little stubby lead pencil. As her fingers flew over the faded lines her cheeks flushed and her eyes shone. She forgot the Murrays although she was writing about them﻿—she forgot her humiliation﻿—although she was describing what had happened; for an hour she wrote steadily by the wretched light of her smoky little lamp, never pausing, save now and then, to gaze out of the window into the dim beauty of the misty night, while she hunted through her consciousness for a certain word she wanted; when she found it she gave a happy sigh and fell to again.
When she heard the Murrays coming upstairs she put her book away. She had finished; she had written a description of the whole occurrence and of that conclave ring of Murrays, and she had wound up by a pathetic description of her own deathbed, with the Murrays standing around imploring her forgiveness. At first she depicted Aunt Ruth as doing it on her knees in an agony of remorseful sobs. Then she suspended her pencil﻿—“Aunt Ruth couldn’t *ever* feel as bad as *that* over anything,” she thought﻿—and drew her pencil through the line.
In the writing, pain and humiliation had passed away. She only felt tired and rather happy. It *had* been fun, finding words to fit Uncle Wallace; and what exquisite satisfaction it had been to describe Aunt Ruth as “a dumpy little woman.”
“I wonder what my uncles and aunts would say if they knew what I *really* think of them,” she murmured as she got into bed.

===Chinese===

这时她的目光落在小桌上那本发黄的旧账本上。一分钟后，艾米丽已经盘腿坐在床上——土耳其人那样盘着——用她那截秃了头的铅笔，在旧账本上奋笔疾书。手指在褪色的账行间飞快地走，她脸颊发烧，两眼放光。她忘了默里家的人，虽然她写的正是他们——她忘了自己的羞辱——虽然她描述的正是方才发生的一切；她就着那盏冒烟的小灯的惨淡光亮，一口气稳稳写了一个钟头，从不停顿，只是偶尔抬眼望望窗外雾夜朦胧的景色，在心里翻来覆去地找一个想要的字眼；一找到，便欢喜地叹一口气，重新埋头写下去。
听见默里家的人上楼来了，她把本子收好。她已经写完了；她把事情始末写了一整篇，写了默里家那一圈围坐的家庭会议，末了还凄凄切切写了自己临终的病榻，写默里家的人围在四周哀求她的宽恕。起初她写的是露丝姨妈跪在地上，悔恨得泣不成声。写着写着她悬住了铅笔——“露丝姨妈无论对什么事，也决不会难受到那个地步，”她想——于是一笔把那一句划掉了。
写完了，痛苦和羞辱都消散了。她只觉得累，还觉得挺快活。找字眼儿去安在华莱士舅舅身上，那真有趣；把露丝姨妈写成一个“矮墩墩的小女人”，又是多么痛快的一桩事。
“不知道我的姨妈舅舅们要是晓得了我心里对他们真正的想法，会说些什么呢，”她钻进被窝时喃喃自语。

## V Diamond Cut Diamond / 五 棋逢对手
===Original===

Emily, who had been pointedly ignored by the Murrays at breakfast, was called into the parlour when the meal was over.
They were all there﻿—the whole phalanx of them﻿—and it occurred to Emily as she looked at Uncle Wallace, sitting in the spring sunshine, that she had not just found the exact word after all to express his peculiar quality of grimness.
Aunt Elizabeth stood unsmilingly by the table with slips of paper in her hand.
“Emily,” she said, “last night we could not decide who should take you. I may say that none of us feel very much like doing so, for you have behaved very badly in many respects﻿—”
“Oh, Elizabeth﻿—” protested Laura. “She﻿—she is our sister’s child.”
Elizabeth lifted a hand regally.
“*I* am doing this, Laura. Have the goodness not to interrupt me. As I was saying, Emily, we could not decide as to who should have the care of you. So we have agreed to Cousin Jimmy’s suggestion that we settle the matter by lot. I have our names here, written on these slips of paper. You will draw one and the one whose name is on it will give you a home.”
Aunt Elizabeth held out the slips of paper. Emily trembled so violently that at first she could not draw one. This was terrible﻿—it seemed as if she must blindly settle her own fate.
“Draw,” said Aunt Elizabeth.
Emily set her teeth, threw back her head with the air of one who challenges destiny, and drew. Aunt Elizabeth took the slip from the little shaking hand and held it up. On it was her own name﻿—“Elizabeth Murray.” Laura Murray suddenly put her handkerchief to her eyes.
“Well, that’s settled,” said Uncle Wallace, getting up with an air of relief. “And if I’m going to catch that train I’ve got to hurry. Of course, as far as the matter of expense goes, Elizabeth, I’ll do my share.”
“We are not paupers at New Moon,” said Aunt Elizabeth rather coldly. “Since it has fallen to me to take her, I shall do all that is necessary, Wallace. I do not shirk my duty.”
“*I* am her duty,” thought Emily. “Father said nobody ever liked a duty. So Aunt Elizabeth will never like me.”
“You’ve got more of the Murray pride than all the rest of us put together, Elizabeth,” laughed Uncle Wallace.
They all followed him out﻿—all except Aunt Laura. She came up to Emily, standing alone in the middle of the room, and drew her into her arms.
“I’m so glad, Emily﻿—I’m so glad,” she whispered. “Don’t fret, dear child.

===Chinese===

早饭时默里家的人故意把艾米丽晾在一边；饭一吃完，她就被叫进了客厅。
他们全都在那儿——整整一队列阵——艾米丽望着坐在春天阳光里的华莱士舅舅（Uncle Wallace），忽然想到，自己到底还是没找到那个恰切的字眼，来说透他那股特有的冷峻。
伊丽莎白姨妈（Aunt Elizabeth）不苟言笑地站在桌旁，手里捏着几张纸签。
“艾米丽，”她说，“昨晚我们定不下由谁来收你。不妨直说，我们谁都不大情愿——因为你在好些事上表现得很坏——”
“哦，伊丽莎白——”劳拉姨妈（Aunt Laura）不平地说，“她——她是我们妹妹的孩子啊。”
伊丽莎白威严地抬起一只手。
“这件事是*我*在主持，劳拉。请你不要打断我。我方才说，艾米丽，我们定不下由谁来照管你。所以我们采纳了吉米表哥（Cousin Jimmy）的提议，抽签来定。我们的名字都写在这些纸签上。你来抽一张，签上是谁，谁就给你一个家。”
伊丽莎白姨妈把纸签递过来。艾米丽抖得厉害，起初竟抽不出来。这太可怕了——她这是要蒙着自己的眼睛定自己的命啊。
“抽。”伊丽莎白姨妈说。
艾米丽咬紧牙关，昂起头，一副向命运叫阵的架势，抽出一签。伊丽莎白姨妈从那只发抖的小手里接过纸签，举起来。上面是她自己的名字——“伊丽莎白·默里”。劳拉·默里突然把手帕按到了眼睛上。
“好啦，就这么定了。”华莱士舅舅说，如释重负地站起身。“要赶那班火车，我得赶紧了。当然，说到花费的事，伊丽莎白，我该出的一份我会出。”
“新月庄园（New Moon）可不是靠人施舍的。”伊丽莎白姨妈相当冷淡地说。“既然摊到我头上收她，必要的事我都会办到，华莱士。我份内的职责我不推。”
“‘我’就是她的职责。”艾米丽想。“爸爸说过，职责从来没人喜欢。这么说，伊丽莎白姨妈永远不会喜欢我了。”
“你身上默里家的傲气，比我们所有人加在一起还多，伊丽莎白。”华莱士舅舅笑道。
众人都跟着他出去——只有劳拉姨妈没走。她走到独自站在屋子当中的艾米丽跟前，把她搂进怀里。
“我真高兴，艾米丽——我真高兴。”她悄悄说。“别发愁，宝贝孩子。

===Original===

I love you already﻿—and New Moon is a nice place, Emily.”
“It has﻿—a pretty name,” said Emily, struggling for self-control. “I’ve﻿—always hoped﻿—I could go with you, Aunt Laura. I think I am going to cry﻿—but it’s not because I’m sorry I’m going there. My manners are *not* as bad as you may think, Aunt Laura﻿—and I wouldn’t have listened last night if I’d known it was wrong.”
“Of course you wouldn’t,” said Aunt Laura.
“But I’m not a Murray, you know.”
Then Aunt Laura said a queer thing﻿—for a Murray.
“Thank heaven for that!” said Aunt Laura.
Cousin Jimmy followed Emily out and overtook her in the little hall. Looking carefully around to ensure privacy, he whispered,
“Your Aunt Laura is a great hand at making an apple turnover, pussy.”
Emily thought apple turnover sounded nice, though she did not know what it was. She whispered back a question which she would never have dared ask Aunt Elizabeth or even Aunt Laura.
“Cousin Jimmy, when they make a cake at New Moon, will they let me scrape out the mixing-bowl and eat the scrapings?”
“Laura will﻿—Elizabeth won’t,” whispered Cousin Jimmy solemnly.
“And put my feet in the oven when they get cold? And have a cooky before I go to bed?”
“Answer same as before,” said Cousin Jimmy. “*I’ll* recite my poetry to you. It’s very few people I do that for. I’ve composed a thousand poems. They’re not written down﻿—I carry them here.” Cousin Jimmy tapped his forehead.
“Is it very hard to write poetry?” asked Emily, looking with new respect at Cousin Jimmy.
“Easy as rolling off a log if you can find enough rhymes,” said Cousin Jimmy.
They all went away that morning except the New Moon people. Aunt Elizabeth announced that they would stay until the next day to pack up and take Emily with them.
“Most of the furniture belongs to the house,” she said, “so it won’t take us long to get ready. There are only Douglas Starr’s books and his few personal belongings to pack.”
“How shall I carry my cats?” asked Emily anxiously.
Aunt Elizabeth stared.
“Cats! You’ll take no cats, miss.”
“Oh, I must take Mike and Saucy Sal!” cried Emily wildly. “I can’t leave them behind. I can’t live without a cat.”
“Nonsense! There are barn cats at New Moon, but they are never allowed in the house.”
“Don’t you like cats?” asked Emily wonderingly.
“No, I do *not*.”
“Don’t you like the *feel* of a nice, soft, fat cat?” persisted Emily.

===Chinese===

我已经喜欢上你了——新月庄园是个好地方，艾米丽。”
“它的——名字很美。”艾米丽说，拼命克制着自己。“我——一直盼着——能跟您走，劳拉姨妈。我想我要哭了——可不是因为去那儿才难过。我的规矩没有您想的那么坏，劳拉姨妈——昨晚要是知道那样做不对，我才不会偷听呢。”
“你当然不会。”劳拉姨妈说。
“可我不是默里家的人，您知道。”
接着劳拉姨妈说了句怪话——对一个默里家的人来说。
“谢天谢地你不是！”劳拉姨妈说。
吉米表哥跟着艾米丽出来，在小门厅里赶上她。他四下里仔细张望了一回，确信没人，才悄悄说：
“你劳拉姨妈做苹果酥卷可是一把好手，小猫咪。”
艾米丽觉得苹果酥卷听着挺美，虽然她不知道那是什么。她也悄悄回问了一个问题——这问题她可从来不敢问伊丽莎白姨妈，连劳拉姨妈也不敢。
“吉米表哥，新月庄园做蛋糕的时候，能让我刮搅面盆、把刮下来的吃掉吗？”
“劳拉让——伊丽莎白不让。”吉米表哥郑重地悄悄说。
“那脚冻凉了，能搁进烤炉里暖着吗？睡觉前能给块甜饼吗？”
“回答照旧。”吉米表哥说。“*我*会给你念我做的诗。这待遇很少人有。我作了一千首诗。都没写下来——全装在这儿呢。”吉米表哥敲敲脑门。
“写诗很难吗？”艾米丽问，带着新的敬意望着吉米表哥。
“只要凑得够韵脚，就跟从原木上滚下来一样容易。”吉米表哥说。
那天早晨他们都走了，只有新月庄园的人留下。伊丽莎白姨妈宣布，他们要住到第二天，收拾收拾，把艾米丽带走。
“家具大都是随房子的。”她说，“所以收拾起来用不了多久。只有道格拉斯·斯塔尔（Douglas Starr）的书和他那几件随身东西要打包。”
“我的猫怎么带呢？”艾米丽着急地问。
伊丽莎白姨妈一愕。
“猫！你一只猫也不许带，小姐。”
“哦，我一定得带上迈克（Mike）和调皮的萨尔（Saucy Sal）！”艾米丽发了狂似地喊。“我不能把他们丢下。没有猫我活不成的。”
“胡说！新月庄园有谷仓猫，可从来不许进屋。”
“您不喜欢猫吗？”艾米丽诧异地问。
“不喜欢，我*就是*不喜欢。”
“您就不喜欢那种又软又胖的好猫摸上去的手感吗？”艾米丽还不死心。

===Original===

“No; I would as soon touch a snake.”
“There’s a lovely old wax doll of your mother’s up there,” said Aunt Laura. “I’ll dress it up for you.”
“I don’t like dolls﻿—they can’t talk,” exclaimed Emily.
“Neither can cats.”
“Oh, can’t they! Mike and Saucy Sal can. Oh, I *must* take them. Oh, *please*, Aunt Elizabeth. I *love* those cats. And they’re the only things left in the world that love me. Please!”
“What’s a cat more or less on two hundred acres?” said Cousin Jimmy, pulling his forked beard. “Take ’em along, Elizabeth.”
Aunt Elizabeth considered for a moment. She couldn’t understand why anybody should want a cat. Aunt Elizabeth was one of those people who never do understand anything unless it is told them in plain language and hammered into their heads. And *then* they understand it only with their brains and not with their hearts.
“You may take *one* of your cats,” she said at last, with the air of a person making a great concession. “One﻿—and no more. No, don’t argue. You may as well learn first as last, Emily, that when *I* say a thing I mean it. That’s enough, Jimmy.”
Cousin Jimmy bit off something he had tried to say, stuck his hands in his pockets, and whistled at the ceiling.
“When she won’t, she won’t﻿—Murray like. We’re all born with that kink in us, small pussy, and you’ll have to put up with it﻿—more by token that you’re full of it yourself, you know. Talk about your not being Murray! The Starr is only skin deep with you.”
“It isn’t﻿—I’m *all* Starr﻿—I *want* to be,” cried Emily. “And, oh, how can I choose between Mike and Saucy Sal?”
This was indeed a problem. Emily wrestled with it all day, her heart bursting. She liked Mike best﻿—there was no doubt of that; but she *couldn’t* leave Saucy Sal to Ellen’s tender mercies. Ellen had always hated Sal; but she rather liked Mike and she would be good to him. Ellen was going back to her own little house in Maywood village and she wanted a cat. At last in the evening, Emily made her bitter decision. She would take Saucy Sal.
“Better take the Tom,” said Cousin Jimmy. “Not so much bother with kittens you know, Emily.”
“Jimmy!” said Aunt Elizabeth sternly. Emily wondered over the sternness. Why weren’t kittens to be spoken of? But she didn’t like to hear Mike called “the Tom.” It sounded insulting, someway.
And she didn’t like the bustle and commotion of packing up.

===Chinese===

“不喜欢；摸蛇还差不多。”
“上头有你母亲留下的一只可爱的老蜡娃娃。”劳拉姨妈说。“我给她打扮打扮给你玩。”
“我不喜欢娃娃——她们不会说话。”艾米丽嚷道。
“猫也不会。”
“哦，才不呢！迈克和调皮的萨尔就会。哦，我*一定*得带他们。哦，*求求*您了，伊丽莎白姨妈。我*爱*这两只猫。这世上爱我的，就剩他们了。求求您了！”
“两百英亩地，多只猫少只猫算什么？”吉米表哥捻着分叉的胡子说。“就让她带上吧，伊丽莎白。”
伊丽莎白姨妈沉吟了一下。她实在不明白为什么会有人想要猫。伊丽莎白姨妈是那样一种人：一件事非得用大白话告诉她、再敲进她脑袋里，她才明白。而且到头来，她也只是脑子明白，心不明白。
“你可以带*一只*猫。”她终于说，一副作出重大让步的口气。“一只——不许再要。行了，别争了。早晓得晚晓得都一样，艾米丽，你趁早明白：*我*说了的话，就是定了的。够了，吉米。”
吉米表哥把想说的话咬回去了，双手插进兜里，对着天花板吹口哨。
“她不肯就是不肯——地道的默里脾气。我们生下来骨头里都拧着这股劲儿，小猫咪，你只好受着——何况你自己一身的这股劲儿，你是晓得的。还说什么你不是默里家的人！你那斯塔尔家的皮子才多薄一层。”
“才不是——我*整个儿*是斯塔尔家的人——我*巴不得*是。”艾米丽喊道。“哦，可我怎么能从迈克和调皮的萨尔中间挑一个呢？”
这可真是个难题。艾米丽心都要裂了，整整翻腾了一天。她最喜欢迈克——这没说的；可她*不能*把调皮的萨尔丢给艾伦（Ellen）去发落。艾伦一直恨萨尔；倒有点喜欢迈克，会好好待他。艾伦要回梅伍德（Maywood）村里她自己的小屋去，她正想要只猫。到了晚上，艾米丽做出了那个苦涩的决定：带调皮的萨尔。
“顶好带那只公猫。”吉米表哥说。“省得小猫崽子添乱，你懂的，艾米丽。”
“吉米！”伊丽莎白姨妈厉声说。艾米丽对这声厉斥好生奇怪。小猫崽子怎么提不得呢？不过她也不喜欢听迈克被叫成“那只公猫”。不知怎么，听着像骂人似的。
她也不喜欢收拾行李的忙乱和折腾。

===Original===

She longed for the old quiet and the sweet, remembered talks with her father. She felt as if he had been thrust far away from her by this influx of Murrays.
“What’s this?” said Aunt Elizabeth suddenly, pausing for a moment in her packing. Emily looked up and saw with dismay that Aunt Elizabeth had in her hands the old account book﻿—that she was opening it﻿—that she was *reading* in it. Emily sprang across the floor and snatched the book.
“You mustn’t read that, Aunt Elizabeth,” she cried indignantly, “that’s mine﻿—my own *private property*.”
“Hoity-toity, Miss Starr,” said Aunt Elizabeth, staring at her, “let me tell you that I have a right to read your books. I am responsible for you now. I am not going to have anything hidden or underhanded, understand that. You have evidently something there that you are ashamed to have seen and I mean to see it. Give me that book.”
“I’m *not* ashamed of it,” cried Emily, backing away, hugging her precious book to her breast. “But I won’t let you﻿—or *anybody*﻿—see it.”
Aunt Elizabeth followed.
“Emily Starr, do you hear what I say? Give me that book﻿—at *once*.”
“No﻿—no!” Emily turned and ran. She would *never* let Aunt Elizabeth see that book. She fled to the kitchen stove﻿—she whisked off a cover﻿—she crammed the book into the glowing fire. It caught and blazed merrily. Emily watched it in agony. It seemed as if part of herself were burning there. But Aunt Elizabeth should never see it﻿—see all the little things she had written and read to Father﻿—all her fancies about the Wind Woman, and Emily-in-the-glass﻿—all her little cat dialogues﻿—all the things she had said in it last night about the Murrays. She watched the leaves shrivel and shudder, as if they were sentient things, and then turn black. A line of white writing came out vividly on one. “Aunt Elizabeth is very cold and *hawty*.” What if Aunt Elizabeth had seen *that*? What if she were seeing it now! Emily glanced apprehensively over her shoulder. No, Aunt Elizabeth had gone back to the room and shut the door with what, in anybody but a Murray, would have been called a bang. The account book was a little heap of white film on the glowing coals. Emily sat down by the stove and cried. She felt as if she had lost something incalculably precious. It was terrible to think that all those dear things were gone.

===Chinese===

她渴念从前的清静，渴念记忆里同爸爸那些甜甜的谈话。她觉得，这一大股涌进来的默里家人，把爸爸远远地挤开了。
“这是什么？”伊丽莎白姨妈忽然说，收拾东西的手停了一下。艾米丽抬头一看，慌了：伊丽莎白姨妈手里拿的正是那本旧账本——她正在翻开——她正在里面*读*。艾米丽一下蹿过地板，把本子夺了过来。
“您不能看那个，伊丽莎白姨妈。”她气愤地喊，“那是我的——我自己的*私人财物*。”
“好大的派头，斯塔尔小姐。”伊丽莎白姨妈瞪着她说，“告诉你，你的本子我有权看。如今我要对你负责。有藏着掖着、偷偷摸摸的事，我不答应，给我记住。你那儿分明有怕人看见的东西，我非看不可。把本子给我。”
“我才不怕人看见呢。”艾米丽喊，一边往后退，把宝贝本子紧紧抱在胸口。“可我不让您看——*谁*都不让看。”
伊丽莎白姨妈逼了上来。
“艾米丽·斯塔尔，听见我的话没有？把本子给我——*马上*。”
“不——不！”艾米丽转身就跑。她*决*不能让伊丽莎白姨妈看到那个本子。她逃进厨房，蹿到炉子跟前——掀开炉盖——把本子塞进了通红的炉火里。火苗一下蹿起来，烧得欢欢实实。艾米丽痛苦地看着。好像她自己的半个身子正在那儿烧着。可伊丽莎白姨妈永远看不着它了——看不着她写在里面、念给爸爸听的那些小东西——她那些关于风之女（the Wind Woman）和镜中艾米丽（Emily-in-the-glass）的遐想——她那些小猫对话——还有她昨晚在本子里写的那些关于默里家的话。她看着纸页蜷缩、发颤，像有知觉的活物似的，然后变黑。有一页上，一行白字清晰地显出来：“伊丽莎白姨妈非常冷淡，非常*傲漫*。”要是伊丽莎白姨妈看见了*那行*可怎么好？要是她这会儿正看见呢！艾米丽提心吊胆地回头看了一眼。没有，伊丽莎白姨妈已经回那间屋去了，还关上了门——那动静，搁在默里家以外任何人身上，都得叫一声“砰地摔门”。账本在通红的煤火上只剩一小堆白灰。艾米丽在炉边坐下来哭了。她觉得自己失去了无比珍贵的东西。一想到那些心爱的东西全没了，就太可怕了。

===Original===

She could never write them again﻿—not just the same; and if she could she wouldn’t dare﻿—she would never dare to write *anything* again, if Aunt Elizabeth must see everything. Father never insisted on seeing them. She liked to read them to *him*﻿—but if she hadn’t wanted to do it he would have made her. Suddenly Emily, with tears glistening on her cheeks, wrote a line in an imaginary account book.
“Aunt Elizabeth is cold and hawty; and she is *not fair*.”
Next morning, while Cousin Jimmy was tying the boxes at the back of the double-seated buggy, and Aunt Elizabeth was giving Ellen her final instructions, Emily said goodbye to everything﻿—to the Rooster Pine and Adam-and-Eve﻿—“they’ll miss me so when I’m gone; there won’t be anyone here to love them,” she said wistfully﻿—to the spider crack in the kitchen window﻿—to the old wing-chair﻿—to the bed of striped grass﻿—to the silver birch-ladies. Then she went upstairs to the window of her own old room. That little window had always seemed to Emily to open on a world of wonder. In the burned account book there had been one piece of which she was especially proud. “A deskripshun of the vew from my Window.” She had sat there and dreamed; at night she used to kneel there and say her little prayers. Sometimes the stars shone through it sometimes the rain beat against it﻿—sometimes the little greybirds and swallows visited it﻿—sometimes airy fragrances floated in from apple and lilac blossom﻿—sometimes the Wind Woman laughed and sighed and sang and whistled round it﻿—Emily had heard her there in the dark nights and in wild, white winter storms. She did not say goodbye to the Wind Woman, for she knew the Wind Woman would be at New Moon, too; but she said goodbye to the little window and the green hill she had loved, and to her fairy-haunted barrens and to little Emily-in-the-glass. There might be another Emily-in-the-glass at New Moon, but she wouldn’t be the same one. And she unpinned from the wall and stowed away in her pocket the picture of the ball dress she had cut from a fashion sheet. It was such a wonderful dress﻿—all white lace and wreaths of rosebuds, with a long, long, train of lace flounces that must reach clear across a room. Emily had pictured herself a thousand times wearing that dress, sweeping, a queen of beauty, across a ballroom floor.
Downstairs they were waiting for her.

===Chinese===

她再也不能把它们写出来了——一模一样地写不出来了；就算写得出来她也不敢了——要是伊丽莎白姨妈什么都要看，她就再也不敢写*任何*东西了。爸爸从不硬要看。她喜欢念给*他*听——可她要是不愿意，他也决不勉强。忽然间，艾米丽颊上还挂着亮晶晶的泪，在一本想象中的账本上写下一行字：
“伊丽莎白姨妈冷淡，傲漫；而且她*不公道*。”
第二天早晨，吉米表哥在双座马车的后头捆箱子，伊丽莎白姨妈在对艾伦做最后的吩咐，艾米丽便同每一样东西道别——同公鸡松和亚当同夏娃道别——“我走了以后他们会想我想得不得了的；这儿再没有人来爱他们了。”她怅怅地说——同厨房窗上那道蛛网似的裂纹道别——同那把老高背椅道别——同那片条纹草地道别——同那些银色的白桦女士们道别。然后她上楼走到自己老房间的窗前。那扇小窗，在艾米丽眼里一直像开向一个奇妙的世界。烧掉的账本里有一篇她顶得意的。“《一偏从我窗前看的风井的描写》”。她曾在那儿坐着做梦；夜里她常跪在那儿做她的小祷告。有时星星透过它闪耀，有时雨点打在它上面——有时小灰雀和燕子来访问它——有时苹果花和丁香花的轻盈香气飘进来——有时风之女绕着它笑、叹、唱、吹哨——在黑夜里，在狂风卷雪的冬夜，艾米丽都在那儿听见过她。她不同风之女道别，因为她知道，风之女在新月庄园也会在的；可她同那扇小窗道了别，同她心爱的绿山丘道了别，同她那些仙灵出没的荒地道了别，同小小的镜中艾米丽道了别。新月庄园也许另有一个镜中艾米丽，可那不会是同一个了。她还从墙上取下那张从时装画页上剪下来的舞会礼服图，藏进口袋。那是一件多么美的裙子啊——通身白蕾丝，缀满玫瑰花蕾的花环，还有一条好长好长的蕾丝荷叶边拖裾，长得能横贯整整一个房间。艾米丽一千次想象自己穿着那件裙子，像一位美的王后，翩然掠过舞厅的地板。
楼下，他们在等她。

===Original===

Emily said goodbye to Ellen Greene rather indifferently﻿—she had never liked Ellen Greene at any time, and since the night Ellen had told her her father was going to die she had hated and feared her.
Ellen amazed Emily by bursting into tears and hugging her﻿—begging her not to forget her﻿—asking her to write to her﻿—calling her “my blessed child.”
“I am not your blessed child,” said Emily, “but I will write to you. And will you be very good to Mike?”
“I b’lieve you feel worse over leaving that cat than you do over leaving me,” sniffed Ellen.
“Why, of course I do,” said Emily, amazed that there could be any question about it.
It took all her resolution not to cry when she bade farewell to Mike, who was curled up on the sun-warm grass at the back door.
“Maybe I’ll see you again sometime,” she whispered as she hugged him. “I’m sure *good* pussy cats go to heaven.”
Then they were off in the double-seated buggy with its fringed canopy, always affected by the Murrays of New Moon. Emily had never driven in anything so splendid before. She had never had many drives. Once or twice her father had borrowed Mr. Hubbard’s old buckboard and grey pony and driven to Charlottetown. The buckboard was rattly and the pony slow, but Father had talked to her all the way and made the road a wonder.
Cousin Jimmy and Aunt Elizabeth sat in front, the latter very imposing in black lace bonnet and mantle. Aunt Laura and Emily occupied the seat behind, with Saucy Sal between them in a basket, shrieking piteously.
Emily glanced back as they drove up the grassy lane, and thought the little, old, brown house in the hollow had a brokenhearted look. She longed to run back and comfort it. In spite of her resolution, the tears came into her eyes; but Aunt Laura put a kid-gloved hand across Sal’s basket and caught Emily’s in a close, understanding squeeze.
“Oh, I just love you, Aunt Laura,” whispered Emily.
And Aunt Laura’s eyes were very, very blue and deep and kind.

===Chinese===

艾米丽同艾伦·格林（Ellen Greene）道别时相当冷淡——她从来就没喜欢过艾伦·格林，而自从艾伦告诉她爸爸要死的那晚起，她就又恨她又怕她了。
艾伦竟泪如泉涌地抱住她，倒叫艾米丽吃了一惊——求她别忘了自己——求她给自己写信——一口一个“我那有福的孩子”。
“我不是您有福的孩子，”艾米丽说，“可我会给您写信的。您会对迈克好一点儿吗？”
“我瞧你丢下那只猫，比丢下我还难受哩。”艾伦吸着鼻子说。
“可不是嘛，当然啦。”艾米丽说，奇怪这怎么还会有疑问。
向后门口蜷在晒暖的草上的迈克告别时，她拿出全部的定力才没有哭出来。
“兴许我往后再见得着你。”她抱着他悄悄说。“我敢说，*好*猫咪是能上天堂的。”
随后他们便坐上那辆带流苏车篷的双座马车出发了——新月庄园的默里家人向来偏爱这种车。艾米丽从前从没坐过这么气派的车。她出门坐车的次数本来就不多。有一两回，爸爸借了哈伯德先生（Mr. Hubbard）的旧平板马车和灰矮马，赶着去夏洛特顿（Charlottetown）。那平板车嘎啦嘎啦响，那灰马慢吞吞，可爸爸一路同她说话，把那条路说成了一个奇迹。
吉米表哥和伊丽莎白姨妈坐在前头，后者戴着黑蕾丝软帽、披着黑蕾丝披风，好不威严。劳拉姨妈和艾米丽坐在后面的座位上，两人中间的篮子里装着调皮的萨尔，凄凄地嚎着。
马车驶上青草小径时，艾米丽回头望了一眼，觉得谷中那座棕色的小老屋一副心碎的样子。她真想跑回去安慰它。她到底没能忍住，眼泪涌上了眼睛；可劳拉姨妈把一只戴小山羊皮手套的手搭在萨尔的篮子上，又把艾米丽的手紧紧一握，握得那样会心。
“哦，我就是爱您，劳拉姨妈。”艾米丽悄悄说。
劳拉姨妈的眼睛，是那么、那么的蓝，那么深，那么慈爱。

## VI New Moon / 六 新月庄园
===Original===

Emily found the drive through the blossomy June world pleasant. Nobody talked much; even Saucy Sal had subsided into the silence of despair; now and then Cousin Jimmy made a remark, more to himself, as it seemed, than to anybody else. Sometimes Aunt Elizabeth answered it, sometimes not. She always spoke crisply and used no unnecessary words.
They stopped in Charlottetown and had dinner. Emily, who had had no appetite since her father’s death, could not eat the roast beef which the boardinghouse waitress put before her. Whereupon Aunt Elizabeth whispered mysteriously to the waitress, who went away and presently returned with a plateful of delicate, cold chicken﻿—fine white slices, beautifully trimmed with lettuce frills.
“Can you eat *that*?” said Aunt Elizabeth sternly, as to a culprit at the bar.
“I’ll﻿—try,” whispered Emily.
She was too frightened just then to say more, but by the time she had forced down some of the chicken she had made up her small mind that a certain matter must be put right.
“Aunt Elizabeth,” she said.
“Hey, what?” said Aunt Elizabeth, directing her steel-blue eyes straight at her niece’s troubled ones.
“I would like you to understand,” said Emily, speaking very primly and precisely so that she would be sure to get things right, “that it was not because I did not like the roast beef I did not eat it. I was not hungry at all; and I just et some of the chicken to oblige you, not because I liked it any better.”
“Children should eat what is put before them and never turn up their noses at good, wholesome food,” said Aunt Elizabeth severely. So Emily felt that Aunt Elizabeth had not understood after all and she was unhappy about it.
After dinner Aunt Elizabeth announced to Aunt Laura that they would do some shopping.
“We must get some things for the child,” she said.
“Oh, please don’t call me ‘the child,’ ” exclaimed Emily. “It makes me feel as if I didn’t belong anywhere. Don’t you like my name, Aunt Elizabeth? Mother thought it so pretty. And I don’t need any ‘things.’ I have two whole sets of underclothes﻿—only one is patched.﻿—”
“S-s-sh!” said Cousin Jimmy, gently kicking Emily’s shins under the table.
Cousin Jimmy only meant that she would better let Aunt Elizabeth buy “things” for her when she was in the humour for it; but Emily thought he was rebuking her for mentioning such matters as underclothes and subsided in scarlet conviction.

===Chinese===

穿过这个繁花盛开的六月世界的一路车程，艾米丽觉得挺愉快。谁都不大多说话；连调皮的萨尔（Saucy Sal）也沉入了绝望的静默；吉米表哥时不时冒出一句，可看那样子，多半是说给自己听的。伊丽莎白姨妈有时答一句，有时不答。她说起话来干脆利落，从不多用一个字。
他们在夏洛特顿（Charlottetown）停下来吃午饭。自从爸爸去世，艾米丽一直没有胃口，宿舍客栈的女招待端上来的烤牛肉她吃不下。于是伊丽莎白姨妈神神秘秘地同女招待咬了阵耳朵，女招待走开，不多时端回一盘精美的冷鸡肉——一片片白生生的细肉，配着生菜叶边，装点得漂漂亮亮。
“*那个*你能吃吗？”伊丽莎白姨妈严厉地说，像审问犯人似的。
“我——试试吧。”艾米丽小声说。
她那会儿吓坏了，不敢多说；可等她强咽下几口鸡肉，她已拿定了她那小小的主意：有件事非说清楚不可。
“伊丽莎白姨妈。”她说。
“嗯，什么？”伊丽莎白姨妈说，一双钢蓝色的眼睛直直地射向侄女那双不安的眼睛。
“我想让您明白，”艾米丽说，说得又规矩又仔细，好把话一字不差地说对，“我不吃那烤牛肉，不是因为我讨厌它。我一点儿也不饿；我方才吃了些鸡肉，是为了让您高兴，可不是因为我更爱吃它。”
“孩子们就该把端上来的东西吃掉，对好的、滋养的食物不许挑三拣四。”伊丽莎白姨妈严肃地说。于是艾米丽觉得，伊丽莎白姨妈到底还是没明白，她为这个很不快活。
饭后，伊丽莎白姨妈对劳拉姨妈（Aunt Laura）宣布，他们要去买些东西。
“得给孩子置办点东西。”她说。
“哦，请您别叫我‘孩子’。”艾米丽嚷道。“这让我觉得自己哪儿都不属于。您不喜欢我的名字吗，伊丽莎白姨妈？妈妈觉得它可美呢。再说我不需要什么‘东西’。我有整整两套内衣——只有一套打着补丁。——”
“嘘——嘘！”吉米表哥（Cousin Jimmy）说，在桌子底下轻轻踢了踢艾米丽的小腿。
吉米表哥的意思不过是：趁伊丽莎白姨妈有这份兴致，最好由着她去买那些“东西”；可艾米丽以为他是在责怪她提起内衣这种事，登时红透了脸，不敢作声了。

===Original===

Aunt Elizabeth went on talking to Laura as if she had not heard.
“She must not wear that cheap black dress in Blair Water. You could sift oatmeal through it. It is nonsense expecting a child of ten to wear black at all. I shall get her a nice white dress with black sash for good, and some black-and-white-check gingham for school. Jimmy, we’ll leave the child with you. Look after her.”
Cousin Jimmy’s method of looking after her was to take her to a restaurant down street and fill her up with ice-cream. Emily had never had many chances at ice-cream and she needed no urging, even with lack of appetite, to eat two saucerfuls. Cousin Jimmy eyed her with satisfaction.
“No use my getting anything for you that Elizabeth could see,” he said. “But she can’t see what is inside of you. Make the most of your chance, for goodness alone knows when you’ll get any more.”
“Do you never have ice-cream at New Moon?”
Cousin Jimmy shook his head.
“Your Aunt Elizabeth doesn’t like newfangled things. In the house, we belong to fifty years ago, but on the farm she has to give way. In the house﻿—candles; in the dairy, her grandmother’s big pans to set the milk in. But, pussy, New Moon is a pretty good place after all. You’ll like it some day.”
“Are there any fairies there?” asked Emily, wistfully.
“The woods are full of ’em,” said Cousin Jimmy. “And so are the columbines in the old orchard. We grow columbines there on purpose for the fairies.”
Emily sighed. Since she was eight she had known there were no fairies anywhere nowadays; yet she hadn’t quite given up the hope that one or two might linger in old-fashioned, out-of-the-way spots. And where so likely as at New Moon?
“Really-truly fairies?” she questioned.
“Why, you know, if a fairy was really-truly it wouldn’t *be* a fairy,” said Uncle Jimmy seriously. “Could it, now?”
Before Emily could think this out the aunts returned and soon they were all on the road again. It was sunset when they came to Blair Water﻿—a rosy sunset that flooded the long, sandy seacoast with colour and brought red road and fir-darkened hill out in fleeting clearness of outline. Emily looked about her on her new environment and found it good.

===Chinese===

伊丽莎白姨妈只管同劳拉说下去，好像什么也没听见。
“她可不能穿着那身廉价的黑裙子在布莱尔沃特（Blair Water）走动。那料子稀得能筛燕麦片。指望一个十岁的孩子穿孝，本来就是胡闹。我要给她买一件配黑腰带的漂亮白裙子当好衣裳，再买些黑白格子的格子布做上学穿。吉米，孩子交给你了。照看着她。”
吉米表哥照看她的法子，是带她上街尾一家餐馆，拿冰淇淋把她填饱。艾米丽从前难得吃上冰淇淋，虽没胃口，也不用劝，两小碟下肚。吉米表哥满意地瞅着她。
“凡是伊丽莎白看得见的东西，我给你买也是白买。”他说。“可她看不见你肚子里装的东西。抓紧机会吃个够吧，天晓得你下一回要等到什么时候。”
“新月庄园（New Moon）从来不冰淇淋吗？”
吉米表哥摇摇头。
“你伊丽莎白姨妈不喜欢新鲜玩意儿。在宅子里，我们还活在五十年前；可在田庄上，她就只好让步了。宅子里——点蜡烛；奶房里，还用她祖母的大浅盆搁奶。不过，小猫咪，新月庄园到底是个挺好的地方。往后你会喜欢上的。”
“那儿有仙子吗？”艾米丽向往地问。
“树林里满是呢。”吉米表哥说。“老果园里的耧斗菜里也满是。我们在那儿种耧斗菜，就是专门给仙子们种的。”
艾米丽叹了口气。打八岁起她就知道，如今这世道哪儿都没有仙子了；可她还没死心，总盼着也许有一两个，还恋着些老派的、偏僻的角落。还有哪儿比新月庄园更有可能呢？
“真真正正的仙子？”她又问。
“啐，你知道的，仙子要是真真正正了，那还*算*仙子吗？”吉米表哥一本正经地说。“你说是不是？”
艾米丽还没想明白这话，两位姨妈回来了，不多时大家又上了路。他们到布莱尔沃特时正是日落——一个玫瑰色的日落，把长长的沙质海岸淹在一片彩色的光里，红土路和冷杉染暗的山丘，刹那间轮廓分明地显现出来。艾米丽环顾她的新环境，觉得它真好。

===Original===

She saw a big house peering whitely through a veil of tall old trees﻿—no mushroom growth of yesterday’s birches but trees that had loved and been loved by three generations﻿—a glimpse of silver water glistening through the dark spruces﻿—that was the Blair Water itself, she knew﻿—and a tall, golden-white church spire shooting up above the maple woods in the valley below. But it was none of these that brought her the flash﻿—*that* came with the sudden glimpse of the dear, friendly, little dormer window peeping through vines on the roof﻿—and right over it, in the opalescent sky, a real new moon, golden and slender. Emily was tingling all over with it as Cousin Jimmy lifted her from the buggy and carried her into the kitchen.
She sat on a long wooden bench that was satin-smooth with age and scrubbing, and watched Aunt Elizabeth lighting candles here and there, in great, shining, brass candlesticks﻿—on the shelf between the windows, on the high dresser where the row of blue and white plates began to wink her a friendly welcome, on the long table in the corner. And as she lighted them, elvish “rabbits’ candles” flashed up amid the trees outside the windows.
Emily had never seen a kitchen like this before. It had dark wooden walls and low ceiling, with black rafters crossing it, from which hung hams and sides of bacon and bunches of herbs and new socks and mittens, and many other things, the names and uses of which Emily could not imagine. The sanded floor was spotlessly white, but the boards had been scrubbed away through the years until the knots in them stuck up all over in funny little bosses, and in front of the stove they had sagged, making a queer, shallow little hollow. In one corner of the ceiling was a large square hole which looked black and spookish in the candlelight, and made her feel creepy. *Something* might pop down out of a hole like that if one hadn’t behaved just right, you know. And candles cast such queer wavering shadows. Emily didn’t know whether she liked the New Moon kitchen or not. It was an interesting place﻿—and she rather thought she would like to describe it in the old account book, if it hadn’t been burned﻿—but Emily suddenly found herself trembling on the verge of tears.
“Cold?” said Aunt Laura kindly. “These June evenings are chilly yet. Come into the sitting-room﻿—Jimmy has kindled a fire in the stove there.”

===Chinese===

她望见一座白色的大房子，从高大的老树的纱幕后面探出脸来——不是昨天才冒出来的白荠新荐，而是被三代人爱过、也爱着三代人的树——一线银色的水光在黑黢黢的云杉间闪烁——她知道，那就是布莱尔沃特本身——还有一座金黄发白的教堂尖顶，从谷底的枫树林里直插上来。可给她带来灵光（the flash）的，哪一样都不是——*它*是在她猛然瞥见屋顶上那扇亲切可爱的小老虎窗、正从藤蔓里探头探脑的那一瞬来的——就在它的正上方，乳彩的天空里，挂着一弯真真正正的新月，金色的，纤纤的。等吉米表哥把她抱下马车、抱进厨房时，艾米丽还浑身为此麻酥酥地发着颤。
她坐在一条长长的木凳上，那凳子叫岁月和刷洗磨得像缎子一样光滑；她看着伊丽莎白姨妈在各处点蜡烛，插在一对对亮闪闪的大铜烛台上——点在两窗之间的搁板上，点在高高的碗柜上，那柜上一排蓝白盘子开始朝她眨眼睛、友好地欢迎她，又点在角落里的长桌上。随着烛光亮起，窗外树林间，小妖精似的“兔子的蜡烛”也一盏盏闪了起来。
艾米丽从没见过这样的厨房。深色的木墙，低低的天花板，黑房梁纵横交错，梁上挂着火腿、腌肉、一束束干香草、新袜子连着露指手套，还有许多别的东西，名字和用处艾米丽都猜不出。铺着细沙的地板白得没有一点儿污渍，只是板子经年累月刷洗，都磨薄了，节疤一颗颗凸起来，满地都是些滑稽的小鼓包，炉子跟前还凹陷下去，成了一小片古怪的浅坑。天花板一角有个四四方方的大洞，烛光里黑黢黢、阴森森的，让她心里发毛。要是谁行事不端正，保不齐就有*什么东西*从那样的洞里一下子跳下来呢，你知道的。再说烛影摇摇曳曳，也真够怪的。艾米丽不知道自己喜不喜欢新月庄园的这间厨房。这是个有意思的地方——她倒真想把它写进那本旧账本里去，要是它没烧掉的话——可艾米丽忽然发觉自己正颤颤地站在眼泪的边沿上。
“冷吗？”劳拉姨妈和气地说。“六月的晚上还凉着呢。进起居室来吧——吉米在炉子里生上火了。”

===Original===

Emily, fighting desperately for self-control, went into the sitting-room. It was much more cheerful than the kitchen. The floor was covered with gay-striped homespun, the table had a bright crimson cloth, the walls were hung with pretty, diamond-patterned paper, the curtains were of wonderful pale-red damask with a design of white ferns scattered all over them. They looked very rich and imposing and Murray-like. Emily had never seen such curtains before. But best of all were the friendly gleams and flickers from the jolly hardwood fire in the open stove that mellowed the ghostly candlelight with something warm and rosy-golden. Emily toasted her toes before it and felt reviving interest in her surroundings. What lovely little leaded glass doors closed the china closets on either side of the high, black, polished mantel! What a funny, delightful shadow the carved ornament on the sideboard cast on the wall behind it﻿—just like a negro’s side-face, Emily decided. What mysteries might lurk behind the chintz-lined glass doors of the bookcase! Books were Emily’s friends wherever she found them. She flew over to the bookcase and opened the door. But before she could see more than the backs of rather ponderous volumes, Aunt Elizabeth came in, with a mug of milk and a plate whereon lay two little oatmeal cakes.
“Emily,” said Aunt Elizabeth sternly, “shut that door. Remember that after this you are not to meddle with things that don’t belong to you.”
“I thought books belonged to everybody,” said Emily.
“Ours don’t,” said Aunt Elizabeth, contriving to convey the impression that New Moon books were in a class by themselves. “Here is your supper, Emily. We are all so tired that we are just having a lunch. Eat it and then we will go to bed.”
Emily drank the milk and worried down the oatcakes, still gazing about her. How pretty the wallpaper was, with the garland of roses inside the gilt diamond! Emily wondered if she could “see it in the air.” She tried﻿—yes, she could﻿—there it hung, a yard from her eyes, a little fairy pattern, suspended in midair like a screen. Emily had discovered that she possessed this odd knack when she was six.

===Chinese===

艾米丽拼命稳住自己，走进了起居室。这儿比厨房亮堂多了。地上铺着条纹鲜亮的家织毯，桌上罩一块鲜红的大红桌布，墙上贴着好看的菱形花纹墙纸，窗帘是妙极了的浅红锦缎，上面撒满了白蕨的花样。瞧着又华贵又气派，一派默里家风。艾米丽从没见过这样的窗帘。可最好的，还是敞开的炉子里那一炉欢快的硬柴火，友善的光焰闪闪烁烁，把幽幽的烛光暖和过来，添上一层玫瑰金色。艾米丽凑上去烤了烤脚趾头，对四周又来了兴致。那高高的、乌黑发亮的壁炉架两边，瓷柜上装着多可爱的小铅条玻璃门啊！餐边柜上的雕花在背后墙上投下的影子多逗人喜欢——活像一张黑人的侧脸，艾米丽认定。书柜那衬着印花布的玻璃门后头，又藏着多少秘密呢！书在哪儿都是艾米丽的朋友。她飞跑到书柜前，打开了柜门。可她刚看清几部大部头书脊的模样，伊丽莎白姨妈就进来了，端着一缸牛奶、一只盘子，盘里搁着两块小小的燕麦饼。
“艾米丽，”伊丽莎白姨妈严厉地说，“把柜门关上。记住，往后不许乱动不属于你的东西。”
“我原以为书是属于每个人的。”艾米丽说。
“我们的书不是。”伊丽莎白姨妈说，硬是让人觉出这么一层意思：新月庄园的书，自成一格。“这是你的晚饭，艾米丽。我们都乏透了，今晚只随便吃点儿。吃了就上床睡觉。”
艾米丽喝了牛奶，勉勉强强咽下燕麦饼，一面仍四下打量。这墙纸多好看呀，金线的菱形里绕着玫瑰花环！艾米丽想，不知能不能把它“悬在空气里看出来”。她试了试——行，能——它就挂在那儿，离她眼睛一码远，一小幅仙子图案，像一面纱屏悬在半空。这手古怪的本事，艾米丽六岁上就发现自己有了。

===Original===

By a certain movement of the muscles of her eyes, which she could never describe, she could produce a tiny replica of the wallpaper in the air before her﻿—could hold it there and look at it as long as she liked﻿—could shift it back and forth, to any distance she chose, making it larger or smaller as it went farther away or came nearer. It was one of her secret joys when she went into a new room anywhere to “see the paper in the air.” And this New Moon paper made the prettiest fairy paper she had ever seen.
“What are you staring at nothing in that queer way for?” demanded Aunt Elizabeth, suddenly returning.
Emily shrank into herself. She couldn’t explain to Aunt Elizabeth﻿—Aunt Elizabeth would be like Ellen Greene and say she was “crazy.”
“I﻿—I wasn’t staring at nothing.”
“Don’t contradict. I say you were,” retorted Aunt Elizabeth. “Don’t do it again. It gives your face an unnatural expression. Come now﻿—we will go upstairs. You are to sleep with me.”
Emily gave a gasp of dismay. She had hoped it might be with Aunt Laura. Sleeping with Aunt Elizabeth seemed a very formidable thing. But she dared not protest. They went up to Aunt Elizabeth’s big, sombre bedroom where there was dark, grim wallpaper that could never be transformed into a fairy curtain, a high black bureau, topped with a tiny swing-mirror, so far above her that there could be no Emily-in-the-glass, tightly closed windows with dark-green curtains, a high bedstead with a dark-green canopy, and a huge, fat, smothering featherbed, with high, hard pillows.
Emily stood still, gazing about her.
“Why don’t you get undressed?” asked Aunt Elizabeth.
“I﻿—I don’t like to undress before you,” faltered Emily.
Aunt Elizabeth looked at Emily through her cold, spectacled eyes.
“Take off your clothes, *at once*,” she said.
Emily obeyed, tingling with anger and shame. It was abominable﻿—taking off her clothes while Aunt Elizabeth stood and watched her. The outrage of it was unspeakable. It was even harder to say her prayers before Aunt Elizabeth. Emily felt that it was not much good to pray under such circumstances. Father’s God seemed very far away and she suspected that Aunt Elizabeth’s was too much like Ellen Greene’s.
“Get into bed,” said Aunt Elizabeth, turning down the clothes.
Emily glanced at the shrouded window.
“Aren’t you going to open the window, Aunt Elizabeth?”

===Chinese===

只要让眼部的肌肉那么一动——那动法她说不上来——她就能在眼前的空气里变出一小张墙纸的复影来——能让它悬在那儿，爱看多久看多久——能把它移远移近，随她高兴，移远了就小些，移近了就大些。每到一处新屋子，“把墙纸悬在空气里看”，是她的一桩隐秘的乐事。而新月庄园的这张墙纸，是她见过的最漂亮的仙子纸。
“你那样怪模怪样地瞪着空气干什么？”伊丽莎白姨妈忽然回来了，喝问。
艾米丽把自己缩了回去。她没法向伊丽莎白姨妈解释——伊丽莎白姨妈准会跟艾伦·格林（Ellen Greene）一个样，说她“疯疯癴癴”。
“我——我没瞪空气。”
“不许顶嘴。我说你瞪了就是瞪了。”伊丽莎白姨妈驳道。“不许再犯。你那样脸相都不自然了。好了——上楼去吧。你跟我睡。”
艾米丽倒抽一口凉气，心里凉了半截。她本来盼着是同劳拉姨妈睡。同伊丽莎白姨妈睡，看来是桩顶顶可怕的事。可她不敢不从。他们上楼进了伊丽莎白姨妈那间又大又暗的卧室：墙纸深暗冷峻，永远变不成仙子的纱帘；高高的黑衣柜，顶上一面小小的摆动镜，高得她够不着，出不来镜中艾米丽（Emily-in-the-glass）；窗子关得严严实实，挂着墨绿窗帘；高架床上罩着墨绿的床幔；还有一床又大又厚、能把人闷死的鸭绒被，枕头又高又硬。
艾米丽站着不动，四下打量。
“怎么还不上来脱衣服？”伊丽莎白姨妈问。
“我——我不喜欢当着您脱。”艾米丽哦嚅道。
伊丽莎白姨妈隔着她那副冷冷的、戴眼镜的眼睛看着艾米丽。
“把衣服脱了，*马上*。”她说。
艾米丽从了，又羞又恼，浑身发烫。真是岂有此理——伊丽莎白姨妈站在那儿看着她脱衣服。这份欺辱没法说出口。比这更难的，是当着伊丽莎白姨妈做祷告。艾米丽觉得，这种情形下祷告也没多大用处。爸爸的上帝显得很远很远，她疑心伊丽莎白姨妈的上帝，同艾伦·格林的那个也差不多。
“上床。”伊丽莎白姨妈说着，把被子翻开。
艾米丽瞥了一眼遮得严严实实的窗子。
“您不把窗子开一开吗，伊丽莎白姨妈？”

===Original===

Aunt Elizabeth looked at Emily as if the latter had suggested removing the roof.
“Open the window﻿—and let in the night air!” she exclaimed. “Certainly not!”
“Father and I always had our window open,” cried Emily.
“No wonder he died of consumption,” said Aunt Elizabeth. “Night air is poison.”
“What air is there at night but night air?” asked Emily.
“Emily,” said Aunt Elizabeth icily, “get﻿—into﻿—bed.”
Emily got in.
But it was utterly impossible to sleep, lying there in that engulfing bed that seemed to swallow her up, with that cloud of blackness above her and not a gleam of light anywhere﻿—and Aunt Elizabeth lying beside her, long and stiff and bony.
“I feel as if I was in bed with a griffin,” thought Emily. “Oh﻿—oh﻿—oh﻿—I’m going to cry﻿—I know I am.”
Desperately and vainly she strove to keep the tears back﻿—they *would* come. She felt utterly alone and lonely﻿—there in that darkness, with an alien, hostile world all around her﻿—for it seemed hostile now. And there was such a strange, mysterious, mournful sound in the air﻿—far away, yet clear. It was the murmur of the sea, but Emily did not know that and it frightened her. Oh, for her little bed at home﻿—oh, for Father’s soft breathing in the room﻿—oh, for the dancing friendliness of well-known stars shining down through her open window! She *must* go back﻿—she couldn’t stay here﻿—she would never be happy here! But there wasn’t any “back” to go to﻿—no home﻿—no father﻿—. A great sob burst from her﻿—another followed and then another. It was no use to clench her hands and set her teeth﻿—and chew the inside of her cheeks﻿—nature conquered pride and determination and had her way.
“What are you crying for?” asked Aunt Elizabeth.
To tell the truth Aunt Elizabeth felt quite as uncomfortable and disjointed as Emily did. She was not used to a bedfellow; she didn’t want to sleep with Emily any more than Emily wanted to sleep with her. But she considered it quite impossible that the child should be put off by herself in one of the big, lonely New Moon rooms; and Laura was a poor sleeper, easily disturbed; children always kicked, Elizabeth Murray had heard. So there was nothing to do but take Emily in with her; and when she had sacrificed comfort and inclination to do her unwelcome duty this ungrateful and unsatisfactory child was not contented.
“I asked you what you were crying for, Emily?” she repeated.

===Chinese===

伊丽莎白姨妈看着艾米丽，好像她提议把屋顶掀了似的。
“开窗——放进夜气来！”她囔道。“绝对不行！”
“爸爸和我总是开着窗睡的。”艾米丽喊道。
“怪不得他死于肺痨（consumption）。”伊丽莎白姨妈说。“夜气是毒药。”
“夜里除了夜气，还有别的什么气吗？”艾米丽问。
“艾米丽，”伊丽莎白姨妈冷冰冰地说，“上——床——去。”
艾米丽上了床。
可躺在那样一张要把人吞下去的床里，头顶一团漆黑，四下没有一丝光亮——身边躺着伊丽莎白姨妈，长长的、僵僵的、一把骨头——要睡着是万万不能的。
“我觉得像跟一头鹰头狮睡在一床。”艾米丽想。“哦——哦——哦——我要哭了——我知道我要哭了。”
她拼命想把眼泪忍回去——白费——它们*偏*要来。她觉得说不出的孤单冷清——在那片黑暗里，四围是一个陌生的、怀着敌意的世界——如今它就是显得怀着敌意。空气里还有一种又古怪、又神秘、又悲凉的声音——远远的，却听得清。那是大海的低语，可艾米丽不知道，只被它吓着。哦，要是能在家里自己的小床上——哦，要是屋里还有爸爸轻轻的呼吸——哦，要是那些熟识的星星还透过敞开的窗子朝下眨着友善的眼睛！她*必须*回去——她待不下去——她在这儿永远快活不起来！可是再没有“回去”的地方了——没有家——没有爸爸——。一声大哭从她胸腔里迸出来——跟着又是一声，又一声。攢紧拳头、咬紧牙关都没用——腮帮子里的肉都咬了——天性到底压倒了傲气和狠劲，由着它去了。
“你哭什么？”伊丽莎白姨妈问。
说句实话，伊丽莎白姨妈这会儿的不自在、不痛快，一点也不比艾米丽少。她不惯同人生睡；艾米丽不想同她睡，她同样不想同艾米丽睡。可她认为，把孩子一个人打发到新月庄园哪间空旷冷清的大屋里去睡，是万万不行的；劳拉觉轻，容易吵醒；小孩子睡觉总要踢人，伊丽莎白·默里是听说过的。所以只好把艾米丽弄到自己房里来；她牺牲了安逸、屈从了心意，尽了这桩不情愿的本分，这孩子倒好，不知好歹，还不安生。
“我问你哭什么呢，艾米丽？”她又问了一遍。

===Original===

“I’m﻿—homesick, I guess,” sobbed Emily.
Aunt Elizabeth was annoyed.
“A nice home you had to be homesick for,” she said sharply.
“It﻿—it wasn’t as elegant﻿—as New Moon,” sobbed Emily, “but﻿—*Father* was there. I guess I’m Father-sick, Aunt Elizabeth. Didn’t you feel awfully lonely when *your* father died?”
Elizabeth Murray involuntarily remembered the ashamed, smothered feeling of relief when old Archibald Murray had died﻿—the handsome, intolerant, autocratic old man who had ruled his family with a rod of iron all his life and had made existence at New Moon miserable with the petulant tyranny of the five years of invalidism that had closed his career. The surviving Murrays had behaved impeccably, and wept decorously, and printed a long and flattering obituary. But had one genuine feeling of regret followed Archibald Murray to his tomb? Elizabeth did not like the memory and was angry with Emily for evoking it.
“I was resigned to the will of Providence,” she said coldly. “Emily, you must understand right now that you are to be grateful and obedient and show your appreciation of what is being done for you. I won’t have tears and repining. What would you have done if you had no friends to take you in? Answer me that.”
“I suppose I would have starved to death,” admitted Emily﻿—instantly beholding a dramatic vision of herself lying dead, looking exactly like the pictures she had seen in one of Ellen Greene’s missionary magazines depicting the victims of an Indian famine.
“Not exactly﻿—but you would have been sent to some orphanage where you would have been half-starved, probably. You little know what you have escaped. You have come to a good home where you will be cared for and educated properly.”
Emily did not altogether like the sound of being “educated properly.” But she said humbly,
“I know it was very good of you to bring me to New Moon, Aunt Elizabeth. And I won’t bother you long, you know. I’ll soon be grown-up and able to earn my own living. What do you think is the earliest age a person can be called grown-up, Aunt Elizabeth?”
“You needn’t think about that,” said Aunt Elizabeth shortly. “The Murray women have never been under any necessity for earning their own living. All we require of you is to be a good and contented child and to conduct yourself with becoming prudence and modesty.”
This sounded terribly hard.

===Chinese===

“我——大概是想家了。”艾米丽抽抽噎噎地说。
伊丽莎白姨妈恼了。
“你想的那个家，可真好呢。”她尖刻地说。
“它——它没有新月庄园这样——讲究，”艾米丽哽咽着说，“可是——*爸爸*在那儿。我大概是想爸爸了，伊丽莎白姨妈。*您*爸爸去世的时候，您不觉得孤零零得难受吗？”
伊丽莎白·默里不由自主地想起了老阿奇博尔德·默里去世时，自己那股又羞愧、又压抑不住的如释重负之感——那个漂亮、专断、说一不二的老头子，一辈子用铁腕统治着这个家，临了五年病人脾气作威作福，把新月庄园的日子搜得苦不堪言，了此一生。活下来的默里家人个个举止无可指摘，哭也哭得体体面面，还登了一篇又长又美的讦文。可当真有一分真心的哀恸，随阿奇博尔德·默里进了坟墓吗？伊丽莎白不爱想起这段旧事，气艾米丽把它翻了出来。
“我顺从了天意的安排。”她冷冷地说。“艾米丽，你这就得明白：你要感恩，要听话，对大家为你做的这一切要领情。哭哭啼啼、怨天怨地，我不答应。倘使没有亲友收留你，你可怎么办？回答我。”
“我想我会饿死的。”艾米丽承认——眼前登时浮现出一幅悲壮的幻象：她自己直挺挺躺着死了，模样同艾伦·格林一本传教士杂志里的画片一模一样——那上面画的是印度饥荒的灾民。
“倒也不至于——可你就会被送进哪家孤儿院，多半得挨个半饿。你逃过了什么，你一点儿不晓得。你如今到了一个好人家，会有人照看你，让你受正当的教育。”
“受正当的教育”这几个字，艾米丽听着不怎么喜欢。可她还是恭恭敬敬地说：
“我知道您把我带来新月庄园，是天大的好心，伊丽莎白姨妈。我也不会烦您太久的，您知道。我很快就长大了，能自己挣饭吃了。您说，一个人最早几岁能算长大成人，伊丽莎白姨妈？”
“那个你不用想。”伊丽莎白姨妈没好气地说。“默里家的女人，从来不需要自己挣饭吃。我们对你的要求只有一条：做个乖乖的、知足的孩子，行事谨慎，守本分。”
这听着可真是难煞人了。

===Original===

“I *will* be,” said Emily, suddenly determining to be heroic, like the girl in the stories she had read. “Perhaps it won’t be so very hard after all, Aunt Elizabeth,”﻿—Emily happened at this point to recall a speech she had heard her father use once, and thought this a good opportunity to work it in﻿—“because, you know, God is good and the devil might be worse.”
Poor Aunt Elizabeth! To have a speech like that fired at her in the darkness of the night from that unwelcome little interloper into her orderly life and peaceful bed! Was it any wonder that for a moment or so she was too paralyzed to reply! Then she exclaimed in tones of horror,
“Emily, *never* say that again.”
“All right,” said Emily meekly. “But,” she added defiantly under her breath, “I’ll go on thinking it.”
“And now,” said Aunt Elizabeth, “I want to say that I am not in the habit of talking all night if you are. I tell you to go to sleep, and I *expect* you to obey me. Good night.”
The tone of Aunt Elizabeth’s good night would have spoiled the best night in the world. But Emily lay very still and sobbed no more, though the noiseless tears trickled down her cheeks in the darkness for some time. She lay so still that Aunt Elizabeth imagined she was asleep and went to sleep herself.
“I wonder if anybody in the world is awake but me,” thought Emily, feeling a sickening loneliness. “If I only had Saucy Sal here! She isn’t so cuddly as Mike but she’d be better than nothing. I wonder where she is. I wonder if they gave her any supper.”
Aunt Elizabeth had handed Sal’s basket to Cousin Jimmy with an impatient, “Here﻿—look to this cat,” and Jimmy had carried it off. Where had he put it? Perhaps Saucy Sal would get out and go home﻿—Emily had heard cats always went back home. She wished *she* could get out and go home﻿—she pictured herself and her cat running eagerly along the dark, starlit roads to the little house in the hollow﻿—back to the birches and Adam-and-Eve and Mike, and the old wing-chair and her dear little cot and the open window where the Wind Woman sang to her and at dawn one could see the blue of the mist on the homeland hills.
“Will it ever be morning?” thought Emily. “Perhaps things won’t be so bad in the morning.”
And then﻿—she heard the Wind Woman at the window﻿—she heard the little, low, whispering murmur of the June night breeze﻿—cooing, friendly, lovesome.

===Chinese===

“我会的。”艾米丽说，忽然打定主意要拿出英雄气概来，像她读过的故事里的女孩子那样。“也许到底也没那么难呢，伊丽莎白姨妈——”说到这儿，艾米丽恰好想起一句有一回听爸爸说过的话，觉得正好借此用上——“因为，您知道，上帝是善良的，魔鬼本来可能更坏。”
可怜的伊丽莎白姨妈！大黑夜里，从那个闯进她井井有条的生活和清清静静的床榻的小不速之客嘴里，冷不丁挨了这么一句！有那么一会儿她惊得回不上话来，这有什么奇怪呢！随后她用吓得变了调的嗓音喊道：
“艾米丽，这话*永远*不许再说。”
“好的。”艾米丽乖乖地说。可她又屏着气不服地添了一句：“我接着这么想。”
“还有，”伊丽莎白姨妈说，“我要说明白：我没同你聊一整夜的习惯，你要有随你。我叫你睡觉，我就*指望*你服从。晚安。”
伊丽莎白姨妈这句晚安的腔调，能把世上顶好的一个夜晚也毁了。可艾米丽一动不动躺着，没再抽噎，只是无声的泪珠还在黑暗里顺着脸颊淌了好一阵。她躺得那样安静，伊丽莎白姨妈只当她睡着了，自己也就睡着了。
“不知道这世上除了我，还有没有人醒着。”艾米丽想，心里泛起一阵发慌的孤单。“要是调皮的萨尔在这儿多好！她不像迈克（Mike）那样让人搂着舒服，可总比没有强。不知道她这会儿在哪儿。不知道他们给没给她晚饭吃。”
伊丽莎白姨妈早不耐烦地把萨尔的篮子塞给了吉米表哥——“喏——把这猫弄走”——吉米便把它拎走了。他把它搁哪儿了呢？说不定调皮的萨尔会逃出来自己回家——艾米丽听说过，猫总会回原来的家。她巴不得*她*自己也能逃出来回家——她想象自己和她的猫顺着黑黑的、星光满天的道路一路跑回谷中小屋——跑回那些白桦、亚当同夏娃和迈克跟前，跑回那把老高背椅、她亲爱的小床和那扇敞开的窗前——风之女（the Wind Woman）在那儿给她唱歌，天亮时还望得见故乡山丘上雾的蓝色。
“天到底还亮不亮啊？”艾米丽想。“也许到了白天，事情就不那么糟了。”
就在这时——她听见风之女到了窗前——她听见了六月夜风那低低的、絮语般的呢喃——咕咕的、亲亲的、惹人疼的。

===Original===

“Oh, you’re out there, are you, dearest one?” she whispered, stretching out her arms. “Oh, I’m so glad to hear you. You’re such company, Wind Woman. I’m not lonesome any more. And the flash came, too! I was afraid it might never come at New Moon.”
Her soul suddenly escaped from the bondage of Aunt Elizabeth’s stuffy featherbed and gloomy canopy and sealed windows. She was out in the open with the Wind Woman and the other gipsies of the night﻿—the fireflies, the moths, the brooks, the clouds. Far and wide she wandered in enchanted reverie until she coasted the shore of dreams and fell soundly asleep on the fat, hard pillow, while the Wind Woman sang softly and luringly in the vines that clustered over New Moon.

===Chinese===

“哦，你在那外头呢，是吗，最亲爱的？”她悄悄说，伸出了双臂。“哦，听见你的声音我多高兴。有你作伴多好，风之女。我再也不孤单了。灵光也来过了呢！我还当它在新月庄园永远不会来了。”
她的魂灵忽然挣脱了伊丽莎白姨妈那闷人的鸭绒被、阴沉沉的床幔和钉死的窗子。她同风之女、同夜里其他的流浪者——萤火虫、飞蛾、溪水、云彩——一起到了旷野上。她出神地神游八方，直到滑到梦的岸边，在那又厚又硬的枕头上沉沉睡去；风之女呢，还在爬满新月庄园的藤蔓里，轻轻地、诱人地唱着。

## VII The Book of Yesterday / 七 昨日之书
===Original===

That first Saturday and Sunday at New Moon always stood out in Emily’s memory as a very wonderful time, so crowded was it with new and generally delightful impressions. If it be true that we “count time by heart throbs” Emily lived two years in it instead of two days. Everything was fascinating from the moment she came down the long, polished staircase into the square hall that was filled with a soft, rosy light coming through the red glass panes of the front door. Emily gazed through the panes delightedly. What a strange, fascinating, red world she beheld, with a weird red sky that looked, she thought, as if it belonged to the Day of Judgment.
There was a certain charm about the old house which Emily felt keenly and responded to, although she was too young to understand it. It was a house which aforetime had had vivid brides and mothers and wives, and the atmosphere of their loves and lives still hung around it, not yet banished by the old-maidishness of the regime of Elizabeth and Laura.
“Why﻿—I’m going to *love* New Moon,” thought Emily, quite amazed at the idea.
Aunt Laura was setting the breakfast table in the kitchen, which seemed quite bright and jolly in the glow of morning sunshine. Even the black hole in the ceiling had ceased to be spookish and become only a commonplace entrance to the kitchen loft. And on the red-sandstone doorstep Saucy Sal was sitting, preening her fur as contentedly as if she had lived at New Moon all her life. Emily did not know it, but Sal had already drunk deep the delight of battle with her peers that morning and taught the barn cats their place once and for all. Cousin Jimmy’s big yellow Tom had got a fearful drubbing, and was minus several bits of his anatomy, while a stuck-up, black lady-cat, who fancied herself considerably, had made up her mind that if that grey-and-white, narrow-faced interloper from goodness knew where was going to stay at New Moon, *she* was not.
Emily gathered Sal up in her arms and kissed her joyously, to the horror of Aunt Elizabeth, who was coming across the platform from the cookhouse with a plate of sizzling bacon in her hands.
“Don’t ever let me see you kissing a cat again,” she ordered.
“Oh, all right,” agreed Emily cheerfully. “I’ll only kiss her when you don’t see me after this.”
“I don’t want any of your pertness, miss. You are not to kiss cats at all.”

===Chinese===

在新月庄园（New Moon）度过的头一个礼拜六和礼拜天，在艾米丽的记忆里永远是一段极美妙的时光，挤满了新奇而大抵可爱的印象。倘若人真是“以心跳计算时间”，那艾米丽在那两天里活了两年。从她走下长长的、擦得锃亮的楼梯、走进方厅的那一刻起，样样都迷人——厅里满是一种柔和的玫瑰色光，是从前门红玻璃格里透进来的。艾米丽透过玻璃格快活地张望。她看见的是一个多么奇妙迷人的红色世界，天也是怪异的红，她想，那仿佛是末日审判的日子才配有的天。
这座老房子自有一种韵味，艾米丽敏锐地感到了，也回应了，虽然她年纪还小，说不明白。这是一座从前住过鲜活的娘子、母亲和妻子的房子，她们的爱与生活的气息仍萦绕四周，还没被伊丽莎白和劳拉这对老姑娘的治家做派驱散。
“咦——我会爱上新月庄园的，”艾米丽想，这念头叫她自己都吃了一惊。
劳拉姨妈（Aunt Laura）正在厨房里摆早饭桌，晨光照得厨房亮堂堂、喜洋洋。连天花板上那个黑洞也不再鬼气森森，只是通往厨房顶棚的一个寻常入口了。红砂石的门槛上蹲着调皮的萨尔（Saucy Sal），心满意足地理着毛，好像它在新月庄园住了一辈子似的。艾米丽并不知道，萨尔那天早晨已经同它的对手们痛痛快快打过一仗，把谷仓的猫们一劳永逸地教训了个够。吉米表哥（Cousin Jimmy）家那只大黄汤姆猫挨了一顿好揍，身上少了几块零件；还有一只自命不凡的黑猫小姐，正暗自盘算：要是天晓得哪里来的这只灰白花、窄脸膛的野猫要在新月庄园住下，它可不奉陪。
艾米丽把萨尔抱起来，欢天喜地亲了它一下，吓坏了正端着一盘滋滋作响的熏肉、从厨屋那边走过平台来的伊丽莎白姨妈。
“别再让我看见你亲猫，”她命令道。
“哦，好吧，”艾米丽痛痛快快地答应，“往后我只在你看不见的时候亲它。”
“少给我贫嘴，小姐。你压根儿不许亲猫。”

===Original===

“But, Aunt Elizabeth, I didn’t kiss her on her mouth, *of course*. I just kissed her between her ears. It’s nice﻿—won’t you just try it for once and see for yourself?”
“That will do, Emily. You have said quite enough.” And Aunt Elizabeth sailed on into the kitchen majestically, leaving Emily momentarily wretched. She felt that she had offended Aunt Elizabeth, and she hadn’t the least notion why or how.
But the scene before her was too interesting to worry long over Aunt Elizabeth. Delicious smells were coming from the cookhouse﻿—a little, slant-roofed building at the corner where the big cooking-stove was placed in summer. It was thickly overgrown with hop vines, as most of the New Moon buildings were. To the right was the “new” orchard, very wonderful now in blossom, but a rather commonplace spot after all, since Cousin Jimmy cultivated it in most up-to-date fashion and had grain growing in the wide spaces between the straight rows of trees that looked all alike. But on the other side of the barn lane, just behind the well, was the “old orchard,” where Cousin Jimmy said the columbines grew and which seemed to be a delightful place where trees had come up at their own sweet will, and grown into individual shapes and sizes, where blue-eyed ivy twined about their roots and wild-briar roses rioted over the grey paling fence. Straight ahead, closing the vista between the orchards, was a little slope covered with huge white birches, among which were the big New Moon barns, and beyond the new orchard a little, lovable red road looped lightly up and up, over a hill, until it seemed to touch the vivid blue of the sky.
Cousin Jimmy came down from the barns, carrying brimming pails of milk, and Emily ran with him to the dairy behind the cookhouse. Such a delightful spot she had never seen or imagined. It was a snow-white little building in a clump of tall balm-of-gileads. Its grey roof was dotted over with cushions of moss like fat green-velvet mice. You went down six sandstone steps, with ferns crowding about them, and opened a white door with a glass panel in it, and went down three more steps.

===Chinese===

“可是，伊丽莎白姨妈，我当然没有亲它的嘴呀。我只亲了亲它两只耳朵中间。可舒服了——你就试一回，自己看看嘛？”
“够了，艾米丽。你说得够多了。”伊丽莎白姨妈威风凛凛地进了厨房，一时把艾米丽撂在原地怪难受的。她觉得自己惹恼了伊丽莎白姨妈，却又一点儿摸不着头脑，不知是为什么，又是怎么惹的。
可是眼前的景致太有趣了，顾不上为伊丽莎白姨妈难过多久。厨屋里飘出喷香的气味——那是角落上一座斜顶的小屋，夏天大灶就安在那儿。它上面密密地爬满啤酒花藤，新月庄园的房子大多这样。右边是“新果园”，如今花开得正盛，煞是好看；可到底是个平常去处，因为吉米表哥用顶时新的法子侍弄它，笔直成行、棵棵一个模样的树与树之间宽宽的空当里还种着谷物。可在谷仓小径的那一边、水井的正后方，是“老果园”，吉米表哥说楼斗菜就长在那儿——那似乎是个可爱的地方，树随自己的心意长出来，长成各自的形状和个头儿，蓝眼睛的常春藤绕着树根，野蔷薇在灰色的栅栏上开得泼泼洒洒。正前方，收拢两片果园之间视线的，是一道覆满高大白桦的小坡，新月庄园的大谷仓就在白桦之间；新果园再往外，一条可爱的小红路轻轻打了个圈，一路爬坡翻过一座山丘，仿佛一直碰到了鲜亮的蓝天上。
吉米表哥从谷仓下来，提着满得快溢出来的牛奶桶，艾米丽跟着他跑到厨屋后面的奶房。这么可爱的地方她从没见过，也没想到过。那是一座雪白的小屋，坐落在一丛高大的香脂白杨里。灰色的屋顶上星星点点缀着苔藓，像一只只胖乎乎的绿丝绒老鼠。你走下六级砂石台阶，四下挤满了蕨草，推开一扇嵌玻璃格的白门，再下三级台阶。

===Original===

And then you were in a clean, earthy-smelling, damp, cool place with an earthen floor and windows screened by the delicate emerald of young hop-vines, and broad wooden shelves all around, whereon stood wide, shallow pans of glossy brown ware, full of milk coated over with cream so rich that it was positively yellow.
Aunt Laura was waiting for them and she strained the milk into empty pans and then skimmed some of the full ones. Emily thought skimming was a lovely occupation and longed to try her hand at it. She also longed to sit right down and write a description of that dear dairy; but alas, there was no account book; still, she could write it in her head. She squatted down on a little three-legged stool in a dim corner and proceeded to do it, sitting so still that Jimmy and Laura forgot her and went away and later had to hunt for her a quarter of an hour. This delayed breakfast and made Aunt Elizabeth very cross. But Emily had found just the right sentence to define the clear yet dim green light that filled the dairy and was so happy over it that she didn’t mind Aunt Elizabeth’s black looks a bit.
After breakfast Aunt Elizabeth informed Emily that henceforth it would be one of her duties to drive the cows to pasture every morning.
“Jimmy has no hired man just now and it will save him a few minutes.”
“And don’t be afraid,” added Aunt Laura, “the cows know the way so well they’ll go of themselves. You have only to follow and shut the gates.”
“I’m not afraid,” said Emily.
But she was. She knew nothing about cows; still, she was determined that the Murrays should not suspect a Starr was scared. So, her heart beating like a trip-hammer, she sallied valiantly forth and found that what Aunt Laura had said was true and cows were not such ferocious animals after all. They went gravely on ahead and she had only to follow, through the old orchard and then through the scrub maple growth beyond, along a twisted ferny path where the Wind Woman was purring and peeping around the maple clumps.
Emily loitered by the pasture gate until her eager eyes had taken in all the geography of the landscape. The old pasture ran before her in a succession of little green bosoms right down to the famous Blair Water﻿—an almost perfectly round pond, with grassy, sloping, treeless margins.

===Chinese===

然后你就到了一个干净、带着泥土气、潮湿阴凉的地方：泥土地面，窗户被嫩啤酒花藤的翠纱掩着，四壁全是宽宽的木架，架上一排排浅口大瓦盆，油亮的棕色瓦盆，盛满了奶，奶上的奶油厚得发黄。
劳拉姨妈等着他们，她把奶滤进空盆，又撇了几盆满的。艾米丽觉得撇奶油是天底下可爱的活计，巴望亲手试一试。她还巴望当场坐下，写一篇这可爱奶房的描写；可惜呀，账本不在跟前；不过，她可以在脑子里写。她在一个暗角落里的小三脚凳上蹲下来，动手写起来，坐得那样纹丝不动，吉米和劳拉把她忘了，自顾自走开，过后又不得不找她一刻钟。这一来早饭误了时，伊丽莎白姨妈很不痛快。可是艾米丽正好找到了一个句子，把奶房里那种亮而朦胧的绿光一语道破，她欢喜得很，一点儿也不在乎伊丽莎白姨妈的黑脸。
早饭后，伊丽莎白姨妈通知艾米丽：从今往后，每天早上把牛赶到牧场去，是她的活计之一。
“吉米眼下没雇工，这能给他省几分钟。”
“别害怕，”劳拉姨妈添上一句，“那些牛认路认得熟极了，自己就会走。你只要跟在后面，把栅栏门关上就是。”
“我不怕，”艾米丽说。
可她怕。她对牛一无所知；不过她打定了主意，决不能让默里家的人疑心一个斯塔尔家的人会胆怯。于是她心里咚咚地敲着小鼓，勇敢地出发了，结果发现劳拉姨妈说的是实情——牛原来不是那么凶的牲口。它们庄重地在头里走，她只消跟着，穿过老果园，再穿过果园外头那片枫树矮林，沿一条弯弯曲曲、长满蕨草的小径走，风之女（the Wind Woman）正在一丛丛枫树四周呜呜低语、吱吱窥望。
艾米丽在牧场门口流连，直到她那双急切的眼睛把这片山水的地理看了个饱。老牧场在她面前铺开，一道又一道绿色的小小胸脯，一直连到那有名的布莱尔沃特（Blair Water）——一个几乎滚圆的池塘，岸边青草斜斜、没有树。

===Original===

Beyond it was the Blair Water valley, filled with homesteads, and further out the great sweep of the white-capped gulf. It seemed to Emily’s eyes a charming land of green shadows and blue waters. Down in one corner of the pasture, walled off by an old stone dyke, was the little private graveyard where the dead-and-gone Murrays were buried. Emily wanted to go and explore it, but was afraid to trust herself in the pasture.
“I’ll go as soon as I get better acquainted with the cows,” she resolved.
Off to the right, on the crest of a steep little hill, covered with young birches and firs, was a house that puzzled and intrigued Emily. It was grey and weatherworn, but it didn’t look old. It had never been finished; the roof was shingled but the sides were not, and the windows were boarded over. Why had it never been finished? And it was meant to be such a pretty little house﻿—a house you could love﻿—a house where there would be nice chairs and cozy fires and bookcases and lovely, fat, purry cats and unexpected corners; then and there she named it the Disappointed House, and many an hour thereafter did she spend finishing that house, furnishing it as it should be furnished, and inventing the proper people and animals to live in it.
To the left of the pasture field was another house of a quite different type﻿—a big, old house, tangled over with vines, flat-roofed, with mansard windows, and a general air of indifference and neglect about it. A large, untidy lawn, overgrown with unpruned shrubs and trees, straggled right down to the pond, where enormous willows drooped over the water. Emily decided that she would ask Cousin Jimmy about these houses when she got a good chance.
She felt that, before she went back, she must slip along the pasture fence and explore a certain path which she saw entering the grove of spruce and maple further down. She did﻿—and found that it led straight into Fairyland﻿—along the bank of a wide, lovely brook﻿—a wild, dear, little path with lady-ferns beckoning and blowing along it, the shyest of elfin June-bells under the firs, and little whims of loveliness at every curve. She breathed in the tang of fir-balsam and saw the shimmer of gossamers high up in the boughs, and everywhere the frolic of elfin lights and shadows.

===Chinese===

再往外是布莱尔沃特山谷，谷里满是庄户人家，更远处是白帽翻卷的大海湾一弯。在艾米丽眼里，这是一片绿荫碧水、可爱非凡的地方。牧场底下一个角落，用一道旧石墙围着，是那座小小的私家墓地，故去的默里家的人都葬在那里。艾米丽想去探一探，又不敢独自进牧场。
“等我跟牛们混熟了就去，”她打定了主意。
右前方，一座陡峭小山的山顶上，覆着幼桦和冷杉，有一座房子叫艾米丽又纳闷又好奇。房子灰扑扑的，饱经风雨，可并不显得旧。它一直没盖完；屋顶钉了木瓦，四面墙却没有，窗户都钉了板。为什么一直没盖完呢？它分明本该是那么漂亮的一座小房子——一座叫人疼的房子——一座里面该有好椅子、暖炉火、书柜、可爱的胖乎乎呼噜噜的猫、出人意料的角落的房子；她当场就给它起了名字，叫“失望之屋”（the Disappointed House）；此后不知多少个钟头，她都在心里把那房子盖完，把它该有的家具一件件配上，再编出合适的人和牲口住进去。
牧场左边还有一座房子，路数全然不同——一座爬满藤蔓的大老房子，平顶，开着孟莎式窗，浑身上下一副漠然失修的神气。一片荒草萋萋的大草坪，长满没人修剪的灌木和树，一直拖沓到池塘边，池边垂着几棵巨大的柳树。艾米丽决定，等寻着好机会，就问问吉米表哥这两座房子的事。
她觉得回去之前，一定要沿着牧场栅栏溜过去，探一探她望见的那条通进下首云杉枫树林的小路。她去了——发现那条路直通仙境——沿着一条宽阔可爱的溪岸——一条野趣天成的小径，贵妇蕨沿途招手拂动，冷杉下躲着最怕羞的精灵六月铃，每一道弯都藏着一点小小的俏皮的美。她吸着冷杉香脂的清冽气味，看见高高的枝头蛛丝闪闪，到处是精灵般的光影嬉戏。

===Original===

Here and there the young maple branches interlaced as if to make a screen for dryad faces﻿—Emily knew all about dryads, thanks to her father and the great sheets of moss under the trees were meet for Titania’s couch.
“This is one of the places where dreams grow,” said Emily happily.
She wished the path might go on forever, but presently it veered away from the brook, and when she had scrambled over a mossy, old board fence she found herself in the “front-garden” of New Moon, where Cousin Jimmy was pruning some spirea bushes.
“Oh, Cousin Jimmy, I’ve found the dearest little road,” said Emily breathlessly.
“Coming up through Lofty John’s bush?”
“Isn’t it our bush?” asked Emily, rather disappointed.
“No, but it ought to be. Fifty years ago Uncle Archibald sold that jog of land to Lofty John’s father﻿—old Mike Sullivan. He built a little house down near the pond and lived there till he quarrelled with Uncle Archibald﻿—which wasn’t long, of course. Then he moved his house across the road﻿—and Lofty John lives there now. Elizabeth has tried to buy the land back from him﻿—she’s offered him far more than it’s worth﻿—but Lofty John won’t sell﻿—just for spite, seeing that he has a good farm of his own and this piece isn’t much good to him. He only pastures a few young cattle on it through the summer, and what was cleared is all growing up with scrub maple. It’s a thorn in Elizabeth’s side and likely to be as long as Lofty John nurses his spite.”
“Why is he called Lofty John?”
“Because he’s a high and lofty fellow. But never mind him. I want to show you round my garden, Emily. It’s mine. Elizabeth bosses the farm; but she lets me run the garden﻿—to make up for pushing me into the well.”
“*Did* she do that?”
“Yes. She didn’t mean to, of course. We were just children﻿—I was here on a visit﻿—and the men were putting a new hood on the well and cleaning it. It was open﻿—and we were playing tag around it. I made Elizabeth mad﻿—forget what I said﻿—’twasn’t *hard* to make her mad, you understand﻿—and she made to give me a bang on the head. I saw it coming﻿—and stepped back to get out of the way﻿—and down I went, head first. Don’t remember anything more about it. There was nothing but mud at the bottom﻿—but my head struck the stones at the side. I was took up for dead﻿—my head all cut up.

===Chinese===

处处有幼枫的枝条交握起来，仿佛给树精的脸搭起一道帷幕——艾米丽对树精无所不晓，多亏她爸爸——树底下大片大片的青苔，正配提坦尼娅安卧。
“这是梦境生长的地方之一，”艾米丽快活地说。
她巴望这条路永远走不到头，可是不久它就拐离了溪岸，等她翻过一道长满青苔的旧木板墙，她已经站在新月庄园的“前园”里了，吉米表哥正在园里修剪绣线菊。
“哦，吉米表哥，我发现了一条顶可爱的小路，”艾米丽气都喘不匀地说。
“是从高个子约翰（Lofty John）的林子里穿上来那条？”
“那不是咱们的林子吗？”艾米丽问，挺失望。
“不是，可它本该是。五十年前，阿奇博尔德舅公把那一角地卖给了高个子约翰的爸爸——老迈克·沙利文（Mike Sullivan）。他在池塘边近处盖了座小屋，住在那儿，直到同阿奇博尔德舅公吵翻了——当然喽，也没住多久。后来他把房子整个挪到了路对面——高个子约翰如今就住在那儿。伊丽莎白一直想从他手里把那块地买回来——出的价远远高过地价——可高个子约翰偏不卖——纯粹赌气，他自己有好农场，这块地对他没多大用处。他不过夏天在上面放几头小牛，开垦过的地全让枫树矮林长满了。这是伊丽莎白的眼中刺，只要高个子约翰还记着那口气，怕要一直刺下去。”
“为什么叫他高个子约翰？”
“因为他是个高傲的家伙呗。别管他了。我要带你看看我的园子，艾米丽。它是我的。农场归伊丽莎白管；可园子她让我管——算是对把我推下井的补偿。”
“她真把你推下井了？”
“真。当然，她不是成心的。那会儿我们都还是孩子——我来这儿做客——长工们正给井换井栏、淘井。井敞着口——我们绕着井追着玩。我把伊丽莎白惹火了——忘了我说了句什么——你要明白，惹火她可不是难事——她要给我脑袋一巴掌。我瞧见巴掌来了——往后一躲——就头朝下载了下去。再往下的事什么都不记得了。井底只有泥——可我的头磕在了井壁的石头上。捞上来的时候都当我死了——头破了好几处。

===Original===

Poor Elizabeth was﻿—” Cousin Jimmy shook his head, as if to intimate that it was impossible to describe how or what poor Elizabeth was. “I got about after a while, though﻿—pretty near as good as new. Folks say I’ve never been quite right since﻿—but they only say that because I’m a poet, and because nothing ever worries me. Poets are so scarce in Blair Water folks don’t understand them, and most people worry so much, they think you’re not right if you don’t worry.”
“Won’t you recite some of your poetry to me, Cousin Jimmy?” asked Emily eagerly.
“When the spirit moves me I will. It’s no use to ask me when the spirit don’t move me.”
“But how am I to know when the spirit moves you, Cousin Jimmy?”
“I’ll begin of my own accord to recite my compositions. But I’ll tell you this﻿—the spirit generally moves me when I’m boiling the pigs’ potatoes in the fall. Remember that and be around.”
“Why don’t you write your poetry down?”
“Paper’s too scarce at New Moon. Elizabeth has some pet economies and writing paper of any kind is one of them.”
“But haven’t you any money of your own, Cousin Jimmy?”
“Oh, Elizabeth pays me good wages. But she puts all my money in the bank and just doles out a few dollars to me once in a while. She says I’m not fit to be trusted with money. When I came here to work for her she paid me my wages at the end of the month and I started for Shrewsbury to put it in the bank. Met a tramp on the road﻿—a poor, forlorn creature without a cent. I gave *him* the money. Why not? *I* had a good home and a steady job and clothes enough to do me for years. I s’pose it was the foolishest thing I ever did﻿—and the nicest. But Elizabeth never got over it. *She’s* managed my money ever since. But come you now, and I’ll show you my garden before I have to go and sow turnips.”
The garden was a beautiful place, well worthy Cousin Jimmy’s pride. It seemed like a garden where no frost could wither or rough wind blow﻿—a garden remembering a hundred vanished summers. There was a high hedge of clipped spruce all around it, spaced at intervals by tall lombardies. The north side was closed in by a thick grove of spruce against which a long row of peonies grew, their great red blossoms splendid against its darkness.
