===Chinese===

我还得同伊丽莎白姨妈睡，这我也不喜欢，因为只要动那么一丁点儿，她就说我在磨蹭，不过她承认我不踢人。她也不许我把窗子支起来。她不喜欢屋里有新鲜空气和光。客厅黑得像坟慕。有一天我进去把百叶帘全卷了起来，伊丽莎白姨妈吓坏了，骂我小贱蹄子，还给了我一个默里家的眼神。人家还当我犯了什么罪呢。我觉得受了大侮辱，跑上阁楼，在一张邮单上写了一篇自己淹死的描写，就好受多了。伊丽莎白姨妈说，没得允许，我再也不许进客厅。可我也不想进。我怕客厅。四面墙上全挂满我们家祖先的画像，一个好看的都没有，只有默里外祖父还算俊，可一脸凶相。客卧在楼上，同客厅一样阴沉沉。伊丽莎白姨妈只让杰出入物睡那儿。白天我喜欢厨房，还有阁楼、户外灶屋、起居室，还有门厅——因为那扇可爱的红前门——我还爱奶房；可新月庄园其余的屋子我都不喜欢。哦，我漏了地窖的小橱。我爱下去看那一排排漂亮的果酱罐和果子冻罐。吉米表哥说，果酱罐永远不许空着，这是新月庄园的一条传通。新月庄园的传通真多呀。这是一座宽敝极了的大房子，树木也可爱。我给花园门口三棵钻天杨起名叫三公主，给那座旧凉亭起名叫艾米丽的凉轩，还给老果园门口那棵大苹果树起名叫祷告树，因为它举起长枝的样子，同戴尔先生（Mr. Dare）在教堂里祷告时举起双臂一模一样。
> “伊丽莎白姨妈把柜子右上那只小抽屉给我放东西了。
> “哦，亲爱的爸爸，我有了一个大发现。真希望是你活着的时候发现的，我想你听了会高兴的。*我会写诗。*也许早就该会，只要试一试。可上学头一天之后，我觉得我在道义上非试不可，一试，竟那么容易。伊丽莎白姨妈的书柜里有一本黑封面的小卷书，叫《汤普森的四季》，我决定写一首咏季节的诗，头三行是这样的——
> “当然，爱德华王子岛上没有桃

===Original===

E. Island and I never heard a sportsman’s horn here either, but you don’t have to stick too close to facts in poetry. I filled a whole letter-bill with it and then I ran and read it to Aunt Laura. I thought she would be overjoyed to find she had a niece who could write poetry but she took it very coolly and said it didn’t sound much like poetry. It’s blank verse I cryed. *Very* blank said Aunt Elizabeth sarkastically though I hadn’t asked *her* opinion. But I think I will write ryming poetry after this so that there will be no mistake about it and I intend to be a poetess when I grow up and become famus. I hope also that I will be silph-like. A poetess should be silph-like. Cousin Jimmy makes poetry too. He has made over 1000 pieces but he never writes any down but carries them in his head. I offered to give him some of my letter-bills﻿—for he is very kind to me﻿—but he said he was too old to learn new habits. I haven’t heard any of his poetry yet because the spirit hasn’t moved him but I am very angshus to and I am sorry they don’t fatten the pigs till the fall. I like Cousin Jimmy more and more all the time, except when he takes his queer spells of looking and talking. Then he fritens me but they never last long. I have read a good many of the books in the New Moon bookcase. A history of the reformation in France, very relijus and sad. A little fat book deskribing the months in England and the afoursaid Thompson’s Seasons. I like to read them because they have so many pretty words in them, but I don’t like the feel of them. The paper is so rough and thick it makes me creepy. Travels in Spain, very fassinating, with lovely smooth shiny paper, a missionary book on the Paciffic Islands, pictures very interesting because of the way the heathen chiefs arange their hair. After they became Christians they cut it off which I think was a pity. Mrs. Hemans Poems. I am passhunately fond of poetry, also of stories about desert islands. Rob Roy, a novel, but I only read a little of it when Aunt Elizabeth said I must stop because I must not read novels. Aunt Laura says to read it on the sly. I don’t see why it wouldn’t be all right to obey Aunt Laura but I have a queer feeling about it and I haven’t yet. A lovely Tiger-book, full of pictures and stories of tigers that make me feel so nice and shivery.

===Chinese===

子，这儿也从没听过猎人的号角，不过写诗用不着太死抠事实。我写满了一整张邮单，就跑去念给劳拉姨妈听。我想她发现自己有个会写诗的外甥女，准会欢喜坏了，可她接收得平平淡淡，说听着不怎么像诗。是无韵诗，我叫道。*够无的，*伊丽莎白姨妈讥剌地说，我又没问她。可我想往后我还是写押韵的诗，省得弄不清，我打定主意长大要做个女诗人，出人头地。我还希望自己身段像莎草一样轻盈。女诗人就该像莎草一样轻盈。吉米表哥也作诗。他作了1000多首，可一首也不写下来，全装在脑袋里。我说要分他几张邮单——他待我真好——可他说他年纪大了，学不来新习惯。他的诗我还没听见过一首，因为圣灵还没感动他，可我急着呢，只可惜他们不到秋天不喂肥猪。我一天比一天更喜欢吉米表哥，只是他犯那古怪病的时候除外——眼神和说话都变了个人。那时候他吓人，好在从来不久。新月庄园书柜里的书，我读了不少了。一本法兰西宗教改革史，非常虔诚，非常悲惨。一本讲英格兰十二个月的小胖书，和前面说的《汤普森的四季》。我爱读它们，里头漂亮字眼多得很，可我不喜欢摸它们。那纸又糙又厚，摸得我起鸡皮疙瘩。《西班牙游记》迷人极了，纸又滑又亮；一本太平洋岛屿的传教书，插图有趣极了，因为那些异教酋长梳头发的方式好玩。他们成了基督徒就把头发剪了，我觉得可惜。海曼斯夫人（Mrs. Hemans）诗集。我酷爱诗，也爱荒岛故事。《罗布·罗伊》（Rob Roy），一部小说，可我才读了一点儿，伊丽莎白姨妈就叫我停了，说我不许读小说。劳拉姨妈说偷偷读。我不明白为什么听劳拉姨妈的就不对，可我心里有点别扭的感觉，还没读。一本可爱的老虎书，全是画和老虎故事，读得人又舒服又发抖。

===Original===

The Royal Road, also relijus but some fun in it so very good for Sundays. Reuben and Grace, a story but not a novel, because Reuben and Grace are brother and sister and there is no getting married. Little Katy and Jolly Jim, same as above but not so exciting and traggic. Nature’s Mighty Wonders which is good and improving. Alice in Wonderland, which is perfectly lovely, and the Memoirs of Anzonett B. Peters who was converted at seven and died at twelve. When anybody asked for a question she answered with a hym verse. That is after she was converted. Before that she spoke English. Aunt Elizabeth told me I ought to try to be like Anzonetta. I think I might be an Alice under more faverable circumstances but I am sure I can never be as good as Anzonetta was and I don’t believe I want to be because she never had any fun. She got sick as soon as she was converted and suffered aggonies for years. Besides I am sure that if I talked hyms to people it would exite ridicule. I tried it once. Aunt Laura asked me the other day if I would like blue stripes better than red in my next winter’s stockings and I ansered just as Anzonetta did when asked a similar question, only different, about a sack,
> And Aunt Laura said was I crazy and Aunt Elizabeth said I was ireverent. So I know it wouldn’t work. Besides, Anzonetta couldn’t eat anything for years having ulsers in her stomach and I am pretty fond of good eating.
> “Old Mr. Wales on the Derry Pond Road is dying of canser. Jennie Strang says his wife has her morning all ready.

> Yours most obedient humble servant,

**Emily B. Starr.**
> P.S. I think Aunt Laura loves me. I like to be loved, Father Dear.
> E. B. S.

===Chinese===

《王家大道》，也虔诚，可里头有些趣事，所以顶适合礼拜天读。《鲁本与格蕾丝》，是故事，不算小说，因为鲁本和格蕾丝是兄妹，没有人结婚。《小凯蒂和快活的吉姆》，同上，只是没那么惊险悲壮。《大自然的伟观》，又有益又长进。《爱丽丝漫游奇境》（Alice in Wonderland），可爱极了；还有《安佐内塔·B·彼得斯回忆录》，她七岁倫依，十二岁去世。谁问她问题，她就用一句圣侍诗回答。那是倫依之后。倫依之前，她说的是人话。伊丽莎白姨妈说，我该努力学安佐内塔。我想，环境再好些，我也许能做个爱丽丝，可我肯定永远做不到安佐内塔那么好，再说我也不想，因为她从来没有过一丁点儿乐子。她一倫依就病了，痛苦了好多年。况且我敢说，我要是跟人说圣侍话，准招人笑话。我试过一回。前两天劳拉姨妈问我，明年冬天的袜子，蓝条纹的比红条纹的好不好，我就照安佐内塔被问类似问题时的样子答了——只是她那次问的是罩衫——
劳拉姨妈就问我疯了没有，伊丽莎白姨妈说我不虔敬。所以我知道这法子行不通。再说，安佐内塔胃里长了溃烊，好多年什么都吃不得，而我挺贪嘴的。
> “德里池塘路（Derry Pond Road）上的老威尔士先生（Mr. Wales）得癌正死了。珍妮·斯特兰说，他太太的丧服都备好了。
> “今天我写了调皮的萨尔的传记，还写了一篇高个儿约翰（Lofty John）林子里那条路的描写。我把它们别在这封信上，你也能读到。晚安，我亲爱的爸爸。
> 你最恭顺最卑微的仆人，
**艾米丽·伯·斯塔尔（Emily B. Starr）。**
> 又及：我觉得劳拉姨妈爱我。我喜欢被爱着，亲爱的爸爸。
> 艾·伯·斯

## X Growing Pains / 十 成长的烦恼
===Original===

There was a great deal of suppressed excitement in school during the last week in June, the cause thereof being Rhoda Stuart’s birthday party, which was to take place early in July. The amount of heart burning was incredible. Who was to be invited? That was the great question. There were some who knew they wouldn’t and some who knew they would; but there were more who were in truly horrible suspense. Everybody paid court to Emily because she was Rhoda’s dearest friend and might conceivably have some voice in the selection of guests. Jennie Strang even went as far as bluntly to offer Emily a beautiful white box with a gorgeous picture of Queen Victoria on the cover, to keep her pencils in, if she would procure her an invitation. Emily refused the bribe and said grandly that she could not interfere in such a delicate matter. Emily really did put on some airs about it. *She* was sure of her invitation. Rhoda had told her about the party weeks before and had talked it all over with her. It was to be a very grand affair﻿—a birthday cake covered with pink icing and adorned with ten tall pink candles﻿—ice-cream and oranges﻿—and written invitations on pink, gilt-edged notepaper *sent through the post-office*﻿—this last being an added touch of exclusiveness. Emily dreamed about that party day and night and had her present all ready for Rhoda﻿—a pretty hair ribbon which Aunt Laura had brought from Shrewsbury.
On the first Sunday in July Emily found herself sitting beside Jennie Strang in Sunday School for the opening exercises. Generally she and Rhoda sat together, but now Rhoda was sitting three seats ahead with a strange little girl﻿—a very gay and gorgeous little girl, dressed in blue silk, with a large, flower-wreathed leghorn hat on her elaborately curled hair, white lacework stockings on her pudgy legs and a bang that came clean down to her eyes. Not all her fine feathers could make a really fine bird of her, however; she was not in the least pretty and her expression was cross and contemptuous.
“Who is the girl sitting with Rhoda?” whispered Emily.
“Oh, she’s Muriel Porter,” answered Jennie. “She’s a towny, you know. She’s come out to spend her vacation with her aunt, Jane Beatty. I hate her. If I was her I’d never *dream* of wearing blue with a skin as dark as hers. But the Porters are rich and Muriel thinks she’s a wonder.

===Chinese===

六月最后一个礼拜，学校里憋着一股压不住的兴奋，起因是罗达·斯图尔特（Rhoda Stuart）的生日会——定在七月初举行。为此熬心的人多得难以置信。谁会被邀请？这是头一号大事。有的孩子知道自己去不了，有的知道自己去得了；可更多的孩子悬在真正可怕的煎熬里。人人都巴结艾米丽，因为她是罗达顶要好的朋友，没准儿在挑客人这件事上说得上话。珍妮·斯特兰（Jennie Strang）甚至直截了当开出了贿赂：一只漂亮的白盒子，盖上印着维多利亚女王光彩夺目的画像，给艾米丽装铅笔——只要她替她弄一张请帖。艾米丽拒了这份贿赂，郑重宣布这样微妙的事她不便插手。其实艾米丽在这件事上确实端起了几分架子。她的请帖是拿得稳的。罗达几个礼拜前就同她商量过整场聚会。那要办得十分隆重——一只生日蛋糕，覆着粉红糖霜，插着十支高高的粉红蜡烛——还有冰淇淋和橙子——请帖用粉色烫金信纸写好，从邮局寄出——这最后一招又平添一分独家的体面。艾米丽日夜都梦见那场聚会，给罗达备的礼物也早齐了——一条漂亮的发带，劳拉姨妈（Aunt Laura）从什鲁斯伯里（Shrewsbury）买回来的。
七月头一个礼拜天，主日学校开课的时候，艾米丽发现自己坐在珍妮·斯特兰旁边。往常她和罗达坐一处，可这会儿罗达坐在前三排，旁边是个陌生的小姑娘——一个打扮得花枝招展的小姑娘，穿蓝绸衣裳，精心烫卷的头发上压一顶花环围绕的大草帽，胖胖的腿上套着白色镂空花袜，刘海齐刷刷垂到眼皮上。可惜这一身漂亮行头也没能把她打扮成一只真正的好鸟；她一点儿也不好看，一脸乖张轻蔑的神气。
“同罗达坐一处的那个姑娘是谁？”艾米丽悄声问。
“哦，那是穆丽尔·波特（Muriel Porter），”珍妮答道，“城里来的，你知道吧。出来到她姨妈简·比蒂家过暑假的。我讨厌她。我要是她，皮肤那么黑，做梦也不敢穿蓝的。可波特家有钱，穆丽尔自以为得了不得。”

===Original===

They say Rhoda and her have been *awful thick* since she came out﻿—Rhoda’s always chasing after anybody she thinks is up in the world.”
Emily stiffened up. She was not going to listen to disparaging remarks about her friends. Jennie felt the stiffening and changed her note.
“Anyway, I’m *glad* I’m not invited to Rhoda’s old party. I wouldn’t *want* to go when Muriel Porter will be there, putting on her airs.”
“How do you know you are not invited?” wondered Emily.
“Why, the invitations went out yesterday. Didn’t you get yours?”
“No﻿—o﻿—o.”
“Did you get your mail?”
“Yes﻿—Cousin Jimmy got it.”
“Well, maybe Mrs. Beecher forgot to give it to him. Likely you’ll get it tomorrow.”
Emily agreed that it was likely. But a queer cold sensation of dismay had invaded her being, which was not removed by the fact that after Sunday School Rhoda strutted away with Muriel Porter without a glance at anyone else. On Monday Emily herself went to the post-office, but there was no pink envelope for her. She cried herself to sleep that night, but did not quite give up hope until Tuesday had passed. Then she faced the terrible truth﻿—that she﻿—she, Emily Byrd Starr, of New Moon﻿—had not been invited to Rhoda’s party. The thing was incredible. There *must* be a mistake somewhere. Had Cousin Jimmy lost the invitation on the road home? Had Rhoda’s grown-up sister who wrote the invitations overlooked her name? Had﻿—Emily’s unhappy doubts were forever resolved into bitter certainty by Jennie, who joined her as she left the post-office. There was a malicious light in Jennie’s beady eyes. Jennie liked Emily quite well by now, in spite of their passage-at-arms on the day of their first meeting, but she liked to see her pride humbled for all that.
“So you’re not invited to Rhoda’s party after all.”
“No,” admitted Emily.
It was a very bitter moment for her. The Murray pride was sorely wrung﻿—and, beneath the Murray pride, something else had been grievously wounded but was not yet quite dead.
“Well, I call it dirt mean,” said Jennie, quite honestly sympathetic in spite of her secret satisfaction. “After all the fuss she’s made over you, too! But that’s Rhoda Stuart all over. Deceitful is no name for *her*.”
“I don’t think she’s deceitful,” said Emily, loyal to the last ditch. “I believe there’s some mistake about my not being invited.”
Jennie stared.
“Then you don’t know the reason?

===Chinese===

“听说她一来，罗达就同她好得蜜里调油——罗达就是这样，见谁发达就追谁。”
艾米丽绷起了脸。她可不要听人说她朋友的坏话。珍妮觉出她绷起来了，忙换了调门。
“反正呀，没请我去罗达那劳什子聚会，我倒高兴。穆丽尔·波特要在场摆她的架子，我才不去呢。”
“你怎么知道没请你？”艾米丽纳闷。
“嗐，请帖昨天都发出去了。你没收到？”
“没——有。”
“邮件你取了没有？”
“取了——吉米表哥取的。”
“那兴许是比彻太太忘了交给他。多半明天就到。”
艾米丽也认多半是。可一种怪怪的、发凉的沮丧已经侵进她心里；主日学校散了课，罗达同穆丽尔·波特扬长而去，对谁也不看一眼，这凉意就更去不掉了。礼拜一，艾米丽亲自跑了一趟邮局，还是没有她的粉信封。那天夜里她哭着睡着了，可还不肯死心，直到礼拜二过完。这时她才面对那可怕的事实——她——她，新月庄园的艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔（Emily Byrd Starr）——竟没被邀请参加罗达的聚会。这不可能。准是哪儿出了错。是吉米表哥把请帖丢在回家的路上了？是代写请帖的罗达她大姐漏了她的名字？还是——艾米丽这些难过的疑团，被珍妮一锤子敲成了苦涩的定局——珍妮在邮局门口追上了她。珍妮那双滴溜溜的小眼睛里亮着恶意。虽说头回见面那天两人交过一回手，珍妮如今挺喜欢艾米丽的；可尽管如此，她还是乐意见她那股傲气栽个跟头。
“这么说，罗达的聚会到底没请你。”
“没请，”艾米丽认了。
这一刻她苦透了。默里家的骄傲被狠狠拧了一把——而在默里家的骄傲底下，还有一样东西受了重伤，只是还没断气。
“嗐，要我说，这可真够下作的，”珍妮说，倒是一片真心地同情，虽然心底里暗暗痛快。“她从前多稀罕你呀！可罗达·斯图尔特就是这么个人。说她口是心非都是轻的。”
“我不觉得她口是心非，”艾米丽说，死也护到底。“我相信没请我这事另有差误。”
珍妮瞪起了眼。
“这么说，你还不知道缘故？”

===Original===

Why, Beth Beatty told me the whole story. Muriel Porter hates you and she just up and told Rhoda that she would not go to her party if you were invited. And Rhoda was so crazy to have a town girl there that she promised she wouldn’t invite you.”
“Muriel Porter doesn’t know me,” gasped Emily. “How can she hate me?”
Jennie grinned impishly.
“*I* can tell you. She’s *dead stuck* on Fred Stuart and Fred knows it and he teased her by praising *you* up to her﻿—told her you were the sweetest girl in Blair Water and he meant to have you for *his girl* when you were a little older. And Muriel was so mad and jealous she made Rhoda leave you out. *I* wouldn’t care if I was you. A Murray of New Moon is away above such trash. As for Rhoda not being deceitful, I can tell you she *is*. Why, she told you that she didn’t know that snake was in the box, when it was her thought of doing it in the first place.”
Emily was too crushed to reply. She was glad that Jennie had to switch off down her own lane and leave her alone. She hurried home, afraid that she could not keep the tears back until she got there. Disappointment about the party﻿—humiliation over the insult﻿—all were swallowed up in the anguish of a faith betrayed and a trust outraged. Her love of Rhoda was quite dead now and Emily smarted to the core of her soul with the pain of the blow that had killed it. It was a child’s tragedy﻿—and all the more bitter for that, since there was no one to understand. Aunt Elizabeth told her that birthday parties were all nonsense and that the Stuarts were not a family that the Murrays had ever associated with. And even Aunt Laura, though she petted and comforted, did not realize how deep and grievous the hurt had been﻿—so deep and grievous that Emily could not even write about it to her father, and had no outlet for the violence of emotion that racked her being.
The next Sunday Rhoda was alone in Sunday School, Muriel Porter having been suddenly summoned back to town by her father’s illness; and Rhoda looked sweetly towards Emily. But Emily sailed past her with a head held very high and scorn on every lineament. She would *never* have anything to do with Rhoda Stuart again﻿—she couldn’t. She despised Rhoda more than ever for trying to get back with her, now that the town girl for whom she had sacrificed her was gone.

===Chinese===

“嗐，贝丝·比蒂把原委全告诉我了。穆丽尔·波特恨你，她直截了当同罗达讲了：要是请你，她就不来。罗达一心巴望聚会上有个城里姑娘，就应承了不请你。”
“穆丽尔·波特根本不认得我，”艾米丽倒吸一口气。“她怎么会恨我？”
珍妮调皮地咧嘴一笑。
“我告诉你吧。她迷弗雷德·斯图尔特迷得要死，弗雷德心里明白，就故意夸你，逗她火上浇油——同她说你是布莱尔沃特（Blair Water）顶招人疼的姑娘，还说他打算等你再大些，就讨你做他的人。穆丽尔又气又妒，就逼罗达把你用了。我要是你，才不在乎呢。新月庄园（New Moon）的默里家，比这种货色高出去老远。至于罗达不口是心非——我告诉你，她就是。她那时跟你说她不知道盒子里有蛇，可那主意本来就是她出的。”
艾米丽被击垮了，答不上话。幸而珍妮到了自家巷口，拐了进去，撇下她一个人。她急急往家赶，只怕眼泪撑不到家门口。对聚会的失望——受辱的羞愤——全让一样更痛的东西吞了：被辜负的信义，被糟蹋的信任。她对罗达的情分如今死透了，那一击打死了它，也痛进了她的灵魂深处。这是一个孩子的悲剧——正因为是孩子的，才更苦，因为没有人懂得。伊丽莎白姨妈只说生日聚会全是胡闹，斯图尔特家从来就不是默里家来往的人家。连劳拉姨妈，虽然又搂又哄，也没悟出这伤有多深多重——深得重得艾米丽连写给爸爸的信里都提不成，满腔翻腾的激愤没个出口。
下一个礼拜天，罗达独自来上主日学校，穆丽尔·波特因为她父亲病了，突然被召回城里去了；罗达朝艾米丽甜甜地望过来。可艾米丽擦着她扬长而过，头抬得高高的，眉眼间全是鄙夷。她再不——再不同罗达·斯图尔特来往了——她做不到。如今那个为她牺牲了她的城里姑娘走了，罗达倒想回来巴结她，这更叫艾米丽瞧她不起。

===Original===

It was not for Rhoda she mourned﻿—it was for the friendship that had been so dear to her. Rhoda *had* been dear and sweet on the surface at least, and Emily had found intense happiness in their companionship. Now it was gone and she could never, *never* love or trust anybody again. *There* lay the sting.
It poisoned everything. Emily was of a nature which, even as a child, did not readily recover from or forget such a blow. She moped about New Moon, lost her appetite and grew thin. She hated to go to Sunday School because she thought the other girls exulted in her humiliation and her estrangement from Rhoda. Some slight feeling of the kind there was, perhaps, but Emily morbidly exaggerated it. If two girls whispered or giggled together she thought she was being discussed and laughed at. If one of them walked home with her she thought it was out of condescending pity because she was friendless. For a month Emily was the most unhappy little being in Blair Water.
“I think I must have been put under a curse at birth,” she reflected disconsolately.
Aunt Elizabeth had a more prosaic idea to account for Emily’s languor and lack of appetite. She had come to the conclusion that Emily’s heavy masses of hair “took from her strength” and that she would be much stronger and better if it were cut off. With Aunt Elizabeth to decide was to act. One morning she coolly informed Emily that her hair was to be “shingled.”
Emily could not believe her ears.
“You don’t mean that you are going to cut off my hair, Aunt Elizabeth,” she exclaimed.
“Yes, I mean exactly that,” said Aunt Elizabeth firmly. “You have entirely too much hair especially for hot weather. I feel sure that is why you have been so miserable lately. Now, I don’t want any crying.”
But Emily could not keep the tears back.
“Don’t cut it *all* off,” she pleaded. “Just cut a good big bang. Lots of the girls have their hair banged clean from the crown of their heads. That would take half my hair off and the rest won’t take too much strength.”
“There will be no bangs here,” said Aunt Elizabeth. “I’ve told you so often enough. I’m going to shingle your hair close all over your head for the hot weather. You’ll be thankful to me some day for it.”
Emily felt anything but thankful just then.
“It’s my one beauty,” she sobbed, “it and my lashes. I suppose you want to cut off my lashes too.”

===Chinese===

她哀悼的不是罗达——是那段曾经那样亲的情分。罗达至少面上到底是亲过、甜过的，艾米丽在两人的伴儿情里尝过浓烈的幸福。如今它没了，她再也不能——再也不能爱谁、信谁了。痛根在这里。
它毒害了一切。艾米丽的性子，打小就是这样：挨了这样的打击，轻易缓不过来，也忘不掉。她在新月庄园里蔫头蔫脑，饭也吃不下，人也瘦了。她不愿上主日学校，觉得别的姑娘都在她的丢脸和同罗达的决裂里称快。这层意思兴许有一星半点，可艾米丽病态地放大了它。两个姑娘凑在一处咬耳朵、咯咯笑，她就当是在议论她、笑话她。有谁同她一道回家，她就当是屈尊可怜她没朋友。整整一个月，艾米丽是布莱尔沃特最不开心的小人儿。
“我看我准是生下来就着了诅咒，”她愁眉苦脸地想。
对艾米丽的蔫弱和没胃口，伊丽莎白姨妈另有个更实际的说法。她得出结论：艾米丽那一头厚发“耗她的血气”，剪掉了人就会结实得多、好得多。伊丽莎白姨妈想到就做。一天早上，她冷冷地通知艾米丽，她的头发要“铰短”。
艾米丽不敢信自己的耳朵。
“你总不是要铰掉我的头发吧，伊丽莎白姨妈，”她喊起来。
“对，就是这个意思，”伊丽莎白姨妈说得斩钉截铁。“你的头发实在太多，大热天尤其如此。我断定你近来这么蔫，病根就在这儿。行了，我不许你哭。”
可艾米丽的眼泪止不住。
“别全铰掉，”她哀求道，“就铰个又好又大的刘海。好多姑娘的头发从头顶齐刷刷铰出刘海呢。那能去我一半头发，剩下的就不费血气了。”
“这个家不许留刘海，”伊丽莎白姨妈说，“我说过不知多少回了。我要把你满头的头发贴着头皮铰短，好过夏天。往后有一天你会为此谢我的。”
这会儿艾米丽只觉得半点儿也谢不出来。
“这是我唯一的美处，”她抽抽噎噎地说，“它，还有我的睫毛。我猜你连我的睫毛也想铰掉呢。”

===Original===

Aunt Elizabeth *did* distrust those long, upcurled fringes of Emily’s, which were an inheritance from the girlish stepmother, and too un-Murray-like to be approved; but she had no designs against them. The hair must go, however, and she curtly bade Emily wait there, without any fuss, until she got the scissors.
Emily waited﻿—quite hopelessly. She must lose her lovely hair﻿—the hair her father had been so proud of. It might grow again in time﻿—if Aunt Elizabeth let it﻿—but that would take years, and meanwhile what a fright she would be! Aunt Laura and Cousin Jimmy were out; she had no one to back her up; this horrible thing must happen.
Aunt Elizabeth returned with the scissors; they clicked suggestively as she opened them; that click, as if by magic, seemed to loosen something﻿—some strange formidable power in Emily’s soul. She turned deliberately around and faced her aunt. She felt her brows drawing together in an unaccustomed way﻿—she felt an uprush as from unknown depths of some irresistible surge of energy.
“Aunt Elizabeth,” she said, looking straight at the lady with the scissors, “*my hair is not going to be cut off*. Let me hear no more of this.”
An amazing thing happened to Aunt Elizabeth. She turned pale﻿—she laid the scissors down﻿—she looked aghast for one moment at the transformed or possessed child before her﻿—and then for the first time in her life Elizabeth Murray turned tail and fled﻿—literally fled﻿—to the kitchen.
“What is the matter, Elizabeth?” cried Laura, coming in from the cookhouse.
“I saw﻿—Father﻿—looking from her face,” gasped Elizabeth, trembling. “And she said, ‘Let me hear no more of this,’﻿—just as *he* always said it﻿—his very words.”
Emily overheard her and ran to the sideboard mirror. She had had, while she was speaking, an uncanny feeling of wearing somebody else’s face instead of her own. It was vanishing now﻿—but Emily caught a glimpse of it as it left﻿—the Murray look, she supposed. No wonder it had frightened Aunt Elizabeth﻿—it frightened herself﻿—she was glad that it had gone. She shivered﻿—she fled to her garret retreat and cried; but somehow, she knew that her hair would not be cut.
Nor was it; Aunt Elizabeth never referred to the matter again. But several days passed before she meddled much with Emily.
It was a rather curious fact that from that day Emily ceased to grieve over her lost friend.

===Chinese===

伊丽莎白姨妈也确实信不过艾米丽那长长的上翘的睫毛帘——那是那位少女继母传下来的，太不像默里家，难获嘉许；可她并没打睫毛的主意。头发却是非去不可的，她三言两语吩咐艾米丽在那儿等着，不许小题大做，她去拿剪子。
艾米丽等着——等得心灰意冷。她心爱的头发是保不住了——爸爸生前多么为它骄傲。日子久了兴许还能长回来——这得看伊丽莎白姨妈容不容——可那要等好几年，这几年里她得多难看！劳拉姨妈和吉米表哥都出去了；没人给她撑腰；这件可怕的事躲不过了。
伊丽莎白姨妈拿着剪子回来了；她开剪子的时候，咔哒一声，意味深长；这一声咔哒，仿佛变戏法似的，松开了艾米丽魂灵里一样东西——一股陌生而可怕的力量。她不慌不忙转过身，正对着姨妈。她觉出自己的眉头拧出了一种生疏的样式——觉出一股无可抗拒的劲头，从不知多深的深处涌上来。
“伊丽莎白姨妈，”她直直望着那位拿剪子的太太，说，“我的头发不铰。这事休要再提。”
伊丽莎白姨妈身上出了一件了不得的事。她脸白了——她放下了剪子——她怔怔地望了那孩子一瞬，仿佛她换了个人、着了魔——然后，伊丽莎白·默里生平头一回掉头就跑——真的跑了——跑进了厨房。
“怎么了，伊丽莎白？”从厨屋进来的劳拉喊道。
“我看见——父亲——从她脸上望出来，”伊丽莎白哆嗦着，喘不上气。“她还说什么‘这事休要再提’——跟他从前说的一字不差——就是他那句话。”
艾米丽听见了，奔到餐柜的镜子前。方才说话的时候，她就有一种怪异的感觉，仿佛戴着一张别人的脸。这会儿它正在消退——可它离开的一霎，艾米丽瞥见了它——想必就是默里家的眼神（the Murray look）。难怪它吓着了伊丽莎白姨妈——它把她自己也吓着了——她庆幸它走了。她打了个寒噤——逃上她的阁楼藏身处，哭了一场；可不知怎么，她知道头发是保住了。
也真保住了；伊丽莎白姨妈再没提过这件事。不过接连好几天，她都没怎么招惹艾米丽。
还有一桩颇古怪的事：打那天起，艾米丽不再为失去的朋友伤心了。

===Original===

The matter had suddenly become of small importance. It was as if it had happened so long ago that nothing, save the mere emotionless memory of it, remained. Emily speedily regained appetite and animation, resumed her letters to her father and found that life tasted good again, marred only by a mysterious prescience that Aunt Elizabeth had it in for her in regard to her defeat in the matter of her hair and would get even sooner or later.
Aunt Elizabeth “got even” within the week. Emily was to go on an errand to the shop. It was a broiling day and she had been allowed to go barefooted at home; but now she must put on boots and stockings. Emily rebelled﻿—it was too hot﻿—it was too dusty﻿—she couldn’t walk that long half-mile in buttoned boots. Aunt Elizabeth was inexorable. No Murray must be seen barefooted away from home﻿—and on they went. But the minute Emily was outside the New Moon gate she deliberately sat down, took them off, stowed them in a hole in the dyke, and pranced away barefooted.
She did her errand and returned with an untroubled conscience. How beautiful the world was﻿—how softly blue was the great, round Blair Water﻿—how glorious that miracle of buttercups in the wet field below Lofty John’s bush! At sight of it Emily stood stock still and composed a verse of poetry.

> Buttercup, flower of the yellow dye,

I see thy cheerful face

Greeting and nodding everywhere

Careless of time and place.
> “In boggy field or public road

Or cultured garden’s pale

You sport your petals satin-soft,

And down within the vale.

So far, so good. But Emily wanted another verse to round the poem off properly and the divine afflatus seemed gone. She walked dreamily home, and by the time she reached New Moon she had got her verse and was reciting it to herself with an agreeable sense of completion.

> You cast your loveliness around

Where’er you chance to be,

And you shall always, buttercup,

Be a flower dear to me.

===Chinese===

那件事忽然变得无足轻重了，仿佛它发生在很久很久以前，除了毫无感情的一段记忆，什么也没剩下。艾米丽很快恢复了胃口和精神，重新给爸爸写起信来，觉得日子又有滋味了——只是心里悬着一种神秘的预感：头发这一仗伊丽莎白姨妈败了，她记下了仇，早晚会找补回来。
不出一个礼拜，伊丽莎白姨妈就“找补”了。艾米丽要去铺子跑一趟腿。那是个毒日头天，在家她已被准许打赤脚；可这会儿她得穿上靴子和袜子。艾米丽造反——天太热——土太大——穿着系扣靴子她走不动那半英里长路。伊丽莎白姨妈毫不动摇：默里家的人出门在外，断不可让人瞧见打赤脚——靴子袜子还是穿上了。可艾米丽一出院门，就一屁股坐下来，脱下靴袜，塞进墙洞里，赤着脚欢蹦乱跳地走了。
她办完事往回走，心里一点儿不亏。世界多美啊——又大又圆的布莱尔沃特那样柔和的蓝——高个子约翰（Lofty John）林子下头湿漉漉的田野里，那片毛茛花的奇迹多么辉煌！一见它，艾米丽站定了，当场作出一节诗：

> 毛茛花，一身染就明黄，

我望见你欢欢喜喜的脸庞，

处处点头，处处问好，

不挑时候，也不挑地方。

> 湿洼地也好，大路旁也好，

或在这园圃的篱边，

你抖开绸缎般柔软的花瓣，

也一路开到谷底田间。

到这里为止还不错。可艾米丽还想再来一节，好把诗囫囵收个尾，神来的气却像是断了。她一路做梦似的走回家，走到新月庄园，那一节也有了，正满心圆满地念给自己听：

> 你把可爱洒向四方，

无论你落脚何地，

毛茛花啊，你将永远永远

是我心头亲爱的花一枝。

===Original===

Emily felt very proud. This was her third poem and undoubtedly her best. Nobody could say *it* was very blank. She must hurry up to the garret and write it on a letter-bill. But Aunt Elizabeth was confronting her on the steps.
“Emily, where are your boots and stockings?”
Emily came back from cloudland with a disagreeable jolt. She had forgotten all about boots and stockings.
“In the hole by the gate,” she said flatly.
“You went to the store barefooted?”
“Yes.”
“After I had told you not to?”
This seemed to Emily a superfluous question and she did not answer it. But Aunt Elizabeth’s turn had come.

===Chinese===

艾米丽得意极了。这是她第三首诗，毫无疑问也是最好的一首。谁也不能说它“空得很”。得赶紧上阁楼，把它写在一封信单上。可伊丽莎白姨妈正站在台阶上迎着她。
“艾米丽，你的靴子和袜子呢？”
艾米丽从云端上叫人一磕，狼狈地跌了回来。靴子袜子，她忘了个精光。
“在门口的墙洞里，”她干巴巴地说。
“你光着脚上的铺子？”
“是。”
“我才嘱咐过你不许，你就去了？”
这在艾米丽看来是句多余的话，她没有答。可这一回，轮到伊丽莎白姨妈了。

## XI Ilse / 十一 伊尔丝
===Original===

Emily was locked in the spare-room and told that she must stay there until bedtime. She had pleaded against such a punishment in vain. She had tried to give the Murray look but it seemed that﻿—in her case at any rate﻿—it did not come at will.
“Oh, don’t shut me up alone there, Aunt Elizabeth,” she implored. “I know I was naughty﻿—but don’t put me in the spare-room.”
Aunt Elizabeth was inexorable. She knew that it was a cruel thing to shut an oversensitive child like Emily in that gloomy room. But she thought she was doing her duty. She did not realize and would not have for a moment believed that she was really wreaking her own smothered resentment with Emily for her defeat and fright on the day of the threatened hair-cutting. Aunt Elizabeth believed she had been stampeded on that occasion by a chance family resemblance coming out under stress, and she was ashamed of it. The Murray pride had smarted under that humbling, and the smart ceased to annoy her only when she turned the key of the spare-room on the white-faced culprit.
Emily, looking very small and lost and lonely, her eyes full of such fear as should have no place in a child’s eyes, shrank close against the door of the spare-room. It was better that way. She could not imagine things behind her then. And the room was so big and dim that a dreadful number of things could be imagined in it. Its bigness and dimness filled her with a terror against which she could not strive. Ever since she could remember she had had a horror of being shut up alone in semidarkness. She was not frightened of twilight out-of-doors, but this shadowy, walled gloom made of the spare-room a place of dread.
The window was hung with heavy, dark-green material, reinforced by drawn slat-blinds. The big canopied bed, jutting out from the wall into the middle of the floor, was high and rigid and curtained also with dark draperies. *Anything* might jump at her out of such a bed. What if some great black hand should suddenly reach out of it﻿—reach right across the floor﻿—and pluck at her? The walls, like those of the parlour, were adorned with pictures of departed relatives. There *was* such a large connection of dead Murrays. The glasses of their frames gave out weird reflections of the spectral threads of light struggling through the slat blinds.

===Chinese===

艾米丽被锁进了客房（the spare-room），还被告知得在里面待到睡觉时分。她怎么哀求也没能免掉这样的惩罚。她试着使出默里家的眼神（the Murray look），可看来——至少在她身上——它不听使唤，想使也使不出来。
“哦，别把我一个人关在那儿，伊丽莎白姨妈，”她恳求道，“我知道我淘气了——可别把我关进客房。”
伊丽莎白姨妈毫不动摇。她知道，把艾米丽这样神经过敏的孩子关进那间阴森森的屋子，是件狠心的事。可她认为她是在尽本分。她没有意识到，也片刻不肯相信，她其实是在发泄自己压下的私愤——为铰头发那天她吃的败仗、受的惊吓，朝艾米丽出气。伊丽莎白姨妈认定，那天她是叫一副偶然冒出来的家族面相在高压之下给吓退的，她为此羞䩂。默里家的骄傲在那次栽面里疼了好久，直到她把钥匙在客房门外一拧、锁住这个吓得脸煞白的罪人，这疼才不再搅扰她。
艾米丽缩在客房门边，显得那样小、那样孤苦无依，一双眼里盛满不该在孩子眼里有的恐惧。贴着门站还好些，那样她就想象不出背后有什么了。屋子又大又暗，能想象出多少可怕的东西来。它的大、它的暗灌了她一肚子恐怖，这恐怖她抗争不动。打记事起，她就怕独自一人关在半黑的地方。户外的黄昏她不怕，可这四面围墙里影子幢幢的昏暗，把客房变成了一个叫人胆寒的去处。
窗上挂着沉甸甸的墨绿帷幔，还加了拉严的百叶帘。那张带华盖的大床从墙边突出来，直伸到屋子当中，高高、硬挺，四围也垂着深色床幔。那样一张床，什么都可能从里头朝她扑出来。万一一只黑色的大手突然从床里伸出来——一直伸过地板——来攞她呢？四壁跟客厅一样，挂着故去亲眷的画像。故去的默里家的人可真是一大窝。画框的玻璃上，映着百叶帘缝里挣扎进来的几缕幽光，发出怪异的反照。

===Original===

Worst of all, right across the room from her, high up on the top of the black wardrobe, was a huge, stuffed white Arctic owl, staring at her with uncanny eyes. Emily shrieked aloud when she saw it, and then cowered down in her corner aghast at the sound she had made in the great, silent, echoing room. She wished that something *would* jump out of the bed and put an end to her.
“I wonder what Aunt Elizabeth would feel like if I was found here *dead*,” she thought, vindictively.
In spite of her fright she began to dramatize it and felt Aunt Elizabeth’s remorse so keenly that she decided only to be unconscious and come back to life when everybody was sufficiently scared and penitent. But people *had* died in this room﻿—dozens of them. According to Cousin Jimmy it was a New Moon tradition that when any member of the family was near death he or she was promptly removed to the spare-room, to die amid surroundings of proper grandeur. Emily could *see* them dying, in that terrible bed. She felt that she was going to scream again, but she fought the impulse down. A Starr must not be a coward. Oh, that owl! Suppose, when she looked away from it and then looked back she would find that it had silently hopped down from the wardrobe and was coming towards her. Emily dared not look at it for fear that was just what had happened. *Didn’t* the bed curtains stir and waver! She felt beads of cold perspiration on her forehead.
Then something did happen. A beam of sunlight struck through a small break in one of the slats of the blind and fell directly athwart the picture of Grandfather Murray hanging over the mantelpiece. It was a crayon “enlargement” copied from the old daguerreotype in the parlour below. In that gleam of light his face seemed veritably to leap out of the gloom at Emily with its grim frown strangely exaggerated. Emily’s nerve gave way completely. In an ungovernable spasm of panic she rushed madly across the room to the window, dashed the curtains aside, and caught up the slat blind. A blessed flood of sunshine burst in. Outside was a wholesome, friendly, human world. And, of all wonders, there, leaning right against the windowsill was a ladder! For a moment Emily almost believed that a miracle had been worked for her escape.
Cousin Jimmy had tripped that morning over the ladder, lying lost among the burdocks under the balm-of-gileads behind the dairy.

===Chinese===

最糟的是，屋子那头，黑漆大衣柜顶上，蹲着一只巨大的白色北极猫头鹰标本，一双眼睛诡异地瞪着她。艾米丽一看见它就失声尖叫，随即又缩在她那角落里，被自己在空旷、寂静、四壁回声的屋子里弄出的响动吓得魂飞魄散。她巴不得真有什么东西从床里跳出来，把她了结算了。
“不知道要是人们发现我死在这儿，伊丽莎白姨妈会是什么滋味，”她恨恨地想。
虽然怕，她还是照旧把这出戏在脑子里演了起来，把伊丽莎白姨妈的悔恨想得那样逼真，以致决定只让自己昏死过去，等人人都吓够、悔够，再活转来。可是这间屋子里真死人——死过几十个呢。照吉米表哥的说法，新月庄园有个规矩：家里谁快不行了，就赶紧抬进客房，好在够气派的氛围里咽气。艾米丽能看见他们在那张可怕的床上断气。她觉得自己又要尖叫了，可她把这冲动压了下去。斯塔尔家的人不能当胆小鬼。哦，那只猫头鹰！万一她把眼光挪开、再挪回来，发现它已经悄悄从柜顶蹿下来、正朝她走来呢？艾米丽不敢再看它，生怕恰是这般光景。那床幔不是在动、在晃么！她觉出额头上沁出一粒粒冷汗。
这时真出事了。一线阳光从百叶帘一块小破口里穿进来，正正落在壁炉上方挂着的默里外公（Grandfather Murray）的画像上。那是张炭笔“放大像”，照楼下客厅里那张老银版照片摹的。在那一线光里，外公的脸仿佛当真从昏暗里朝艾米丽扑出来，那副冷峻的皱眉夸张得吓人。艾米丽的神经彻底垮了。一阵按捺不住的惊惶，她疯了一样冲过屋子，扑到窗前，扯开帷幔，一把拉起百叶帘。一股蒙恩般的阳光涌了进来。外头是个健全、友好、人间烟火的世界。而且——奇迹中的奇迹——窗台上正正靠着一架梯子！有一霎艾米丽几乎相信，是特意显了个圣迹放她逃走。
那天早上，吉米表哥绊了一跤，才在奶房后头香脂白杨丛里的牛蒡棵子底下发现了这架梯子。

===Original===

It was very rotten and he decided it was time it was disposed of. He had shouldered it up against the house so that he would be sure to see it on his return from the hayfield.
In less time than it takes to write of it Emily had got the window up, climbed out on the sill, and backed down the ladder. She was too intent on escaping from that horrible room to be conscious of the shakiness of the rotten rungs. When she reached the ground she bolted through the balm-of-gileads and over the fence into Lofty John’s bush, nor did she stop running till she reached the path by the brook.
Then she paused for breath, exultant. She was full of a fearful joy with an elfin delight running through it. Sweet was the wind of freedom that was blowing over the ferns. She had escaped from the spare-room and its ghosts﻿—she had got the better of mean old Aunt Elizabeth.
“I feel as if I was a little bird that had just got out of a cage,” she told herself; and then she danced with joy of it all along her fairy path to the very end, where she found Ilse Burnley huddled up on the top of a fence panel, her pale-gold head making a spot of brilliance against the dark young firs that crowded around her. Emily had not seen her since that first day of school and again she thought she had never seen or pretended anybody just like Ilse.
“Well, Emily of New Moon,” said Ilse, “where are you running to?”
“I’m running away,” said Emily frankly. “I was bad﻿—at least, I was a little bad﻿—and Aunt Elizabeth locked me in the spare-room. I hadn’t been bad enough for *that*﻿—it wasn’t fair﻿—so I got out of the window and down the ladder.”
“You little cuss! I didn’t think you’d gimp enough for that,” said Ilse.
Emily gasped. It seemed very dreadful to be called a little cuss. But Ilse had said it quite admiringly.
“I don’t think it was gimp,” said Emily, too honest to take a compliment she didn’t deserve. “I was too scared to stay in that room.”
“Well, where are you going now?” asked Ilse. “You’ll have to go somewhere﻿—you can’t stay outdoors. There’s a thunderstorm coming up.”
So there was. Emily did not like thunderstorms. And her conscience smote her.
“Oh,” she said, “do you suppose God is bringing up that storm to punish me because I’ve run away?”
“No,” said Ilse scornfully. “If there is any God he wouldn’t make such a fuss over nothing.”
“Oh, Ilse, don’t you believe there is a God?”
“I don’t know.

===Chinese===

梯子朽得厉害，他断定该把它处置掉了。他把它扛过来靠在房子上，好打草场回来时保险瞧得见。
说时迟那时快，艾米丽推起窗子，爬上窗台，倒退着下了梯子。她一门心思逃出那间可怕的屋子，都顾不上觉出朽梯级的晃悠。脚一沾地，她一头钻过香脂白杨丛，翻过栅栏，进了高个子约翰（Lofty John）的林子，一口气跑到溪边小路上才停住。
她停下来喘气，欢欣鼓舞。她满心是一种带怕的欢喜，里头窜着一股精灵般的快乐。拂过蕨草的，是自由的、甜丝丝的风。她从客房和客房的鬼影里逃出来了——她斗赢了刻薄的老伊丽莎白姨妈。
“我觉得自己像只刚出笼的小鸟儿，”她对自己说；随后她一路欢喜地跳着舞，沿着她那条仙境小径走到头，只见伊尔丝·伯恩利（Ilse Burnley）蜷坐在一段栅栏顶上，她淡金色的脑袋衬着四围黑黢黢的幼冷杉，亮得像一团火。开学头一天之后艾米丽就没见过她，她又想：从没见过、也没假装见过哪个像伊尔丝这样的人。
“喂，新月庄园的艾米丽，”伊尔丝说，“你往哪儿跑？”
“我离家出走，”艾米丽坦坦荡荡地说。“我坏了——至少，坏了一点点——伊丽莎白姨妈把我锁进了客房。我这点儿坏，不该受那个罚——不公道——所以我就翻窗户、下梯子出来了。”
“你这个小鬼头！想不到你有这个胆量，”伊尔丝说。
艾米丽倒吸一口气。被人叫小鬼头，听着怪吓人的。可伊尔丝那口气分明是佩服。
“我看这不算什么胆量，”艾米丽说——她老实，不该领的恭维不肯领。“我是怕得待不住了才跑的。”
“那你这会儿往哪儿去？”伊尔丝问，“你总得有个去处——不能老待在露天地里。雷雨就要上来了。”
可不是嘛。艾米丽不喜欢雷雨。她的良心又谴责起她来。
“哦，”她说，“你说，是不是因为我逃跑了，上帝才兴起这场雷雨来罚我？”
“不是，”伊尔丝满脸不屑。“就算真有上帝，他也不会为这点儿鸡毛蒜皮大惊小怪。”
“哦，伊尔丝，你不信有上帝吗？”
“我不知道。

===Original===

Father says there isn’t. But in that case how did things happen? Some days I believe there’s a God and some days I don’t. You’d better come home with me. There’s nobody there. I was so dod-gastedly lonesome I took to the bush.”
Ilse sprang down and held out her sunburned paw to Emily. Emily took it and they ran together over Lofty John’s pasture to the old Burnley house which looked like a huge grey cat basking in the warm late sunshine, that had not yet been swallowed up by the menacing thunderheads. Inside, it was full of furniture that must have been quite splendid once; but the disorder was dreadful and the dust lay thickly over everything. Nothing was in the right place apparently, and Aunt Laura would certainly have fainted with horror if she had seen the kitchen. But it was a good place to play. You didn’t have to be careful not to mess things up. Ilse and Emily had a glorious game of hide and seek all over the house until the thunder got so heavy and the lightning so bright that Emily felt she must huddle on the sofa and nurse her courage.
“Aren’t you ever afraid of thunder?” she asked Ilse.
“No, I ain’t afraid of anything except the devil,” said Ilse.
“I thought you didn’t believe in the devil either﻿—Rhoda said you didn’t.”
“Oh, there’s a devil all right, Father says. It’s only God he doesn’t believe in. And if there *is* a devil and no God to keep him in order, is it any wonder I’m scared of him? Look here, Emily Byrd Starr, I like you﻿—heaps. I’ve always liked you. I knew you’d soon be good and sick of that little, white-livered, lying sneak of a Rhoda Stuart. *I* never tell lies. Father told me once he’d kill me if he ever caught me telling a lie. I want you for *my* chum. I’d go to school regular if I could sit with you.”
“All right,” said Emily offhandedly. No more sentimental Rhodian vows of eternal devotion for her. *That* phase was over.
“And you’ll tell me things﻿—nobody ever tells me things. And let *me* tell *you* things﻿—I haven’t anybody to tell things to,” said Ilse. “And you won’t be ashamed of me because my clothes are always queer and because I don’t believe in God?”
“No. But if you knew Father’s God you’d believe in *Him*.”
“I wouldn’t. Besides, there’s only one God if there is any at all.”
“I don’t know,” said Emily perplexedly. “No, it can’t be like that.

===Chinese===

爸爸说没有。可要是没有，万事万物又是怎么来的呢？有的日子我信有上帝，有的日子我不信。你顶好跟我回家吧。家里没人。我闷得要死，才钻林子来的。”
伊尔丝跳下栅栏，把她晒得黢黑的手爪子伸给艾米丽。艾米丽握住它，两人一同跑过高个子约翰的牧场，跑到伯恩利家的老宅——那房子活像一只灰色的大猫，正在还没让险恶的雷雨云吞掉的暖融融的夕阳里晒太阳。屋里满堂家具，从前想必也讲究气派过；如今乱得可怕，样样蒙着厚厚的灰。看样子没有一样东西待在该在的地方，厨房要是让劳拉姨妈（Aunt Laura）瞧见了，非晕过去不可。可它是个玩耍的好地方，不必小心翼翼怕弄乱了什么。伊尔丝和艾米丽满屋子痛痛快快玩了一场捉迷藏，直到雷声越来越沉、电光越来越亮，艾米丽觉得只好缩到沙发上给自己壮胆了。
“你就从来不怕打雷？”她问伊尔丝。
“不怕，我什么都不怕，就怕魔鬼，”伊尔丝说。
“我当你连魔鬼也不信呢——罗达说你不信。”
“哦，魔鬼是有的，爸爸说的。他只是不信上帝。再说，真要有魔鬼、又没有上帝管着他，我怕他有什么稀奇？听着，艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔（Emily Byrd Starr），我喜欢你——顶喜欢。我一直喜欢你。我早知道你很快就会烦透罗达·斯图尔特（Rhoda Stuart）那个没胆量的撒谎精。我从来不说谎。爸爸说过，他要逮住我说一回谎，就杀了我。我要你做我的好朋友。要是能同你坐一桌，我天天都肯上学。”
“好，”艾米丽随口应了。什么罗达式的山盟海誓、永不变心，她再不要了。那个阶段过去了。
“你得讲事情给我听——从来没人讲事情给我听。也让我讲给你听——我没人可讲，”伊尔丝说。“还有，别嫌我的衣裳总是古里古怪，别嫌我不信上帝，行吗？”
“不嫌。可你要是认得我爸爸的上帝，你就会信他了。”
“我不会。再说，要真有上帝，也只能有一个。”
“我不知道，”艾米丽困惑地说。“不对，不能是这样的。

===Original===

Ellen Greene’s God isn’t a bit like Father’s, and neither is Aunt Elizabeth’s. I don’t think I’d *like* Aunt Elizabeth’s, but He is a *dignified* God at least, and Ellen’s isn’t. And I’m sure Aunt Laura’s is another one still﻿—nice and kind but not wonderful like Father’s.”
“Well never mind﻿—I don’t like talking about God,” said Ilse uncomfortably.
“*I* do,” said Emily. “I think God is a very interesting subject, and I’m going to pray for you, Ilse, that you can believe in Father’s God.”
“Don’t you dast!” shouted Ilse, who for some mysterious reason did not like the idea. “I won’t be prayed for!”
“Don’t you ever pray yourself, Ilse?”
“Oh, now and then﻿—when I feel lonesome at night﻿—or when I’m in a scrape. But I don’t want anyone else to pray for me. If I catch you doing it, Emily Starr, I’ll tear your eyes out. And don’t you go sneaking and praying for me behind my back either.”
“All right, I won’t,” said Emily sharply, mortified at the failure of her well-meant offer. “I’ll pray for every single soul I know, but I’ll leave you out.”
For a moment Ilse looked as if she didn’t like this either. Then she laughed and gave Emily a volcanic hug.
“Well, anyway, please like me. Nobody likes me, you know.”
“Your father *must* like you, Ilse.”
“He doesn’t,” said Ilse positively. “Father doesn’t care a hoot about me. I think there’s times when he hates the sight of me. I wish he *did* like me because he can be awful nice when he likes anyone. Do you know what I’m going to be when I grow up? I’m going to be an elo-cu-tion-ist.”
“What’s that?”
“A woman who recites at concerts. I can do it dandy. What are you going to be?”
“A poetess.”
“Golly!” said Ilse, apparently overcome. “I don’t believe *you* can write poetry,” she added.
“I can so, too,” cried Emily. “I’ve written three pieces﻿—‘Autumn’ and ’Lines to Rhoda’﻿—only I burned *that*﻿—and ’An Address to a Buttercup.’ I composed it today and it is my﻿—my masterpiece.”
“Let’s hear it,” ordered Ilse.
Nothing loth, Emily proudly repeated her lines. Somehow she did not mind letting Ilse hear them.
“Emily Byrd Starr, you *didn’t* make that out of your own head?”
“I did.”
“Cross your heart?”
“Cross my heart.”
“Well”﻿—Ilse drew a long breath﻿—“I guess you *are* a poetess all right.”
It was a very proud moment for Emily﻿—one of the great moments of life, in fact. Her world had conceded her standing.

===Chinese===

艾伦·格林（Ellen Greene）的上帝一点儿不像我爸爸的，伊丽莎白姨妈的也不像。伊丽莎白姨妈那个，我想我不会喜欢，可他至少是位有威严的上帝，艾伦那个不是。劳拉姨妈的那个肯定又是另一位——和善是好，可不像爸爸的那样了不起。”
“行了，别说了——我不爱谈上帝，”伊尔丝不自在地说。
“我爱谈，”艾米丽说。“我觉得上帝是个顶有意思的题目。我要为你祷告，伊尔丝，好让你能信爸爸的上帝。”
“你敢！”伊尔丝嚷起来，也不知为什么，她就是不喜欢这个主意。“我不许人替我祷告！”
“那你自己从来不祷告吗，伊尔丝？”
“哦，偶尔——夜里觉得孤单的时候——或者闯了祸的时候。可我不要别人替我祷告。叫我逮着你祷告，艾米丽·斯塔尔，我就挖掉你的眼睛。也不许你背着我偷偷摸摸替我祷告。”
“好，不祷，”艾米丽没好气地说，一片好意碰了钉子，脸上臊得慌。“我认识的人，我一个个都祷告到，单单把你落下。”
有一会儿，伊尔丝好像连这个也不乐意。随即她笑起来，火山爆发似的搂了艾米丽一把。
“反正，请你喜欢我。你是知道的，没有人喜欢我。”
“你爸爸总该喜欢你吧，伊尔丝。”
“他不喜欢，”伊尔丝说得斩钉截铁。“爸爸压根儿不管我。我看有些时候他一见我就恨。我巴望他喜欢我，因为他喜欢起谁来，能好得不得了。你知道我长大了要做什么吗？我要当个演——说——家。”
“那是什么？”
“就是在音乐会上朗诵的女人。我干这个可拿手了。你长大了要做什么？”
“女诗人。”
“乖乖！”伊尔丝喊，分明给镇住了。“我不信你会写诗，”她又添一句。
“我就会，”艾米丽喊道。“我写了三首了——《秋》，还有《致罗达》——那首我烧了——还有《致一朵毛茛花》。今天刚作的，它是我的——我的杰作。”
“念念，”伊尔丝命令道。
艾米丽正求之不得，骄傲地背了她的诗句。不知怎么，让伊尔丝听听，她一点儿不拘束。
“艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔，这真不是你自己脑袋里编出来的？”
“是。”
“发誓？”
“发誓。”
“好家伙——”伊尔丝长长吸了口气——“我看你真是块当诗人的料。”
这是艾米丽顶得意的一刻——事实上，是人生的大时刻之一。她的世界承认了她的地位。

===Original===

But now other things had to be thought of. The storm was over and the sun had set. It was twilight﻿—it would soon be dark. She must get home and back into the spare-room before her absence was discovered. It was dreadful to think of going back but she must do it lest a worse thing come upon her at Aunt Elizabeth’s hands. Just now, under the inspiration of Ilse’s personality, she was full of Dutch courage. Besides, it would soon be her bedtime and she would be let out. She trotted home through Lofty John’s bush, that was full of the wandering, mysterious lamps of the fireflies, dodged cautiously through the balm-of-gileads﻿—and stopped short in dismay. The ladder was gone!
Emily went around to the kitchen door, feeling that she was going straight to her doom. But for once the way of the transgressor was made sinfully easy. Aunt Laura was alone in the kitchen.
“Emily dear, where on earth did you come from?” she exclaimed. “I was just going up to let you out. Elizabeth said I might﻿—she’s gone to prayer-meeting.”
Aunt Laura did not say that she had tiptoed several times to the spare-room door and had been racked with anxiety over the silence behind it. Was the child unconscious from fright? Not even while the thunderstorm was going on would relentless Elizabeth allow that door to be opened. And here was Miss Emily walking unconcernedly in out of the twilight after all this agony. For a moment even Aunt Laura was annoyed. But when she heard Emily’s tale her only feeling was thankfulness that Juliet’s child had not broken her neck on that rotten ladder.
Emily felt that she had got off better than she deserved. She knew Aunt Laura would keep the secret; and Aunt Laura let her give Saucy Sal a whole cupful of strippings, and gave her a big plummy cooky and put her to bed with kisses.
“You oughtn’t to be so good to me because I *was* bad today,” Emily said, between delicious mouthfuls. “I suppose I disgraced the Murrays going barefoot.”
“If I were you I’d hide my boots every time I went out of the gate,” said Aunt Laura. “But I wouldn’t forget to put them on before I came back. What Elizabeth doesn’t know will never hurt her.”
Emily reflected over this until she had finished her cooky. Then she said,
“That would be nice, but I don’t mean to do it any more. I guess I must obey Aunt Elizabeth because she’s the head of the family.”
“Where do you get such notions?”

===Chinese===

可眼下得想想别的了。雷雨过去了，太阳也落了。已是黄昏——天很快就要黑。她得赶在事情败露之前回到家里、回到客房里去。一想到要回去就发怵，可她必须回去，免得伊丽莎白姨妈给她更厉害的。这会儿，伊尔丝的性子给她打足了气。再说，很快就到睡觉的钟点，就会放她出来了。她一路小跑着穿过高个子约翰的林子回家去，林子里满是流萤明明灭灭的神秘的灯；她小心翼翼躲着穿过香脂白杨丛——一下子惊得僵住了。梯子不见了！
艾米丽绕到厨房门口，觉得这一步一步是在走向自己的末日。可这一回，罪人的路偏偏好走得不像话。厨房里只有劳拉姨妈一个人。
“艾米丽，亲爱的，你到底打哪儿冒出来的？”她喊道。“我正要上去放你呢。伊丽莎白答应我了——她上祷告会去了。”
劳拉姨妈没说，她已经踮着脚到客房门口去过好几趟，让门里那片死寂绞尽了心。孩子别是吓昏过去了？任凭外头雷雨大作，铁面无私的伊丽莎白也不许开那扇门。而这位小姐倒好，受完这一场罪，黄昏里安安然然从外头踱进来了。有一霎，连劳拉姨妈都有气了。可她一听艾米丽的故事，心里就只剩一样：谢天谢地，朱丽叶的孩子没在那架朽梯子上摔断脖子。
艾米丽觉得自己逃得比应得的好处还大。她知道劳拉姨妈会守住这个秘密；劳拉姨妈让她给调皮的萨尔（Saucy Sal）满满喝了一杯撇出来的奶油，又给了她一块又大又多葡萄干的甜饼，亲了亲她，送她上床。
“我今天干了坏事，你不该对我这么好，”艾米丽一边香喷喷地嚼着一边说。“我光着脚跑出去，怕是丢了默里家的脸。”
“我要是你，每回出院门都把靴子藏起来，”劳拉姨妈说。“可回来之前别忘了穿上。伊丽莎白不知道的事，伤不着她。”
艾米丽一路琢磨这话，直到把甜饼吃完。然后她说：
“那敢情好，可我往后不打算这么办了。我想我还是得听伊丽莎白姨妈的话，因为她是一家之主。”
“你这都打哪儿来的想法？”

===Original===

said Aunt Laura.
“Out of my head. Aunt Laura, Ilse Burnley and I are going to be chums. I like her﻿—I’ve always felt I’d like her if I had a chance. I don’t believe I can ever *love* any girl again, but I *like* her.”
“Poor Ilse!” said Aunt Laura, sighing.
“Yes, her father doesn’t like her. Isn’t it dreadful?” said Emily. “Why doesn’t he?”
“He does﻿—really. He only thinks he doesn’t.”
“But *why* does he think it?”
“You are too young to understand, Emily.”
Emily hated to be told she was too young to understand. She felt that she could understand perfectly well if only people would take the trouble to explain things to her and not be so mysterious.
“I wish I could pray for her. It wouldn’t be fair, though, when I know how she feels about it. But I’ve always asked God to bless all my friends so she’ll be in *that* and maybe some good will come of it. Is ’golly’ a proper word to say, Aunt Laura?”
“No﻿—no!”
“I’m sorry for that,” said Emily, seriously, “because it’s very striking.”

===Chinese===

劳拉姨妈问。
“我脑袋里自己长的。劳拉姨妈，伊尔丝·伯恩利和我要做好朋友了。我喜欢她——我一直觉得，只要有机会我准会喜欢她。我想我再也——再也不能爱哪个姑娘了，可我喜欢她。”
“可怜的伊尔丝！”劳拉姨妈叹了口气。
“是啊，她爸爸不喜欢她。多可怕呀，”艾米丽说。“他为什么不喜欢她？”
“他喜欢——真心喜欢。他只是自以为不喜欢。”
“可他为什么要那么想呢？”
“你还太小，不懂，艾米丽。”
艾米丽最恨人家说她太小、不懂。她觉得，只要人家肯费心给她讲明白，别这么神神秘秘的，她全能懂。
“我巴望能替她祷告。可明知道她是那个脾气，祷告就不公道了。不过我一直求上帝保佑我所有的朋友，她也就包括在里头了，说不定会有点好处。‘乖乖’这词儿说起来体面吗，劳拉姨妈？”
“不——不体面！”
“那可惜了，”艾米丽一本正经地说，“这词儿多响亮啊。”

## XII The Tansy Patch / 十二 艾菊园
===Original===

Emily and Ilse had a splendid fortnight of fun before their first fight. It was really quite a terrible fight, arising out of a simple argument as to whether they would or would not have a parlour in the playhouse they were building in Lofty John’s bush. Emily wanted a parlour and Ilse didn’t. Ilse lost her temper at once, and went into a true Burnley tantrum. She was very fluent in her rages and the volley of abusive “dictionary words” which she hurled at Emily would have staggered most of the Blair Water girls. But Emily was too much at home with words to be floored so easily; she grew angry too, but in the cool, dignified, Murray way which was more exasperating than violence. When Ilse had to pause for breath in her diatribes, Emily, sitting on a big stone with her knees crossed, her eyes black and her cheeks crimson, interjected little sarcastic retorts that infuriated Ilse still further. Ilse was crimson, too, and her eyes were pools of scintillating, tawny fire. They were both so pretty in their fury that it was almost a pity they couldn’t have been angry all the time.
“You needn’t suppose, you little puling, snivelling chit, that you are going to boss *me*, just because you live at New Moon,” shrieked Ilse, as an ultimatum, stamping her foot.
“I’m not going to boss you﻿—I’m not going to associate with you ever again,” retorted Emily, disdainfully.
“I’m glad to be rid of you﻿—you proud, stuck-up, conceited, top-lofty *biped*,” cried Ilse. “Never you speak to me again. And don’t you go about Blair Water saying things about me, either.”
This was unbearable to a girl who *never* “said things” about her friends or once-friends.
“I’m not going to *say* things about you,” said Emily deliberately. “I am just going to *think* them.”
This was far more aggravating than speech and Emily knew it. Ilse was driven quite frantic by it. Who knew what unearthly things Emily might be thinking about her any time she took the notion to? Ilse had already discovered what a fertile invention Emily had.
“Do you suppose I care what you think, you insignificant serpent? Why, you haven’t *any* sense.”
“I’ve got something then that’s far better,” said Emily, with a maddening superior smile. “Something that *you* can *never* have, Ilse Burnley.”
Ilse doubled her fists as if she would like to demolish Emily by physical force.

===Chinese===

艾米丽和伊尔丝痛痛快快玩了两个礼拜，才有了第一场架。那可真是场天翻地覆的架，起因不过是她们在高个儿约翰（Lofty John）林子里搭游戏屋，要不要隔出一间客厅。艾米丽要客厅，伊尔丝不要。伊尔丝当场就动了气，发起一阵地道的伯恩利式牛脾气。她发起火来口若悬河，那一串串骂人的“词典字眼”泼向艾米丽，换了布莱尔沃特大多数女孩都得被砸懵。可艾米丽同字词打惯了交道，哪有这么容易被打趴；她也恼了，却是那种冷冷的、端凝的、默里式的恼法，比撒泼更气人。伊尔丝的连珠炮骂到换气的空当，艾米丽便盘腿坐在一块大石头上，眼睛乌黑，脸蛋绯红，不紧不慢地插上一两句挖苦话，把伊尔丝气得更是火上浇油。伊尔丝也绯红着脸，一双眼睛成了两汪闪闪的黄火。她们俩发起怒来都那么好看，简直可惜了，不能一辈子气鼓鼓的。
“你少做梦，你这个哭哭啼啼、吸溜鼻涕的小丫头片子，以为你住新月庄园（New Moon），就想骑到*我*头上。”伊尔丝跺着脚，甩出最后通牌。
“我不骑你头上——我往后再也不理你了。”艾米丽鄙夷地回敬。
“甩了你我才高兴呢——你这个傲气冲天、自命不凡、鼻孔朝天的*两条腿*！”伊尔丝喊道。“往后休得再同我说话。也不许你在布莱尔沃特四处编排我。”
这话对一个*从不*编排朋友、哪怕是从前朋友的女孩子来说，是可忍孰不可忍。
“我不*说*你什么。”艾米丽一字一顿地说。“我只在心里*想*。”
这可比说出口气人得多，艾米丽心里有数。伊尔丝简直被逼疯了。谁知道艾米丽兴头一来，会在心里把她想成什么妖魔鬼怪？艾米丽那副想象力多么旺盛，伊尔丝早就领教过了。
“你当我稀帘你想什么，你这条没用的毒蛇？啐，你压根儿*没有*脑子。”
“我有的东西可比脑子强多了。”艾米丽说，带着叫人发狂的居高临下的微笑。“一样*你*永远*得不到*的东西，伊尔丝·伯恩利（Ilse Burnley）。”
伊尔丝攢紧双拳，活像要凭武力把艾米丽就地正法。

===Original===

“If I couldn’t write better poetry than you, I’d hang myself,” she derided.
“I’ll lend you a dime to buy a rope,” said Emily.
Ilse glared at her, vanquished.
“You go to the devil!” she said.
Emily got up and went, not to the devil, but back to New Moon. Ilse relieved *her* feelings by knocking the boards of their china closet down, and kicking their “moss gardens” to pieces, and departed also.
Emily felt exceedingly badly. Here was another friendship destroyed﻿—a friendship, too, that had been very delightful and satisfying. Ilse *had* been a splendid chum﻿—there was no doubt about that. After Emily had cooled down she went to the dormer window and cried.
“Wretched, wretched me!” she sobbed, dramatically, but very sincerely.
Yet the bitterness of her break with Rhoda was not present. *This* quarrel was fair and open and aboveboard. She had not been stabbed in the back. But of course she and Ilse would never be chums again. You couldn’t be chums with a person who called you a chit and a biped, and a serpent, and told you to go to the devil. The thing was impossible. And besides, Ilse could *never* forgive *her*﻿—for Emily was honest enough to admit to herself that she had been very aggravating, too.
Yet, when Emily went to the playhouse next morning, bent on retrieving her share of broken dishes and boards, there was Ilse, skipping around, hard at work, with all the shelves back in place, the moss garden remade, and a beautiful parlour laid out and connected with the living-room by a spruce arch.
“Hello, you. Here’s your parlour and I hope you’ll be satisfied now,” she said gaily. “What’s kept you so long? I thought you were never coming.”
This rather posed Emily after her tragic night, wherein she had buried her second friendship and wept over its grave. She was not prepared for so speedy a resurrection. As far as Ilse was concerned it seemed as if no quarrel had ever taken place.
“Why, that was *yesterday*,” she said in amazement, when Emily, rather distantly, referred to it. Yesterday and today were two entirely different things in Ilse’s philosophy. Emily accepted it﻿—she found she had to. Ilse, it transpired, could no more help flying into tantrums now and then than she could help being jolly and affectionate between them.

===Chinese===

“我要是写诗写不过你，早就吊死了。”她讥笑道。
“我借一毛钱给你买绳子。”艾米丽说。
伊尔丝瞪着她，败下阵来。
“你见鬼去吧！”她说。
艾米丽起身走了——没去见鬼，而是回了新月庄园。伊尔丝呢，把她们的小瓷柜隔板统统掀翻，把她们的“苔藓花园”踢了个稀烂，也算出了*她*的气，随后也走了。
艾米丽心里难受极了。又一段友谊毁了——还是一段顶快活、顶美满的友谊。伊尔丝*本来*是个顶呱呱的伙伴——这没说的。等火气消下去，艾米丽走到老虎窗前哭了。
“可怜的我，可怜的我呀！”她抽抽搭搭地哭，带着戏味儿，却是真心实意。
可同罗达决裂时的那股苦味，这回没有。*这*一场架吵得光明正大、摆在明处。她没有被背后捅刀子。不过她同伊尔丝当然再也不能做伙伴了。一个叫你小丫头片子、叫你两条腿、叫你毒蛇、还叫你见鬼去的人，你怎么同她做伙伴？没这个道理。再说，伊尔丝也*决*不会原谅*她*——艾米丽诚实，自己承认，她方才也气人得很。
可是，第二天早晨，艾米丽抱着取回属于她的那份破碗破板子的心思到游戏屋去时，伊尔丝正在那儿蹦来蹦去地忙乎：隔板全归了位，苔藓花园重新做好，还隔出了一间漂亮的客厅，一道云杉拱门把它同起居室连了起来。
“喂，你来啦。你的客厅在这儿，这回该满意了吧。”她欢欢喜喜地说。“怎么这么半天才来？我还当你不来了呢。”
艾米丽凄凄惨惨地哀悼了第二段友谊、在它坟头哭了一场，这下倒被问住了。这么快的复活，她没防备。在伊尔丝那头，仿佛压根儿没吵过架。
“啐，那是*昨天*的事呀。”艾米丽淡淡地提起时，她诧异地说。在伊尔丝的哲学里，昨天和今天是全然不同的两码事。艾米丽认了——她发现不认不行。原来伊尔丝隔三差五要发一顿牛脾气，是身不由己，正如两顿脾气之间她那份快活和热乎劲儿，也是身不由己。

===Original===

What amazed Emily, in whom things were bound to rankle for a time, was the way in which Ilse appeared to forget a quarrel the moment it was over. To be called a serpent and a crocodile one minute and hugged and darling-ed the next was somewhat disconcerting until time and experience took the edge off it.
“Aren’t I nice enough between times to make up for it?” demanded Ilse. “Dot Payne never flies into tempers, but would you like *her* for a chum?”
“No, she’s too stupid,” admitted Emily.
“And Rhoda Stuart is never out of temper, but you got enough of *her*. Do you think I’d ever treat you as she did?”
No, Emily had no doubt on this point. Whatever Ilse was or was not, she was loyal and true.
And certainly Rhoda Stuart and Dot Payne compared to Ilse were “as moonlight unto sunlight and as water unto wine”﻿—or would have been if Emily had as yet known anything more of her Tennyson than the “Bugle Song.”
“You can’t have everything,” said Ilse. “I’ve got Dad’s temper and that’s all there is to it. Wait till you see *him* in one of his rages.”
Emily had not seen this so far. She had often been down in the Burnley’s house but on the few occasions when Dr. Burnley had been home he had ignored her save for a curt nod. He was a busy man, for, whatever his shortcomings were, his skill was unquestioned and the bounds of his practice extended far. By the sickbed he was as gentle and sympathetic as he was brusque and sarcastic away from it. As long as you were ill there was nothing Dr. Burnley would not do for you; once you were well he had apparently no further use for you. He had been absorbed all through July trying to save Teddy Kent’s life up at the Tansy Patch. Teddy was out of danger now and able to be up, but his improvement was not speedy enough to satisfy Dr. Burnley. One day he held up Emily and Ilse, who were heading through the lawn to the pond, with fishing-hooks and a can of fat, abominable worms﻿—the latter manipulated solely by Ilse﻿—and ordered them to betake themselves up to the Tansy Patch and play with Teddy Kent.
“He’s lonesome and moping. Go and cheer him up,” said the doctor.
Ilse was rather loath to go. She liked Teddy, but it seemed she did not like his mother. Emily was secretly not averse. She had seen Teddy Kent but once, at Sunday School the day before he was taken seriously ill, and she had liked his looks.

===Chinese===

最叫艾米丽惊奇的是——她这人，事情总要在心里烂一阵子——伊尔丝吵完架，转眼就忘得一干二净。前一分钟还骂你是毒蛇、是鳄鱼，后一分钟就搂着你“亲爱的”长“亲爱的”短，这总有点让人发懵，直到时间和阅历把它的棱角磨钝。
“两顿脾气之间，我不够好吗，抵不了账吗？”伊尔丝质问道。“多特·佩恩（Dot Payne）从来不发脾气，可你愿意*她*做你的伙伴吗？”
“不愿意，她太蠢。”艾米丽承认。
“罗达·斯图尔特从来不发脾气，可*她*那点滋味你也尝够了。你想我会像她那样待你吗？”
不会，这一点艾米丽毫不怀疑。伊尔丝不管有这样那样的毛病，她是忠心的、实心实意的。
当然，罗达·斯图尔特和多特·佩恩比起来伊尔丝，是“月光之于阳光、水之于酒”——或者说，倘若艾米丽对她的丁尼生的了解不止于一首《号角歌》，本来会这么想的。
“人不能十全十美。”伊尔丝说。“我承了我爸的脾气，就这么回事。等着瞧*他*发火的样子吧。”
艾米丽到现在还没见识过。她常去伯恩利家，可伯恩利医生（Dr. Burnley）在家的那几回，除了生硬地点一下头，从没理过她。他是个大忙人——不管他有多少毛病，医术是没得说的，出诊的范围远远近近。在病床边，他温柔体贴；一离开病床，又粗暴又尖刻，判若两人。只要你病着，伯恩利医生什么都肯为你做；你一好，他对你似乎就再无用处了。整个七月，他都扑在艾菊园，全力抢救泰迪·肯特（Teddy Kent）的性命。泰迪如今脱离了危险，能下床了，可好得还不够快，不能让伯恩利医生满意。有一天，他把艾米丽和伊尔丝拦下来——两人正穿过草坪往池塘去，拿着钓钩和一罐肥滚滚、脏兮兮的蚯蚓，后者全归伊尔丝经手——命令她们上艾菊园去陪泰迪·肯特玩。
“他闷得慌，芫头芫脑的。去给他打打气。”医生说。
伊尔丝不大乐意去。她喜欢泰迪，可看样子不喜欢他母亲。艾米丽暗暗地并不抗拒。泰迪·肯特她只见过一回，在他重病前一天的主日学校里，她喜欢他的模样。

===Original===

It had seemed that he liked hers, too, for she caught him staring shyly at her over the intervening pews several times. He was very handsome, Emily decided. She liked his thick, dark-brown hair and his black-browed blue eyes, and for the first time it occurred to her that it might be rather nice to have a boy playmate, too. Not a “beau” of course. Emily hated the school jargon that called a boy your “beau” if he happened to give you a pencil or an apple and picked you out frequently for his partner in the games.
“Teddy’s nice but his mother is queer,” Ilse told her on their way to the Tansy Patch. “She never goes out anywhere﻿—not even to church﻿—but I guess it’s because of the scar on her face. They’re not Blair Water people﻿—they’ve only been living at the Tansy Patch since last fall. They’re poor and proud and not many people visit them. But Teddy is awfully nice, so if his mother gives us some black looks we needn’t mind.”
Mrs. Kent gave them no black looks, though her reception was rather distant. Perhaps she, too, had received some orders from the doctor. She was a tiny creature, with enormous masses of dull, soft, silky, fawn hair, dark, mournful eyes, and a broad scar running slantwise across her pale face. Without the scar she must have been pretty, and she had a voice as soft and uncertain as the wind in the tansy. Emily, with her instinctive faculty of sizing up people she met, felt that Mrs. Kent was not a happy woman.
The Tansy Patch was east of the Disappointed House, between the Blair Water and the sand-dunes. Most people considered it a bare, lonely, neglected place, but Emily thought it was fascinating. The little clapboarded house topped a small hill, over which tansy grew in a hard, flaunting, aromatic luxuriance, rising steeply and abruptly from a main road. A straggling rail fence, almost smothered in wild rosebushes, bounded the domain, and a sagging, ill-used little gate gave ingress from the road. Stones were let into the side of the hill for steps up to the front door. Behind the house was a tumbledown little barn, and a field of flowering buckwheat, creamy green, sloping down to the Blair Water. In front was a crazy veranda around which a brilliant band of red poppies held up their enchanted cups.
Teddy was unfeignedly glad to see them, and they had a happy afternoon together.

===Chinese===

看样子他也喜欢她的模样，因为她好几次撞见他隔着中间几排座位，羞答答地朝她张望。他长得很好看，艾米丽断定。她喜欢他浓密的深棕色头发和他黑眉下的蓝眼睛，并且头一回想到：有个男孩子做玩伴，说不定也挺美。当然不是“相好的”。学校里那套黑话，男孩送过你一支铅笔、一个苹果，做游戏常挑你搭伴，就管他叫你的“相好的”——艾米丽讨厌这套。
“泰迪人好，可他母亲古怪。”去艾菊园的路上伊尔丝告诉她。“她哪儿都不去——连教堂都不去——不过我猜是因为脸上那道疤。他们不是布莱尔沃特本地人——去年秋天才住到艾菊园来的。又穷又傲，没多少人上门。可泰迪好得不得了，所以他母亲要是给我们脸色看，只管别理她。”
肯特太太（Mrs. Kent）倒没给她们脸色看，只是接待得淡淡的。或许她也领了医生的什么吩咐。她是个小小的女人，一头灰扑扑、软滑如丝的小鹿色浓发堆在头上，一双幽幽的、哀愁的眼睛，一道宽宽的疤斜斜横过她苍白的脸。没有那道疤，她必定是好看的；她还有一副嗓音，又软又飘忽，像艾菊丛里的风。艾米丽天生会捨量初见的人，她觉出肯特太太不是个幸福的女人。
艾菊园在失望屋的东边，夹在布莱尔沃特（Blair Water）和沙丘之间。多数人觉得那是个荒凉、冷清、没人照管的地方，艾米丽却觉得它迷人极了。那座装板条墙的小屋坐落在一座小山包顶上，满山的艾菊长得又泼辣又茂盛，香气冲鼻，山包从大路边陡陡地拔起来。一道歪歪扭扭的栅栏，差不多被野玫瑰埋没了，圈住这片领地；一扇松垮垮、饱经作践的小门供人从大路进来。山包侧面嵌着一块块石头当台阶，一直通到前门。屋后是一座塌了架的小谷仓，还有一片开着花的荞麦田，绿得发奶白，一路斜下去接到布莱尔沃特。屋前是一条歪歪斜斜的游廊，一圈红艳艳的罂粟绕着它，举着它们着了魔的小酒杯。
泰迪见她们来，是真心欢喜，他们痛痛快快玩了一下午。

===Original===

There was some colour in Teddy’s clear olive skin when it ended and his dark-blue eyes were brighter. Mrs. Kent took in these signs greedily and asked the girls to come back, with an eagerness that was yet not cordiality. But they had found the Tansy Patch a charming place and were glad to go again. For the rest of the vacation there was hardly a day when they did not go up to it﻿—preferably in the long, smoky, delicious August evenings when the white moths sailed over the tansy plantation and the golden twilight faded into dusk and purple over the green slopes beyond and fireflies lighted their goblin torches by the pond. Sometimes they played games in the tansy patch, when Teddy and Emily somehow generally found themselves on the same side and then no more than a match for agile, quick-witted Ilse; sometimes Teddy took them to the barn loft and showed them his little collection of drawings. Both girls thought them very wonderful without knowing in the least how wonderful they really were. It seemed like magic to see Teddy take a pencil and bit of paper and with a few quick strokes of his slim brown fingers bring out a sketch of Ilse or Emily or Smoke or Buttercup, that looked ready to speak﻿—or meow.
Smoke and Buttercup were the Tansy Patch cats. Buttercup was a chubby, yellow, delightful creature hardly out of kittenhood. Smoke was a big Maltese and an aristocrat from the tip of his nose to the tip of his tail. There was no doubt whatever that he belonged to the cat caste of Vere de Vere. He had emerald eyes and a coat of plush. The only white thing about him was an adorable dicky.
Emily thought of all the pleasant hours spent at the Tansy Patch the pleasantest were those, when, tired with play, they all three sat on the crazy veranda steps in the mystery and enchantment of the borderland ’tween light and dark when the little clump of spruce behind the barn looked like beautiful, dark, phantom trees. The clouds of the west faded into grey and a great round yellow moon rose over the fields to be reflected brokenly in the pond, where the Wind Woman was making wonderful, woven lights and shadows.
Mrs. Kent never joined them, though Emily had a creepy conviction that she was watching them stealthily from behind the kitchen blind.

===Chinese===

一下午玩下来，泰迪清亮的橄榄色皮肤上有了血色，深蓝的眼睛也更亮了。肯特太太如饥似渴地看在眼里，请两个女孩再来——那殷切里却没有热络。可她们已经发觉艾菊园是个迷人的地方，乐得再去。假期剩下的日子，几乎没有一天她们不上那儿去——顶爱赶在八月里长长的、烟霭蒙蒙、妙不可言的傍晚：白蛾子驶过艾菊田，金色的暮光在对岸绿坡上褪成苍茫的紫，萤火虫在池边点起小妖精的火把。有时他们在艾菊丛里做游戏，泰迪同艾米丽不知怎么总归一拨，俩人加起来也才勉强敌得住手脚利落、鬼点子多的伊尔丝；有时泰迪带她们上谷仓的阁楼，给她们看他那一小收藏的画。两个女孩都觉得那些画神极了，却全然不知它们到底有多神。看泰迪拿一支铅笔、一张纸，细长的棕色手指刷刷几下，就勾出伊尔丝、艾米丽、烟儿（Smoke）或者毛茛（Buttercup）——活像马上要开口说话——或者啺一声——简直像魔法。
烟儿和毛茛是艾菊园的两只猫。毛茛是只圆滚滚、黄澄澄的小东西，才出猫崽期不久，惹人疼。烟儿是只大马耳他猫，从鼻尖到尾尖浑身一副贵族气。毫无疑问，它属于“维尔德维尔”那一阶层的猫。它有绿宝石的眼睛，天鹅绒似的皮毛，全身上下只有一处白：一小片可爱的白衬胸。
艾米丽想，在艾菊园度过的所有快乐时辰里，顶快乐的是这些时候：玩乏了，三个人坐在歪斜的游廊台阶上，浸在明暗之交那片神秘和魔魅里——谷仓后那一小丛云杉，看着就像一株株美丽、幽暗的幻影树。西天的云褪成灰色，一轮又大又圆的黄月亮从田野上升起，碎碎地映在池塘里，风之女正在那儿织着奇妙的光影。
肯特太太从不来凑他们的堆，虽然艾米丽有种毛毛的笃定：她正躲在厨房窗帘后头，悄悄窥着他们。

===Original===

Teddy and Ilse sang school ditties, and Ilse recited, and Emily told stories; or they sat in happy silence, each anchored in some secret port of dreams, while the cats chased each other madly over the hill and through the tansy, tearing round and round the house like possessed creatures. They would spring up at the children with sudden pounces and spring as suddenly away. Their eyes gleamed like jewels, their tails swayed like plumes. They were palpitating with nervous, stealthy life.
“Oh, isn’t it good to be alive﻿—like this?” Emily said once. “Wouldn’t it be dreadful if one had never lived?”
Still, existence was not wholly unclouded﻿—Aunt Elizabeth took care of that. Aunt Elizabeth only permitted the visits to the Tansy Patch under protest, and because Dr. Burnley had ordered them.

> “Aunt Elizabeth does not aprove of Teddy,”

Emily wrote in one of her letters to her father﻿—which epistles were steadily multiplying on the old garret sofa shelf.

===Chinese===

泰迪和伊尔丝唱学校的歌谣，伊尔丝朗诵，艾米丽讲故事；要不然，他们就快快活活地静静坐着，各自泊在梦的某个秘密港湾里，任两只猫满山疯追、穿过艾菊丛，绕着屋子一圈圈狂奔，像中了邪。它们冷不丁朝孩子们猛扑过来，又冷不丁弹开。眼睛亮得像宝石，尾巴摇得像羽翎。它们浑身上下颤着一种神经质的、潜行的生命力。
“哦，这样活着，多好啊。”有一回艾米丽说。“要是从来没活过，那才可怕呢，是不是？”
不过日子也并非全无阴翳——伊丽莎白姨妈管着这一条呢。伊丽莎白姨妈准她们去艾菊园，是勉勉强强、留了异议的，还因为那是伯恩利医生的吩咐。

> “伊丽莎白姨妈不赞成泰迪，”

艾米丽在给爸爸的一封信里写道——那些书信，正一封一封在阁楼旧沙发的搁架上积多起来。

===Original===

> “The first time I asked her if I might go and play with Teddy she looked at me *severely* and said, Who is this Teddy person. We do not know anything about these Kents. Remember, Emily, the Murrays do not assosiate with everyone. I said I am a Starr﻿—I am not a Murray, you said so yourself. Dear Father I did not mean to be impertnent but Aunt Elizabeth said I was and would not speak to me the rest of the day. She seemed to think that was a very bad punishment but I did not mind it much only it is rather unpleasant to have your own family preserve a disdaneful silence towards you. But since then she lets me go to the Tansy Patch because Dr. Burnley came and told her to. Dr. Burnley has a *strange inflewence* over Aunt Elizabeth. I do not understand it. Rhoda said once that Aunt Elizabeth hoped Dr. Burnley and Aunt Laura would make a match of it﻿—which, you know means get married﻿—but that is not so. Mrs. Thomas Anderson was here one afternoon to tea. (Mrs. Thomas Anderson is a big fat woman and her grandmother was a Murray and there is nothing else to say about *her*.) She asked Aunt Elizabeth if she thought Dr. Burnley would marry again and Aunt Elizabeth said no, he would not and she did not think it right for people to marry a second time. Mrs. Anderson said Sometimes I have thought he would take Laura. Aunt Elizabeth just swept her a hawty glance. There is no use in denying it, there are times when I am very proud of Aunt Elizabeth, even if I do not like her.
> “Teddy is a very nice boy, Father. I think you would aprove of him. Should there be two *p*’s in aprove? He can make splendid pictures and he is going to be a famus artist some day, and then he is going to paint my portrate. He keeps his pictures in the barn loft because his mother doesn’t like to see them. He can whistle just like a bird. The Tansy Patch is a *very quante* place﻿—espesially at night. I love the twilight there. We always have such fun in the twilight. The Wind Woman makes herself small in the tansy just like a tiny, tiny fairy and the cats are so queer and creepy and delightful then. They belong to Mrs. Kent and Teddy is afraid to pet them much for fear she will drown them. She drowned a kitten once because she thought he liked it better than her. But he didn’t because Teddy is *very much attatched* to his mother. He washes the dishes for her and helps her in all the house work.

===Chinese===

> “头一回我问她可不可以去找泰迪玩，她*严厉*地看着我说，这个泰迪是什么人。这些肯特家的人，我们一无所知。记住，艾米丽，默里家的人不跟什么人都来往。我说，我是斯塔尔家的人——我不是默里家的人，您自己说过的。亲爱的爸爸，我没想冒犯，可伊丽莎白姨妈说我就是冒犯了，一整天不理我。她好像觉得这是顶厉害的责罚，可我不大在乎，只是自家人朝你端出一副鄙夷的沉默，终究不大好受。不过打那以后她准我去艾菊园了，因为伯恩利医生登门吩咐了她。伯恩利医生对伊丽莎白姨妈有一种*奇妙的影向力*。我不明白。罗达有一回说，伊丽莎白姨妈盼着伯恩利医生同劳拉姨妈结成一对——结成一对，你知道，就是结婚——可不是那么回事。有一天下午托马斯·安德森太太（Mrs. Thomas Anderson）来喝茶。（托马斯·安德森太太是个大胖女人，她祖母是默里家的人，关于*她*没有别的可说了。）她问伊丽莎白姨妈，觉得伯恩利医生会不会再婚，伊丽莎白姨妈说不会，而且她认为人们再婚是不对的。安德森太太说，有时候我想他会娶劳拉的。伊丽莎白姨妈只朝她扫了一眼傲慢的眼风。没法否认，有些时候我挺为伊丽莎白姨妈骄傲的，哪怕我不喜欢她。
> “泰迪是个很好的男孩，爸爸。我想你会赞成他的。对了，“赞成”是不是要两个“p”？他会画顶棒的画，往后要成了名的画家，那时他就来给我画像。他的画都藏在谷仓阁楼里，因为他母亲不爱看见它们。他吹口哨像鸟儿一样。艾菊园是个*顶别致*的地方——尤其在夜里。我爱那儿的暮色。我们在暮色里总是玩得那么开心。风之女把自己变小，躲进艾菊丛里，就像一个小小的、小小的仙子；那时候猫也变得那么古怪、瘆人又可爱。猫是肯特太太的，泰迪不敢太爱抚它们，怕她淹死它们。她淹死过一只小猫，因为她以为他爱那只猫胜过爱她。其实他才没有，因为泰迪*十分恋着*他母亲。他替她洗碗，家务活儿样样帮忙。

===Original===

Ilse says the boys in school call him sissy for that but I think it is noble and manley of him. Teddy wishes she would let him have a dog but she wont. I have thought Aunt Elizabeth was tirannical but Mrs. Kent is far worse in some ways. But then she loves Teddy and Aunt Elizabeth does not love me.
> “But Mrs. Kent doesn’t like Ilse or me. She never says so but we *feel* it. She never asks us to stay to tea﻿—and we’ve always been so polite to her. I believe she is jellus of us because Teddy likes us. Teddy gave me the sweetest picture of the Blair Water he had painted on a big white cowhawk shell but he said I mustn’t let his mother know about it because she would cry. Mrs. Kent is a very misterious person, very like some people you read of in books. I like misterious people but not too close. Her eyes always look hungry though she has plenty to eat. She never goes anywhere because she has a scar on her face where she was burned with a lamp exploding. It made my blood run cold, dear Father. How thankful I am that Aunt Elizabeth only burns candles. Some of the Murray tradishuns are very sensible. Mrs. Kent is very relijus﻿—what she calls relijus. She prays even in the middle of the day. Teddy says that before he was born into this world he lived in another one where there were two suns, one red and one blue. The days were red and the nights blue. I don’t know where he got the idea but it sounds atractive to me. And he says the brooks run honey instead of water. But what did you do when you were thirsty, I said. Oh, we were never thirsty there. But I think I would *like* to be thirsty because then cold water tastes so good. *I* would like to live in the moon. It must be such a nice silvery place.
> “Ilse says Teddy ought to like her best because there is more fun in her than in me but that is not true. There is just as much fun in me when my conshence doesn’t bother me. I guess Ilse wants Teddy to like her best but she is not a jellus girl.
> “I am glad to say that Aunt Elizabeth and Aunt Laura both aprove of my friendship with Ilse. It is so seldom they aprove of the same thing. I am getting used to fighting with Ilse now and don’t mind it much. Besides I can fight pretty well myself when my blood is up. We fight about once a week but we make up right away and Ilse says things would be dull if there was never a row.

===Chinese===

伊尔丝说学校的男孩们为这个叫他娘娘腔，可我觉得他这样又高贵又有男子气。泰迪盼着她肯让他养条狗，可她不肯。我从前以为伊丽莎白姨妈专治，可肯特太太有些地方还狠得多。不过她爱泰迪，伊丽莎白姨妈可不爱我。
> “可肯特太太不喜欢伊尔丝，也不喜欢我。她从不说，可我们*感觉得到*。她从不留我们喝茶——我们对她一向多客气呀。我相信她是忌妒我们，因为泰迪喜欢我们。泰迪送了我一幅顶可爱的画，是画在一只大白螺壳上的布莱尔沃特，可他说千万别让他母亲知道，因为她会哭。肯特太太是个顶顶神秘的人，很像书里读到过的某类人。我喜欢神秘的人，可不能太贴近。她的眼睛总显得饿，虽然她吃的不缺。她哪儿都不去，因为脸上有道疤，是灯爆炸烧的。听得我血都凉了，亲爱的爸爸。我多庆幸伊丽莎白姨妈只点蜡烛。默里家的有些传通真是很有道理。肯特太太非常虔诚——她自己所谓的虔诚。她连大白天都祷告。泰迪说，他生到这世上之前，住在另一个世界，那儿有两个太阳，一个红的，一个蓝的。白天是红的，夜是蓝的。我不知道他这念头打哪儿来的，可我觉得怪吸引人的。他还说那儿的溪里流的是蜜，不是水。那你们口渴了怎么办呢，我说。哦，我们那儿从来不口渴。可我想我*倒愿意*口渴，因为口渴的时候凉水才那么好喝。*我*愿意住在月亮上。那儿一定是个银灿灿的好地方。
> “伊尔丝说泰迪本该最喜欢她，因为她比我好玩。才不是呢。只要良心不来烦我，我一样好玩。我猜伊尔丝是想让泰迪最喜欢她，可她不是个爱忌妒的女孩。
> “我很高兴地说，伊丽莎白姨妈和劳拉姨妈都赞成我同伊尔丝交朋友。她们俩赞成同一件事，可真难得。如今我同伊尔丝吵架吵惯了，不怎么在乎了。再说急起来我自己也吵得挺厉害。我们大约一个礼拜吵一回，可吵完马上和好，伊尔丝说，要是永远不吵一场，日子就寡淡了。

===Original===

I would like it better without rows but you can never tell what will make Ilse mad. She never gets mad twice over the same thing. She calls me dreadful names. Yesterday she called me a lousy lizard and a toothless viper. But somehow I didn’t mind it much because I knew I wasn’t lousy or toothless and she knew it too. I don’t call names because that is unladylike but I smile and that makes Ilse far madder than if I skowled and stamped as she does, and that is why I do it. Aunt Laura says I must be careful not to pick up the words Ilse uses and try to set her a good example because the poor child has no one to look after her propperly. I wish I could use some of her words because they are so striking. She gets them from her father. I think my aunts are too perticular. One night when the Rev. Mr. Dare was here to tea I used the word bull in my conversashun. I said Ilse and I were afraid to go through Mr. James Lee’s pasture where the old well was because he had a cross bull there. After Mr. Dare had gone Aunt Elizabeth gave me an awful skolding and told me I was never to use *that word* again. But she had been talking of tigers at tea﻿—in connexshun with missionaries﻿—and I can’t understand why it is more disgraceful to talk about bulls than tigers. Of course bulls are feroshus animals but so are tigers. But Aunt Elizabeth says I am always disgracing them when they have company. When Mrs. Lockwood was here from Shrewsbury last week they were talking about Mrs. Foster Beck, who is a bride, and I said Dr. Burnley thought she was devilishly pretty. Aunt Elizabeth said **Emily** in an awful tone. She was pale with rath. Dr. Burnley said it, I cryed, I am only kwoting. And Dr. Burnley did say it the day I stayed to dinner with Ilse and Dr. Jameson was there from Shrewsbury. I saw Dr. Burnley in one of his rages that afternoon over something Mrs. Simms had done in his office. It was a groosome sight. His big yellow eyes blazed and he tore about and kicked over a chair and threw a mat at the wall and fired a vase out of the window and said *terrible things*. I sat on the sofa and stared at him like one fassinated. It was so interesting I was sorry when he cooled down which he soon did because he is like Ilse and never stays mad long. He never gets mad at Ilse though. Ilse says she wishes he would﻿—it would be better than being taken no notis of.

===Chinese===

要是不吵我更喜欢，可你永远说不准什么会惹伊尔丝发火。同一件事她从不发两回火。她骂我可怕的名字。昨天她骂我长虱子的蜥蜴、没牙的毒蛇。可不知怎么我不大在乎，因为我知道我不长虱子也没掉牙，她也知道。我不骂人，因为那不淑女，可我微笑，这比像她那样皱脸跺脚更叫她冒火，所以我才这么办。劳拉姨妈说我要小心，别染上伊尔丝用的字眼，要给她树个好榜样，因为可怜的孩子没人好好照管她。我倒真想用用她的一些字眼，因为那么有力气。她那些话是跟她爸爸学的。我觉得姨妈们太龟毛。有天晚上戴尔牧师（Rev. Mr. Dare）来喝茶，我在谈话里用了公牛这个词。我说伊尔丝和我害怕穿过老井所在的詹姆斯·李先生（Mr. James Lee）家的牧场，因为他在那儿养了头凶公牛。戴尔牧师走后，伊丽莎白姨妈把我臭骂了一顿，说*那个词*我永远不许再用。可喝茶的时候她自己一直在谈老虎——联系着传教士谈的——我不明白谈公牛怎么就比谈老虎更不体面。公牛当然是凶猛的动物，老虎也是呀。可伊丽莎白姨妈说，家里一来客我总要丢她们的脸。上礼拜什鲁斯伯里（Shrewsbury）的洛克伍德太太（Mrs. Lockwood）来，她们正说着新娘子福斯特·贝克太太（Mrs. Foster Beck），我就说，伯恩利医生觉得她漂亮得要命。伊丽莎白姨妈用可怕的声调叫了一声**艾米丽**。她脸都气白了。是伯恩利医生说的呀，我叫道，我只是引用。伯恩利医生确实说过，就是那天我留在伊尔丝家吃饭、什鲁斯伯里的詹姆森医生（Dr. Jameson）也在的那天。那天下午我还看见了伯恩利医生发火，为的是西姆斯太太在他诊室里做的事。那景象吓人。他的大黄眼睛喷着火，人冲来冲去，踢翻一把椅子，拿垫子砸墙，把一只花瓶掷出窗外，嘴里说着*可怕的话*。我坐在沙发上，像中了魔一样盯着他看。太有意思了，他冷下来我倒觉得可惜。他冷得很快，因为他像伊尔丝，气不长。可他从不冲伊尔丝发火。伊尔丝说她倒希望他发——那也比没人理睬强。

===Original===

She is as much of an orfan as I am, poor child. Last Sunday she went to church with her old faded blue dress on. There was a tare right in front of it. Aunt Laura wepped when she came home and then spoke to Mrs. Simms about it because she did not dare speak to Dr. Burnley. Mrs. Simms was cross and said it was not her place to look after Ilses close but she said she had got Dr. Burnley to get Ilse a nice sprigged muslin dress and Ilse had got egg stane on it, and when Mrs. Simms skolded her for being so careless Ilse flew into a rage and went upstairs and tore the muslin dress to pieces, and Mrs. Simms said she wasn’t going to bother her head again about a child like that and there was nothing for her to ware but her old blue but Mrs. Simms didn’t know it was tore. So I sneaked Ilses dress over to New Moon and Aunt Laura mended it neetly and hid the tare with a pocket. Ilse said she tore up her muslin dress one of the days she didn’t believe in God and didn’t care what she did. Ilse found a mouse in her bed one night and she just shook it out and jumped in. Oh, how brave. I could never be as brave as that. It is not true that Dr. Burnley never smiles. I have seen him do it but not often. He just smiles with his lips but not his eyes and it makes me feel uncomfortable. Mostly he laughs in a horrid sarkastic way like *Jolly Jim’s* uncle.
> “We had barley soup for dinner that day﻿—very watery.
> “Aunt Laura is giving me five cents a week for washing the dishes. I can only spend one cent of it and the other four have to be put in the toad bank in the sitting-room on the mantel. The toad is made of brass and sits on top of the bank and you put the cents in his mouth one at a time. He swallows them and they drop into the bank. It’s very fassinating (I should not write fassinating again because you told me I must not use the same word too often but I cant think of any other that deskribes my feelings so well). The toad bank is Aunt Laura’s but she said I could use it. I just hugged her. Of course I never hug Aunt Elizabeth. She is too rijid and bony. She does not aprove of Aunt Laura paying me for washing dishes. I tremble to think what she would say if she knew Cousin Jimmy gave me a whole dollar on the sly last week.
> “I wish he had not given me so much. It worrys me. It is an awful responsibility.

===Chinese===

她同我一样是个孤儿，可怜的孩子。上礼拜日她穿着那件褪了色的旧蓝裙子上教堂。裙子前头破了个口子。劳拉姨妈回家哭了一场，然后去找西姆斯太太说，因为她不敢去同伯恩利医生说。西姆斯太太没好气，说照管伊尔丝的衣裳不归她管，不过她说她本来托伯恩利医生给伊尔丝弄了件小白点细布裙，伊尔丝把鸡蛋汤洒上头了，西姆斯太太骂她不小心，伊尔丝发起火来，上楼把细布裙撕了个粉碎，西姆斯太太说她再也不为这种孩子操心费神了，她除了旧蓝裙没的穿——可西姆斯太太不知道裙子破了。于是我把伊尔丝的裙子偷偷带过新月庄园来，劳拉姨妈细细补好，用一个口袋盖住了破口。伊尔丝说，她撕碎细布裙的那天，正是不信上帝的日子，什么都不在乎。有天夜里伊尔丝在床上发现一只老鼠，她把它抖出去，照旧钻进被窝。哦，多勇敢。我永远勇敢不到那个地步。伯恩利医生从来不笑，这话不确实。我见过他笑，只是不多。他光用嘴唇笑，眼睛不笑，看得我不自在。多数时候他笑得又可憎又讥剌，像*快活的吉姆*的叔叔。
> “那天我们午饭喝的是大麦汤——稀得很。
> “劳拉姨妈一礼拜给我五分钱，让我洗碗。我只许花一分，另外四分都得投进起居室壁炉架上的癞蛤蟆储蓄罐。那蛤蟆是铜做的，蹲在罐顶上，你把分币一枚一枚放进它嘴里。它咽下去，钱就掉进罐里。太迷人了（我不该再写迷人这个字了，因为你叮嘱过同一个字不能用得太勤，可我想不出别的字这么配我的心情）。蛤蟆罐是劳拉姨妈的，她说我可以使。我搂了她一下。当然我从不搂伊丽莎白姨妈。她太僵太瘦。她不赞成劳拉姨妈付我洗碗钱。要是她知道吉米表哥上礼拜偷偷给了我一整块钱，不知会说什么，我想想就发抖。
> “我真希望他没给我这么多。它让我发愁。这是可怕的责任。

===Original===

It will be so diffikult to spend it wisely also without Aunt Elizabeth finding out about it. I hope I shall never have a million dollars. I am sure it would crush me utterly. I keep my dollar hid on the shelf with my letters and I put it in an old envelope and wrote on it Cousin Jimmy Murray gave me this so that if I died suddenly and Aunt Elizabeth found it she would know I came by it honestly.
> “Now that the days are getting cool Aunt Elizabeth makes me wear my thick flannel petticoat. I hate it. It makes me so bunchy. But Aunt Elizabeth says I must wear it because you died of consumption. I wish close could be both graceful and helthy. I read the story of Red Riding Hood today. I think the wolf was the most intresting caracter in it. Red Riding Hood was a stupid little thing so easily fooled.
> “I wrote two poems yesterday. One was short and entitelled Lines Adressed to a blue-eyed-grass flower gathered in the Old Orchard. Here it is.
> “The other poem was long and I wrote it on a letter-bill. It is called The Monark of the Forest. The Monark is the big birch in Lofty John’s bush. I love that bush so much it hurts. Do you understand that kind of hurting. Ilse likes it too and we play there most of the time when we are not at the Tansy Patch. We have three paths in it. We call them the Today Road, the Yesterday Road and the Tomorrow Road. The Today Road is by the brook and we call it that because it is lovely now. The Yesterday Road is out in the stumps where Lofty John cut some trees down and we call it that because it used to be lovely. The Tomorrow Road is just a tiny path in the maple clearing and we call it that because it is going to be lovely some day, when the maples grow bigger. But oh Father dear I haven’t forgotten the dear old trees down home. I always think of them after I go to bed. But I am happy here. It isn’t wrong to be happy, is it Father. Aunt Elizabeth says I got over being homesick very quick but I am often homesick *inside*. I have got akwanted with Lofty John. Ilse is a great friend of his and often goes there to watch him working in his carpenter shop. He says he has made enough ladders to get to heaven without the priest but that is just his joke. He is really a very devowt Catholic and goes to the chapel at White Cross every Sunday. I go with Ilse though perhaps I ought not to when he is an enemy of my family.

===Chinese===

要把它花得聪明，又不叫伊丽莎白姨妈发觉，多难呀。但愿我永远没有一百万块钱。我敢说它准会把我整个压垮。我那块钱同信一起藏在搁架上，装在一只旧信封里，信封上写着：这是吉米·默里表哥给我的。这样我万一猛地死了，伊丽莎白姨妈找着它，也知道我这是清清白白得来的。
> “如今天气转凉，伊丽莎白姨妈叫我穿上厚法兰绒衬裙。我恨它。穿得我鼓鼓囊囊。可伊丽莎白姨妈说非穿不可，因为你是死于肺痨的。衣裳要是既好看又卫生就好了。今天我读了小红帽的故事。我看那狼是里面顶有意思的角色。小红帽是个蠢东西，那么容易上当。
> “昨天我写了两首诗。一首短的，题作《致老果园里采得的一朵蓝眼草》。诗在这儿。
> “另一首长，写在一张邮单上。题目是《森林之王》。森林之王就是高个儿约翰林子里那棵大白桦。我爱那片林子，爱得发疼。你懂这种疼吗。伊尔丝也喜欢它，我们不去艾菊园的时候，多半在那儿玩。我们在里面踩出了三条小径。我们叫它们今日路、昨日路和明日路。今日路在小溪边，因为现在它可爱。昨日路在树桩地里，高个儿约翰在那儿砍倒过一些树，因为从前它可爱。明日路只是枫树空地里一条小径，因为等枫树长大了，有朝一日它会可爱。可是，哦，亲爱的爸爸，我没有忘记老家那些亲爱的老树。上床之后我总想起它们。可我在这儿是快活的。快活不是罪过吧，爸爸。伊丽莎白姨妈说我想家好得快，可我常常是心里头想家。我同高个儿约翰认识了。伊尔丝同他很要好，常去看他在木匠铺里做活。他说他做的梯子已经够爬上天堂、不用神父帮忙了，可那只是他的玩笑。他其实是个顶顶虔敬的天主教徒，每个礼拜日都上白十字（White Cross）的小教堂。我随伊尔丝一起去，虽说他是我们家的仇人，我也许不该去。

===Original===

He is of stately baring and refined manners﻿—very sivil to me but I don’t always like him. When I ask him a serius question he always winks over my head when he ansers. That is insulting. Of course I never ask any questions on relijus subjects but Ilse does. She likes him but she says he would burn us all at the stake if he had the power. She asked him right out if he wouldn’t and he winked at me and said Oh, we wouldn’t burn nice pretty little Protestants like you. We would only burn the old ugly ones. That was a frivellus reply. Mrs. Lofty John is a nice woman and not at all proud. She looks just like a little rosy rinkled apple.
> “On rainy days we play at Ilse’s. We can slide down the bannisters and do what we like. Nobody cares only when the doctor is home we have to be quiet because he cant bear any noise in the house except what he makes himself. The roof is flat and we can get out on it through a door in the garret ceiling. It is very exiting to be up on the roof of a house. We had a yelling contest there the other night to see which could yell the loudest. To my surprise I found I could. You never can tell what you can do till you try.
