“你太谦虚了，艾米丽。整整一石板的——诗——想想吧，孩子们——诗。咱们学校出了一位会写——诗——的学生。可她还不愿让我们读这——诗。我看艾米丽是自私。我拿准我们人人都会欣赏这——诗——的。”
布朗内尔小姐每说一回“诗”——带着讥诮的重音、前头带着那可恨的顿挫——艾米丽就抽缩一回。许多孩子咯咯笑，一半因为看“新月庄园（New Moon）的默里”受煎熬他们开心，一半因为她们觉出布朗内尔小姐指望她们笑。罗达·斯图尔特（Rhoda Stuart）笑得比谁都响；可开学头一天折磨过艾米丽的珍妮·斯特兰偏不笑，反倒朝布朗内尔小姐黑沉沉地皱眉。
布朗内尔小姐举起石板，拖长调子、带着鼻音把艾米丽的诗当众念了，用的是荒唐的腔调和手势，把诗念成了一件可笑透顶的东西。艾米丽自以为最精彩的句子，显得最可笑。别的学生笑得更凶了，艾米丽觉得这一刻的苦涩永远不会从心里出去。那些落笔时那样美的小小情致，如今都摔碎了、碰伤了，像撕裂的、血肉模糊的蝴蝶——“仙境梦中的幽径啊”，布朗内尔小姐闭着眼、左右晃着脑袋，拖腔念道。咯咯的笑变成哄堂大笑。
“哦，”艾米丽攢紧拳头想，“我巴望——我巴望《圣经》里吃掉坏孩子的熊来把你吃掉。”
学校林子里却没有什么行善罚恶的熊，布朗内尔小姐把整首“诗”念完了。她痛快极了。羞辱学生向来叫她开心，而这学生是新月庄园的艾米丽——她一直隐隐觉出这孩子的心底灵魂里有种同她截然两样的东西——这开心便更其精妙。
念完，她把石板递还给满脸通红的艾米丽。

===Original===

“Take your﻿—*poetry*, Emily,” she said.
Emily snatched the slate. No slate “rag” was handy but Emily gave the palm of her hand a fierce lick and one side of the slate was wiped off. Another lick﻿—and the rest of the poem went. It had been disgraced﻿—degraded﻿—it must be blotted out of existence. To the end of her life Emily never forgot the pain and humiliation of that experience.
Miss Brownell laughed again.
“What a pity to obliterate such﻿—*poetry*, Emily,” she said. “Suppose you do those sums now. They are not﻿—*poetry*, but I am in this school to teach arithmetic and I am not here to teach the art of writing﻿—*poetry*. Go to your own seat. Yes, Rhoda?”
For Rhoda Stuart was holding up her hand and snapping her fingers.
“Please, Miss Brownell,” she said, with distinct triumph in her tones, “Emily Starr has a whole bunch of poetry in her desk. She was reading it to Ilse Burnley this morning while you thought they were learning history.”
Perry Miller turned around and a delightful missile, compounded of chewed paper and known as a “spit pill,” flew across the room and struck Rhoda squarely in the face. But Miss Brownell was already at Emily’s desk, having reached it one jump before Emily herself.
“Don’t touch them﻿—you have no *right*!” gasped Emily frantically.
But Miss Brownell had the “bunch of poetry” in her hands. She turned and walked up to the platform. Emily followed. Those poems were very dear to her. She had composed them during the various stormy recesses when it had been impossible to play out of doors and written them down on disreputable scraps of paper borrowed from her mates. She had meant to take them home that very evening and copy them on letter-bills. And now this horrible woman was going to read them to the whole jeering, giggling school.
But Miss Brownell realized that the time was too short for that. She had to content herself with reading over the titles, with some appropriate comments.
Meanwhile Perry Miller was relieving his feelings by bombarding Rhoda Stuart with spit pills, so craftily timed that Rhoda had no idea from what quarter of the room they were coming and so could not “tell” on anyone. They greatly interfered with her enjoyment of Emily’s scrape, however. As for Teddy Kent, who did not wage war with spit pills but preferred subtler methods of revenge, he was busy drawing something on a sheet of paper.

“拿好你的——诗，艾米丽，”她说。
艾米丽一把抢过石板。手边没有擦石板的破布，她狠狠舔了一下手心，石板一面擦净了。再舔一下——诗的其余部分也没了。它已经受了辱——贬了值——必须让它从世上抹掉。到老到死，艾米丽忘不了这一场的疼痛和羞辱。
布朗内尔小姐又笑了。
“把这样的——诗——毁掉，多可惜，艾米丽，”她说。“这会儿做算术题吧。它们不是——诗——可我在这学校是教算术的，不是教写——诗——的。回你自己座位去。什么事，罗达？”
原来罗达·斯图尔特举着手，啪啪地打响指。
“报告布朗内尔小姐，”她说，话音里透着明明白白的得意，“艾米丽·斯塔尔课桌里还有一整沓诗。今儿早上您当她俩在学历史的时候，她正念给伊尔丝·伯恩利（Ilse Burnley）听呢。”
佩里·米勒（Perry Miller）转过身，一枚可爱的暗器——嚼碎的纸团成的，名叫“唾沫弹”——横飞过教室，正中罗达的脸。可布朗内尔小姐已经到了艾米丽的课桌前——比艾米丽本人早到一步。
“不许碰——你没有权利！”艾米丽发疯似的喘着喊。
可布朗内尔小姐已经把“那一沓诗”抓在手里。她转身走上讲台。艾米丽跟上去。那些诗是她的宝贝。都是风雪交加、没法出门去玩的课间里作的，写在她跟同学讨来的皱皱巴巴的纸片上。她本来打算当天傍晚就带回家，誊到信单上。如今这个可怕的女人要当着满堂噝笑的学校念它们了。
好在布朗内尔小姐明白时间不够。她只好满足于把各首标题念一遍，配上几句恰如其分的评语。
这当口，佩里·米勒正拿唾沫弹轰击罗达·斯图尔特出气，发射的时机刁钻得很，罗达摸不清弹从教室哪个角落来，也就没法“告发”谁。不过这大大搅了她看艾米丽倒霉的兴致。至于泰迪·肯特（Teddy Kent）——他不打唾沫弹的仗，偏爱更精微的报复法子——他正埋头在一张纸上画着什么。

第二天早上罗达在自己课桌上发现了那张纸：纸上画着一只瘦骨嶙峋的小猴，尾巴倒挂在树枝上；那猴脸，活脱是罗达·斯图尔特的脸。罗达勃然大怒，可为了自己的体面，把画撕了个粉碎，绝口不提。她不知道泰迪还画了另一张相仿的：布朗内尔小姐化作一只蝙蝠精的模样——散学时塞进了艾米丽手里。
“《失落的钻石（Dimond）——一个浪漫故事》，”布朗内尔小姐念道。“《咏一棵桦树》——我看更像咏一张很脏的纸，艾米丽——《作于园中日晷》——同上——《咏我爱猫》——想必又是一段浪漫的‘尾’巴——《致伊尔丝颂》——‘你的颈项泛着奇妙的珠光’——未必吧，我看。伊尔丝的脖子晒得黑着呢——《记（Deskripshun）我们客厅》、《紫罗兰的拼字（Spell）》——但愿紫罗兰拼字比你强，艾米丽——《失望之屋》——

> “‘百合花举起白杯，

请蜜蜂来喝——喝——喝。’”

“我不是那样写的！”备受折磨的艾米丽喊。
“《咏劳拉姨妈柜（Burow）抽屉里的一块锦缎》、《离家别辞》、《咏一棵云杉》——‘它挡住暑气、烈日和炫光，依我看（I ween）是棵好树’——艾米丽，你拿得准你懂‘依我看’是什么意思吗？——《汤姆·贝内特先生的田》之诗、《咏伊丽莎白姨妈窗外风景（Vew）》之诗——艾米丽，你写‘风景’是一把好手——《淹死的小猫墓志（Epitaff）》、《在我高祖母墓前的沉思（Meditashuns）》——可怜的太太——《致我北方的鸟》、《作于布莱尔沃特岸边观星》——哼——哼——

> “‘缀满数不清的宝石，

那些星辰，遥远、清冷而忠贞。’”

===Original===

Don’t try to pass those lines off as your own, Emily. You couldn’t have written them.”
“I did﻿—I did!” Emily was white with sense of outrage. “And I’ve written lots far better.”
Miss Brownell suddenly crumpled the ragged little papers up in her hand.
“We have wasted enough time over this trash,” she said. “Go to your seat, Emily.”
She moved towards the stove. For a moment Emily did not realize her purpose. Then, as Miss Brownell opened the stove door, Emily understood and bounded forward. She caught at the papers and tore them from Miss Brownell’s hand before the latter could tighten her grasp.
“You *shall not* burn them﻿—you shall not have them,” gasped Emily. She crammed the poems into the pocket of her “baby apron” and faced Miss Brownell in a kind of calm rage. The Murray look was on her face﻿—and although Miss Brownell was not so violently affected by it as Aunt Elizabeth had been, it nevertheless gave her an unpleasant sensation, as of having roused forces with which she dared not tamper further. This tormented child looked quite capable of flying at her, tooth and claw.
“Give me those papers, Emily,”﻿—but she said it rather uncertainly.
“I will not,” said Emily stormily. “They are mine. You have no right to them. I wrote them at recesses﻿—I didn’t break any rules. You”﻿—Emily looked defiantly into Miss Brownell’s cold eyes﻿—“You are an unjust, tyrannical *person*.”
Miss Brownell turned to her desk.
“I am coming up to New Moon tonight to tell your Aunt Elizabeth of this,” she said.
Emily was at first too much excited over saving her precious poetry to pay much heed to this threat. But as her excitement ebbed cold dread flowed in. She knew she had an unpleasant time ahead of her. But at all events they should not get her poems﻿—not one of them, no matter what they did to *her*. As soon as she got home from school she flew to the garret and secreted them on the shelf of the old sofa.
She wanted terribly to cry but she would not. Miss Brownell was coming and Miss Brownell should *not* see her with red eyes. But her heart burned within her. Some sacred temple of her being had been desecrated and shamed. And more was yet to come, she felt wretchedly sure. Aunt Elizabeth was certain to side with Miss Brownell. Emily shrank from the impending ordeal with all the dread of a sensitive, fine strung nature facing humiliation.

这几句可别想冒充是你自己写的，艾米丽。你写不出这样的句子。”
“我写的——就是我写的！”艾米丽气得脸煞白。“我还写了好些强得多的呢。”
布朗内尔小姐忽然把那沓皱巴巴的小纸片攢成一团。
“这堆破烂浪费的时间够多了，”她说。“回座位去，艾米丽。”
她朝火炉走去。有一霎艾米丽没明白她的用意。随后布朗内尔小姐打开炉门，艾米丽懂了，一个箭步蹿上去。她抢住那些纸，赶在布朗内尔小姐攢紧之前，从她手里扯了下来。
“你不许烧——不许拿走，”艾米丽喘着说。她把诗一股脑塞进“娃娃围裙”的兜里，带着一种冷静的怒气面对布朗内尔小姐。默里家的眼神挂在她脸上——布朗内尔小姐虽不像伊丽莎白姨妈那样被它狠狠震动，却也起了一阵不痛快的感觉，仿佛惊动了什么她不敢再招惹的力量。这备受折磨的孩子，看样子完全敢朝她扑上来，又抓又咬。
“把那些纸给我，艾米丽”——可她说得挺没底气。
“不给，”艾米丽怒冲冲地说。“它们是我的。你没有权利。我都是课间写的——我没违反一条规矩。你——”艾米丽挑衅地直视布朗内尔小姐冷漠的眼睛——“你是个不公正的、专横的人。”
布朗内尔小姐转向讲台。
“我今晚上新月庄园，把这一切告诉你伊丽莎白姨妈，”她说。
起初艾米丽为抢救出心爱的诗，激动得顾不上理会这句威胁。可激动一退，冰冷的恐惧涌了进来。她知道前头有她好受的。但无论如何，他们休想夺走她的诗——一首也不行，随他们对“她”怎么样。一放学回到家，她飞奔上阁楼，把诗藏进旧沙发的搁板上。
她拼命想哭，可她不哭。布朗内尔小姐要来了，布朗内尔小姐不能看见她红眼圈。可她心里烧着火。她生命里某座圣殿，遭了亵渎和羞辱。而且还有更难堪的在后头，她愁苦地拿得准。伊丽莎白姨妈必定向着布朗内尔小姐。艾米丽怀着敏感、纤细的天性面对羞辱时的全部恐惧，缩着身子等那场即将到来的审判。

===Original===

She would not have been afraid of justice; but she knew at the bar of Aunt Elizabeth and Miss Brownell she would not have justice.
“And I can’t write Father about it,” she thought, her little breast heaving. The shame of it all was too deep and intimate to be written out, and so she could find no relief for her pain.
They did not have supper at New Moon in winter time until Cousin Jimmy had finished his chores and was ready to stay in for the night. So Emily was left undisturbed in the garret.
From the dormer window she looked down on a dreamland scene that would ordinarily have delighted her. There was a red sunset behind the white, distant hills, shining through the dark trees like a great fire; there was a delicate blue tracery of bare branch shadows all over the crusted garden; there was a pale, ethereal alpenglow all over the southeastern sky; and presently there was a little, lovely new moon in the silvery arch over Lofty John’s bush. But Emily found no pleasure in any of them.
Presently she saw Miss Brownell coming up the lane, under the white arms of the birches, with her mannish stride.
“If my father was alive,” said Emily, looking down at her, “you would go away from this place with a flea in your ear.”
The minutes passed, each seeming very long to Emily. At last Aunt Laura came up.
“Your Aunt Elizabeth wants you to come down to the kitchen, Emily.”
Aunt Laura’s voice was kind and sad. Emily fought down a sob. She hated to have Aunt Laura think she had been naughty, but she could not trust herself to explain. Aunt Laura would sympathize and sympathy would break her down. She went silently down the two long flights of stairs before Aunt Laura and out to the kitchen.
The supper table was set and the candles were lighted. The big black-raftered kitchen looked spookish and weird, as it always did by candlelight. Aunt Elizabeth sat rigidly by the table and her face was very hard. Miss Brownell sat in the rocking-chair, her pale eyes glittering with triumphant malice. There seemed something baleful and poisonous in her very glance. Also, her nose was very red﻿—which did not add to her charm.
Cousin Jimmy, in his grey jumper, was perched on the edge of the wood-box, whistling at the ceiling, and looking more gnome-like than ever. Perry was nowhere to be seen. Emily was sorry for this.

===Chinese===

她不怕公正的裁决；可她知道，在伊丽莎白姨妈和布朗内尔小姐的公堂上，她得不到公正。
“这事也不能写信告诉爸爸，”她想，小小的胸口起伏着。这份耻辱太深、太贴己，写不出来，她的苦楚便没了出口。
冬天，新月庄园要等吉米表哥忙完杂活、预备过夜不出门了才开晚饭。于是艾米丽在阁楼里落得清静。
她从老虎窗望下去，望见一幅往常准叫她心醉的梦境景色：远处白色的山峦背后一轮红红的落日，像一场大火，从黑黢黢的树后照过来；冻硬的园子上，秃枝的影子织满淡蓝的细纹；东南天是一片缥缈的雪峰般的晚照；不多时，高个子约翰（Lofty John）林子上空的银色穹顶里，升起一弯可爱的新月。可艾米丽对哪一样也乐不起来。
不久她看见布朗内尔小姐顺着小径走来，从白桦白色的臂弯底下走过，迈着她那男人似的大步。
“我爸爸要是活着，”艾米丽低头望着她说，“管叫你碰一鼻子灰，夹着尾巴离开这地方。”
时间一分一分地过，艾米丽只觉每一分都长。末了劳拉姨妈（Aunt Laura）上来了。
“你伊丽莎白姨妈要你下楼到厨房去，艾米丽。”
劳拉姨妈的声音又和善又难过。艾米丽咽下一声呜咽。她不愿让劳拉姨妈当她不乖，可又不敢开口分辩。劳拉姨妈会同情她，而同情会叫她垮下来。她默默走在劳拉姨妈前头，下完两道长楼梯，进了厨房。
晚饭桌摆好了，蜡烛点上了。黑梁大厨房一烛光底下照例显得鬼气森森。伊丽莎白姨妈僵直地坐在桌旁，脸色铁硬。布朗内尔小姐坐在摇椅里，浅色的眼睛闪着得意的恶意。她的目光本身就带着一股凶邪的毒气。还有，她的鼻子很红——这可没给她添什么风采。
吉米表哥穿着灰色工装衫，蹲在木箱边上，对着天花板吹口哨，比往常更像个小地精。佩里不见了人影。艾米丽很遗憾。

===Original===

The presence of Perry, who was on her side, would have been a great moral support.
“I am sorry to say, Emily, that I have been hearing some very bad things about your behaviour in school today,” said Aunt Elizabeth.
“No, I don’t think you are sorry,” said Emily, gravely.
Now that the crisis had come she found herself able to confront it coolly﻿—nay, more, to take a curious interest in it under all her secret fear and shame, as if some part of her had detached itself from the rest and was interestedly absorbing impressions and analyzing motives and describing settings. She felt that when she wrote about this scene later on she must not forget to describe the odd shadows the candle under Aunt Elizabeth’s nose cast upward on her face, producing a rather skeletonic effect. As for Miss Brownell, could *she* ever have been a baby﻿—a dimpled, fat, laughing baby? The thing was unbelievable.
“Don’t speak impertinently to *me*,” said Aunt Elizabeth.
“You see,” said Miss Brownell, significantly.
“I don’t mean to be impertinent, but you are *not* sorry,” persisted Emily. “You are angry because you think I have disgraced New Moon, but you are a little glad that you have got someone to agree with you that I’m bad.”
“What a *grateful* child,” said Miss Brownell﻿—flashing her eyes up at the ceiling﻿—where they encountered a surprising sight. Perry Miller’s head﻿—and no more of him﻿—was stuck down out of the “black hole” and on Perry Miller’s upside-down face was a most disrespectful and impish grimace. Face and head disappeared in a flash, leaving Miss Brownell staring foolishly at the ceiling.
“You have been behaving disgracefully in school,” said Aunt Elizabeth, who had not seen this byplay. “I am ashamed of you.”
“It was not as bad as that, Aunt Elizabeth,” said Emily steadily. “You see it was this way﻿—”
“I don’t want to hear anything more about it,” said Aunt Elizabeth.
“But you must,” cried Emily. “It isn’t fair to listen only to *her* side. I was a little bad﻿—but not so bad as she says﻿—”
“Not another word! I have heard the whole story,” said Aunt Elizabeth grimly.
“You heard a pack of lies,” said Perry, suddenly sticking his head down through the black hole again.
Everybody jumped﻿—even Aunt Elizabeth, who at once became angrier than ever because she *had* jumped.
“Perry Miller, come down out of that loft instantly!” she commanded.
“Can’t,” said Perry laconically.

===Chinese===

佩里是站在她这边的，有他在场，就是一个大大的道义后盾。
“我很遗憾地听说，艾米丽，今天你在学校的表现非常糟糕，”伊丽莎白姨妈说。
“不，我看你并不遗憾，”艾米丽正色说。
危机真来了，她反倒发现自己能冷静应对——不止冷静，在暗地的恐惧和羞惭底下，还对这场面生出一种古怪的兴趣，仿佛她身上有一部分脱离了其余部分，正饶有兴味地收纳印象、剖析动机、描画布景。她想，回头写到这一场时，千万别忘了写伊丽莎白姨妈鼻子底下那支蜡烛往脸上投的怪影子，投出一种骷髅似的效果。至于布朗内尔小姐——她难道也当过婴儿——当过一个有小酒窝、胖乎乎、咯咯笑的婴儿？这事没法信。
“不许对我无礼，”伊丽莎白姨妈说。
“你瞧见了吧，”布朗内尔小姐意味深长地说。
“我不是存心无礼，可你确实不遗憾，”艾米丽不让步。“你是生气，因为你当我丢了新月庄园的脸；可你也有点儿高兴，因为总算有个人肯附和你，说我是坏孩子了。”
“好一个不知好歹的孩子，”布朗内尔小姐说着，眼睛倏地翻上去瞪天花板——却撞见一桩惊人的光景。佩里·米勒的脑袋——也仅有脑袋——从那个“黑洞”里倒探下来，倒挂着的那张脸上，是一副放肆透顶的顽童鬼脸。脸和脑袋一闪就没了，撇下布朗内尔小姐傻瞪着天花板。
“你在学校的表现丢尽了脸，”没瞧见这一幕的伊丽莎白姨妈说。“我为你害臊。”
“没有那么糟，伊丽莎白姨妈，”艾米丽稳稳地说。“是这样的，您听我说——”
“我不想再听了，”伊丽莎白姨妈说。
“可您必须听，”艾米丽喊，“只听她一面之词，不公道。我是坏了一点儿——可没她说的那么坏——”
“不许再说一个字！事情我已经听全了，”伊丽莎白姨妈冷冷地说。
“您听的是一大堆谎话，”佩里忽然又从黑洞里倒探下脑袋来说。
人人一跳——连伊丽莎白姨妈也跳了，而且立刻火冒三丈，因为她居然跳了。
“佩里·米勒，马上给我从那顶棚上下来！”她喝令。
“下不来，”佩里言简意赅。

===Original===

“At once, I say!”
“Can’t,” repeated Perry, winking audaciously at Miss Brownell.
“Perry Miller, come down! I *will* be obeyed. I am mistress here *yet*.”
“Oh, all right,” said Perry cheerfully. “If I must.”
He swung himself down until his toes touched the ladder. Aunt Laura gave a little shriek. Everybody else seemed to be stricken dumb.
“I’ve just got my wet duds off,” Perry was saying cheerfully, waving his legs about to get a foothold on the ladder while he hung to the sides of the black hole with his elbows. “Fell into the brook when I was watering the cows. Was going to put on dry ones﻿—but just as you say﻿—”
“Jimmy,” implored poor Elizabeth Murray, surrendering at discretion. *She* could not cope with the situation.
“Perry, get back into that loft and get your clothes on this minute!” ordered Cousin Jimmy.
The bare legs shot up and disappeared. There was a chuckle as mirthful and malicious as an owl’s beyond the black hole. Aunt Elizabeth gave a convulsive gasp of relief and turned to Emily. She was determined to regain ascendancy and Emily must be thoroughly humbled.
“Emily, kneel down here before Miss Brownell and ask her pardon for your conduct today,” she said.
Into Emily’s pale cheek came a scarlet protest. She could not do this﻿—she would ask pardon of Miss Brownell but not on her knees. To kneel to this cruel woman who had hurt her so﻿—she could not﻿—would not do it. Her whole nature rose up in protest against such a humiliation.
“Kneel down,” repeated Aunt Elizabeth.
Miss Brownell looked pleased and expectant. It would be very satisfying to see this child who had defied her kneeling before her as a penitent. Never again, Miss Brownell felt, would Emily be able to look levelly at her with those dauntless eyes that bespoke a soul untamable and free, no matter what punishment might be inflicted upon body or mind. The memory of this moment would always be with Emily﻿—she could never forget that she had knelt in abasement. Emily felt this as clearly as Miss Brownell did and remained stubbornly on her feet.
“Aunt Elizabeth, *please* let me tell my side of the story,” she pleaded.
“I have heard all I wish to hear of the matter. You will do as I say, Emily, or you will be outcast in this house until you do. No one will talk to you﻿—play with you﻿—eat with you﻿—have anything to do with you until you have obeyed me.”
Emily shuddered.

===Chinese===

“我说立刻！”
“下不来，”佩里再说一遍，放肆地朝布朗内尔小姐挤挤眼。
“佩里·米勒，下来！我说了就得依。眼下这个家还是我当。”
“哦，行，”佩里痛痛快快地说。“非下不可就下。”
他把身子荡下来，直到脚尖够着梯子。劳拉姨妈轻轻尖叫了一声。其余人人像被掐住了脖子。
“我那身湿衣裳刚脱下来，”佩里一边快快活活地说，一边晃着两条腿在梯子上找落脚处，两个胳膊肘还扒着黑洞的边。“饮牛的时候掉溪里了。本来要换干衣裳——不过您怎么说就怎么办——”
“吉米！”可怜的伊丽莎白·默里告饶了。她应付不了这个局面。
“佩里，马上回顶棚去，把衣裳穿上！”吉米表哥下令。
那两条光腿烀地缩上去，没了。黑洞那头传来一声猫头鹰似的又乐又损的笑。伊丽莎白姨妈痉挛似的松了口气，转向艾米丽。她拿定主意要夺回威势，艾米丽必须被彻底压服。
“艾米丽，跪到布朗内尔小姐跟前来，为你今天的行为请罪，”她说。
艾米丽苍白的脸上腾起一片抗议的红。这个她做不来——她可以向布朗内尔小姐道歉，可不能跪着。跪向这个深深伤害过她的狠毒女人——她不能——决不。她整个的天性都起来抗议这样的羞辱。
“跪下，”伊丽莎白姨妈又说一遍。
布朗内尔小姐露出快意而期待的神色。眼看这个忤逆过她的孩子跪在跟前悔罪，将多么称心。布朗内尔小姐觉得，从此往后，无论怎样责罚她的身心，艾米丽再也不能用那双无所畏惧的眼睛——那宣告着灵魂不羁、不可驯服的眼睛——平平地直视她了。这一刻的记忆将缠住艾米丽一辈子——她永远忘不了自己曾卑辱地下跪。这一点，艾米丽感得同布朗内尔小姐一样清楚，所以执拗地站着不动。
“伊丽莎白姨妈，求您让我讲讲我这方面的原委，”她恳求道。
“该听的我都听够了。你就照我说的办，艾米丽，不然在这个家里，你就被弃绝到你照办为止。谁也不同你说话——不同你玩——不同你吃饭——同你老死不相往来，直到你依了我。”
艾米丽抖了一下。

===Original===

*That* was a punishment she could not face. To be cut off from her world﻿—she knew it would bring her to terms before long. She might as well yield at once﻿—but, oh, the bitterness, the shame of it!
“A human being should not kneel to anyone but God,” said Cousin Jimmy, unexpectedly, still staring at the ceiling.
A sudden strange change came over Elizabeth Murray’s proud, angry face. She stood very still, looking at Cousin Jimmy﻿—stood so long that Miss Brownell made a motion of petulant impatience.
“Emily,” said Aunt Elizabeth in a different tone, “I was wrong﻿—I shall not ask you to kneel. But you must apologize to your teacher﻿—and I shall punish you later on.”
Emily put her hands behind her and looked straight into Miss Brownell’s eyes again.
“I am sorry for anything I did today that was wrong,” she said, “and I ask your pardon for it.”
Miss Brownell got on her feet. She felt herself cheated of a legitimate triumph. Whatever Emily’s punishment would be she would not have the satisfaction of seeing it. She could have shaken “simple Jimmy Murray” with a right good will. But it would hardly do to show all she felt. Elizabeth Murray was not a trustee but she was the heaviest ratepayer in New Moon and had great influence with the School Board.
“I shall excuse your conduct if you behave yourself in future, Emily,” she said coldly. “*I* feel that I have only done my duty in putting the matter before your aunt. No, thank you, Miss Murray, I cannot stay to supper﻿—I want to get home before it is too dark.”
“God speed all travellers,” said Perry cheerfully, climbing down his ladder﻿—this time with his clothes on.
Aunt Elizabeth ignored him﻿—she was not going to have a scene with a hired boy before Miss Brownell. The latter switched herself out and Aunt Elizabeth looked at Emily.
“You will eat your supper alone tonight, Emily, in the pantry﻿—you will have bread and milk only. And you will not speak one word to anyone until tomorrow morning.”
“But you won’t forbid me to think?” said Emily anxiously.
Aunt Elizabeth made no reply but sat haughtily down at the supper table. Emily went into the pantry and ate her bread and milk, with the odour of the delicious sausages the others were eating for savour. Emily liked sausages, and New Moon sausages were the last word in sausages.

===Chinese===

那个罚她受不住。同她的世界隔绝——她知道，这要不了多久就会叫她屈膝。不如立刻就屈——可是，哦，这份苦涩，这份羞辱！
“人除了上帝，不该跪任何人，”吉米表哥忽然说，眼睛仍旧望着天花板。
伊丽莎白·默里那张高傲、愠怒的脸上，骤然起了一种奇异的变化。她一动不动地站着，望着吉米表哥——站了那么久，布朗内尔小姐都不耐烦地动了动。
“艾米丽，”伊丽莎白姨妈换了一种口气说，“我错了——我不该叫你跪。可你必须向老师赔不是——往后我还要罚你。”
艾米丽把双手背到身后，又直直望进布朗内尔小姐的眼睛。
“今天我做的什么事错了，我很抱歉，”她说，“我为此请您原谅。”
布朗内尔小姐站起身。她觉得一场名正言顺的胜利被人骗走了。不管艾米丽受什么罚，她都没眼福亲见了。她真恨不得把“傻吉米·默里”好好摇一顿。可把心里的话全摆到脸上，到底不妥。伊丽莎白·默里虽然不是校董，却是新月庄园缴税最重的人家，在学校董事会里说得上话。
“往后你规矩些，今天的事我就不计较了，艾米丽，”她冷冷地说。“我不过是尽了本分，把事情摆到你姨妈面前。不用了，谢谢，默里小姐，晚饭我不留了——我得赶在天黑透之前到家。”
“上帝保佑赶路的，”佩里快快活活地说，从梯子上爬下来——这回衣裳穿在身上了。
伊丽莎白姨妈只当没听见——她不肯当着布朗内尔小姐同一个雇来的小子闹一场。布朗内尔小姐扭身出门，伊丽莎白姨妈看着艾米丽。
“今晚你一个人在配膳室吃晚饭，艾米丽——只有面包和牛奶。到明天早上以前，不同任何人说一句话。”
“可您总不至于不许我想吧？”艾米丽急切地问。
伊丽莎白姨妈不答，高傲地在饭桌旁坐下。艾米丽进了配膳室吃她的面包牛奶，别人吃的喷香的腊肠，香味直往她鼻子里钻。艾米丽爱吃腊肠，新月庄园的腊肠更是腊肠里的登峰造极。

===Original===

Elizabeth Burnley had brought the recipe out from the Old Country and its secret was carefully guarded. And Emily was hungry. But she had escaped the unbearable, and things might be worse. It suddenly occurred to her that she would write an epic poem in imitation of “The Lay of the Last Minstrel.” Cousin Jimmy had read “The Lay” to her last Saturday. She would begin the first canto right off. When Laura Murray came into the pantry, Emily, her bread and milk only half eaten, was leaning her elbows on the dresser, gazing into space, with faintly moving lips and the light that never was on land or sea in her young eyes. Even the aroma of sausages was forgotten﻿—was she not drinking from a fount of Castaly?
“Emily,” said Aunt Laura, shutting the door, and looking very lovingly upon Emily out of her kind blue eyes, “you can talk to *me* all you want to. I don’t like Miss Brownell and I don’t think you were altogether in the wrong﻿—although of course you shouldn’t be writing poetry when you have sums to do. And there are some ginger cookies in that box.”
“I don’t want to talk to anyone, dear Aunt Laura﻿—I’m too happy,” said Emily dreamily. “I’m composing an epic﻿—it is to be called ‘The White Lady,’ and I’ve got twenty lines of it made already﻿—and two of them are thrilling. The heroine wants to go into a convent and her father warns her that if she does she will never be able to

> Come back to the life you gave

With all its pleasures to the grave.

Oh, Aunt Laura, when I composed those lines the flash came to me. And ginger cookies are nothing to me any more.”
Aunt Laura smiled again.
“Not just now perhaps, dear. But when the moment of inspiration has passed it will do no harm to remember that the cookies in the box have not been counted and that they are as much mine as Elizabeth’s.”

===Chinese===

这方子是伊丽莎白·伯恩利从故国带来的，秘方严加看守。艾米丽也饿了。可她逃过了那受不了的一跪，事情本来还可能更糟。她忽然想起来：要模仿《最后一个吟游诗人的歌》，写一部史诗。上个礼拜六吉米表哥刚给她念过这首《歌》。第一歌这就可以动笔。劳拉·默里走进配膳室时，艾米丽面包牛奶才吃了一半，正胳膊肘支在碗柜上，眼睛望着虚空出神，嘴唇微微翕动，年轻的眼睛里亮着人间地上都没有的光。连腊肠的香味都忘了——她这不是正饮着卡斯塔利亚灵泉么？
“艾米丽，”劳拉姨妈掩上门，那双和善的蓝眼睛满怀怜爱地望着艾米丽，“你想同我说多少话都行。我不喜欢布朗内尔小姐，我也不认为你全错——虽说有算术题的时候你自然不该写诗。还有，那盒子里有姜汁饼干。”
“我谁也不想同谁说话，亲爱的劳拉姨妈——我太快活了，”艾米丽梦悠悠地说。“我在作一部史诗——就叫《白衣夫人》，我已经作出二十行了——其中两行是惊心动魄的。女主角要进修道院，她父亲警告她：要是进了，就再也不能

> 回到你赋予的生命，

带着它全部的欢乐，直到坟墓。

哦，劳拉姨妈，我作出这两行的时候，灵光（the flash）降临了。姜汁饼干对我来说已经不算什么了。”
劳拉姨妈又笑了。
“眼下也许不算什么，亲爱的。可等灵感的劲头过去了，不妨记着一点：盒子里的饼干没人数过，伊丽莎白的有多少，我的也有多少。”

## XVII Living Epistles / 十七 活的书信
===Original===

> “O, I have such an exiting thing to tell you. I have been the heroin of an adventure. One day last week Ilse asked me if I would go and stay all night with her because her father was away and wouldn’t be home till very late and Ilse said she wasn’t fritened but very lonesome. So I asked Aunt Elizabeth if I could. I hardly dared hope, dear Father, that she would let me, for she doesn’t aprove of little girls being away from home at night but to my surprise she said I could go very kindly. And then I heard her say in the pantry to Aunt Laura It is a shame the way the doctor leaves that poor child so much alone at nights. It is *wikked* of him. And Aunt Laura said The poor man is warped. You know he was not a bit like that before his wife﻿—and then just as it was getting intresting Aunt Elizabeth gave Aunt Laura a nudge and said s-s-s-h, little pitchers have big ears. I knew she meant me though my ears are not big, only pointed. I do wish I could find out what Ilse’s mother did. It worrys me after I go to bed. I lie awake for ever so long thinking about it. Ilse has no idea. Once she asked her father and he told her (in a *voice of thunder*) never to mention *that woman* to him again. And there is something else that worrys me too. I keep thinking of Silas Lee who killed his brother at the old well. How dreadful the poor man must have felt. And what is it to be warped.
> “I went over to Ilses and we played in the garret. I like playing there because we dont have to be careful and tidy like we do in our garret. Ilses garret is very untidy and cant have been dusted for years. The rag room is worse than the rest. It is boarded off at one end of the garret and it is full of old close and bags of rags and broken furniture. I dont like the smell of it. The kitchen chimney goes up through it and things hang round it (or did). For all this is in the past now, dear Father.
> “When we got tired playing we sat down on an old chest and talked. This is splendid in daytime I said but it must be awful queer at night. Mice, said Ilse﻿—and spiders and gosts. I dont believe in gosts I said skornfully. There isnt any such thing. (But maybe there is for all that, dear Father.) I believe this garret is hawnted, said Ilse. They say garrets always are. Nonsense I said. You know dear Father it would not do for a New Moon person to believe in gosts. But I felt very queer.

===Chinese===

> “哦，我有件顶刺激的事要告诉你。我当了一回历险的女主人公。上礼拜有一天，伊尔丝问我要不要去她家过夜，因为她爸爸出诊去了，要很晚才回来，伊尔丝说她不怕，就是太冷清。我就去问伊丽莎白姨妈行不行。亲爱的爸爸，我几乎不敢指望她答应，因为她不赞成小姑娘夜里离家在外，可她竟很和气地说我能去。后来我听见她在配膳室对劳拉姨妈说：医生老让那可怜的孩子夜里一个人在家，真不像话。他这是*作孽*。劳拉姨妈说：可怜的人，他是扭曲了。要知道，在他妻子之前他一点儿也不是这样的——正到有趣的地方，伊丽莎白姨妈捅了劳拉姨妈一下，说嘘——小水壶长着大耳朵。我知道她指的是我，虽然我的耳朵不大，只是尖。我真想知道伊尔丝的娘到底做了什么。上床之后这事就折磨我。我醒着躺好久好久想它。伊尔丝一点也不知道。有一回她问她父亲，他（用*打雷一样的嗓音*）叫她永远不许再向他提*那个女人*。还有一件事也折磨我。我总想着赛拉斯·李，在老井边杀死他兄弟的那个人。那可怜人当时心里得多可怕呀。还有，“扭曲了”是什么意思。
> “我去了伊尔丝家，我们在阁楼上玩。我喜欢在那儿玩，因为不用像在我们家阁楼那样小心整洁。伊尔丝家的阁楼乱极了，准有好多年没掉过灰了。破布间比哪儿都糟。它在阁楼一头，用板子隔出来，堆满旧衣裳、一袋袋破布和破烂家具。我不喜欢那股味儿。厨房的烟囱从它中间穿上去，烟囱四围挂着东西（或者说从前挂着。这一切如今都过去了，亲爱的爸爸）。
> “玩累了，我们坐在一口旧箱子上说话。白天这儿棒极了，我说，可到了夜里一定怪得很。老鼠，伊尔丝说——还有蜘蛛和鬼。我不信鬼，我鄙夷地说。根本没有那种东西。（可说不定还是有的，亲爱的爸爸。）我看这阁楼就闹鬼，伊尔丝说。人家说阁楼都闹鬼。胡说，我说。你知道的，亲爱的爸爸，新月庄园（New Moon）的人不能信鬼。可我觉得很古怪。

===Original===

Its easy to talk said Ilse beginning to be mad (though I wasnt trying to run down her garret) but you wouldnt stay here alone at night. I wouldnt mind it a bit I said. Then I dare you to do it said Ilse. I dare you to come up here at bedtime and sleep here all night. Then I saw I was in an awful skrape Father dear. It is a foolish thing to bost. I knew not what to do. It was dreadful to think of sleeping alone in that garret but if I didnt Ilse would always cast it up to me whenever we fought and worse than that she would tell Teddy and he would think me a coward. So I said proudly Ill do it Ilse Burnley and Im not afraid to either. (But oh I was﻿—inside.) The mice will run over you said Ilse. O I wouldnt be you for the world. It was mean of Ilse to make things worse than they were. But I could feel she admired me too and that helped me a great deal. We dragged an old feather bed out of the rag room and Ilse gave me a pillow and half her close. It was dark by this time and Ilse wouldnt go up into the garret again. So I said my prayers very carefully and then I took a lamp and started up. I am so used to candles now that the lamp made me nervus. Ilse said I looked scared to death. My knees shook dear Father but for the honnor of the Starrs (and the Murrays too) I went on. I had undressed in Ilses room, so I got right into bed and blew out the lamp. But I couldnt go to sleep for a long time. The moonlight made the garret look weerd. I dont know exactly what weerd means but I feel the garret was it. The bags and old close hanging from the beams looked like creatures. I thought I need not be fritened. The angels are here. But then I felt as if I would be as much fritened of angels as of anything else. And I could hear rats and mice scrambling over things. I thought What if a rat was to run over me, and then I thought that next day I would write out a descripshon of the garret by moonlight and my feelings. At last I heard the doctor driving in and then I heard him knocking round in the kitchen and I felt better and before very long I went to sleep and I dreamed a dreadful dream. I dreamed the door of the rag room opened and a big newspaper came out and chased me all around the garret. And then it went on fire and I could smell the smoke plain as plain and it was just on me when I skreamed and woke up.

===Chinese===

嘴上说说容易，伊尔丝开始来气了（我并没有贬她家阁楼的意思），可你才不敢夜里一个人待在这儿呢。我才不在乎呢，我说。那我就激你去做，伊尔丝说。我激你睡觉时候上来，在这儿睡一整夜。这下我明白自己进退两难了，亲爱的爸爸。吹牛是件蠢事。我不知道怎么办。一个人睡在那阁楼里，想起来多可怕，可我要是不做，往后我们每回吵架伊尔丝都要翻出来数落我，更糟的是她还会告诉泰迪，他就会当我是个胆小鬼。于是我骄傲地说：我做得到，伊尔丝·伯恩利，而且我也不怕。（可哦，我怕——心里怕。）老鼠会从你身上跑过去，伊尔丝说。哦，给我全世界我也不换做你。伊尔丝这样把事情说得比实际更糟，真恶劣。可我感觉得出她也佩服我，这给了我很大的支撑。我们从破布间拖出一床旧鸭绒被，伊尔丝给了我一个枕头和她的半数衣裳。这时天黑了，伊尔丝再也不肯上阁楼去了。于是我仔仔细细做了祷告，端起一盏油灯往上走。我如今点惯了蜡烛，那盏灯叫我心里发毛。伊尔丝说我看上去吓得半死。我的膝盖直打颤，亲爱的爸爸，可为了斯塔尔家的荣誉（也为了默里家），我还是上去了。我已在伊尔丝房里换好了衣裳，便径直钻进被窝，吹灭了灯。可好久好久睡不着。月光把阁楼照得怪模怪样。我说不清怪模怪样到底是什么意思，可我觉着这阁楼就是它。挂在梁上的一袋袋东西和旧衣裳，看着像一个个活物。我想，我用不着怕。天使在这儿呢。可接着我又觉得，天使同别的任何东西一样叫我怕。我还听得见老鼠在东西上面爬来爬去。我想，万一一只老鼠从我身上跑过去怎么办；接着我想，明天我要写一篇月光下的阁楼和我当时心情的描写。后来我终于听见医生赶车回来了，又听见他在厨房里磕磕碰碰，我心里踏实了些，没多一会儿就睡着了，还做了一个可怕的梦。我梦见破布间的门开了，一张大报纸钻出来，绕着阁楼一路追我。后来它着了火，那烟味我闻得真真的，它眼看就要扑到我身上，我尖叫一声醒了。

===Original===

I was sitting right up in bed and the newspaper was gone but I could smell smoke still. I looked at the rag room door and smoke was coming out under it and I saw firelight through the cracks of the boards. I just yelled at the top of my voice and tore down to Ilses room and she rushed across the hall and woke her father. He said dam but he got right up and then all three of us kept running up and down the garret stairs with pails of water and we made an awful mess but we got the fire out. It was just the bags of wool that had been hanging close to the chimney that had caught fire. When all was over the doctor wiped the persperation from his manly brow and said That was a close call. A few minutes later would have been too late. I put on a fire when I came in to make a cup of tea and I suppose those bags must have caught fire from a spark. I see theres a hole here where the plaster has tumbled out. I must have this whole place cleaned out. How in the world did you come to diskover the fire, Emily. I was sleeping in the garret I said. Sleeping in the garret said the doctor, what in﻿—what the﻿—*what* were you doing there. Ilse dared me I said. She said Id be too scared to stay there and I said I wouldnt. I fell asleep and woke up and smelled smoke. You little devil, said the doctor. I suppose it was a dreadful thing to be called a devil but the doctor looked at me so admiringly that I felt as if he was paying me a compelment. He has queer ways of talking. Ilse says the only time he ever said a kind thing to her was once when she had a sore throat he called her ‘a poor little animal’ and looked as if he was sorry for her. I feel sure Ilse feels dreadfully bad because her father doesnt like her though she pretends she does not care. But oh dear Father there is more to tell. Yesterday the Shrewsbury *Weekly Times* came and in the Blair Notes it told all about the fire at the doctors and said it had been fortunately diskovered in time by Miss Emily Starr. I cant tell you what I felt like when I saw my name in the paper. I felt *famus*. And I never was called Miss in ernest before.
> “Last Saturday Aunt Elizabeth and Aunt Laura went to Shrewsbury for the day and left Cousin Jimmy and me to keep house. We had such fun and Cousin Jimmy let me skim all the milk pans. But after dinner unexpekted company came and there was no cake in the house.

===Chinese===

我在床上直直坐起来，报纸没了，可我还闻得见烟味。我朝破布间的门一看，烟正从门底下冒出来，板缝里还透出火光。我扯开嗓子就是一嗓子喊，一头冲下楼到伊尔丝房里，她冲过门厅叫醒了她父亲。他说了句该死，可立刻就起来了，随后我们三个提着一桶桶水在阁楼楼梯上跑上跑下，泼得一塌糊涂，到底把火浇灭了。着火的不过是挂在烟囱近旁的那几袋羊毛。一切平息之后，医生从他男子汉的额头上抹了一把汗说：好险。再晚几分钟就迟了。我回来生了火想烧杯茶，那些袋子准是让火星溅着了。这儿墙灰掉了，有个窟窿。这地方得整个清一清。你到底是怎么发视火情的，艾米丽。我睡在阁楼上，我说。睡在阁楼上？医生说，你——你到底——*你*在那儿干什么。伊尔丝激我，我说。她说我不敢待，我说我敢。我睡着了，醒过来就闻见烟味。你这个小魔鬼，医生说。被叫作魔鬼想必是件可怕的事，可医生那样带着激赏望着我，我觉得他这是在恭维我。他说话的路数古怪。伊尔丝说，他只对她说过一句好话，就是有一回她嗓子疼，他叫她“可怜的小牲口”，那样子还挺心疼她。我敢肯定，伊尔丝因为父亲不喜欢她，心里苦得很，尽管她装作不在乎。可是哦，亲爱的爸爸，还有下文呢。昨天什鲁斯伯里（Shrewsbury）的《每周时报》（*Weekly Times*）来了，布莱尔简讯里把医生家的火灾讲了一遍，说幸得艾米丽·斯塔尔小姐及时发视。看见自己的名字登在报上，我说不出是什么滋味。我觉得自己*出名*了。而且我从来没有人正经叫过“小姐”呢。
> “上礼拜六，伊丽莎白姨妈和劳拉姨妈去什鲁斯伯里过了一天，家里留给吉米表哥和我照看。我们玩得痛快极了，吉米表哥还让我撇了所有的奶盆。可午饭后来了个料想不到的客，家里偏偏没有蛋糕。

===Original===

That was a dreadful thing. It never happened before in the annels of New Moon. Aunt Elizabeth had toothache all day yesterday and Aunt Laura was away at Priest Pond visiting Great Aunt Nancy, so no cake was made. I prayed about it and then I went to work and made a cake by Aunt Laura’s receet and it turned out all right. Cousin Jimmy helped me set the table and get supper, and I poured the tea and never slopped any over in the saucers. You would have been proud of me Father. Mrs. Lewis took a second piece of cake and said I would know Elizabeth Murrays cake if I found it in central Africa. I said not a word for the honnor of the family. But I felt very proud. I had saved the Murrays from disgrace. When Aunt Elizabeth came home and heard the tale she looked grim and tasted a piece that was left and then she said Well, you have got *some* Murray in you anyway. That is the first time Aunt Elizabeth has ever praised me. She had three teeth out so they will not ache any more. I am glad for her sake. Before I went to bed I got the cook book and picked out all the things Id like to make. Queen Pudding, Sea-foam Sauce, Black-eyed Susans, Pigs In Blankets. They sound just lovely.
> “I can see such beautiful fluffy white clouds over Lofty Johns bush. I wish I could sore up and drop right into them. I cant believe they would be wet and messy like Teddy says. Teddy cut my initials and his together on the Monark of The Forest but somebody has cut them out. I don’t know whether it was Perry or Ilse.
> “Miss Brownell hardly ever gives me good deportment marks now and Aunt Elizabeth is much displeased on Friday nights but Aunt Laura understands. I wrote an account of the afternoon when Miss Brownell made fun of my poems and put it in an old envelope and wrote Aunt Elizabeths name on it and put it among my papers. If I die of consumption Aunt Elizabeth will find it and know the rites of it and mourn that she was so unjust to me. But I don’t think I will die because Im getting much fatter and Ilse told me she heard her father tell Aunt Laura I would be handsome if I had more color. Is it wrong to want to be handsome, dearest Father. Aunt Elizabeth says it is and when I said to her Wouldn’t *you* like to be handsome, Aunt Elizabeth, she seemed anoyed about something.

===Chinese===

那是件可怕的事。新月庄园的编年史上从没出过这种事。伊丽莎白姨妈昨天牙疼了一整天，劳拉姨妈又去普里斯特池塘（Priest Pond）看南希姨婆（Great-Aunt Nancy）了，所以没做蛋糕。我为这事做了祷告，然后动手照劳拉姨妈的方子做了一个蛋糕，做得很好。吉米表哥帮我摆桌子、备晚饭，我斟的茶一滴也没洒到茶托外。你准会为我骄傲的，爸爸。刘易斯太太（Mrs. Lewis）吃了第二块蛋糕，还说：这蛋糕哪怕在非洲中部撞见，我也认得出是伊丽莎白·默里的。我一个字也没说，为了家族的荣誉。可我心里骄傲得很。我把默里家从丢脸里救出来了。伊丽莎白姨妈回家听了这事，绷着脸尝了一块剩下的，然后说：哼，你身上到底还有*几分*默里家的东西。这是伊丽莎白姨妈头一回夸我。她拔了三颗牙，往后不再疼了。我为她高兴。上床前我找出食谱，把想做的都挑了出来：女王布丁、海泡沙司、黑眼苏珊、毯子里的猪。名字听着就可爱。
> “我望得见高个儿约翰林子上空那些蓬蓬的白云，美极了。真想飞上去一头扎进它们里头。我不信它们会像泰迪说的那样又湿又黏糊。泰迪把我的名字缩写同他的一起刻在森林之王上了，可有人把它们挖掉了。不知是佩里还是伊尔丝。
> “布朗内尔小姐如今几乎不给我品行好分了，礼拜五晚上伊丽莎白姨妈很不满意，可劳拉姨妈懂。我把布朗内尔小姐取笑我的诗的那个下午写了一篇，装进旧信封，信封上写了伊丽莎白姨妈的名字，夹在我的文件里。我若是死于肺痨，伊丽莎白姨妈会找到它，明白个中情由，为自己那样不公道而懊悔。不过我看我死不了，因为我胖多了；伊尔丝还告诉我，她听见她爸爸对劳拉姨妈说，我要是气色好些会很好看。想好看难道是错的吗，最亲爱的爸爸。伊丽莎白姨妈说是错的，我对她说：*您*难道不想好看吗，伊丽莎白姨妈？她好像为什么事不痛快起来。

===Original===

> “Miss Brownell has had a spite at Perry ever since that evening and treats him very mean but he is meek and says he wont kick up any fuss in school because he wants to learn and get ahead. He keeps saying his rymes are as good as mine and I know they are not and it exassperates me. If I do not pay attention all the time in school Miss Brownell says I suppose you are composing﻿—poetry Emily and then everybody laughs. No not everybody. I must not exagerate. Teddy and Perry and Ilse and Jennie never laugh. It is funny that I like Jennie so well now and I hated her so that first day in school. Her eyes are not piggy after all. They are small but they are jolly and twinkly. She is quite poplar in school. I do hate Frank Barker. He took my new reader and wrote in a big sprawly way all over the front page
> “That is not a refined poem and besides it is not the rite way to speak about God. I tore out the leaf and burned it and Aunt Elizabeth was angry and even when I explained why her rath was not apeased. Ilse says she is going to call God Alla after this. I think it is a nicer name myself. It is so soft and doesn’t sound so stern. But I fear its not relijus enough.

===Chinese===

> “打那天晚上起，布朗内尔小姐就一直记恨佩里，待他刻薄得很，可他忍着，说在学校他不会闹事，因为他要学习、要上进。他老说他的韵句同我的一样好，我知道不是，气得我不行。我在学校但凡有一会儿不专心，布朗内尔小姐就说：我猜你在作——诗呢，艾米丽，于是人人都笑。不对，不是人人。我不能夸大。泰迪、佩里、伊尔丝和珍妮从来不笑。真有意思，我如今这么喜欢珍妮，上学头一天却那么恨她。她的眼睛到底不算猪眼。是小，可是快活、亮闪闪。她在学校人缘挺好。我恨透了弗兰克·巴克（Frank Barker）。他拿走我的新课本，在扉页上用歪歪扭扭的大字写满了
> “那不是一首高雅的诗，再说那样说上帝也不对。我把那一页撕下来烧了，伊丽莎白姨妈生了气，我解释了缘由，她的怒气也没消。伊尔丝说，往后她要管上帝叫阿拉（Alla）。我自己也觉得这个名字好听些。那么软，不那么凶。可我怕它不够虔诚。

===Original===

> “Yesterday was my birthday dear Father. It will soon be a year since I came to New Moon. I feel as if I had always lived here. I have grown two inches. Cousin Jimmy measured me by a mark on the dairy door. My birthday was very nice. Aunt Laura made a lovely cake and gave me a beautiful new white pettycoat with an embroidered flounce. She had run a blue ribbon through it but Aunt Elizabeth made her pull it out. And Aunt Laura also gave me that piece of pink satin brokade in her burow drawer. I have longed for it ever since I saw it but never hoped to possess it. Ilse asked me what I meant to do with it but I dont mean to do anything with it. Only keep it up here in the garret with my treasures and look at it, because it is beautiful. Aunt Elizabeth gave me a dixonary. That was a useful present. I feel I ought to like it. You will soon notice an improovement in my spelling, I hope. The only trouble is when I am writing something interesting I get so exited it is just awful to have to stop and hunt up a word to see how it is spelled. I looked up ween in it and Miss Brownell was right. I did not know what it really meant. It rymed so well with sheen and I thought it meant to behold or see but it means to think. Cousin Jimmy gave me a big thick blank book. I am so proud of it. It will be so nice to write pieces in. But I will still use the letter-bills to write to you, dear Father, because I can fold each one up by itself and adress it like a real letter. Teddy gave me a picture of myself. He painted it in water colors and called it The Smiling Girl. I look as if I was listening to something that made me very happy. Ilse says it flatters me. It does make me better looking than I am but not any better looking than I would be if I could have a bang. Teddy says he is going to paint a real big picture of me when he grows up. Perry walked all the way to Shrewsbury to get me a necklace of pearl beads and lost it. He had no more money so he went home to Stovepipe Town and got a young hen from his Aunt Tom and gave me that. He is a very persistent boy. I am to have all the eggs the hen lays to sell the peddler for myself. Ilse gave me a box of candy. I am only going to eat one piece a day to make it last a long time.

===Chinese===

> “昨天是我的生日，亲爱的爸爸。我来新月庄园快一年了。我觉得自己仿佛一直住在这儿。我长高了两英寸。吉米表哥照着奶房门上的记号给我量的。我的生日过得真好。劳拉姨妈做了个可爱的蛋糕，还送我一条漂亮的新白衬裙，缀着绣花荷叶边。她本来在花边里穿了一根蓝缎带，可伊丽莎白姨妈叫她抽掉了。劳拉姨妈还把她柜子抽屉里那块粉缎锦缎给了我。打第一回看见我就想要它，从没敢指望拥有它。伊尔丝问我打算拿它做什么，可我不打算拿它做什么。只把它放在阁楼上同我的宝贝们一起，看着它，因为它美。伊丽莎白姨妈送我一本词典。那是件有用的礼物。我觉得我该喜欢它。我想你不久就会注意到我拼写的进步。唯一的麻烦是，写着有趣的东西正起劲，中途还得停下来查一个词怎么写，太难受了。我查了ween，布朗内尔小姐是对的。我原先不知道它真正的意思。它同sheen押韵押得那么好，我以为它是看见、仰望的意思，原来是想、以为的意思。吉米表哥送我一本厚厚的大空白簿子。我为它骄傲极了。往里面写作品多好呀。可给你写信我还是要用邮单，亲爱的爸爸，因为每一张可以单独折起来，像真正的信一样写上地址。泰迪送了我一张我的画像。他用水彩画的，题作《微笑的女孩》。我看上去像在听什么让我非常快活的东西。伊尔丝说它美化了我。它确实让我比本人好看，可再好看也好看不过我要是能留刘海的样子。泰迪说他长大了要给我画一张真正的大画。佩里一路走到什鲁斯伯里，要给我买一串珍珠珠子项链，却把它丢了。他一个钱也没有了，只好回炉筒镇（Stovepipe Town）的家里，从汤姆婶那儿抓了只小母鸡送我。他是个顶顶不死心的男孩。母鸡下的蛋全归我，卖给我货郎，钱归我自己。伊尔丝送了我一盒糖果。我一天只吃一块，好叫它吃得久些。

===Original===

I wanted Ilse to eat some but she said she wouldnt because it would be mean to help eat a present you had given and I insisted and then we fought over it and Ilse said I was a caterwawling quadruped (which was ridiklus) and didn’t know enough to come in when it rained. And I said I knew enough to have some manners at least. Ilse got so mad she went home but she cooled off soon and came back for supper.
> “It is raining tonight and it sounds like fairies feet dancing over the garret roof. If it had not rained Teddy was going to come down and help me look for the Lost Dimond. Wouldnt it be splendid if we could find it.
> “Cousin Jimmy is fixing up the garden. He lets me help him and I have a little flower bed of my own. I always run out first thing every morning to see how much the things have grown since yesterday. Spring is such a happyfying time isnt it, Father. The little Blue People are all out round the summer house. That is what Cousin Jimmy calls the violets and I think it is lovely. He has names like that for all the flowers. The roses are the Queens and the June lilies are the Snow Ladies and the tulips are the Gay Folk and the daffodils are the Golden Ones and the China Asters are My Pink Friends.
> “Mike II is here with me, sitting on the windowsill. Mike is a smee cat. Smee is not in the dictionary. It is a word I invented myself. I could not think of any English word which just describes Mike II so I made this up. It means sleek and glossy and soft and fluffy all in one and something else besides that I cant express.
> “Aunt Laura is teaching me to sew. She says I must learn to make a hem on muslin that can’t be seen (tradishun). I hope she will teach me how to make point lace some day. All the Murrays of New Moon have been noted for making point lace (I mean all the women Murrays). None of the girls in school can make point lace. Aunt Laura says she will make me a point lace hangkerchief when I get married. All the New Moon brides had point lace hangkerchiefs except my mother who ran away. But you didnt mind her not having one did you Father. Aunt Laura talks a good bit about my mother to me but not when Aunt Elizabeth is around. Aunt Elizabeth never mentions her name. Aunt Laura wants to show me Mothers room but she has never been able to find the key yet because Aunt Elizabeth keeps it hid.

===Chinese===

我想让伊尔丝也吃几块，可她说不吃，因为帮忙吃掉人家送你的礼物太刻薄了，我非叫她吃，为这个我们吵了一架，伊尔丝说我是一头嚎春的四脚兽（真是荒唐），下雨都不知道进屋。我说我至少还知道点儿规矩。伊尔丝气得回家了，可很快就消了气，又回来吃了晚饭。
> “今晚下雨了，雨声像仙子的脚步在阁楼顶上跳舞。要不是下雨，泰迪本要来帮我找失落的钻石的。我们要是能找着它，多好啊。
> “吉米表哥在拾掇园子。他让我帮忙，我还有了自己的一小块花圃。每天早晨我第一件事就是跑出去，看东西们比昨天长高了多少。春天是这么叫人欢欣的时节，是不是，爸爸。小小的蓝衣人全在凉亭四周出来了。那是吉米表哥对紫罗兰的叫法，我觉得可爱极了。他给所有的花都起了这样的名字。玫瑰是王后们，六月百合是雪夫人，郁金香是快活人，黄水仙是金人儿，翠菊是我的粉红朋友。
> “迈克二世（Mike II）这会儿在我身边，蹲在窗台上。迈克是一只斯迷猫。斯迷不在词典里。它是我自己造的词。我想不出哪个英文词恰好形容迈克二世，就造了这一个。它的意思是又光又滑、又软又蓬，四样合一，另外还有一样什么，我表达不出来。
> “劳拉姨妈在教我做针线。她说我必须学会在细布上缝出看不见的卷边（传通）。我希望她哪天教我针绣花边。新月庄园的默里家人都以会做针绣花边出名（我是说默里家的女人们）。学校里哪个女孩都不会做针绣花边。劳拉姨妈说我结婚时她要给我做一块针绣花边手绢。新月庄园的新娘个个都有针绣花边手绢，只有私奔的我母亲没有。可你并不在乎她没有，对吧，爸爸。劳拉姨妈常同我讲我母亲，只是不当着伊丽莎白姨妈。伊丽莎白姨妈从不提她的名字。劳拉姨妈想带我看看母亲的房间，可钥匙一直找不着，因为伊丽莎白姨妈把它藏着。

===Original===

Aunt Laura says Aunt Elizabeth loved my mother very much. You would think she would love her daughter some wouldnt you. But she doesnt. She is just bringing me up as a duty.

> “This has been a very important day. I wrote my first letter, I mean the first letter that was really to go in the mail. It was to Great-Aunt Nancy who lives at Priest Pond and is very old. She wrote Aunt Elizabeth and said I might write now and then to a poor old woman. My heart was touched and I wanted to. Aunt Elizabeth said We might as well let her. And she said to me You must be careful to write a nice letter and I will read it over when it is written. If you make a good impression on Aunt Nancy she may do something for you. I wrote the letter very carefully but it didn’t sound a bit like me when it was finished. I couldn’t write a good letter when I knew Aunt Elizabeth was going to read it. I felt paralized.

===Chinese===

劳拉姨妈说，伊丽莎白姨妈从前非常爱我母亲。你准以为她会多少爱她的女儿一点吧。可她不爱。她只是在尽本分把我养大。

> “这是顶顶要紧的一天。我写了我的第一封信，我是说第一封真正要付邮的信。是写给普里斯特池塘的南希姨婆的，她年纪很大了。她写信给伊丽莎白姨妈，说我可以时不时给一个可怜的老太婆写写信。我的心被打动了，愿意写。伊丽莎白姨妈说：就依她吧。又对我说：你得用心写一封像样的信，写好了我要过目。你要是给南希姨婆留个好印象，她兴许会为你做点什么。我仔仔细细写了那封信，可写完一看，一点儿也不像我。知道伊丽莎白姨妈要读，我就写不出好信来。我觉得自己滩焕了。

===Original===

> “Dear Father, my letter did not make a good impression on Great-Aunt Nancy. She did not answer it but she wrote Aunt Elizabeth that I must be a very stupid child to write such a stupid letter. I feel insulted because I am not stupid. Perry says he feels like going to Priest Pond and knocking the daylights out of Great-Aunt Nancy. I told him he must not talk like that about my family, and anyhow I dont see how knocking the daylights out of Great-Aunt Nancy would make her change her opinion about me being stupid. (I wonder what daylights are and how you knock them out of people.)
> “I have three cantos of ‘The White Lady’ finished. I have the heroin imured in a convent and I dont know how to get her out because I am not a Catholic. I suppose it would have been better if I had a Protestant heroin but there were no Protestants in the days of shivalry. I might have asked Lofty John last year but this year I cant because Ive never spoken to him since he played that horrid joke on me about the apple. When I meet him on the road I look straight ahead just as lofty as he does. I have called my pig after him to get square. Cousin Jimmy has given me a little pig for my own. When it is sold I am to have the money. I mean to give some for missionaries and put the rest in the bank to go to my educashun. And I thought if I ever had a pig I would call it Uncle Wallace. But now it does not seem to me propper to call pigs after your uncles even if you dont like them.
> “Teddy and Perry and Ilse and I play we are living in the days of shivalry and Ilse and I are distressed damsels reskued by galant knites. Teddy made a splendid suit of armor out of old barrel staves and then Perry made a better one out of old tin boilers hammered flat with a broken saucepan for a helmit. Sometimes we play at the Tansy Patch. I have a queer feeling that Teddys mother hates me this summer. Last summer she just didnt like me. Smoke and Buttercup are not there now. They disappeared misteriously in the winter. Teddy says he feels sure his mother poisoned them because she thought he was getting too fond of them. Teddy is teaching me to whistle but Aunt Laura says it is unladylike. So many jolly things seem to be unladylike. Sometimes I almost wish my aunts were infidels like Dr. Burnly. *He* never bothers whether Ilse is unladylike or not. But no, it would not be good manners to be an infidel.

===Chinese===

> “亲爱的爸爸，我的信没给南希姨婆留下好印象。她没回信，倒是写信告诉伊丽莎白姨妈，说我准是个顶愚蠢的孩子，才写得出这么愚蠢的信。我觉得受了侮辱，因为我并不愚蠢。佩里说他恨不得去普里斯特池塘，把南希姨婆的“天光”都揍出来。我告诉他不许这样说我家里人，再说我也不明白把南希姨婆的天光揍出来，怎么能让她改变我愚蠢的看法。（不知道天光是什么，又怎么从人身上揍出来。）
> “我的《白衣女子》（'The White Lady'）写完三章了。女主人公被我幽禁在修道院里，我不知道怎么救她出来，因为我不是天主教徒。当初要是有个新教女主人公就好了，可骑士时代没有新教徒。去年我本可以去问高个儿约翰，今年不行了，因为他拿苹果同我开了那个可恶的玩笑之后，我再没同他说过话。路上遇见他，我目不斜视，同他一样趾高气扬。我把我的猪用他的名字命名，算扯平了。吉米表哥给了我一头小猪，算我自己的。卖了钱归我。我打算捐一些给传教事业，剩下的存进银行，做我的教育费。我原想着要是有一头猪，就叫它华莱士舅舅。可如今我觉得，拿舅舅的名字叫猪终究不合适，哪怕你不喜欢他们。
> “泰迪、佩里、伊尔丝和我玩骑士时代的游戏，我和伊尔丝是落难的少女，让英勇的骑士搭救。泰迪用旧桶板做了一副顶棒的铠甲，佩里随后用敲平的旧白铁锅做了副更好的，头盔用一只破汤锅。有时我们在艾菊园玩。我有个怪怪的感觉：今年夏天，泰迪的母亲恨我了。去年夏天她只是不喜欢我。烟儿和毛茛如今不在了。它们冬天神秘地消失了。泰迪说他敢肯定是他母亲毒死了它们，因为她觉得他太粘着它们了。泰迪在教我吹口哨，可劳拉姨妈说那不淑女。好玩的事好像那么多都不淑女。有时候我几乎巴不得姨妈们像伯恩利医生一样不信神。*他*从来不管伊尔丝淑女不淑女。不，不行，不信神到底不合规矩。

===Original===

It would not be a New Moon tradishun.
> “Today I taught Perry that he must not eat with his knife. He wants to learn all the *rules of etiket*. And I am helping him learn a recitation for school examination day. I wanted Ilse to do it but she was mad because he asked me first and she wouldnt. But she should because she is a far better reciter than I am. I am too nervus.

===Chinese===

那也不是新月庄园的传通。
> “今天我教佩里不许用刀吃东西。他要把*礼节的规矩*统统学会。我还在帮他背一篇朗诵，学校考试日要用的。我本想让伊尔丝来，可他先求了我，她生了气，不肯。可她应该来，因为她朗诵比我强得多。我太容易紧张。

===Original===

> “Dear Father, we have composition in school now and I learned today that you put in things like this “ ” when you write anything anybody has said. I didnt know that before. I must go over all my letters to you and put them in. And after a question you must put a mark like this ? and when a letter is left out a postroffe which is a comma up in the air. Miss Brownell is sarkastic but she *does* teach you things. I am putting that down because I want to be fair even if I do hate her. And she is interesting although she is not nice. I have written a descripshun of her on a letter-bill. I like writing about people I don’t like better than about those I do like. Aunt Laura is nicer to live with than Aunt Elizabeth, but Aunt Elizabeth is nicer to write about. I can deskribe *her* fawlts but I feel wikked and ungrateful if I say anything that is not compelmentary about dear Aunt Laura. Aunt Elizabeth has locked your books away and says I’m not to have them till I’m grown up. Just as if I wouldn’t be careful of them, dear Father. She says I wouldn’t because she found that when I was reading one of them I put a tiny pencil dot under every beautiful word. It didn’t hurt the book a bit, dear Father. Some of the words were dingles, pearled, musk, dappled, intervales, glen, bosky, piping, shimmer, crisp, beechen, ivory. I think those are all lovely words, Father.
> “Aunt Laura lets me read her copy of A Pilgrims Progress on Sundays. I call the big hill in the road to White Cross the Delectable Mountain because it is such a beautiful one.
> “Teddy lent me 3 books of poetry. One of them was Tennyson and I have learned The Bugle Song off by heart so I will always have it. One was Mrs. Browning. She is lovely. I would like to meet her. I suppose I will when I die but that may be a long time away. The other was just one poem called Sohrab and Rustum. After I went to bed I cried over it. Aunt Elizabeth said ‘what are you sniffling about?’ I wasn’t sniffling﻿—I was weeping sore. She made me tell her and then she said ‘You must be crazy.’ But I couldn’t go to sleep until I had thought out a different end for it﻿—a happy one.

===Chinese===

> “亲爱的爸爸，学校现在有作文课了，我今天学到，写谁说过的话，要加上像“”这样的记号。我从前不知道。我得把你所有的信都过一遍，把它们补上。问句后面要加一个像？这样的记号，省掉一个字母要加个撇号，就是逗号飞到半空。布朗内尔小姐爱讥剌人，可她*确实*教你东西。我记下这一笔，因为我要公道，哪怕我恨她。她也有趣，虽然人不和善。我在一张邮单上写了她的描写。写我不喜欢的人，比写我喜欢的人写得来劲。劳拉姨妈比伊丽莎白姨妈好相处，可伊丽莎白姨妈比劳拉姨妈好写。她的毛病我描写得出，可爱的劳拉姨妈身上但凡说一句不恭维的话，我就觉得又缺德又忘恩。伊丽莎白姨妈把你的书锁起来了，说我长大了才许碰。就好像我不会爱惜似的，亲爱的爸爸。她说我不会，因为她发现我读其中一本的时候，在每个漂亮的词底下都用铅笔轻轻点了个小点。那一点儿也没伤着书呀，亲爱的爸爸。有些词是：幽谷响泉、缀珠、麝香、斑驳、山间洼地、幽谷、树影蓊郁、笛音、微光闪动、爽脆、山毛榉、象牙。这些词个个都美，爸爸。
> “礼拜日劳拉姨妈让我读她那本《天路历程》。我把去白十字（White Cross）路上那座大山叫作怡悦山，因为它太美了。
> “泰迪借了我3本诗集。一本是丁尼生，《号角歌》我已背熟了，它就永远归我了。一本是勃朗宁夫人（Mrs. Browning）。她可爱极了。我想见见她。大概我死的时候能见到，可那也许还早得很。另一本只有一首诗，叫《索拉布与鲁斯图姆》（Sohrab and Rustum）。上床后我为它哭了。伊丽莎白姨妈说：‘抽什么鼻子？’我不是抽鼻子——我是恸哭。她逼我说了缘由，然后说：‘你准是疯了。’可直到我为它想出另一个结尾——一个快活的结尾——我才睡得着。

===Original===

> “There has been a dark shadow over this day. I dropped my cent in church. It made a dreadful noise. I felt as if everybody looked at me. Aunt Elizabeth was much annoyed. Perry dropped his too soon after. He told me after church he did it on purpose because he thought it would make me feel better but it didn’t because I was afraid the people would think it was me dropping mine again. Boys do such queer things. I hope the minister did not hear because I am beginning to like him. I never liked him much before last Tuesday. His family are all boys and I suppose he doesn’t understand little girls very well. Then he called at New Moon. Aunt Laura and Aunt Elizabeth were both away and I was in the kitchen alone. Mr. Dare came in and sat down on Saucy Sal who was asleep in the rocking-chair. He was comfortable but Saucy Sal wasn’t. He didn’t sit on her stomach. If he had I suppose he would have killed her. He just sat on her legs and tail. Sal yowled but Mr. Dare is a little deaf and didn’t hear her and I was too shy to tell him. But Cousin Jimmy came in just as he was asking me if I knew my catechism and said ‘Catechism, is it? Lawful heart, man, listen to that poor dum beast. Get up if you’re a Christian.’ So Mr. Dare got up and said, ‘Dear me, this is very remarkable. I thought I felt something moving.’
> “I thought I would write this to you, dear Father, because it struck me as humerus.
> “When Mr. Dare finished asking me questions I thought it was my turn and I would ask him some about some things I’ve wanted to know for years. I asked him if he thought God was very perticular about every little thing I did and if he thought my cats would go to heaven. He said he hoped I never did wrong things and that animals had no souls. And I asked him why we shouldn’t put new wine in old bottles. Aunt Elizabeth does with her dandelion wine and the old bottles do just as well as new ones. He explained quite kindly that the Bible bottles were made of skins and got rotten when they were old. It made it quite clear to me. Then I told him I was worried because I knew I ought to love God better than anything but there were things I loved better than God. He said ‘What things?’ and I said flowers and stars and the Wind Woman and the Three Princesses and things like that. And he smiled and said ‘But they are just a part of God, Emily﻿—every beautiful thing is.’

===Chinese===

> “今天蒙着一道黑影。我在教堂里把分币掉在地上了。响得吓人。我觉得人人都朝我看。伊丽莎白姨妈懊恼极了。不多时佩里也把他的掉在地上。礼拜散了他告诉我，他是故意的，想着能让我好受些，可并没有，因为我怕大家以为又是我掉了。男孩尽干这种怪事。我希望牧师没听见，因为我开始喜欢他了。上礼拜二以前我一直不大喜欢他。他家清一色男孩，我想他不大懂小女孩。那天他来新月庄园拜访。劳拉姨妈和伊丽莎白姨妈都不在家，就我一个人在厨房。戴尔先生进来，一屁股坐在摇椅里睡着的调皮的萨尔（Saucy Sal）身上。他坐着舒服，萨尔可不舒服。他没坐在她肚子上。要是坐了，我想她准没命了。他只坐在她腿和尾巴上。萨尔嚎起来，可戴尔先生有点耳背，没听见，我又太害羞，不敢告诉他。可他正问我懂不懂要理问答，吉米表哥进来了，说：‘要理问答，是吗？老天爷，伙计，听听那可怜的哑巴畜生。你要是个基督徒，就起来吧。’于是戴尔先生站起身，说：‘哎呀呀，真是稀奇。我还当觉得有什么东西在动呢。’
> “我想把这个写给你，亲爱的爸爸，因为我觉得它好笑。
> “戴尔先生问完我，我想该轮到我了，就问他几件我盼望了许多年的事。我问他，上帝是不是顶顶计较我做的每件小事；又问他，我的猫能不能上天堂。他说他希望我永远不做坏事，还说动物没有灵魂。我又问他，为什么新酒不能装在旧瓶里。伊丽莎白姨妈的蒲公英酒就装旧瓶，旧瓶同新瓶一样好使。他挺和气地解释说，圣经里的瓶是皮子做的，旧了会朽。这下我明白了。然后我告诉他我的一桩烦恼：我知道我该最爱上帝胜过一切，可爱有些东西我爱得胜过上帝。他说‘什么东西？’我说花呀，星星呀，风之女（the Wind Woman）呀，三公主呀，这些。他笑了，说：‘可它们正是上帝的一部分呀，艾米丽——每一样美的东西都是。’

===Original===

And all at once I liked him ever so much and didn’t feel shy with him any more. He preeched a sermon on heaven last Sunday. It seemed like a dull place. I think it must be more exiting than that. I wonder what I will do when I go to heaven since I cant sing. I wonder if they will let me write poetry. But I think church is interesting. Aunt Elizabeth and Aunt Laura always read their Bibles before the servis begins but I like to stare around and see everybody and wonder what they are thinking of. It’s so nice to hear the silk dresses swishing up the isles. Bustles are very fashunable now but Aunt Elizabeth will not wear them. I think Aunt Elizabeth *would* look funny with a bustle. Aunt Laura wears a very little one.
> “Your lovingest daughter,

**“Emily B. Starr.**
> “P.S. Dear Father, it is lovely to write to you. But O, I never get an answer back.
> “E. B. S.”

===Chinese===

一下子我就非常喜欢他，同他在一起再不害羞了。上礼拜日他讲了一场天堂的道。听着像个乏味的地方。我看天堂一定比那刺激。不知道我上了天堂干什么，因为我不会唱歌。不知道他们让不让写诗。不过我觉得教堂有意思。礼拜开始前，伊丽莎白姨妈和劳拉姨妈总读圣经，我却爱东张西望看看每个人，猜她们在想什么。丝绸裙子在走道里窸窸窣窣地过去，多好听呀。如今很时兴臀垫，可伊丽莎白姨妈不肯戴。我看伊丽莎白姨妈要是戴上臀垫，*准*滑稽。劳拉姨妈戴很小的一个。
> “你最最亲爱的女儿，

**“艾米丽·伯·斯塔尔（Emily B. Starr）。**
> “又及：亲爱的爸爸，给你写信真好。可是哦，我从来收不到回信。
> “艾·伯·斯（E. B. S.）”

## XVIII Father Cassidy / 十八 卡西迪神父
===Original===

Consternation reigned at New Moon. Everybody was desperately unhappy. Aunt Laura cried. Aunt Elizabeth was so cantankerous that there was no living with her. Cousin Jimmy went about as one distracted and Emily gave up worrying about Ilse’s mother and Silas Lee’s remorseful ghost after she went to bed, and worried over this new trouble. For it had all originated in her disregard of New Moon tradition in making calls on Lofty John, and Aunt Elizabeth did not mince matters in telling her so. If she, Emily Byrd Starr, had never gone to Lofty John’s she would never have eaten the Big Sweet apple, and if she had never eaten the Big Sweet apple Lofty John would not have played a joke on her and if he had not played a joke on her Aunt Elizabeth would never have gone and said bitter, Murray-like things to him; and if Aunt Elizabeth had never said bitter Murray-like things to him Lofty John would not have become offended and revengeful; and if Lofty John had not become offended and revengeful he would never have taken it into his lofty head to cut down the beautiful grove to the north of New Moon.
For this was exactly where this house-that-Jack-built progression had landed them all. Lofty John had announced publicly in the Blair Water blacksmith shop that he was going to cut down the bush as soon as harvest was over﻿—every last tree and sapling was to be laid low. The news was promptly carried to New Moon and upset the inhabitants thereof as they had not been upset for years. In their eyes it was nothing short of a catastrophe.
Elizabeth and Laura could hardly bring themselves to believe it. The thing was incredible. That big, thick, protecting bush of spruce and hardwood had *always* been there; it belonged to New Moon *morally*; even Lofty John Sullivan would not *dare* to cut it down. But Lofty John had an uncomfortable reputation for doing what he said he would do; that was a part of his loftiness; and if he did﻿—if he did﻿—
“New Moon will be ruined,” wailed poor Aunt Laura. “It will look *dreadful*﻿—*all* its beauty will go﻿—and we will be left open to the north wind and the sea storms﻿—we have always been so warm and sheltered here. And Jimmy’s garden will be ruined too.”
“This is what comes of bringing Emily here,” said Aunt Elizabeth.

===Chinese===

新月庄园人心惶惶。人人愁云惨雾。劳拉姨妈直哭。伊丽莎白姨妈别扭得没法同住。吉米表哥失魂落魄地转来转去；艾米丽上了床，也顾不上再操心伊尔丝的妈妈和塞拉斯·李悔恨的鬼魂，改操这份新的心。因为这一切全起因于她无视新月庄园的传统去拜访高个子约翰，这一点伊丽莎白姨妈说得毫不含糊。倘若她艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔（Emily Byrd Starr）从没去过高个子约翰家，她就决不会吃掉那只大甜苹果；倘若她没吃那只大甜苹果，高个子约翰就不会拿她打趣；倘若他没拿她打趣，伊丽莎白姨妈就不会跑去对他说那些刻毒的、默里做派的话；倘若伊丽莎白姨妈没说那些刻毒的默里做派的话，高个子约翰就不会记恨、寻仇；倘若他不记恨、寻仇，就决不会鬼迷心窍，打定主意砍掉新月庄园（New Moon）北边那片美丽的林子。
因为“杰克盖的房子”似的这一串因果，恰恰把他们全都栽到了这里。高个子约翰（Lofty John）已在布莱尔沃特（Blair Water）的铁匠铺里当众宣布：庄稼一收完，他就动手砍林子——最后一棵树、最后一株苗，都要放倒。消息立刻传到新月庄园，把里头的人搅得多少年没这么厉害过。在他们眼里，这简直就是一场大灾难。
伊丽莎白和劳拉几乎没法让自己相信。这事没影儿。那片又大又密、挡风护院的云杉杂木林一直就在那儿；论情理它属于新月庄园；连高个子约翰·沙利文总也不敢砍它吧。可高个子约翰说到做到的名声摆在那儿，叫人不踏实；那正是他“高傲”的一部分；要是他真砍——要是他真砍——
“新月庄园就毁了，”可怜的劳拉姨妈哭着说。“那要多难看有多难看——它的美全完了——北风和海上的风暴要直灌进来——我们这儿一向这么暖和、这么有遮蔽。吉米的园子也要毁了。”
“这就是把艾米丽领来的下场，”伊丽莎白姨妈说。

===Original===

It was a cruel thing to say, even when all allowances were made﻿—cruel and unjust, since her own sharp tongue and Murray sarcasm had had quite as much to do with it as Emily. But she said it and it pierced Emily to the heart with a pang that left a scar for years. Poor Emily did not feel as if she needed any additional anguish. She was already feeling so wretched that she could not eat or sleep. Elizabeth Murray, angry and unhappy as she was, slept soundly at nights; but beside her in the darkness, afraid to move or turn, lay a slender little creature whose tears, stealing silently down her cheeks, could not ease her breaking heart. For Emily thought her heart *was* breaking; she couldn’t go on living and suffering like this. Nobody could.
Emily had lived long enough at New Moon for it to get pretty thoroughly into her blood. Perhaps it had even been born there. At any rate, when she came to it she fitted into its atmosphere as a hand into a glove. She loved it as well as if she had lived there all her short life﻿—loved every stick and stone and tree and blade of grass about it﻿—every nail in the old kitchen floor, every cushion of green moss on the dairy roof, every pink and white columbine that grew in the old orchard, every “tradition” of its history. To think of its beauty being in a large measure reft from it was agony to her. And to think of Cousin Jimmy’s garden being ruined! Emily loved that garden almost as much as he did; why, it was the pride of Cousin Jimmy’s life that he could grow there plants and shrubs that would winter nowhere else in P.E. Island; if the northern shelter were removed they would die. And to think of that beautiful bush itself being cut down﻿—the Today Road and the Yesterday Road and the Tomorrow Road being swept out of existence﻿—the stately Monarch of the Forest discrowned﻿—the little playhouse where she and Ilse had such glorious hours destroyed﻿—the whole lovely, ferny, intimate place torn out of her life at one fell swoop.
Oh, Lofty John had chosen and timed his vengeance well!
When would the blow fall? Every morning Emily listened miserably as she stood on the sandstone doorstep of the kitchen, for the sound of axe blows on the clear September air. Every evening when she returned from school she dreaded to see that the work of destruction had begun. She pined and fretted.

===Chinese===

这话残忍，就算千容万恕——也残忍，而且不公道：她自己的利舌和默里式的挖苦，在这件事里出的力同艾米丽一样多。可她说了，这话像刀子扎进艾米丽心口，留下的疤好多年都在。可怜的艾米丽觉得自己实在不需要再添什么苦了。她已经愁得吃不下、睡不着。伊丽莎白·默里又气又苦，夜里照样睡得沉；可她身边黑地里，躺着一个一动不敢动、翻身不敢翻的纤细小人儿，泪水顺着脸颊无声地淌，也缓不了那颗碎掉的心。艾米丽只当自己的心真碎了；这样活着、这样受着，她熬不下去。谁也熬不下去。
艾米丽在新月庄园住得够久，它已经渗进她的血里。说不定她根本就是生在它那里的。无论如何，她一来就同它的气韵严丝合缝，像手伸进手套。她爱它，就仿佛短短一辈子都住在那儿——爱它的一草一木、一砖一石——爱老厨房地板上的每一颗钉子，爱奶房屋顶的每一垫绿苔，爱老果园里每一株粉的白的楼斗菜，爱它历史上的每一条“传统”。一想到它的美要被大片夺去，她就痛如刀剉。一想到吉米表哥的园子要毁，更了不得！艾米丽爱那园子，几乎不亚于他；要知道，吉米表哥一辈子引以为傲的，就是他能在那园子里种活爱德华王子岛上别处过不了冬的花木；北边的屏障一撤，它们就全得死。再想到那片美丽的林子本身要被砍倒——“今天之路”、“昨天之路”、“明天之路”要被一扫而光——堂堂的森林之王要被摘下王冠——她和伊尔丝度过多少美妙时光的小游戏屋要被毁掉——整个可爱的、蕨草萋萋、亲亲密密的地方，要一斧头从她的生命里挖走。
哦，高个子约翰这份仇，选的时机、下的狠劲，都够老到！
斧子几时落下来？每天早上，艾米丽站在厨房的砂石门槛上，凄凄惶惶地听，听九月清冽的空气里有没有斧声。每天傍晚放学回来，她都怕看见那毁林的工程已经动工。她憔悴了，焦灼了。

===Original===

There were times when it seemed to her she couldn’t bear her life any longer. Every day Aunt Elizabeth said something imputing the whole blame to her and the child grew morbidly sensitive about it. Almost she wished Lofty John would begin and be done with it. If Emily had ever heard the classic story of Damocles she would have heartily sympathized with him. If she had had any hope that it would do any good she would have swallowed Murray pride and Starr pride and every other kind of pride and gone on her knees to Lofty John to entreat him to hold his revengeful hand. But she believed it would not. Lofty John had left no doubt in anybody’s mind as to his bitter determination in the matter. There was much talk about it in Blair Water and some were very well pleased at this blow to New Moon pride and prestige, and some held that it was low and unclean behaviour on Lofty John’s part, and all agreed that this was what they had prophesied all along as bound to happen some day when the old Murray-Sullivan feud of three generations should have come to its inevitable head. The only surprising thing was that Lofty John hadn’t done it long ago. He had always hated Elizabeth Murray since their schooldays, when her tongue had not spared him.
One day by the banks of Blair Water Emily sat down and wept. She had been sent to trim the dead blossoms off the rosebushes on Grandmother Murray’s grave; having finished her task she had not the heart to go back to the house where Aunt Elizabeth was making everybody miserable because she was herself so unhappy. Perry had reported that Lofty John had stated the day before at the blacksmith’s that he was going to begin cutting down the big bush on Monday morning.
“I *can’t* bear it,” sobbed Emily to the rosebushes.
A few late roses nodded at her; the Wind Woman combed and waved and stirred the long green grasses on the graves where proud Murrays, men and women, slept calmly, unstirred by old feuds and passions; the September sunlight shone beyond on old harvest fields mellowly bright and serene, and very softly against its green, shrub-hung bank, purred and lapped the blue Blair Water.
“I don’t see why God doesn’t *stop* Lofty John,” said Emily passionately. Surely the New Moon Murrays had a right to expect that much from Providence.

===Chinese===

有时她觉得自己再也熬不住了。伊丽莎白姨妈天天说些什么，把罪责全推在她身上，孩子为这落下了病态的敏感。她几乎巴望高个子约翰快点儿动手，早死早超生。艾米丽要是听过达摩克利斯那个古典故事，准会跟他同声一哭。要是她觉得有用，她肯把默里家的傲气、斯塔尔家的傲气、各家各姓的傲气全咽下去，跪到高个子约翰跟前，求他收回那只寻仇的手。可她信那没用。高个子约翰在这件事上狠死的决心，没让任何人存半点疑心。布莱尔沃特议论纷纷：有的拍手称快，乐见新月庄园的威风体面栽这一跤；有的说高个子约翰此举下作、不干净；人人一致说，这正是他们早就预言过的——默里、沙利文三代的宿仇，早晚有一天要炸开，如今应验了。唯一叫人意外的是他没早动手。打上学那年起，他就一直恨伊丽莎白·默里——那时候她那张嘴就不饶他。
一天，艾米丽在布莱尔沃特岸边坐下哭了。她被打发来剪掉默里外婆坟头玫瑰上的谢花；活儿做完了，她没心思回宅子——伊丽莎白姨妈正把她自己也苦着，弄得人人不痛快。佩里捎话来，说高个子约翰前一天在铁匠铺讲了，礼拜一早上就动手砍那大片林子。
“我熬不住，”艾米丽对着玫瑰抽抽噎噎。
几朵迟开的玫瑰朝她点头；风之女（the Wind Woman）梳着、拂着、掀动着坟头长长的青草——骄傲的默里家的人，男男女女，就安睡在底下，旧的宿怨情仇，再搅不动他们；九月的太阳照在远处旧年的庄稼地上，温煦、明亮、安详；蓝蓝的布莱尔沃特贴着它绿树垂岸的水湾，柔柔地打着旋、脘着岸。
“我不明白上帝为什么不制住高个子约翰，”艾米丽愤愤地说。新月庄园的默里家，向天意讨这一点点，总不算过分吧。

===Original===

Teddy came whistling down the pasture, the notes of his tune blowing across the Blair Water like elfin drops of sound, vaulted the graveyard fence and perched his lean, graceful body irreverently on the “Here I stay” of Great-Grandmother Murray’s flat tombstone.
“What’s the matter?” he said.
“Everything’s the matter,” said Emily, a little crossly. Teddy had no business to be looking so cheerful. She was used to more sympathy from Teddy and it aggravated her not to find it. “Don’t you know Lofty John is going to begin cutting down the bush Monday?”
Teddy nodded.
“Yep. Ilse told me. But look here, Emily, I’ve thought of something. Lofty John wouldn’t dare cut down that bush if the priest told him not to, would he?”
“Why?”
“Because the Catholics have to do just what their priests tell them to, haven’t they?”
“I don’t know﻿—I don’t know anything about them. *We* are Presbyterians.”
Emily gave her head a little toss. Mrs. Kent was known to be an “English Church” woman and though Teddy went to the Presbyterian Sunday School, that fact gave him scanty standing among bred-in-the-bone Presbyterian circles.
“If your Aunt Elizabeth went to Father Cassidy at White Cross and asked him to stop Lofty John, maybe he’d do it,” persisted Teddy.
“Aunt Elizabeth would never do that,” said Emily positively. “I’m sure of it. She’s too proud.”
“Not even to save the bush?”
“Not even for that.”
“Then I guess nothing can be done,” said Teddy rather crestfallen. “Look here﻿—see what I’ve made. This is a picture of Lofty John in purgatory, with three little devils sticking red-hot pitch forks into him. I copied some of it out of one of mother’s books﻿—Dante’s Infernal, I think it was﻿—but I put Lofty John in place of the man in the book. You can have it.”
“I don’t want it.” Emily uncoiled her legs and got up. She was past the stage when inflicting imaginary torments on Lofty John could comfort her. She had already slain him in several agonizing ways during her night vigils. But an idea had come to her﻿—a daring, breathless idea. “I must go home now, Teddy﻿—it’s supper time.”
Teddy pocketed his despised sketch﻿—which was really a wonderful bit of work if either of them had had the sense to know it; the expression of anguish in Lofty John’s face as a merry little devil touched him up with a pitchfork would have been the despair of many a trained artist.

===Chinese===

泰迪吹着口哨跑下牧场，调子的音符像精灵的声珠洒过布莱尔沃特，他翻身跳过墓园栅栏，大不敬地把他瘦长灵巧的身子往高祖母那块扁石碑的“我就在这儿待下了”上一坐。
“怎么了？”他说。
“什么都怎么了，”艾米丽说，有点儿冲。泰迪没理由这么一脸欢喜。她习惯了从泰迪那儿多得些同情，如今捞不着，就叫她恼。“你不知道高个子约翰礼拜一就要动刀砍林子了？”
泰迪点点头。
“知道。伊尔丝告诉我的。可你听着，艾米丽，我想出个法子。要是神父不许，高个子约翰就不敢砍那林子，对吧？”
“为什么？”
“天主教徒不是得一切听神父的吗？”
“我不知道——他们的事我一概不知道。我们是长老会的。”
艾米丽小脑袋一扬。肯特太太出了名是“英国国教”的，泰迪虽然上长老会的主日学校，在根正苗红的长老会圈子里，这一点也没给他挣来几分地位。
“要是你伊丽莎白姨妈去白十字（White Cross）找卡西迪神父（Father Cassidy），求他拦住高个子约翰，兴许他就肯，”泰迪不死心。
“伊丽莎白姨妈决不会去的，”艾米丽说得笃定。“我拿得准。她太骄傲。”
“救林子也不去？”
“救林子也不去。”
“那我看没辙了，”泰迪挺垂头丧气。“你瞧——瞧我画的。这是高个子约翰在炼狱里，三个小鬼拿烧红的草叉子戳他。有一半是从我妈一本书上抄的——我记得是但丁的《地狱》——不过我把高个子约翰换了书里那个人。送给你。”
“我不要。”艾米丽伸直盘着的腿站起来。拿想象中的酷刑折磨高个子约翰已安慰不了她了——那一步她早过去了。守夜的工夫，她已经用好几种痛法杀过他几回了。可一个念头冒了出来——一个大胆的、叫人屏息的念头。“我得回家了，泰迪——到吃晚饭的钟点了。”
泰迪把那张遭冷落的画揣进兜里——其实那是一件很了不起的作品，可惜他俩谁都没眼光；画上那个欢天喜地的小鬼拿草叉一戳，高个子约翰脸上那份苦相，多少受过训练的画师也要自愧不如。

===Original===

He went home wishing he could help Emily; it was all wrong that a creature like Emily﻿—with soft purple-gray eyes and a smile that made you think of all sorts of wonderful things you couldn’t put into words﻿—should be unhappy. Teddy felt so worried about it that he added a few more devils to his sketch of Lofty John in purgatory and lengthened the prongs of their pitchforks quite considerably.
Emily went home with a determined twist to her mouth. She ate as much supper as she could﻿—which wasn’t much, for Aunt Elizabeth’s face would have destroyed her appetite if she had had any﻿—and then sneaked out of the house by the front door. Cousin Jimmy was working in his garden but he did not call her. Cousin Jimmy was always very sorrowful now. Emily stood a moment on the Grecian porch and looked at Lofty John’s bush﻿—green-bosomed, waving, all lovely. Would it be a desecrated waste of stumps by Monday night? Goaded by the thought Emily cast fear and hesitation to the winds and started briskly off down the lane. When she reached the gate she turned to the left on the long red road of mystery that ran up the Delectable Mountain. She had never been on that road before; it ran straight to White Cross; Emily was going to the parish house there to interview Father Cassidy. It was two miles to White Cross and Emily walked it all too soon﻿—not because it was a beautiful road of wind and wild fern, haunted by little rabbits﻿—but because she dreaded what awaited her at the end. She had been trying to think what she should say﻿—how she should say it; but her invention failed her. She had no acquaintance with Catholic priests, and couldn’t imagine how you should talk to them at all. They were even more mysterious and unknowable than ministers. Suppose Father Cassidy should be dreadfully angry at her daring to come there and ask a favour. Perhaps it *was* a dreadful thing to do from every point of view. And very likely it would do no good. Very likely Father Cassidy would refuse to interfere with Lofty John, who was a good Catholic, while she was, in his opinion, a heretic. But for any chance, even the faintest, of averting the calamity impending over New Moon, Emily would have faced the entire Sacred College. Horribly frightened, miserably nervous as she was, the idea of turning back never occurred to her. She was only sorry that she hadn’t put on her Venetian beads.

===Chinese===

他一路回家，直盼着能帮上艾米丽；像艾米丽这样的人——一双柔柔的紫灰色眼睛，一个笑起来叫你想起种种说不出口的美妙事情的笑靥——竟该这么愁苦，天理不通。泰迪为这事这么犯愁，回家又给炼狱图里的高个子约翰添了几个小鬼，把草叉的齿也加长了好一截。
艾米丽回家时，嘴角拧出一股决断。她能吃多少就吃了多少晚饭——其实没吃下多少，她就算有胃口，伊丽莎白姨妈那张脸也够把它毁了——随后从前门溜出了宅子。吉米表哥在园子里干活，没喊她。吉米表哥如今总是愁眉不展。艾米丽在希腊式门廊上站了一霎，望着高个子约翰的林子——绿胸膛，波涛涌，处处可爱。到礼拜一夜里，它就要成一片满目树桩的荒场了？这个念头鞭子似的赶着她，她把胆怯和迟疑全抛到九霄云外，快步顺着小径出发了。走到大门口，她向左一拐，上了那条通向怡人山的、长长的红土神秘之路。这条路她从没走过；它直通白十字；艾米丽是要上那儿的本堂神父宅去见卡西迪神父。到白十字两英里，艾米丽只嫌一下子就走完了——倒不是因为那是条风与野蕨的路，有小兔出没——是因为她怕那一头的等待。她一路想该说什么——该怎么说；可想不出。她不认识天主教神父，想象不出该怎样同他们讲话。他们比牧师还神秘、还深不可测。万一卡西迪神父见她胆敢上门求事，大发雷霆呢？兴许从哪头看，这都是一件可怕的冒失事。多半还没用。多半卡西迪神父会拒绝插手高个子约翰的事——他是个好天主教徒，而她，在他眼里，是个异端。可只要有一线希望——哪怕最最微弱的一线——能挽回悬在新月庄园头顶的大祸，叫艾米丽去面对整个枢机团她也敢。她怕得要命，紧张得要命，可回头这两个字压根儿没进过她的脑子。她只后悔没戴上那串威尼斯珠子。

===Original===

They might have impressed Father Cassidy.
Although Emily had never been to White Cross she knew the parish house when she saw it﻿—a fine, tree-embowered residence near the big white chapel with the flashing gilt cross on its spire and the four gilt angels, one on each of the little spires at the corners. Emily thought them very beautiful as they gleamed in the light of the lowering sun, and wished they could have some on the plain white church at Blair Water. She couldn’t understand why Catholics should have all the angels. But there was not time to puzzle over this, for the door was opening and the trim little maid was looking a question.
“Is﻿—Father Cassidy﻿—at﻿—home?” asked Emily, rather jerkily.
“Yes.”
“Can﻿—I﻿—see﻿—him?”
“Come in,” said the little maid. Evidently there was no difficulty about seeing Father Cassidy﻿—no mysterious ceremonies such as Emily had half expected, even if she were allowed to see him at all. She was shown into a book-lined room and left there, while the maid went to call Father Cassidy, who, she said, was working in the garden. *That* sounded quite natural and encouraging. If Father Cassidy worked in a garden, he could not be so very terrible.
Emily looked about her curiously. She was in a very pretty room﻿—with cosy chairs, and pictures and flowers. Nothing alarming or uncanny about it﻿—except a huge black cat who was sitting on the top of one of the bookcases. It was really an enormous creature. Emily adored cats and had always felt at home with any of them. But she had never seen such a cat as this. What with its size and its insolent, gold-hued eyes, set like living jewels in its black velvet face, it did not seem to belong to the same species as nice, cuddly, respectable kittens at all. Mr. Dare would never have had such a beast about his manse. All Emily’s dread of Father Cassidy returned.
And then in came Father Cassidy, with the friendliest smile in the world. Emily took him in with her level glance as was her habit﻿—or gift﻿—and never again in the world was she the least bit afraid of Father Cassidy. He was big and broad-shouldered, with brown eyes and brown hair; and his very face was so deeply tanned from his inveterate habit of going about bareheaded in merciless sunshine, that it was brown, too. Emily thought he looked just like a big nut﻿—a big, brown, wholesome nut.

===Chinese===

珠子兴许能唬住卡西迪神父。
艾米丽虽没到过白十字，可一见就认出了本堂神父宅——一座精致的、绿树环合的住宅，紧挨着那座白色大教堂：尖顶上闪着镀金的十字架，四角小尖顶上各立一位镀金天使。夕阳低垂，天使们映着光，艾米丽觉得美极了，巴不得布莱尔沃特那座素白的教堂顶上也来几个。她不明白，凭什么天主教徒把天使全占了。可没工夫琢磨这个，门开了，一个齐整的小女仆探出疑问的眼色。
“卡西迪——神父——在家吗？”艾米丽问，一句一顿。
“在。”
“我——能——见见他吗？”
“进来，”小女仆说。显然，见卡西迪神父毫无难处——没有艾米丽半料到的什么神秘仪节。她被领进一间四壁是书的屋子，留在那儿，女仆去请卡西迪神父，说他正在园子里干活。这话听着自然极了，也叫人壮胆。卡西迪神父既然在园子里干活，想必可怕不到哪儿去。
艾米丽好奇地四下打量。这是一间很好看的屋子——有舒舒服服的椅子，有画，有花。没什么吓人的、邪性的——只有一只巨大的黑猫蹲在一个书柜顶上。那真是个庞然大物。艾米丽顶爱猫，见了哪只猫都跟到家一样。可她从没见过这样的猫。论块头，论那双傲慢的金色眼睛——像两颗活的宝石嵌在天鹅绒的黑脸上——它简直同那种乖顺、可抱、体面的小猫咪不是一个种类。戴尔先生的牧师住宅里，决不会有这样一头畜生。艾米丽对卡西迪神父的恐惧全回来了。
就在这时，卡西迪神父进来了，带着世上顶和气的笑。艾米丽按她的习惯——或者说天分——平平地一瞥，把他收进眼里；从此她再没怕过卡西迪神父一丝一毫。他高大，宽肩膀，棕色的眼睛棕色的头发；因为养成了在毒日头底下光着脑袋走来走去的习惯，脸也晒成了棕色，深深的棕。艾米丽觉得他活像一颗大坚果——一颗又大又棕、结实饱满的坚果。

===Original===

Father Cassidy looked at her as he shook hands; Emily had one of her visitations of beauty just then. Excitement had brought a wild-rose hue to her face, the sunlight brought out the watered-silk gloss of her black hair; her eyes were softly dark and limpid; but it was at her ears Father Cassidy suddenly bent to look. Emily had a moment of agonized wonder if they were clean.
“She’s got pointed ears,” said Father Cassidy, in a thrilling whisper. “Pointed ears! I *knew* she came straight from fairyland the minute I saw her. Sit down, Elf﻿—if elves do sit﻿—sit down and give me the latest news av Titania’s court.”
Emily’s foot was now on her native heath. Father Cassidy talked her language, and he talked it in such a mellow, throaty voice, slurring his “ofs” ever so softly as became a proper Irishman. But she shook her head a little sadly. With the burden of her errand on her soul she could not play the part of ambassadress from Elfland.
“I’m only Emily Starr of New Moon,” she said; and then gasped hurriedly, because there must be no deception﻿—no sailing under false colours, “and I’m a Protestant.”
“And a very nice little Protestant you are,” said Father Cassidy. “But for sure I’m a bit disappointed. I’m used to Protestants﻿—the woods hereabouts being full av them﻿—but it’s a hundred years since the last elf called on me.”
Emily stared. Surely Father Cassidy wasn’t a hundred years old. He didn’t look more than fifty. Perhaps, though, Catholic priests did live longer than other people. She didn’t know exactly what to say so she said, a bit lamely,
“I see you have a cat.”
“Wrong.” Father Cassidy shook his head and groaned dismally. “A cat has me.”
Emily gave up trying to understand Father Cassidy. He was nice but ununderstandable. She let it go at that. And she must get on with her errand.
“You are a kind of minister, aren’t you?” she asked timidly. She didn’t know whether Father Cassidy would like being called a minister.
“Kind av,” he agreed amiably. “And you see ministers and priests can’t do their own swearing. They have to keep cats to do it for them. I never knew any cat that could sware as genteelly and effectively as the B’y.”
“Is that what you call him?” asked Emily, looking at the black cat in some awe. It seemed hardly safe to discuss him right before his face.
“That’s what he calls himself. My mother doesn’t like him because he steals the cream.

===Chinese===

卡西迪神父握手的时候端详她；这一霎，艾米丽的美丽正巧降临。激动给她脸上添了一抹野蔷薇色，阳光把她黑发那水绸般的光泽照了出来；她的眼睛柔黑清亮；可卡西迪神父忽然俯身细看的，是她的耳朵。艾米丽痛苦地疑心了一霎：耳朵干净吗？
“她长着尖耳朵，”卡西迪神父用激动人心的耳语说。“尖耳朵！我一见就知道，她是从仙境直接来的。坐吧，小精灵——要是精灵也坐的话——坐下，给我讲讲提坦尼娅宫廷的最新消息。”
艾米丽这就算踏上自家荒原了。卡西迪神父说的是她的语言，而且用一副圆润沙糯的嗓子说，“of”轻轻带过——地道的爱尔兰人做派。可她微微摇头，有点难过。使命的担子压在心头，她扮不成仙境来的女使臣。
“我只是新月庄园的艾米丽·斯塔尔，”她说；随即慌忙喘着补上一句——不能骗人——不能挂假旗子——“而且我是新教徒。”
“是个顶好的小新教徒，”卡西迪神父说。“不过说实话，我有点儿失望。新教徒我见多了——这一带林子里满都是——可上一个精灵来看我，还是一百年前的事哩。”
艾米丽瞪眼。卡西迪神父总不会有一百岁吧。他看着不过五十。不过，兴许天主教神父比旁人活得长。她不知道该说什么好，就有点没底气地说：
“我看您养着一只猫。”
“错了。”卡西迪神父摇头，凄凄惨惨地哼了一声。“是猫养着我。”
艾米丽放弃了理解卡西迪神父的努力。他人好，可没法懂。就随他去吧。她也该办正事了。
“您算是牧师的一种，对吧？”她怯怯地问。她不知道卡西迪神父爱不爱听人叫他牧师。
“算是吧，”他和和气气地认了。“你知道，牧师和神父不便亲口骂人。他们得养猫代劳。我从没见过哪只猫骂人骂得像‘小子’这样文雅、这样有力。”
“您这么叫它？”艾米丽问，有点敬畏地望着那只黑猫。当着它的面议论它，似乎不大安全。
“是它这么叫自己。我妈不喜欢它，它偷奶油。

===Original===

Now, *I* don’t mind his doing that; no, it’s his way av licking his jaws after it that I can’t stand. Oh, B’y, we’ve a fairy calling on us. Be excited for once, I implore you﻿—there’s a duck av a cat.”
The B’y refused to be excited. He winked an insolent eye at Emily.
“Have you any idea what goes on in the head av a cat, elf?”
What queer questions Father Cassidy asked. Yet Emily thought she would like his questions if she were not so worried. Suddenly Father Cassidy leaned across the table and said,
“Now, just what’s bothering you?”
“I’m so unhappy,” said Emily piteously.
“So are lots av other people. Everybody is unhappy by spells. But creatures who have pointed ears shouldn’t be unhappy. It’s only mortals who should be that.”
“Oh, please﻿—please﻿—” Emily wondered what she should call him. Would it offend him if a Protestant called him “Father”? But she had to risk it﻿—“please, Father Cassidy, I’m in such trouble and I’ve come to ask a *great favour* of you.”
Emily told him the whole tale from beginning to end﻿—the old Murray-Sullivan feud, her erstwhile friendship with Lofty John, the Big Sweet apple, the unhappy consequence, and Lofty John’s threatened revenge. The B’y and Father Cassidy listened with equal gravity until she had finished. Then the B’y winked at her, but Father Cassidy put his long brown fingers together.
“Humph,” he said.
(“That’s the first time,” reflected Emily, “that I’ve ever heard anyone outside of a book say ‘Humph.’ ”)
“Humph,” said Father Cassidy again. “And you want me to put a stop to this nefarious deed?”
“If you can,” said Emily. “Oh, it would be so splendid if you could. Will you﻿—will you?”
Father Cassidy fitted his fingers still more carefully together.
“I’m afraid I can hardly invoke the power av the keys to prevent Lofty John from disposing as he wishes av his own lawful property, you know, elf.”
Emily didn’t understand the allusion to the keys but she did understand that Father Cassidy was declining to bring the lever of the Church to bear on Lofty John. There was no hope, then. She could not keep the tears of disappointment out of her eyes.
“Oh, come now, darling, don’t cry,” implored Father Cassidy. “Elves never cry﻿—they can’t. It would break my heart to discover you weren’t av the Green Folk.

===Chinese===

喏，偷奶油我不计较；不，我受不了的，是它偷完舔嘴巴那副德行。哦，小子，来精灵看咱们啦。就激动这么一回吧，我求你——瞧这猫，多俊。”
“小子”不肯激动。它朝艾米丽傲慢地眨了一只眼。
“精灵，你可知道一只猫脑袋里装的都是什么名堂？”
卡西迪神父问的问题多古怪。可艾米丽想，若不是愁着心事，她准喜欢他这些问题。卡西迪神父忽然隔着桌子俯过身来，说：
“说吧，到底什么在折磨你？”
“我愁死了，”艾米丽可怜巴巴地说。
“愁的人多着呢。人人都有愁的时辰。可长了尖耳朵的不该发愁。该愁的只有凡人。”
“哦，求您——求您——”艾米丽不知该怎样称呼他。一个新教徒叫他“神父”，他会见怪吗？可她只好冒险——“求您了，卡西迪神父，我遭了大难，来求您一件大事。”
艾米丽把事情从头到尾讲了一遍——默里、沙利文两家的老仇，她同高个子约翰的一段交情，那只大甜苹果，闯下的祸，高个子约翰扬言的报复。“小子”和卡西迪神父同样郑重地听她讲完。随后“小子”朝她眨眨眼，卡西迪神父却把棕色的长手指对了起来。
“哼，”他说。
（“这是头一回，”艾米丽想，“我听见书以外的人说‘哼’。”）
“哼，”卡西迪神父又说。“你要我拦下这桩恶行？”
“要是您能，”艾米丽说。“哦，您要是能，多好。您肯吗——肯吗？”
卡西迪神父把手指对得更仔细了。
“恐怕，精灵，我很难动用钥匙的权柄，去拦着高个子约翰处置他自家合法的产业。”
艾米丽不懂“钥匙”的典故，可她听懂了：卡西迪神父不肯把教会这根杠杆压到高个子约翰身上。这么说，没指望了。失望的泪一下涌上她的眼睛，忍也忍不住。
“哦，好了好了，小宝贝，别哭，”卡西迪神父夬求道。“精灵从来不哭——哭不了。要是发现你不是绿衣族的人，我的心都要碎啦。

===Original===

You may call yourself av New Moon and av any religion you like, but the fact remains that you belong to the Golden Age and the old gods. That’s why I must save your precious bit av greenwood for you.”
Emily stared.
