“I think it can be done,” Father Cassidy went on. “I think if I go to Lofty John and have a heart-to-heart talk with him I can make him see reason. Lofty John and I are very good friends. He’s a reasonable creature, if you know how to take him﻿—which means to flatter his vanity judiciously. I’ll put it to him, not as priest to parishioner, but as man to man, that no decent Irishman carries on a feud with women and that no sensible person is going to destroy for nothing but a grudge those fine old trees that have taken half a century to grow and can never be replaced. Why, the man who cuts down such a tree except when it is really necessary should be hanged as high as Haman on a gallows made from the wood av it.”
(Emily thought she would write that last sentence of Father Cassidy’s down in Cousin Jimmy’s blank book when she got home.)
“But I won’t say *that* to Lofty John,” concluded Father Cassidy. “Yes, Emily av New Moon, I think we can consider it a settled thing that your bush will not be cut down.”
Suddenly Emily felt very happy. Somehow she had entire confidence in Father Cassidy. She was sure he would twist Lofty John around his little finger.
“Oh, I can never thank you enough!” she said earnestly.
“That’s true, so don’t waste breath trying. And now tell me things. Are there any more av you? And how long have you been yourself?”
“I’m twelve years old﻿—I haven’t any brothers or sisters. And I *think* I’d better be going home.”
“Not till you’ve had a bite av lunch.”
“Oh, thank you, I’ve had my supper.”
“Two hours ago and a two-mile walk since. Don’t tell me. I’m sorry I haven’t any nectar and ambrosia on hand﻿—such food as elves eat﻿—and not even a saucer av moonshine﻿—but my mother makes the best plum cake av any woman in P.E. Island. And we keep a cream cow. Wait here a bit. Don’t be afraid av the B’y. He eats tender little Protestants sometimes, but he never meddles with leprechauns.”
When Father Cassidy came back his mother came with him, carrying a tray. Emily had expected to see her big and brown too, but she was the tiniest woman imaginable, with snow-white, silky hair, mild blue eyes, and pink cheeks.

===Chinese===

你可以自称新月庄园的、信什么都随你，可事实是：你属于黄金时代，属于老神祇们。所以我得替你保住那片宝贝的小绿林。”
艾米丽直瞪眼。
“我看能成，”卡西迪神父接着说。“我看，只要我去找高个子约翰，同他掏心窝子谈一谈，就能叫他明白过来。高个子约翰同我是顶好的朋友。他是个讲理的家伙——只要你会请——也就是说，会看火候捧他的虚荣心。我要同他讲：不是神父对教民，是人对人——正经爱尔兰人不跟娘儿们结仇；明白人不为赌一口气，就去毁那些长了半个世纪、毁一棵少一棵的好树。要知道，砍这样一棵树的人——除非真有必要——就该拿那树的木头给他造绞架，把他吊得跟哈曼一样高。”
（艾米丽想，回家要把卡西迪神父这末一句话抄进吉米表哥的白本子里。）
“可这话我不对高个子约翰讲，”卡西迪神父收了尾。“就这样吧，新月庄园的艾米丽，我看可以算定局了：你那片林子，砍不成了。”
忽然艾米丽觉得快活极了。不知怎么，她对卡西迪神父有十足的信任。她拿准，他要把高个子约翰玩弄于股掌之间。
“哦，我怎么谢您也谢不够！”她真心实意地说。
“这话不假，所以别费唾沫了。现在讲讲你的事。你们那儿还有像你这样的吗？你当你自己多久啦？”
“我十二岁——没有兄弟姐妹。我想——我该回家了。”
“吃口点心再走。”
“哦，谢谢，我吃过晚饭了。”
“两个钟头前吃的，又走了两英里。别嘴硬。可惜我手头没有花蜜仙馔——精灵的吃食——连一碟月光也没有——可我妈做的葡萄干蛋糕，是全爱德华王子岛女人里头一号。我们还养着一头奶油牛。在这儿等一忽儿。别怕‘小子’。它有时吃嫩嫩的小新教徒，可它从不招惹矮精灵。”
卡西迪神父回来时，他母亲也来了，端着托盘。艾米丽原想她也是高大棕色的，可她是想象里顶小巧的女人，雪白的丝发，温顺的蓝眼睛，粉扑扑的脸蛋。

===Original===

“Isn’t she the sweetest thing in the way av mothers?” asked Father Cassidy. “I keep her to look at. Av course﻿—” Father Cassidy dropped his voice to a pig’s whisper﻿—“there’s something odd about her. I’ve known that woman to stop right in the middle av housecleaning, and go off and spend an afternoon in the woods. Like yourself, I’m thinking she has some truck with fairies.”
Mrs. Cassidy smiled, kissed Emily, said she must go out and finish her preserving, and trotted off.
“Now you sit right down here, Elf, and be human for ten minutes and we’ll have a friendly snack.”
Emily *was* hungry﻿—a nice comfortable feeling she hadn’t experienced for a fortnight. Mrs. Cassidy’s plum cake was all her reverend son claimed, and the cream cow seemed to be no myth.
“What do you think av me now?” asked Father Cassidy suddenly, finding Emily’s eyes fixed on him speculatively.
Emily blushed. She had been wondering if she dared ask another favour of Father Cassidy.
“I think you are awfully good,” she said.
“I *am* awfully good,” agreed Father Cassidy. “I’m so good that I’ll do what you want me to do﻿—for I feel there’s something else you want me to do.”
“I’m in a scrape and I’ve been in it all summer. You see”﻿—Emily was very sober﻿—“I am a poetess.”
“Holy Mike! That *is* serious. I don’t know if I can do much for you. How long have you been that way?”
“Are you making fun of me?” asked Emily gravely.
Father Cassidy swallowed something besides plum cake.
“The saints forbid! It’s only that I’m rather overcome. To be after entertaining a lady av New Moon﻿—and an elf﻿—and a poetess all in one is a bit too much for a humble praste like meself. Have another slice av cake and tell me all about it.”
“It’s like this﻿—I’m writing an epic.”
Father Cassidy suddenly leaned over and gave Emily’s wrist a little pinch.
“I just wanted to see if you were real,” he explained. “Yes﻿—yes, you’re writing an epic﻿—go on. I think I’ve got my second wind now.”
“I began it last spring. I called it ‘The White Lady’ first but now I’ve changed it to ‘The Child of the Sea.’ Don’t you think that’s a better title?”
“Much better.”
“I’ve got three cantos done, and I can’t get any further because there’s something I don’t know and can’t find out. I’ve been so worried about it.”
“What is it?”

===Chinese===

“这样的母亲，天底下是不是头一号？”卡西迪神父问。“我留着她，专门用来看。当然——”卡西迪神父把嗓门压到猪叫似的耳语——“她有点儿怪。我见过那女人大扫除扫到一半，撂下扫帚，进林子里消磨一下午去了。同你一样，我看她也同精灵有来往。”
卡西迪太太笑着吻了吻艾米丽，说她得出去把果酱收尾，颠颠地走了。
“来，小精灵，就在这儿坐下，做十分钟的人，咱们友好地吃一顿点心。”
艾米丽真饿了——一种舒服的好感觉，她两个礼拜没尝过了。卡西迪太太的葡萄干蛋糕全配得上她那可敬的儿子自夸的，那头奶油牛看来也不是吹的。
“这会儿你觉得我怎么样了？”卡西迪神父忽然问，他发现艾米丽正打量地盯着他。
艾米丽脸红了。她正琢磨着，敢不敢再求卡西迪神父一件事。
“我觉得您好得不得了，”她说。
“我确实好得不得了，”卡西迪神父附和。“我好到愿意替你办你想让我办的事——因为我觉出来，你还有事要我办。”
“我闯了个祸，闯了一夏天了。是这样——”艾米丽很郑重——“我是个女诗人。”
“老天爷！这病可不轻。我能帮上多少，难说。得这病多久了？”
“您拿我打趣吗？”艾米丽正色问。
卡西迪神父咽下了一口葡萄干蛋糕以外的东西。
“圣人不容！我只是有点儿招架不住。又要招待新月庄园的贵女——又是精灵——又是女诗人，三合一，对我这样一个卑微的小神父，实在有点儿重。再吃一片蛋糕，把事情统统讲给我听。”
“是这样的——我在写一部史诗。”
卡西迪神父忽然俯过身，轻轻拧了一下艾米丽的手腕。
“我就是想看看你是不是真的，”他解释道。“好——好，你在写一部史诗——接着讲。我看我这口气缓过来了。”
“去年春天开的头。起先叫《白衣夫人》，如今改成《海的女儿》了。您不觉得这题目更好？”
“好得多。”
“写完三歌了，再写不下去，因为有件事我不知道，也打听不着。我一直为这个愁。”
“什么事？”

===Original===

“My epic,” said Emily, diligently devouring plum cake, “is about a very beautiful highborn girl who was stolen away from her real parents when she was a baby and brought up in a woodcutter’s hut.”
“One av of the seven original plots in the world,” murmured Father Cassidy.
“What?”
“Nothing. Just a bad habit av thinking aloud. Go on.”
“She had a lover of high degree but his family did not want him to marry her because she was only a woodcutter’s daughter﻿—”
“Another of the seven plots﻿—excuse me.”
“﻿—so they sent him away to the Holy land on a crusade and word came back that he was killed and then Editha﻿—her name was Editha﻿—went into a convent﻿—”
Emily paused for a bite of plum cake and Father Cassidy took up the strain.
“And now her lover comes back very much alive, though covered with Paynim scars, and the secret av her birth is discovered through the dying confession av the old nurse and the birthmark on her arm.”
“How did you know?” gasped Emily in amazement.
“Oh, I guessed it﻿—I’m a good guesser. But where’s your bother in all this?”
“I don’t know how to get her out of the convent,” confessed Emily. “I thought perhaps you would know how it could be done.”
Again Father Cassidy fitted his fingers.
“Let us see, now. It’s no light matter you’ve undertaken, young lady. How stands the case? *Editha* has taken the veil, not because she has a religious vocation but because she imagines her heart is broken. The Catholic Church does not release its nuns from their vows because they happen to think they’ve made a little mistake av that sort. No, no﻿—we must have a better reason. Is this Editha the sole child av her real parents?”
“Yes.”
“Oh, then the way is clear. If she had had any brothers or sisters you would have had to kill them off, which is a messy thing to do. Well, then, she is the sole daughter and heiress av a noble family who have for years been at deadly feud with another noble family﻿—the family av the lover. Do you know what a feud is?”
“Of course,” said Emily disdainfully. “And I’ve got all that in the poem already.”
“So much the better. This feud has rent the kingdom in twain and can only be healed by an alliance between Capulet and Montague.”
“Those aren’t their names.”
“No matter. This, then, is a national affair, with far-reaching issues, therefore an appeal to the Supreme Pontiff is quite in order.

===Chinese===

“我的史诗，”艾米丽说，一边勤勤恳恳地消灭葡萄干蛋糕，“讲的是一个出身高门的绝色姑娘，襁褓里就从亲生父母身边被偷走，在一座伐木人的小屋里长大。”
“天下七大原装故事情节之一，”卡西迪神父喃喃。
“什么？”
“没什么。不过是有出声想事儿的毛病。往下讲。”
“她有一位高贵的情人，可他家里人不要他娶她，因为她不过是个伐木人的女儿——”
“七大情节里的又一桩——失敬。”
“——他们就把他打发去圣地参加十字军，传回话说他死了，于是埃迪莎——她叫埃迪莎——进修道院做了修女——”
艾米丽停下来咬一口蛋糕，卡西迪神父接过了话头。
“如今她的情人活蹦乱跳地回来了，虽然满身异教徒刀疤；而她的身世之秘，靠着老乳妈临终的招供和她臂上的胎记，水落石出。”
“您怎么知道的？”艾米丽惊得直喘。
“哦，我猜的——我猜功不错。可你到底卡在哪儿？”
“我不知道怎么把她从修道院里弄出来，”艾米丽招了。“我想，兴许您知道该怎么办。”
卡西迪神父又把手指对了起来。
“咱们来合计合计。小姑娘，你揽的这可不是小事。案情如何？埃迪莎立誓出家，不是因为宗教圣召，是她自以为心碎了。天主教会不会因为自家修女觉得犯了这么点儿小糊涂，就放她还俗。不行不行——咱们得有个像样的理由。这位埃迪莎是她亲生父母独生的孩子吗？”
“是。”
“哦，那就好办了。她要是还有兄弟姐妹，你就得把他们杀光，那可是件拖泥带水的麻烦事。好，那么，她是名门独女兼继承人，这门名门同另一门名门——情人家那门——世代血仇。你知道什么叫世仇吧？”
“当然，”艾米丽一脸不屑。“这些我早写进诗里了。”
“那就更好。这场世仇把王国撕成两半，唯有凯普莱特同蒙太古两家联姻，才能弥合。”
“他们不叫这些名字。”
“不要紧。既然如此，这就是一桩国事，牵涉深远，向教宗大人呈文，完全合乎章法。

===Original===

What you want,” Father Cassidy nodded solemnly, “is a dispensation from Rome.”
“Dispensation is a hard word to work into a poem,” said Emily.
“Undoubtedly. But young ladies who *will* write epic poems and who *will* lay the scenes thereof amid times and manners av hundreds av years ago, and *will* choose heroines av a religion quite unknown to them, *must* expect to run up against a few snags.”
“Oh, I think I’ll be able to work it in,” said Emily cheerfully. “And I’m so much obliged to you. You don’t know what a relief it is to my mind. I’ll finish the poem right up now in a few weeks. I haven’t done a thing at it all summer. But then of course I’ve been busy. Ilse Burnley and I have been making a new language.”
“Making a﻿—new﻿—excuse me. *Did* you say *language*?”
“Yes.”
“What’s the matter with English? Isn’t it good enough for you, you incomprehensible little being?”
“Oh, yes. *That* isn’t why we’re making a new one. You see in the spring, Cousin Jimmy got a lot of French boys to help plant the potatoes. I had to help too, and Ilse came to keep me company. And it was so annoying to hear those boys talking French when we couldn’t understand a word of it. They did it just to make us mad. Such jabbering! So Ilse and I just made up our minds we’d invent a new language that *they* couldn’t understand. We’re getting on fine and when the potato picking time comes we’ll be able to talk to each other and those boys won’t be able to understand a word we’re saying. Oh, it will be great fun!”
“I haven’t a doubt. But two girls who will go to all the trouble av inventing a new language just to get square with some poor little French boys﻿—you’re beyond me,” said Father Cassidy, helplessly. “Goodness knows what you’ll be doing when you grow up. You’ll be Red Revolutionists. I tremble for Canada.”
“Oh, it isn’t a trouble﻿—it’s fun. And all the girls in school are just wild because they hear us talking in it and can’t make it out. We can talk secrets right before them.”
“Human nature being what it is, I can see where the fun comes in all right. Let’s hear a sample av your language.”
“*Nat millan O ste dolman bote ta Shrewsbury fernas ta poo litanos*,” said Emily glibly. “That means, ‘Next summer I am going to Shrewsbury woods to pick strawberries.’ I yelled that across the playground to Ilse the other day at recess and oh, how everybody stared.”
“Staring, is it?

===Chinese===

你要的东西，”卡西迪神父郑重点头，“是罗马的特恩。”
“‘特恩’这词儿太难嵌进诗里了，”艾米丽说。
“毫无疑问。可年轻小姐们偏要写史诗，偏要把故事搁在几百年前的年代风俗里，偏要选自己一无所知的宗教里的女主角，那就得准备磕几个绊子。”
“哦，我想我嵌得进去，”艾米丽轻快地说。“真是感激不尽。您不知道我心里松了多大一股劲。几个礼拜我就能把诗写完。一夏天我没动一笔。不过我当然忙来着。我和伊尔丝·伯恩利（Ilse Burnley）在造一种新语言。”
“造一种——新——失敬。你是说语言？”
“是啊。”
“英语怎么得罪你了？你还嫌它不够好，你这莫名其妙的小东西？”
“哦，不。我们造新的，不为那个。是这样的：春天，吉米表哥雇了几个法国男孩帮忙种土豆。我也得帮忙，伊尔丝来给我作伴。那些男孩净讲法国话，我们一个字听不懂，烦死人了。他们就是存心气我们。叽里哵啦！我和伊尔丝一合计：发明一种他们听不懂的新语言。进展好极了，等起土豆的时候，我们就能当着他们说悄悄话，一个字也休想叫他们听懂。哦，多好玩！”
“这我不怀疑。可两个姑娘，肯费这么大劲发明一种语言，就为报复几个可怜的法国小子——你们超出我的理解了，”卡西迪神父束手无策地说。“天知道你们长大了还要干什么。要闹红色革命的。我为加拿大发抖。”
“哦，这不费劲——是好玩。学校里的姑娘们全疯了似的，听我们说又听不懂。我们当着她们的面说秘密话。”
“人性如此，乐趣所在我明白了。让我听听你们语言的样品。”
“Nat millan O ste dolman bote ta Shrewsbury fernas ta poo litanos，”艾米丽流利地说。“意思是：‘明年夏天我要去什鲁斯伯里（Shrewsbury）的林子里采草莓。’前几天课间我在操场那头冲伊尔丝喊的就是这句，哦，人人瞪圆了眼。”
“瞪圆了眼，可不？

===Original===

I should say so. My own poor old eyes are all but dropping out av me head. Let’s hear a bit more av it.”
“*Mo tral li dead seb ad li mo trene. Mo bertral seb mo bertrene das sten dead e ting setra.* *That* means ‘My father is dead and so is my mother. My grandfather and grandmother have been dead a long time.’ We haven’t invented a word for ’dead’ yet. I think I will soon be able to write my poems in our language and then Aunt Elizabeth will not be able to read them if she finds them.”
“Have you written any other poetry besides your epic?”
“Oh, yes﻿—but just short pieces﻿—dozens of them.”
“H’m. Would you be so kind as to let me hear one av them?”
Emily was greatly flattered. And she did not mind letting Father Cassidy hear her precious stuff.
“I’ll recite my last poem,” she said, clearing her throat importantly. “It’s called ‘Evening Dreams.’ ”
Father Cassidy listened attentively. After the first verse a change came over his big brown face, and he began patting his fingertips together. When Emily finished she hung down her lashes and waited tremblingly. What if Father Cassidy said it was no good? No, he wouldn’t be so impolite﻿—but if he bantered her as he had done about her epic﻿—she would know what *that* meant.
Father Cassidy did not speak all at once. The prolonged suspense was terrible to Emily. She was afraid he could not praise and did not want to hurt her feelings by dispraise. All at once her “Evening Dreams” seemed trash and she wondered how she could ever have been silly enough to repeat it to Father Cassidy.
Of course, it *was* trash. Father Cassidy knew that well enough. All the same, for a child like this﻿—and rhyme and rhythm were flawless﻿—and there was one line﻿—just one line﻿—“the light of faintly golden stars”﻿—for the sake of that line Father Cassidy suddenly said,
“Keep on﻿—keep on writing poetry.”
“You mean?”﻿—Emily was breathless.
“I mean you’ll be able to do something by and by. Something﻿—I don’t know how much﻿—but keep on﻿—keep on.”
Emily was so happy she wanted to cry. It was the first word of commendation she had ever received except from her father﻿—and a father might have too high an opinion of one. *This* was different. To the end of her struggle for recognition Emily never forgot Father Cassidy’s “Keep on” and the tone in which he said it.
“Aunt Elizabeth scolds me for writing poetry,” she said wistfully.

===Chinese===

我说也是。我这双可怜的老眼珠子都快瞪出来啦。再来一段。”
“Mo tral li dead seb ad li mo trene. Mo bertral seb mo bertrene das sten dead e ting setra. 意思是：‘我父亲死了，我母亲也死了。我祖父祖母死了很久了。’‘死’字的词我们还没造出来呢。我想，过不了多久我就能用我们的语言写诗了，这样伊丽莎白姨妈捾着了也念不懂。”
“除了史诗，你还写过别的诗吗？”
“哦，写过——不过是些短章——几十首。”
“嗯。可否赏脸让我听一首？”
艾米丽大大地受宠若惊。让卡西迪神父听听她的宝贝，她不介意。
“我背我最新的一首，”她郑重其事地清了清嗓子说。“题目叫《夜梦》。”
卡西迪神父凝神细听。第一节没完，他那张棕色大脸上起了变化，指尖轻轻拍起板来。艾米丽背完，垂下睫毛，瑟瑟地等着。万一卡西迪神父说它不成呢？不，他不会那么失礼——可他要是像方才打趣史诗那样打趣它——她就知道那是什么意思了。
卡西迪神父没有立刻开口。悬着的那一霎对艾米丽真是酷刑。她怕他夸不出口、又不忍心贬。忽然间，她的《夜梦》显得一钱不值，她奇怪自己怎么会傻到背给他听。
当然，它确实不值什么。这一点卡西迪神父清楚得很。可即便如此——出自这样一个孩子——韵和节拍都无懈可击——而且有一行——只此一行——“淡淡金色星辰的光”——就为这一行，卡西迪神父突然说：
“写下去——接着写诗，写下去。”
“您是说——”艾米丽气都屏住了。
“我是说，往后你能成些事。成些事——成多大，我说不准——可要写下去——写下去。”
艾米丽快活得想哭。这是除爸爸以外她生平得到的第一句夸奖——爸爸的话呢，兴许把人看得过高。这一句不同。直到她为成名的奋斗终了，她始终忘不了卡西迪神父这句“写下去”，和他说这话时的声调。
“伊丽莎白姨妈为写诗骂我，”她幽幽地说。

===Original===

“She says people will think I’m as simple as Cousin Jimmy.”
“The path of genius never did run smooth. But have another piece av cake﻿—do, just to show there’s something human about you.”
“*Ve, merry ti. O del re dolman cosey aman ri sen ritter.* *That* means, ‘No, thank you. I must be going home before it gets dark.’ ”
“I’ll drive you home.”
“Oh, no, no. It’s very kind of you”﻿—the English language was quite good enough for Emily now. “But I’d rather *walk*. It’s﻿—it’s﻿—such good exercise.”
“Meaning,” said Father Cassidy with a twinkle in his eye, “that we must keep it from the old lady. Goodbye, and may you always see a happy face in your looking-glass!”
Emily was too happy to be tired on the way home. There seemed to be a bubble of joy in her heart﻿—a shimmering, prismatic bubble. When she came to the top of the big hill and looked across to New Moon, her eyes were satisfied and loving. How beautiful it was, lying embowered in the twilight of the old trees; the tips of the loftiest spruces came out in purple silhouette against the northwestern sky of rose and amber; down behind it the Blair Water dreamed in silver; the Wind Woman had folded her misty bat-wings in a valley of sunset and stillness lay over the world like a blessing. Emily felt sure everything would be all right. Father Cassidy would manage it in some way.
And he had told her to “keep on.”

===Chinese===

“她说人家会当我和吉米表哥一样傻。”
“天才的路从来没有平坦的。可再吃一块蛋糕吧——务必，只当证明你身上也有凡人的一点东西。”
“Ve, merry ti. O del re dolman cosey aman ri sen ritter. 意思是：‘不，谢谢您。我得在天黑前回家。’”
“我驾车送你回去。”
“哦，不，不。您太好了”——眼下英语对艾米丽又够用了。“可我情愿走。走——走路——锻炼身体。”
“意思是，”卡西迪神父眼里闪着一丝狡黠，“咱俩得瞒着老太太。再见啦，愿你的镜子里永远照见一张快活的脸！”
艾米丽一路回家，快活得不知道累。她心里像含着一泡欢喜——一泡亮晶晶、七彩的欢喜。走到大山顶上望见新月庄园时，她的目光又满足又深情。它多美啊，卧在古树四合的暮色里；最高的几株云杉的尖梢，在西北方玫瑰色、琥珀色的天上，衬出紫黑的剪影；它背后，布莱尔沃特在银光里做着梦；风之女把雾蒙蒙的蝙蝠翅膀收拢在落日的山谷里，一片安宁像祝福一样罩着世界。艾米丽拿准一切都会好起来。卡西迪神父自有办法。
而且他对她说了：“写下去。”

## XIX Friends Again / 十九 言归于好
===Original===

Emily listened very anxiously on Monday morning, but “no sound of axe, no ponderous hammer rang” in Lofty John’s bush. That evening on her way home from school, Lofty John himself overtook her in his buggy and for the first time since the night of the apple stopped and accosted her.
“Will ye take a lift, Miss Emily av New Moon?” he said affably.
Emily climbed in, feeling a little bit foolish. But Lofty John looked quite friendly as he clucked to his horse.
“So you’ve clean wiled the heart out av Father Cassidy’s body,” he said. “ ‘The sweetest scrap av a girl I’ve iver seen,’ says he to me. Sure an’ ye might lave the poor prastes alone.”
Emily looked at Lofty John out of the corner of her eye. He did not seem angry.
“Ye’ve put *me* in a nice tight fix av it,” he went on. “I’m as proud as any New Moon Murray av ye all and your Aunt Elizabeth said a number av things that got under my skin. I’ve many an old score to settle with her. So I thought I’d get square by cutting av the bush down. And you had to go and quare me wid me praste bekase av it and now I make no doubt I’ll not be after daring to cut a stick av kindling to warm me shivering carcase without asking lave av the Pope.”
“Oh, Mr. Sullivan, are you going to leave the bush alone?” said Emily breathlessly.
“It all rests with yourself, Miss Emily av New Moon. Ye can’t be after expecting a Lofty John to be too humble. I didn’t come by the name bekase av me makeness.”
“What do you want me to do?”
“First, then, I’m wanting you to let bygones be bygones in that matter av the apple. And be token av the same come over and talk to me now and then as ye did last summer. Sure now, and I’ve missed ye﻿—ye and that spitfire av an Ilse who’s never come aither bekase she thinks I mistrated you.”
“I’ll come of course,” said Emily doubtfully, “if only Aunt Elizabeth will let me.”
“Tell her if she don’t the bush’ll be cut down﻿—ivery last stick av it. That’ll fetch her. And there’s wan more thing. Ye must ask me rale make and polite to do ye the favour av not cutting down the bush. If ye do it pretty enough sure niver a tree will I touch. But if ye don’t down they go, praste or no praste,” concluded Lofty John.
Emily summoned all her wiles to her aid.

===Chinese===

礼拜一早晨，艾米丽提心吊胆地听了一路，高个儿约翰（Lofty John）的林子里却“斧声不闻，重锤不响”。当晚放学回家的路上，高个儿约翰亲自赶着单马车载过了她，还自苹果那晚以来头一回停下车同她搭话。
“新月庄园的艾米丽小姐，捎你一段不？”他和和气气地说。
艾米丽爬上车，觉得自己有点傻。可高个儿约翰朝马哚了个嘴，神色相当友善。
“这么着，你把卡西迪神父（Father Cassidy）的心整个儿迷走啦。”他说。“他对我说：‘我平生见过的顶顶甜的小丫头。’可不，你就不能放过那可怜的神父们。”
艾米丽拿眼角瞟着高个儿约翰。他像是没生气。
“你倒把*我*架在火上烤了。”他接着说。“新月庄园的默里家人自家孩子个个傲气，我比起他们也不差；你伊丽莎白姨妈先前说了好些话，句句扎进我皮肉里。我跟她之间的旧账多着呢。我就想着砍了那林子，好出一口气。可你偏去为我这点事同我的神父闹了一场，如今我毫不怀疑：往后再要砍根引火柴暖暖我这把发抖的老骨头，也少不得先讨罗马教皇的示下了。”
“哦，沙利文先生（Mr. Sullivan），您真打算放过那片林子吗？”艾米丽屏着呼吸说。
“这全看你自己了，新月庄园（New Moon）的艾米丽小姐。你可别指望一个高个儿约翰低三下四。我这名字可不是白得的，凭的就是不低人一头。”
“您要我做什么呢？”
“头一件，苹果那笔旧账，我要你一笔勾销。作为凭据，往后常来同我说说话，像去年夏天那样。说真的，我还怪想你的——想你，也想那炮仗似的伊尔丝，她再没来过，只当我虐待了你。”
“我当然会去，”艾米丽迟疑地说，“只要伊丽莎白姨妈肯放我去。”
“告诉她，她要不放，林子就砍——一根枝子都不留。这一准治得了她。还有一件。你得恭恭敬敬、客客气气地求我赏你这个面子，别砍那林子。你求得好听，我保准一棵树也不碰。求不好，全放倒，神父不神父的不管用。”高个儿约翰下了结语。
艾米丽把她全部的小手腕都召来听用。

===Original===

She clasped her hands, she looked up through her lashes at Lofty John, she smiled as slowly and seductively as she knew how﻿—and Emily had considerable native knowledge of that sort. “Please, Mr. Lofty John,” she coaxed, “won’t you leave me the dear bush I love?”
Lofty John swept off his crumpled old felt hat. “To be sure an’ I will. A proper Irishman always does what a lady asks him. Sure an’ it’s been the ruin av us. We’re at the mercy av the petticoats. If ye’d come and said that to me afore ye’d have had no need av your walk to White Cross. But mind ye keep the rest av the bargin. The reds are ripe and the scabs soon will be﻿—and all the rats have gone to glory.”
Emily flew into the New Moon kitchen like a slim whirlwind.
“Aunt Elizabeth, Lofty John isn’t going to cut down the bush﻿—he told me he wouldn’t﻿—but I have to go and see him sometimes﻿—if you don’t object.”
“I suppose it wouldn’t make much difference to you if I did,” said Aunt Elizabeth. But her voice was not so sharp as usual. She would not confess how much Emily’s announcement relieved her; but it mellowed her attitude considerably. “There’s a letter here for you. I want to know what it means.”
Emily took the letter. It was the first time she had ever received a real letter through the mail and she tingled with the delight of it. It was addressed in a heavy black hand to “Miss Emily Starr, New Moon, Blair Water.” But﻿—
“You opened it!” she cried indignantly.
“Of course I did. You are not going to receive letters I am not to see, Miss. What I want to know is﻿—how comes Father Cassidy to be writing to you﻿—and writing such nonsense?”
“I went to see him Saturday,” confessed Emily, realizing that the cat was out of the bag. “And I asked him if he couldn’t prevent Lofty John from cutting down the bush.”
“Emily﻿—Byrd﻿—Starr!”
“I *told* him I was a Protestant,” cried Emily. “He understands all about it. And he was just like anybody else. I like him *better* than Mr. Dare.”
Aunt Elizabeth did not say much more. There did not seem to be much she *could* say. Besides the bush wasn’t going to be cut down. The bringer of good news is forgiven much. She contented herself with glaring at Emily﻿—who was too happy and excited to mind glares. She carried her letter off to the garret dormer and gloated over the stamp and the superscription a bit before she took out the enclosure.

===Chinese===

她合起双手，隔着睫毛仰起眼望向高个儿约翰，又尽她所会的那样缓缓地、勾人地一笑——这一类的本事，艾米丽天生的储备可观。“求求您，高个儿约翰先生，”她央告道，“我爱的那片亲爱的林子，您就不肯给我留下吗？”
高个儿约翰挥帽摘下他那顶皱巴巴的旧毛帽。“那还用说，肯的。地道的爱尔兰人，凡是小姐开口求的，没有不答应的。可不，我们就是叫这个给害苦了。我们啊，天生落在裙钗手里。你早些来这么一句，也就用不着白走一趟白十字（White Cross）了。可你记着，交易的其余各条也得守。红苹果熟了，锈皮苹果也快了——老鼠嘛，全升天啦。”
艾米丽像一旋细瘦的小旋风，卷进了新月庄园的厨房。
“伊丽莎白姨妈，高个儿约翰不砍林子了——他亲口说的——只是我得时不时去看看他——要是您不反对的话。”
“我反对不反对，对你大概也差不太多。”伊丽莎白姨妈说。可她的嗓音不像平常那么硬。艾米丽带来的这个信儿让她松了多大一口气，她是不会承认的；可她的态度着实软和了不少。“这儿有你一封信。我要知道它是什么意思。”
艾米丽接过信。这是她头一回真正从邮局收到一封信，欢喜得浑身发麻。信封上是遵劲的黑字：“新月庄园，布莱尔沃特，艾米丽·斯塔尔小姐收。”可是——
“您拆过了！”她愤愤地喊。
“当然拆了。往后你收的信，不许有我看不见的，小姐。我想知道的是——卡西迪神父怎么会给你写信——还写的这些胡话？”
“礼拜六我去看他了。”艾米丽坦白了，明白纸已包不住火。“我求他想想办法，别让高个儿约翰砍那片林子。”
“艾米丽——伯德——斯塔尔！”
“我*告诉*过他我是新教徒了。”艾米丽喊道。“这层他全明白。他也就是个寻常人。我喜欢他*胜过*戴尔先生。”
伊丽莎白姨妈没再多说什么。看来她也没多少*可*说的了。再说林子不砍了。报喜的人，多大的过错都可得赦。她只朝艾米丽狠狠瞪了几眼了事——艾米丽正欢喜、正兴奋，全不理会瞪。她把信带到阁楼的老虎窗前，先把邮票和信封上的字迹赏玩了一阵，才抽出信瓢。

===Original===

> “Dear Pearl of Emilys,” wrote Father Cassidy. “I’ve seen our lofty friend and I feel sure your green outpost of fairyland will be saved for your moonlit revels. I know you *do* dance there by light o’ moon when mortals are snoring. I think you’ll have to go through the form of asking Mr. Sullivan to spare those trees, but you’ll find him quite reasonable. It’s all in the knowing how and the time of the moon. How goes the epic and the language? I hope you’ll have no trouble in freeing the ‘Child of the Sea’ from her vows. Continue to be the friend of all good elves, and of
> “Your admiring friend,

**“James Cassidy.**
> “P.S. The B’y sends respects. What word have you for ’cat’ in your language? Sure and you can’t get anything cattier than ’cat’ can you, now?”

===Chinese===

> “亲爱的艾米丽们的明珠，”卡西迪神父写道。“我见过我们那位高个子的朋友了，我确信你那座仙界的绿色前哨保住了，尽够你月下欢舞。我知道凡人鼾声大作时，你*确乎*在月光里跳舞来着。我想你总还得走个过场，求一求沙利文先生饶下那些树，不过你会发现他通情达理得很。诀窍全在会求、还得挑月亮的时辰。史诗和那门语言进展如何？愿你解救‘海之女’脱离誓约时不遇磨难。愿你继续做一切善良精灵的朋友，并且做
> “你满怀钦慕的朋友，

**“詹姆斯·卡西迪（James Cassidy）。**
> “又及：那小子向你致意。你们语言里‘猫’怎么说？可不，你再找不出比‘猫’更像猫的词了，是不是？”

===Original===

Lofty John spread the story of Emily’s appeal to Father Cassidy far and wide, enjoying it as a good joke on himself. Rhoda Stuart said she always knew Emily Starr was a bold thing and Miss Brownell said she would be surprised at *nothing* Emily Starr would do, and Dr. Burnley called her a Little Devil more admiringly than ever, and Perry said she had pluck and Teddy took credit for suggesting it, and Aunt Elizabeth endured, and Aunt Laura thought it might have been worse. But Cousin Jimmy made Emily feel very happy.
“It would have spoiled the garden and broken my heart, Emily,” he told her. “You’re a little darling girl to have prevented it.”
One day a month later, when Aunt Elizabeth had taken Emily to Shrewsbury to fit her out with a winter coat, they met Father Cassidy in a store. Aunt Elizabeth bowed with great stateliness, but Emily put out a slender paw.
“What about the dispensation from Rome?” whispered Father Cassidy.
One Emily was quite horrified lest Aunt Elizabeth should overhear and think she was having sly dealings with the Pope, such as no good Presbyterian half-Murray of New Moon should have. The other Emily thrilled to her toes with the dramatic delight of a secret understanding of mystery and intrigue. She nodded gravely, her eyes eloquent with satisfaction.
“I got it without any trouble,” she whispered back.
“Fine,” said Father Cassidy. “I wish you good luck, and I wish it hard. Goodbye.”
“Farewell,” said Emily, thinking it a word more in keeping with dark secrets than goodbye. She tasted the flavour of that half-stolen interview all the way home, and felt quite as if she were living in an epic herself. She did not see Father Cassidy again for years﻿—he was soon afterwards removed to another parish; but she always thought of him as a very agreeable and understanding person.

===Chinese===

高个儿约翰把艾米丽向卡西迪神父求情的事传得四面八方，还当拿自己开涮的一个好笑话讲。罗达·斯图尔特说，她早就知道艾米丽·斯塔尔是个胆大包天的东西；布朗内尔小姐说，艾米丽·斯塔尔干什么她都*不会*吃惊；伯恩利医生叫她小魔鬼，叫得比先前更带激赏；佩里说她有胆量；泰迪把这主意记在自己头上；伊丽莎白姨妈忍了；劳拉姨妈想，事情本可能更糟。可吉米表哥一句话，让艾米丽快活到了底。
“那林子要是毁了，园子就毁了，我的心也就碎了，艾米丽。”他对她说。“多亏你拦下了，你真是个可爱的小姑娘。”
一个月后的一天，伊丽莎白姨妈带艾米丽去什鲁斯伯里（Shrewsbury）置办冬大衣，在一家铺子里撞见了卡西迪神父。伊丽莎白姨妈极庄重地领首为礼，艾米丽却伸出了一只纤细的爪子。
“罗马的特赦令下来没有？”卡西迪神父悄声问。
这一个艾米丽吓得不行，生怕伊丽莎白姨妈听见，只当她同教皇有什么私下的勾连——新月庄园规规矩矩的长老会半个默里人，哪能干这个。另一个艾米丽却激动到脚趾尖，为一桩秘密的默契、一桩神秘与阴谋的默契，尝到了戏剧般的欢喜。她郑重地点点头，眼睛里满是得意，会说话。
“一帆风顺，拿到了。”她悄声回。
“好。”卡西迪神父说。“祝你走运，真心地祝。再见。”
“后会有期。”艾米丽说，觉得这个词比起“再见”，更配那些黑暗中的秘密。回家的路上，她一路回味着那次半偷来的会面，只觉得自己也活在一部史诗里。此后好几年她没再见过卡西迪神父——不久他就调去了别的教区；可她始终觉得，他是个非常宜人、非常知心的人。

## XX By Aerial Post / 二十 天上邮递
===Original===

> “Dearest Father:
> “My heart is very sore tonight. Mike died this morning. Cousin Jimmy says he must have been poisoned. Oh, Father dear, I felt so bad. He was such a lovely cat. I cried and cried and cried. Aunt Elizabeth was disgusted. She said, ‘You did not make half so much fuss when your father died.’ What a crewel speech. Aunt Laura was nicer but when she said, ‘Don’t cry dear. I will get you another kitten,’ I saw she didn’t understand either. I don’t want another kitten. If I had *millions* of kittens they wouldn’t make up for Mike.
> “Ilse and I buried him in Lofty John’s bush. I am so thankful the ground wasn’t frozen yet. Aunt Laura gave me a shoe box for a coffin, and some pink tissue paper to wrap his poor little body in. And we put a stone over the grave and I said ‘Blessed are the dead who die in the Lord.’ When I told Aunt Laura about it she was horrified and said, ‘Oh, Emily, that was a dreadful thing. You should not have said that over a cat.’ And Cousin Jimmy said, ‘Don’t you think, Laura, that an innocent little dum creature has a share in God? Emily loved him and all love is part of God.’ And Aunt Laura said, ‘Maybe you are right, Jimmy. But I am thankful Elizabeth did not hear her.’
> “Cousin Jimmy may not be all there, but what is there is very nice.
> “But oh, Father, I am so lonesome for Mike tonight. Last night he was here playing with me, so cunning and pretty and *smee*, and now he is cold and dead in Lofty John’s bush.

===Chinese===

> “最亲爱的爸爸：
> “今晚我心里很难过。迈克（Mike）今天早晨死了。吉米表哥说他准是被人毒了。哦，亲爱的爸爸，我难受极了。它是只多可爱的猫啊。我哭了又哭，哭了又哭。伊丽莎白姨妈烦透了。她说：‘你父亲去世的时候，你也没这么大闹过。’多么狠心的话。劳拉姨妈好些，可她说‘别哭了，宝贝。我再给你弄只小猫来’，我就知道她也不明白。我不想要别的小猫。我就是有*一百万只*小猫，也抵不了迈克。
> “我和伊尔丝把它埋在高个儿约翰的林子里了。谢天谢地地还没上冻。劳拉姨妈给我一只鞋盒当棺材，还给了些粉色棉纸，裹它可怜的小身子。我们在坟上放了块石头，我说了一句：‘在主里面而死的人有福了。’我把这告诉劳拉姨妈，她吓了一跳，说：‘哦，艾米丽，那可不得了。你不该对着猫说这个。’吉米表哥说：‘劳拉，难道你不觉得，一只无辜的小哑巴畜生也有一份神性吗？艾米丽爱它，而一切爱都是上帝的一部分。’劳拉姨妈说：‘也许你说得对，吉米。不过幸好伊丽莎白没听见她。’
> “吉米表哥的脑袋里兴许缺些什么，可缺的那点，都顶好。
> “可是，哦，爸爸，今晚我多想迈克呀。昨儿夜里它还在我这儿玩，那么乖、那么美、那么*斯迷*，如今它已经又冷又死地躺在高个儿约翰的林子里了。

===Original===

> “I am here in the garret. The Wind Woman is very sorry about something tonight. She is sying so sadly around the window. And yet the first time I heard her tonight the flash came﻿—I felt as if I had just seen something that happened long, long ago﻿—something so lovely that it hurt me.
> “Cousin Jimmy says there will be a snow storm tonight. I am glad. I like to hear a storm at night. It’s so cozy to snuggle down among the blankets and feel it can’t get at you. Only when I snuggle Aunt Elizabeth says I skwirm. The idea of anyone not knowing the difference between snuggling and skwirming.
> “I am glad we will have snow for Christmas. The Murray dinner is to be at New Moon this year. It is our turn. Last year it was at Uncle Oliver’s but Cousin Jimmy had grippe and couldn’t go so I stayed home with him. I will be right in the thick of it this year and it exites me. I will write you all about it after it is over, dearest.
> “I want to tell you something, Father. I am ashamed of it, but I think I’ll feel better if I tell you all about it. Last Saturday Ella Lee had a birthday party and I was invited. Aunt Elizabeth let me put on my new blue cashmere dress. It is a very pretty dress. Aunt Elizabeth wanted to get a dark brown but Aunt Laura insisted on blue. I looked at myself in my glass and I remembered that Ilse had told me her father told her I would be handsome if I had more colour. So I pinched my cheeks to make them red. I looked ever so much nicer but it didn’t last. Then I took an old red velvet flower that had once been in Aunt Laura’s bonnet and wet it and then rubbed the red on my cheeks. I went to the party and the girls all *looked* at me but nobody said anything, only Rhoda Stuart giggled and giggled. I meant to come home and wash the red off before Aunt Elizabeth saw me. But she took a notion to call for me on her way home from the store. She did not say anything there but when we got home she said, ‘What have you been doing to your face, Emily?’ I told her and I expected an awful scolding, but all she said was, ‘Don’t you know that you have made yourself *cheap*?’ I did know it, too. I had felt that all along although I couldn’t think of the right word for it before. ‘I will never do such a thing again, Aunt Elizabeth,’ I said. ‘You’d better not,’ she said. ‘Go and wash your face this instant.’

===Chinese===

> “我这会儿在阁楼上。风之女今晚为什么事伤心着。她绕着窗子叹息得那么悲。可今晚我头一回听见她时，灵光来了——我觉得自己仿佛刚刚望见了一件很久很久以前发生的事——那么美，美得让我疼。
> “吉米表哥说今夜有暴风雪。我很高兴。我爱夜里听风暴。缩进毯子里，觉着它够不着你，多暖和呀。只是我一缩，伊丽莎白姨妈就说我扭动。竟然有人分不清‘缩’和‘扭动’，真是的。
> “我真高兴圣诞节能有雪。今年默里家的圣诞宴设在新月庄园。轮到我们家了。去年在奥利弗舅舅家，可吉米表哥得了流感去不了，我便留在家里陪他。今年我可要置身漩涡中心了，一想就兴奋。过完了我就把一切写给你，最亲爱的。
> “爸爸，我要告诉你一件事。我为此害臊，可我想全说出来会好受些。上礼拜六艾拉·李（Ella Lee）开生日会，请了我。伊丽莎白姨妈让我穿新的蓝羊绒裙。那裙子很好看。伊丽莎白姨妈本想买深棕的，劳拉姨妈坚持要蓝的。我照了照镜子，想起伊尔丝告诉过我，她父亲说我要是气色好些会很好看。我就掐自己的腮帮子，掐红它们。好看多了，可一会儿就褪了。于是我取了一朵旧的红丝绒花——从前缀在劳拉姨妈帽子上的——蘸湿了，把红色往腮上擦。我去了宴会，女孩们都*盯*着我看，可谁也没说什么，只有罗达·斯图尔特咯咯咯笑个不住。我原打算回家先洗掉，再让伊丽莎白姨妈看见。可她临时起意，从铺子回家路上来接我。当着人她什么也没说，一到家就说：‘你把脸怎么弄的，艾米丽？’我说了，等着挨一顿好骂，可她只说了一句：‘你知不知道你把自己弄得*轻贱*了？’我还真知道。我一路都有这个感觉，只是先前想不出那个恰当的词。‘我再不干这种事了，伊丽莎白姨妈。’我说。‘你最好不干。’她说。‘马上洗你的脸去。’

===Original===

I did and I was not half so pretty but I felt ever so much better. Strange to relate, dear Father, I heard Aunt Elizabeth laughing about it in the pantry to Aunt Laura afterwards. You never can tell what will make Aunt Elizabeth laugh. I am sure it was ever so much funnier when Saucy Sal followed me to prayer meeting last Wednesday night, but Aunt Elizabeth never laughed a bit then. I don’t often go to prayer meeting but Aunt Laura couldn’t go that night so Aunt Elizabeth took me because she doesn’t like to go alone. I didn’t know Sal was following us till just as we got to the church I saw her. I shooed her away but after we went in I suppose Sal sneaked in when someone opened the door and got upstairs into the galery. And just as soon as Mr. Dare began to pray Sal began to yowl. It sounded awful up in that big empty galery. I felt so gilty and miserable. I did not need to paint my face. It was just burning red and Aunt Elizabeth’s eyes glittered feendishly. Mr. Dare prayed a long time. He is deaf, so he did not hear Sal any more than when he sat on her. But everyone else did and the boys giggled. After the prayer Mr. Morris went up to the galery and chased Sal out. We could hear her skrambling over the seats and Mr. Morris after her. I was wild for fear he’d hurt her. I ment to spank her myself with a shingle next day but I did not want her to be kicked. After a long time he got her out of the galery and she tore down the stairs and into the church, up one isle and down the other two or three times as fast as she could go and Mr. Morris after her with a broom. It is awfully funny to think of it now but I did not think it so funny at the time I was so ashamed and so afraid Sal would be hurt.
> “Mr. Morris chased her out at last. When he sat down I made a face at him behind my hymn book. Coming home Aunt Elizabeth said, ‘I hope you have disgraced us enough tonight, Emily Starr. I shall never take you to prayer meeting again.’ I am sorry I disgraced the Murrays but I don’t see how I was to blame and anyway I don’t like prayer meeting because it is dull.
> “But it wasn’t dull that night, dear Father.
> “Do you notice how my spelling is improved? I have thought of such a good plan. I write my letter first and then I look up all the words I’m not sure of and correct them. Sometimes though I think a word is all right when it isn’t.

===Chinese===

我洗了，人不及方才一半好看，心里却痛快多了。说来奇怪，亲爱的爸爸，后来我听见伊丽莎白姨妈在配膳室里对劳拉姨妈笑这事来着。你永远猜不透什么事能叫伊丽莎白姨妈发笑。上礼拜三晚上调皮的萨尔跟我去祷告会，那才叫好笑得多呢，伊丽莎白姨妈却一丝笑意也没有。我不常去祷告会，可那晚劳拉姨妈去不了，伊丽莎白姨妈便带上我，因为她不喜欢一个人去。我一直不知道萨尔跟着，直到快到教堂才看见它。我嘘走了它，可我们进去之后，它准是趁谁开门溜了进去，上了楼座。戴尔先生刚开始祷告，萨尔就嚎开了。在那空荡荡的大楼座上，声音难听极了。我又心虚又难受。脸上倒不用擦红了，本来就烧得通红，伊丽莎白姨妈的眼睛恶狠狠地放光。戴尔先生祷告了老半天。他耳背，听不见萨尔——同他坐在它身上那回一样听不见。别人可都听见了，男孩们直嗤笑。祷告完，莫里斯先生（Mr. Morris）上楼座去赶萨尔。我们听得见它翻越座椅的扑腾声，还有追它的莫里斯先生。我急疯了，生怕他伤了它。我本打算第二天亲手拿木瓦片打它屁股，可我不愿叫人踢它。闹了半天，他总算把它轰出了楼座，它一溜烟冲下楼梯，冲进教堂，一条走道上去、另一条走道下来，飞快地跑了三四趟，莫里斯先生抡着扫帚在后面追。现在想起来可笑得要命，可当时一点也不觉得好笑，我又臊又怕萨尔受伤。
> “莫里斯先生总算把它撵出去了。他坐下时，我在赞美诗后面冲他做了个鬼脸。回家路上伊丽莎白姨妈说：‘但愿你今晚把我们家的脸丢够了，艾米丽·斯塔尔。我再也不带你去祷告会了。’我给默里家丢了脸，很难过，可我不明白这怎么怪我；再说，我也不喜欢祷告会，因为它乏味。
> “可那天晚上倒不乏味，亲爱的爸爸。
> “你注意到我的拼写进步了吧？我想出了一个好法子。先把信写完，再把拿不准的词挨个查了改过来。不过有时候，一个词明明错了，我还当它对呢。

===Original===

> “Ilse and I have given up our language. We fought over the verbs. Ilse didn’t want to have any tenses for the verbs. She just wanted to have a different word altogether for every tense. I said if I was going to make a language it was going to be a proper one and Ilse got mad and said she had enough bother with grammer in English and I could go and make my old language by myself. But that is no fun so I let it go too. I was sorry because it was very interesting and it was such fun to puzzle the other girls in school. We weren’t able to get square with the French boys after all for Ilse had sore throat all through potato picking time and couldn’t come over. It seems to me that life is full of disappointments.
> “We had examinations in school this week. I did pretty well in all except arithmetic. Miss Brownell explained something about the questions but I was busy composing a story in my mind and did not hear her so I got poor marks. The story is called ‘Madge MacPherson’s Secret.’ I am going to buy four sheets of foolscap with my egg money and sew them into a book and write the story in it. I can do what I like with my egg money. I think maybe I’ll write novels when I grow up as well as poetry. But Aunt Elizabeth won’t let me read any novels so how can I find out how to write them? Another thing that worries me, if I do grow up and write a wonderful poem, perhaps people won’t see how wonderful it is.
> “Cousin Jimmy says that a man in Priest Pond says the end of the world is coming soon. I hope it won’t come till I’ve seen everything in it.
> “Poor Elder MacKay has the mumps.
> “I was over sleeping with Ilse the other night because her father was away. Ilse says her prayers now and she said she’d bet me anything she could pray longer than me. I said she couldn’t and I prayed ever so long about everything I could think of and when I couldn’t think of anything more I thought at first I’d begin over again. Then I thought, ‘No, that would not be honerable. A Starr must be honerable.’ So I got up and said ‘You win’ and Ilse never answered. I went around the bed and there she was asleep on her knees. When I woke her up she said we’d have to call the bet off because she could have gone on praying for ever so long if she hadn’t fell asleep.
> “After we got into bed I told her a lot of things I wished afterwards I hadn’t. Secrets.

===Chinese===

> “我和伊尔丝把我们的语言放弃了。为动词吵翻了。伊尔丝不想让动词有时态。她只想每个时态都用一个完全不同的词。我说我要造语言，就得造一门像样的，伊尔丝发起火来，说英语的语法已经够她受的了，我那破语言爱谁做谁做去。可一个人做没意思，我便也撂下了。怪可惜的，它非常有趣，把学校里别的女孩绕糊涂尤其好玩。到底还是没能同法国男孩们算账，因为整个捡土豆的时节伊尔丝嗓子疼，过不来。我觉着，生活里满是失望。
> “这礼拜学校考试了。除了算术，我都考得不错。布朗内尔小姐讲解题目要求来着，可我正忙着在心里构思一个故事，没听见，分数就糟了。故事叫《玛奇·麦克弗森的秘密》。我要用鸡蛋钱买四大张稿纸，缝成一本簿子，把故事写进去。鸡蛋钱随我怎样花都行。我想也许长大了，除了诗，我还要写小说。可伊丽莎白姨妈不许我读小说，我怎么知道小说怎么写呢？还有一桩心事：万一我真长大了，写出一首了不起的诗，人们兴许看不出它多了不起呢。
> “吉米表哥说，普里斯特池塘有个人说世界末日快到了。但愿等我把世界看遍了它再来。
> “可怜的麦凯长老（Elder MacKay）得了腮腺炎。
> “前几天夜里我睡在伊尔丝家，因为她父亲出诊去了。伊尔丝如今做祷告了，她说敢同我打任何赌，她祷告得比我久。我说她赢不了，我便把想得起来的一切都祷告了个天长地久，什么都想遍了的时候，起初想从头再来一遍。随后又想：‘不行，那不光彩。斯塔尔家的人必须光彩。’于是我起身说‘你赢了’，伊尔丝却没应声。我绕到床那边一看，她跪着睡着了。我把她叫醒，她说这赌得算无效，因为她要不是睡着，还能一直祷告下去呢。
> “上了床，我对她讲了许多事，后来懊悔讲了。秘密。

===Original===

> “The other day in history class Miss Brownell read that Sir Walter Raleigh had to lie in the Tower for fourteen years. Perry said ‘Wouldn’t they let him get up sometimes?’ Then Miss Brownell punished him for impertinence, but Perry was in earnest. Ilse was mad at Miss Brownell for whipping Perry and mad at Perry for asking such a fool question as if he didn’t know anything. But Perry says he is going to write a history book some day that won’t have such puzzling things in it.
> “I am finishing the Disappointed House in my mind. I’m furnishing the rooms like flowers. I’ll have a rose room all pink and a lily room all white and silver and a pansy room, blue and gold. I wish the Disappointed House could have a Christmas. It never has any Christmasses.
> “Oh, Father, I’ve just thought of something nice. When I grow up and write a great novel and make lots of money, I will buy the Disappointed House and finish it. Then it won’t be Disappointed any more.
> “Ilse’s Sunday School teacher, Miss Willeson, gave her a Bible for learning two hundred verses. But when she took it home her father laid it on the floor and kicked it out in the yard. Mrs. Simms says a judgment will come on him but nothing has happened yet. The poor man is warped. That is why he did such a wicked thing.
> “Aunt Laura took me to old Mrs. Mason’s funeral last Wednesday. I like funerals. They are so dramatic.
> “My pig died last week. It was a *great finanshul loss* to me. Aunt Elizabeth says Cousin Jimmy fed it too well. I suppose I should not have called it after Lofty John.
> “We have maps to draw in school now. Rhoda Stuart always gets the most marks. Miss Brownell doesn’t know that Rhoda just puts the map up against a window pane and the paper over it and copies it off. I like drawing maps. Norway and Sweden look like a tiger with mountains for stripes and Ireland looks like a little dog with its back turned on England, and its paws held up against its breast, and Africa looks like a big pork ham. Australia is a lovely map to draw.
> “Ilse is getting on real well in school now. She says she isn’t going to have me beating her. She can learn like the dickins, as Perry says, when she tries, and she has won the silver metal for Queen’s County. The W.C.T.U. in Charlottetown gave it for the best reciter. They had the contest in Shrewsbury and Aunt Laura took Ilse because Dr.

===Chinese===

> “前几天历史课上，布朗内尔小姐念到沃尔特·雷利爵士（Sir Walter Raleigh）不得不在伦敦塔里躺十四年。佩里说：‘他们就不让他偶尔起来一下吗？’布朗内尔小姐罚了他，说他放肆，可佩里是当真的。伊尔丝气布朗内尔小姐鞭佩里，又气佩里问出这么个傻问题，好像他什么都不懂似的。可佩里说，他往后要写一本历史书，里面绝不放这种叫人糊涂的事。
> “我在心里继续盖失望屋。我把各间屋子布置得像花。我要一间全粉红的玫瑰屋，一间全白全银的百合屋，一间蓝与金的三色茧屋。真希望失望屋能过一个圣诞。它从来没过过圣诞。
> “哦，爸爸，我刚想起一件美事。等我长大写出伟大的小说、挣了大钱，我就把失望屋买下来，把它盖完。那它就再也不失望了。
> “伊尔丝的主日学校老师威尔森小姐（Miss Willeson）因为她背会了两百节经文，奖了她一部圣经。可她拿回家，她父亲把它搁在地上，一脚踢进了院子。西姆斯太太说上天会降罚于他，可至今还没降。可怜的人，他是扭曲了。所以他才干出这样缺德的事。
> “上礼拜三劳拉姨妈带我去参加了老梅森太太（Mrs. Mason）的葬礼。我喜欢葬礼。它们多富戏味。
> “我的猪上礼拜死了。对我是*巨大的财正损失*。伊丽莎白姨妈说是吉米表哥把它喂得太好了。大概我不该拿高个儿约翰的名字叫它。
> “学校现在要画地图了。罗达·斯图尔特总得最高分。布朗内尔小姐不知道，罗达是把地图贴在窗玻璃上、纸蒙在上面描的。我喜欢画地图。挪威和瑞典像一只老虎，条纹是山；爱尔兰像一只小狗，背冲着英格兰，两只爪子抱在胸前；非洲像一只大猪腿。澳大利亚画起来最可爱。
> “伊尔丝如今在学校学得真好。她说她可不打算让我压过她。她用起功来学得飞快，佩里这么说的，她还赢得了王后县（Queen's County）的银质奖章。是夏洛特顿（Charlottetown）的基督教妇女禁酒联合会（W.C.T.U.）颁给最佳朗诵者的。比赛在什鲁斯伯里举行，劳拉姨妈带伊尔丝去的，因为伯恩利医生

===Original===

Burnley wouldn’t and Ilse won it. Aunt Laura told Dr. Burnley when he was here one day that he ought to give Ilse a good education. He said ‘I’m not going to waste money educating any she-thing.’ And he looked black as a thunder cloud. Oh, I wish Dr. Burnley would love Ilse. I’m so glad *you* loved *me*, Father.

> “Dear Father: We had our school examination today. It was a great occasion. Almost everybody was there except Dr. Burnley and Aunt Elizabeth. All the girls wore their best dresses but me. I knew Ilse had nothing to wear but her shabby old last winter’s plaid that is too short for her, so to keep her from feeling bad, I put on my old brown dress, too. Aunt Elizabeth did not want to let me do it at first because New Moon Murrays should be well dressed but when I explained about Ilse she looked at Aunt Laura and then said I might.
> “Rhoda Stuart made fun of Ilse and me but I heaped coals of fire on her head. (That is what is called a figure of speech.) She got stuck in her recitation. She had left the book home and nobody else knew the piece but me. At first I looked at her triumphantly. But then a queer feeling came into me and I thought ‘What would I feel like if I was stuck before a big crowd of people like this? And besides the honour of the school is at stake,’ so I whispered it to her because I was quite close. She got through the rest all right. The strange thing is, dear Father, that now I don’t feel any more as if I hated her. I feel quite kindly to her and it is much nicer. It is uncomfortable to hate people.

===Chinese===

不肯去——伊尔丝赢了。有一天伯恩利医生来的时候，劳拉姨妈对他说，他该让伊尔丝受个好教育。他说：‘我可不在什么母玩意儿身上浪费教育的钱。’脸黑得像雷雨云。哦，我真希望伯恩利医生能爱伊尔丝。我多庆幸*你*爱过*我*呀，爸爸。

> “亲爱的爸爸：今天我们学校考试了。那是件大事。差不多人人都到了，除了伯恩利医生和伊丽莎白姨妈。所有女孩都穿了最好的裙子，只我没穿。我知道伊尔丝除了她那身旧得寒酸的去年格子呢没的穿，裙子还短了，为了不叫她难受，我也穿了旧的棕裙。伊丽莎白姨妈起先不肯，因为新月庄园的默里家人出门该穿得体面，可我讲了伊尔丝的情形，她看了看劳拉姨妈，就准了。
> “罗达·斯图尔特取笑我和伊尔丝，可我把炭火堆在了她头上。（这就是所谓的修辞。）她朗诵到一半卡了壳。书落在家里了，全会场只有我一个人会那篇。起初我得胜地望着她。可随后我心里涌起一种怪怪的感觉，我想：‘要是换我当着这么一大群人卡了壳，我什么滋味？再说学校的荣誉攸关呢。’于是我——我正好坐在她近旁——把词悄悄递给了她。她顺顺当当朗诵完了剩下的。奇怪的是，亲爱的爸爸，如今我再也不觉得恨她了。我对她心里挺和善，这样舒服多了。恨人是不舒服的。

===Original===

> “Christmas is over. It was pretty nice. I never saw so many good things cooked all at once. Uncle Wallace and Aunt Eva and Uncle Oliver and Aunt Addie and Aunt Ruth were here. Uncle Oliver didn’t bring any of his children and I was much disappointed. We had Dr. Burnley and Ilse too. Everyone was dressed up. Aunt Elizabeth wore her black satin dress with a pointed lace collar and cap. She looked quite handsome and I was proud of her. You like your relations to look well even if you don’t like *them*. Aunt Laura wore her brown silk and Aunt Ruth had on a grey dress. Aunt Eva was *very* elegant. Her dress had a train. But it smelled of moth balls.
> “I had on my blue cashmere and wore my hair tied with blue ribbons, and Aunt Laura let me wear mother’s blue silk sash with the pink daisies on it that she had when she was a little girl at New Moon. Aunt Ruth sniffed when she saw me. She said, ‘You have grown a good deal, Em’ly. I hope you are a better girl.’
> “But she *didn’t* hope it (really). I saw that quite plain. Then she told me my bootlace was untyed.
> “ ‘She looks better,’ said Uncle Oliver. ‘I wouldn’t wonder if she grew up into a strong, healthy girl after all.’
> “Aunt Eva sighed and shook her head. Uncle Wallace didn’t say anything but shook hands with me. His hand was as cold as a fish. When we went out to the sitting room for dinner I stepped on Aunt Eva’s train and I could hear some stitches rip somewhere. Aunt Eva pushed me away and Aunt Ruth said, ‘What a very awkward child you are, Emily.’ I stepped behind Aunt Ruth and stuck out my tongue at her. Uncle Oliver makes a noise eating his soup. We had all the good silver spoons out. Cousin Jimmy carved the turkeys and he gave me two slices of the breast because he knows I like the white meat best. Aunt Ruth said ‘When I was a little girl the wing was good enough for me,’ and Cousin Jimmy put *another* white slice on my plate. Aunt Ruth didn’t say anything more then till the carving was done, and then she said, ‘I saw your school teacher in Shrewsbury last Saturday, Em’ly, and she did not give me a very good account of you. If you were *my* daughter I would expect a different report.’
> “ ‘I am very glad I am not your daughter,’ I said in my mind. I didn’t say it out loud of course but Aunt Ruth said, ‘Please do not look so sulky when I speak to you, Em’ly.’

===Chinese===

> “圣诞过完了。挺好的。我从没一次见过做这么多好菜。华莱士舅舅和伊娃舅妈（Aunt Eva）、奥利弗舅舅和艾迪舅妈（Aunt Addie）、露丝姨妈都来了。奥利弗舅舅一个孩子也没带来，我失望极了。伯恩利医生和伊尔丝也在我们中间。人人穿戴一新。伊丽莎白姨妈穿了她的黑缎子裙，配尖花边领和便帽。她看着相当好看，我为她骄傲。亲人哪怕你不喜欢*他们*，你也喜欢他们看着体面。劳拉姨妈穿了她的棕色绸裙，露丝姨妈一身灰。伊娃舅妈*非常*高雅。她的裙子带拖裾。可是一股樟脑丸味。
> “我穿着蓝羊绒裙，头发上系蓝缎带，劳拉姨妈还让我系了母亲的蓝丝腰带，上面缀着粉雏菊，是母亲小时候在新月庄园用过的。露丝姨妈一见我就吸鼻子。她说：‘你长高了不少，艾米丽。愿你更乖了些。’
> “可她（其实）并*不*愿。这一点我看得分明。接着她告诉我靴带松了。
> “‘她气色好些了。’奥利弗舅舅说。‘说不定她到底会长成个结结实实的健康姑娘呢。’
> “伊娃舅妈叹气摇头。华莱士舅舅没说话，只同我握了握手。他的手冷得像鱼。我们出去到起居室吃晚饭时，我一脚踩上伊娃舅妈的拖裾，听得见不知哪里绷开了几针。伊娃舅妈把我推开，露丝姨妈说：‘你这孩子多笨手笨脚呀，艾米丽。’我绕到露丝姨妈背后，朝她吐了吐舌头。奥利弗舅舅喝汤出声。我们的好银匙全摆出来了。吉米表哥片火鸡，给了我两片胸肉，因为他知道我最爱吃白肉。露丝姨妈说：‘我做小姑娘的时候，有只翅膀就不错了。’吉米表哥又往我盘子里搭了*一片*白肉。切肉切完之前露丝姨妈没再说什么，切完了她说：‘上礼拜六我在什鲁斯伯里看见你的老师了，艾米丽，她可没说你的好话。你要是*我*的女儿，我可指望另一份评语。’
> “‘我真庆幸我不是你的女儿。’我在心里说。当然没说出声，可露丝姨妈说：‘我同你说话，请不要哭丧着脸，艾米丽。’

===Original===

And Uncle Wallace said, ‘It is a pity she has such an unattractive expression.’
> “ ‘*You* are conceited and domineering and stingy,’ I said, still in my mind. ‘I heard Dr. Burnley say you were.’
> “ ‘I see there is an ink-stain on her finger,’ said Aunt Ruth. (I had been writing a poem before dinner.)
> “And then a most surprising thing happened. Relations are always surprising you. Aunt Elizabeth spoke up and said, ‘*I do wish, Ruth, that you and Wallace would leave that child alone.*’ I could hardly believe my ears. Aunt Ruth looked annoyed but she *did* leave me alone after that and only sniffed when Cousin Jimmy slipped a bit more white meat on my plate.
> “After that the dinner was nice. And when they got as far as the pudding they all began to talk and it was splendid to listen to. They told stories and jokes about the Murrays. Even Uncle Wallace laughed and Aunt Ruth told some things about Great-Aunt Nancy. They were sarcastic but they were interesting. Aunt Elizabeth opened Grandfather Murray’s desk and took out an old poem that had been written to Aunt Nancy *by a lover* when she was young and Uncle Oliver read it. Great-Aunt Nancy must have been very beautiful. I wonder if anyone will ever write a poem to me. If I could have a bang somebody might. I said, ‘Was Great Aunt Nancy really as pretty as that?’ and Uncle Oliver said, ‘They say she was 70 years ago’ and Uncle Wallace said, ‘She hangs on well﻿—she’ll see the century mark yet,’ and Uncle Oliver said, ‘Oh, she’s got so in the habit of living she’ll never die.’
> “Dr. Burnley told a story I didn’t understand. Uncle Wallace hawhawed right out and Uncle Oliver put his napkin up to his face. Aunt Addie and Aunt Eva looked at each other sidewise and then at their plates and smiled a little bit. Aunt Ruth seemed offended and Aunt Elizabeth looked *coldly* at Dr. Burnley and said, ‘I think you forget that there are children present.’ Dr. Burnley said, ‘I beg your pardon, Elizabeth,’ *very* politely. He can speak with a *grand air* when he likes. He is very handsome when he is dressed up and shaved. Ilse says she is proud of him even if he hates her.
> “After dinner was over the presents were given. That is a Murray tradishun. We never have stockings or trees but a big bran pie is passed all around with the presents buried in it and ribbons hanging out with names on them. It was fun.

===Chinese===

华莱士舅舅说：‘可惜她这副表情不讨人喜欢。’
> “‘*您*又自负、又专横、又小气。’我还是在心里说。‘我听伯恩利医生这么说过您。’
> “‘她手指上有块墨水印。’露丝姨妈说。（晚饭前我写了一首诗来着。）
> “接着发生了一件顶顶出人意料的事。亲人们总爱吓你一跳。伊丽莎白姨妈开了口：‘*露丝，我真心希望，你和华莱士放过这孩子吧。*’我几乎不敢相信自己的耳朵。露丝姨妈一脸不痛快，可她后来*确实*放过我了，只在吉米表哥再往我盘子里塞白肉时吸了吸鼻子。
> “打那以后，晚饭就美了。吃到布丁那段，大家全聊开了，听得人痛快极了。他们讲默里家的旧事和笑话。连华莱士舅舅都笑了，露丝姨妈还讲了些南希姨婆的事。话里带刺，可有意思。伊丽莎白姨妈打开默里外祖父的书桌，取出一首旧诗——是南希姨婆年轻时*一位追求者*写给她的——奥利弗舅舅念了。南希姨婆当年一定非常美。不知将来会不会有人给我写诗。要是我能留刘海，兴许会有。我问：‘南希姨婆真有那么美吗？’奥利弗舅舅说：‘据说70年前是。’华莱士舅舅说：‘她熬得住——准能熬到百岁。’奥利弗舅舅说：‘啐，她活成习惯了，死不了啦。’
> “伯恩利医生讲了个故事，我没听懂。华莱士舅舅哈哈大笑，奥利弗舅舅用餐巾掩住了脸。艾迪舅妈和伊娃舅妈斜着眼对视了一下，又看看各自的盘子，微微一笑。露丝姨妈像是受了冒犯，伊丽莎白姨妈*冷冷地*瞪着伯恩利医生说：‘我想你忘了席上还有孩子。’伯恩利医生说：‘请你原谅，伊丽莎白。’*非常*彬彬有礼。他高兴起来，说话自有*一派堂皇气度*。他盛装修面之后相当英俊。伊尔丝说，哪怕他恨她，她也为他骄傲。
> “晚饭吃完就发礼物。这是默里家的传通。我们从来不用袜子也不架圣诞树，而是端上来一大盆麸子馅饼，礼物都埋在里面，缎带露在外面，拴着名字。好玩极了。

===Original===

My relations all gave me useful presents except Aunt Laura. She gave me a bottle of perfume. I love it. I love nice smells. Aunt Elizabeth does not approve of perfumes. She gave me a new apron but I am thankful to say not a baby one. Aunt Ruth gave me a New Testament and said ‘Em’ly, I hope you will read a portion of that every day until you have read it through,’ and I said, ‘Why, Aunt Ruth, I’ve read the whole New Testament a dozen times (and so I have) I *love* Revelations.’ (And I *do*. When I read the verse ‘and the twelve gates were twelve pearls’ I just *saw* them and the flash came.) ‘The Bible is not to be read as a story book,’ Aunt Ruth said coldly. Uncle Wallace and Aunt Eva gave me a pair of black mits and Uncle Oliver and Aunt Addie gave me a whole dollar in nice new silver dimes and Cousin Jimmy gave me a hair ribbon. Perry had left a silk bookmark for me. He had to go home to spend Christmas day with his Aunt Tom at Stovepipe Town but I saved a lot of nuts and raisins for him. I gave him and Teddy handkerchiefs (Teddy’s was a *little* the nicest) and I gave Ilse a hair ribbon. I bought them myself out of my egg money. (I will not have any more egg money for a long time because my hen has stopped laying.) Everybody was happy and once Uncle Wallace smiled right at me. I did not think him so ugly when he smiled.
> “After dinner Ilse and I played games in the kitchen and Cousin Jimmy helped us make taffy. We had a big supper but nobody could eat much because they had had such a dinner. Aunt Eva’s head ached and Aunt Ruth said she didn’t see why Elizabeth made the sausages so rich. But the rest were good humored and Aunt Laura kept things pleasant. She is good at making things pleasant. And after it was all over Uncle Wallace said (this is another Murray tradishun) ‘Let us think for a few moments of those who have gone before.’ I liked the way he said it﻿—very solemnly and kind. It was one of the times when I am glad the blood of the Murrays flows in my vains. And I thought of *you*, darling Father, and Mother and poor little Mike and Great-great-Grandmother Murray, and of my old account book that Aunt Elizabeth burned, because it seemed just like a person to me. And then we all joined hands and sung ‘For Auld Lang Syne’ before they went home. I didn’t feel like a stranger among the Murrays any more.

===Chinese===

亲人们送我的礼物件件实用，只有劳拉姨妈例外。她送了我一瓶香水。我爱它。我爱好闻的气味。伊丽莎白姨妈不赞成香水。她送了我一条新围裙，不过谢天谢地，不是娃娃款。露丝姨妈送我一本《新约》，说：‘艾米丽，望你每天读一段，把它通读一遍。’我说：‘啐，露丝姨妈，整部《新约》我读了一打了（真的）我*爱*《启示录》。’（我*确实*爱。读到‘十二个门是十二颗珍珠’那节，我简直*看见*了它们，灵光就来了。）‘圣经不是当故事书读的。’露丝姨妈冷冷地说。华莱士舅舅和伊娃舅妈送我一双黑露指手套，奥利弗舅舅和艾迪舅妈送我整整一块钱，全是崭新的银角子，吉米表哥送了我一条发带。佩里给我留下了一枚丝质书签。他得回炉筒镇同汤姆婶过圣诞，可我给他留了好多坚果和葡萄干。我送了他和泰迪一人一方手绢（泰迪那方*稍稍*好看些），送了伊尔丝一条发带。都是我拿鸡蛋钱自己买的。（往后好一阵子我不会有鸡蛋钱了，因为我的母鸡停产了。）人人都快活，有一回华莱士舅舅还朝我笑了笑。他笑的时候，我倒不觉得他那么丑了。
> “饭后我和伊尔丝在厨房做游戏，吉米表哥帮我们熬太妃糖。晚饭后又上了丰盛的一餐，可谁也吃不动了，晚饭吃得太撑。伊娃舅妈头疼，露丝姨妈说她不明白，伊丽莎白干嘛把香肠做得那么腻。其余的人都和和气气，劳拉姨妈让样样事情都熨帖。她顶会叫事情熨帖。末了，华莱士舅舅说（这是默里家的又一条传通）：‘让我们静默片刻，怀念先行去的故人。’我喜欢他说这话的样子——庄重，又和善。这是默里家的血在我血管里流淌、让我觉得庆幸的那些时刻之一。我想起了*你*，亲爱的爸爸，还有母亲，可怜的小迈克，高外曾祖母默里，还有被伊丽莎白姨妈烧掉的那本旧账本——它在我心里就像一个人。随后大家手拉着手，唱了《友谊地久天长》（For Auld Lang Syne），才各自回家。在默里家人中间，我再也不觉得自己是外人了。

===Original===

Aunt Laura and I stood out on the porch to watch them go. Aunt Laura put her arm around me and said, ‘Your mother and I used to stand like this long ago, Emily, to watch the Christmas guests go away.’ The snow creaked and the bells rang back through the trees and the frost on the pighouse roof sparkled in the moonlight. And it was all so lovely (the bells and the frost and the big shining white night) that *the flash* came and that was best of all.”

===Chinese===

劳拉姨妈同我站在门廊上看他们离去。劳拉姨妈搂住我说：‘很久以前，你母亲同我也这样站着，艾米丽，看圣诞的客人走远。’积雪咯吱作响，铃声穿过树林回荡，猪舍屋顶的霜在月光下闪亮。这一切那么可爱（铃声、寒霜，还有这亮堂堂的茫茫白夜），*灵光*来了——这是一切之中最好的。”

## XXI Romantic but Not Comfortable / 二十一 浪漫而不舒服
===Original===

A certain thing happened at New Moon because Teddy Kent paid Ilse Burnley a compliment one day and Emily Starr didn’t altogether like it. Empires have been overturned for the same reason.
Teddy was skating on Blair Water and taking Ilse and Emily out in turns for “slides.” Neither Ilse nor Emily had skates. Nobody was sufficiently interested in Ilse to buy skates for her, and as for Emily, Aunt Elizabeth did not approve of girls skating. New Moon girls had never skated. Aunt Laura had a revolutionary idea that skating would be good exercise for Emily and would, moreover, prevent her from wearing out the soles of her boots sliding. But neither of these arguments was sufficient to convince Aunt Elizabeth, in spite of the thrifty streak that came to her from the Burnleys. The latter, however, caused her to issue an edict that Emily was not to “slide.” Emily took this very hardly. She moped about in a woebegone fashion and she wrote to her father, “I *hate* Aunt Elizabeth. She is so unjust. She never plays fair.” But one day Dr. Burnley stuck his head in at the door of the New Moon kitchen and said gruffly, “What’s this I hear about you not letting Emily slide, Elizabeth?”
“She wears out the soles of her boots,” said Elizabeth.
“Boots be﻿—” the doctor remembered that ladies were present just in time. “Let the creature slide all she wants to. She ought to be in the open air all the time. She ought”﻿—the doctor stared at Elizabeth ferociously﻿—“she ought to sleep out of doors.”
Elizabeth trembled lest the doctor should go on to insist on this unheard-of proceeding. She knew he had absurd ideas about the proper treatment of consumptives and those who might become such. She was glad to appease him by letting Emily stay out-of-doors in daytime and do what seemed good to her, if only he would say no more about staying out all night too.
“He is much more concerned about Emily than he is about his own child,” she said bitterly to Laura.
“Ilse is too healthy,” said Aunt Laura with a smile. “If she were a delicate child Allan might forgive her for﻿—for being her mother’s daughter.”
“S﻿—s﻿—h,” said Aunt Elizabeth. But she “s﻿—s﻿—s﻿—h’d” too late. Emily, coming into the kitchen, had heard Aunt Laura and puzzled over what she had said all day in school. Why had Ilse to be forgiven for being her mother’s daughter? Everybody was her mother’s daughter, wasn’t she?

===Chinese===

新月庄园出了件事，起因是泰迪·肯特（Teddy Kent）有一天夸了伊尔丝·伯恩利（Ilse Burnley）一句，而艾米丽·斯塔尔不完全乐意。帝国的倾覆，也常常是为着同样的缘故。
泰迪在布莱尔沃特（Blair Water）上滑冰，轮流拉着伊尔丝和艾米丽“打滑溜”。伊尔丝和艾米丽都没有冰鞋。没有谁对伊尔丝关心到肯给她买冰鞋；至于艾米丽，伊丽莎白姨妈不赞成女孩子滑冰。新月庄园（New Moon）的姑娘家从来不滑冰。劳拉姨妈（Aunt Laura）有个革命性的想法：滑冰对艾米丽是顶好的锻炼，还能省得她蹭冰玩把靴底磨穿。可这两条道理，任哪条也说不转伊丽莎白姨妈，虽然她身上流着伯恩利家节俭的血。倒是这节俭的血，促使她下了一道敕令：艾米丽不许“打滑溜”。艾米丽咽不下这口气。她哭丧着脸转来转去，还写信给爸爸：“我恨伊丽莎白姨妈。她这么不公道。她从来不肯按规矩玩。”可有一天，伯恩利大夫把脑袋探进新月庄园厨房门，粗声粗气地说：“听说你不让艾米丽打滑溜，伊丽莎白，怎么回事？”
“她把靴底都磨穿了，”伊丽莎白说。
“靴子见——”大夫赶在节骨眼上想起有女士在场，把后半截咽了回去。“随她滑个够。她就该成天待在露天里。她就该——”大夫恶狠狠地瞪着伊丽莎白——“她就该睡在户外。”
伊丽莎白发着抖，只怕大夫接着坚持这闻所未闻的做法。她知道，对肺痨病人和可能成肺痨的人该怎么料理，他有一套荒唐主张。她庆幸能哄住他：白天放艾米丽待在露天、随她高兴干什么，只要他别再提整夜待在外头。
“他对艾米丽，比对他自己的孩子上心多了，”她酸酸地对劳拉说。
“伊尔丝太皮实了，”劳拉姨妈含笑说。“她要是个娇弱的孩子，艾伦兴许就肯原谅她——原谅她是她妈妈的女儿了。”
“嘘——嘘——”伊丽莎白姨妈说。可她“嘘”晚了。艾米丽正进厨房，听见了劳拉姨妈的话，在学校里为这话纳闷了一整天。为什么伊尔丝是妈妈的女儿就得被原谅？人人都是妈妈的女儿，不是吗？

===Original===

Wherein did the crime consist? Emily worried over it so much that she was inattentive to her lessons and Miss Brownell raked her fore and aft with sarcasm.
It is time we got back to Blair Water where Teddy was just bringing Emily in from a glorious spin clear round the great circle of ice. Ilse was waiting for her turn, on the bank. Her golden cloud of hair aureoled her face and fell in a shimmering wave over her forehead under the faded, little red tam she wore. Ilse’s clothes were always faded. The stinging kiss of the wind had crimsoned her cheeks and her eyes were glowing like amber pools with fire in their hearts. Teddy’s artistic perception saw her beauty and rejoiced in it.
“Isn’t Ilse handsome?” he said.
Emily was not jealous. It never hurt her to hear Ilse praised. But somehow she did not like this. Teddy was looking at Ilse altogether *too* admiringly. It was all, Emily believed, due to that shimmering fringe on Ilse’s white brows.
“If *I* had a bang Teddy might think me handsome too,” she thought resentfully. “Of course, black hair isn’t as pretty as gold. But my forehead is too high﻿—everybody says so. And I *did* look nice in Teddy’s picture because he drew some curls over it.”
The matter rankled. Emily thought of it as she went home over the sheen of the crusted snowfield slanting to the light of the winter sunset, and she could not eat her supper because she did not have a bang. All her long hidden yearning for a bang seemed to come to a head at once. She knew there was no use in coaxing Aunt Elizabeth for one. But when she was getting ready for bed that night she stood on a chair so that she could see little Emily-in-the-glass, then lifted the curling ends of her long braid and laid them over her forehead. The effect, in Emily’s eyes at least, was very alluring. She suddenly thought﻿—what if she cut a bang herself? It would take only a minute. And once done what could Aunt Elizabeth do? She would be very angry and doubtless inflict some kind of punishment. But the bang would be there﻿—at least until it grew out long.
Emily, her lips set, went for the scissors. She unbraided her hair and parted the front tresses. Snip﻿—snip﻿—went the scissors. Glistening locks fell at her feet. In a minute Emily had her long-desired bang. Straight across her brows fell the lustrous, softly curving fringe. It changed the whole character of her face.

===Chinese===

罪过在哪儿呢？艾米丽为这事愁得上课走了神，布朗内尔小姐（Miss Brownell）把她从头到尾犁了一遍，句句带刺。
该回到布莱尔沃特了。泰迪刚拉着艾米丽沿着大冰圈痛痛快快溜了一整圈回来。伊尔丝在岸上等着轮到自己。她金色的云发像一圈光轮罩着脸，从褪了色的小红贝雷帽底下泼下一绺亮闪闪的波，垂在额前。伊尔丝的衣裳总是褪色的。风蜇人的吻把她两颊吹得通红，眼睛亮得像两汪烧着火的琥珀池。泰迪的艺术家眼光看见了这份美，为之心喜。
“伊尔丝是不是很俊？”他说。
艾米丽不吃醋。夸伊尔丝从来伤不着她。可不知怎么，她不喜欢这个。泰迪望着伊尔丝，那眼神佩服得——太过分了。艾米丽认定，全怪伊尔丝白眉毛上那道闪闪的刘海。
“我要是有刘海，泰迪兴许也当我俊，”她愤愤地想。“当然，黑头发不如金头发好看。可我的脑门太高了——人人都这么说。泰迪那张画里我确实好看，因为他在脑门上画了几绺鬈发。”
这事在心里扎了根。艾米丽踏着冻雪原的光泽走回家——雪原斜斜地映着冬日西沉的光——一路想的就是它；晚饭也吃不下，只因为没有刘海。她埋藏了好久的对刘海的渴望，仿佛一齐涌上了心头。她知道求伊丽莎白姨妈给剪刘海是白搭。可当晚梳洗上床的时候，她站上椅子，好照见小小的镜中艾米丽（Emily-in-the-glass），随后把长辫子末梢的鬈发撩起来，覆在额前。那效果，至少在艾米丽眼里，迷人极了。她忽然想——要是自己动手剪个刘海呢？只要一分钟。剪成了，伊丽莎白姨妈还能怎样？她会大发雷霆，多半还要罚。可刘海就在那儿了——至少，在它慢慢长长之前都在。
艾米丽捉紧嘴唇，去拿了剪子。她解开辫子，分出前额的发绺。剪子咔嚓——咔嚓——亮闪闪的发绺落在脚边。一分钟，艾米丽就有了她渴望多年的刘海。柔柔弯弯、亮泽泽的一排，齐眉覆下。它把她整张脸的神韵都换了。

===Original===

It made it arch, provocative, elusive. For one brief moment Emily gazed at her reflection in triumph.
And then﻿—sheer terror seized her. Oh, what had she done? How angry Aunt Elizabeth would be! Conscience suddenly awoke and added its pang also. She had been wicked. It was wicked to cut a bang when Aunt Elizabeth had forbidden it. Aunt Elizabeth had given her a home at New Moon﻿—hadn’t Rhoda Stuart that very day in school twitted her again with “living on charity”? And she was repaying her by disobedience and ingratitude. A Starr should not have done that. In a panic of fear and remorse Emily snatched the scissors and cut the bang off﻿—cut it close against the hair line. Worse and worse! Emily beheld the result in dismay. Anyone could see that a bang *had* been cut, so Aunt Elizabeth’s anger was still to face. And she had made a terrible fright of herself. Emily burst into tears, snatched up the fallen locks and crammed them into the wastebasket, blew out her candle and spring into bed, just as Aunt Elizabeth came in.
Emily burrowed face downward in the pillows, and pretended to be asleep. She was afraid Aunt Elizabeth would ask her some question and insist on her looking up while she answered it. That was a Murray tradition﻿—you looked people in the face when you spoke to them. But Aunt Elizabeth undressed in silence and came to bed. The room was in darkness﻿—thick darkness. Emily sighed and turned over. There was a hot gin-jar in the bed, she knew, and her feet were cold. But she did not think she ought to have the privilege of the gin-jar. She was too wicked﻿—too ungrateful.
“*Do* stop squirming,” said Aunt Elizabeth.
Emily squirmed no more﻿—physically at least. Mentally she continued to squirm. She could not sleep. Her feet or her conscience﻿—or both﻿—kept her awake. And fear, also. She dreaded the morning. Aunt Elizabeth would see then what had happened. If it were only over﻿—if the revelation were only over. Emily forgot and squirmed.
“What makes you so restless tonight?” demanded Aunt Elizabeth, in high displeasure. “Are you taking a cold?”
“No, ma’am.”
“Then go to sleep. I can’t bear such wriggling. One might as well have an eel in bed﻿—O﻿—W!”
Aunt Elizabeth, in squirming a bit herself, had put her own foot against Emily’s icy ones.
“Goodness, child, your feet are like snow. Here, put them on the gin-jar.”

===Chinese===

脸显得俏皮、撩人、捉摸不定了。有一短短一霎，艾米丽得意地望着镜中的自己。
随后——纯粹的恐怖攢住了她。哦，她干了什么呀！伊丽莎白姨妈要气成什么样！良心猛然醒来，也添上一刀。她坏了。伊丽莎白姨妈不许剪刘海，她偏剪，就是坏。伊丽莎白姨妈收留她、给了她新月庄园一个家——当天罗达·斯图尔特（Rhoda Stuart）不还在学校里挖苦她“靠人施舍过活”吗？她却拿不听话、没良心来报答。斯塔尔家的人不该干出这种事。恐惧和悔恨里，艾米丽慌慌张张抓起剪子，把刘海齐根剪掉了——贴着发际剪得光光。越弄越糟！艾米丽沮丧地看着结果。谁都看得出剪过刘海，伊丽莎白姨妈的火还是躲不过。而且她把自己弄成了一个吓死人的怪样子。艾米丽放声大哭，抓起落发塞进纸篓，吹灭蜡烛，一头蹦进被窝——正赶上伊丽莎白姨妈进来。
艾米丽把脸埋进枕头装睡。她怕伊丽莎白姨妈问她什么话，还非要她抬头看着回答。那是默里家的规矩——同人说话要看着人的脸。可伊丽莎白姨妈一言不发地脱衣上了床。屋里黑了——浓黑。艾米丽叹口气，翻了个身。她知道被窝里有个烫烫的杜松子酒罐，她的脚冰凉。可她想自己不配受用酒罐的特权。她太坏了——太没良心了。
“别乱动了，”伊丽莎白姨妈说。
艾米丽不再乱动——至少身上不动了。心里还在动个不停。她睡不着。她的脚，或她的良心——或两样一起——不让她睡。还有怕。她怕天亮。到时候伊丽莎白姨妈就全看见了。要是熬过了——要是这场揭发熬过了就好了。艾米丽一忘形，又动了动。
“你今晚怎么这样不老实？”伊丽莎白姨妈十分不悦地质问。“着凉了？”
“没有，姨妈。”
“那就睡觉。我可受不了这么扭来扭去。跟床上放了条泥鳅似的——哎——哟！”
伊丽莎白姨妈自己挪了挪，一只脚正碰上艾米丽冰凉的脚。
“天哪，孩子，你的脚跟雪似的。来，搁到酒罐上。”

===Original===

Aunt Elizabeth pushed the gin-jar over against Emily’s feet. How lovely and warm and comforting it was!
Emily worked her toes against it like a cat. But she suddenly knew she could not wait for morning.
“Aunt Elizabeth, I’ve got something to confess.”
Aunt Elizabeth was tired and sleepy and did not want confessions just then. In no very gracious tone she said,
“What have you been doing?”
“I﻿—I cut a bang, Aunt Elizabeth.”
“A bang?”
Aunt Elizabeth sat up in bed.
“But I cut it off again,” cried Emily hurriedly. “Right off﻿—close to my head.”
Aunt Elizabeth got out of bed, lit a candle, and looked Emily over.
“Well you *have* made a sight of yourself,” she said grimly. “I never saw anyone as ugly as you are this minute. And you have behaved in a most underhanded fashion.”
This was one of the times Emily felt compelled to agree with Aunt Elizabeth.
“I’m sorry,” she said, lifting pleading eyes.
“You will eat your supper in the pantry for a week,” said Aunt Elizabeth. “And you will not go to Uncle Oliver’s next week when I go. I had promised to take you. But I shall take no one who looks as you do anywhere with me.”
This was hard. Emily had looked forward to that visit to Uncle Oliver’s. But on the whole she was relieved. The worst was over and her feet were getting warm. But there was one thing yet. She might as well unburden her heart completely while she was at it.
“There’s another thing I feel I ought to tell you.”
Aunt Elizabeth got into bed again with a grunt. Emily took it for permission.
“Aunt Elizabeth, you remember that book I found in Dr. Burnley’s bookcase and brought home and asked you if I could read it? It was called ‘The History of Henry Esmond.’ You looked at it and said you had no objections to my reading history. So I read it. But, Aunt Elizabeth, it wasn’t history﻿—it was a novel. And I *knew it when I brought it home*.”
“You know that I have forbidden you to read novels, Emily Starr. They are wicked books and have ruined many souls.”
“It was very dull,” pleaded Emily, as if dullness and wickedness were quite incompatible. “And it made me feel unhappy. Everybody seemed to be in love with the wrong person. I have made up my mind, Aunt Elizabeth, that I will never fall in love. It makes too much trouble.”
“Don’t talk of things you can’t understand, and that are not fit for children to think about. This is the result of reading novels.

===Chinese===

伊丽莎白姨妈把酒罐推到艾米丽脚边。多可爱，多暖，多熨帖啊！
艾米丽的脚趾像猫似的在罐上蹭来蹭去。可她忽然明白，等不到天亮了。
“伊丽莎白姨妈，我有件事要坦白。”
伊丽莎白姨妈又累又困，不想这会儿听人坦白。她的口气不大客气：
“你干什么了？”
“我——我剪了个刘海，伊丽莎白姨妈。”
“刘海？”
伊丽莎白姨妈在床上坐了起来。
“可我又把它剪掉了，”艾米丽慌忙喊，“马上——贴着头皮剪光了。”
伊丽莎白姨妈下床，点起蜡烛，把艾米丽端详了一遍。
“好啊，你可真把自己弄成个样子了，”她冷冷地说。“我这辈子没见过像你这会儿这么丑的人。而且你的行径鬼鬼祟祟，不成体统。”
这是艾米丽没法不同意的时辰之一。
“对不起，”她说，抬起一双恳求的眼睛。
“一个礼拜，你的晚饭在配膳室吃，”伊丽莎白姨妈说。“还有，下礼拜我去奥利弗舅舅（Uncle Oliver）家，不带你了。我本来答应带你的。可是这副模样的人，我哪儿也不带。”
这可疼。艾米丽一直盼着去奥利弗舅舅家。可总的想来，她松了口气。最坏的过去了，脚也暖起来了。还剩一件事。索性趁这会儿把心里的担子卸干净吧。
“还有一件事，我觉得该告诉您。”
伊丽莎白姨妈哼了一声，回床上躺下。艾米丽只当是准了。
“伊丽莎白姨妈，您记得我在伯恩利大夫书柜里发现、带回家问您能不能读的那本书吗？叫《亨利·埃斯蒙德的历史》。您看了看，说您不反对我读历史。我就读了。可是，伊丽莎白姨妈，那不是历史——是小说。我带回家的时候就知道。”
“你知道我是禁你读小说的，艾米丽·斯塔尔。那种书是坏书，毁了许多灵魂。”
“它闷得很，”艾米丽辩道，仿佛闷和坏绝不相容似的。“还弄得我难受。人人好像都爱错了人。我拿定主意了，伊丽莎白姨妈，我这辈子绝不恋爱。麻烦太多。”
“不懂的事不许说，小孩子也不许想这些。这就是读小说的下场。

===Original===

I shall tell Dr. Burnley to lock his bookcase up.”
“Oh, don’t do that, Aunt Elizabeth,” exclaimed Emily. “There are no more novels in it. But I’m reading such an interesting book over there. It tells about everything that’s inside of you. I’ve got as far along as the liver and its diseases. The pictures are so interesting. Please let me finish it.”
This was worse than novels. Aunt Elizabeth was truly horrified. Things that were inside of you were not to be read about.
“Have you no shame, Emily Starr? If you have not I am ashamed for you. Little girls do not read books like that.”
“But, Aunt Elizabeth, why not? I *have* a liver, haven’t I﻿—and heart and lungs﻿—and stomach﻿—and﻿—”
“That will do, Emily. Not another word.”
Emily went to sleep unhappily. She wished she had never said a word about “Esmond.” And she knew she would never have a chance to finish that other fascinating book. Nor had she. Dr. Burnley’s bookcase was locked thereafter and the doctor gruffly ordered her and Ilse to keep out of his office. He was in a very bad humour about it for he had words with Elizabeth Murray over the matter.
Emily was not allowed to forget her bang. She was twitted and teased in school about it and Aunt Elizabeth looked at it whenever she looked at Emily and the contempt in her eyes burned Emily like a flame. Nevertheless, as the mistreated hair grew out and began to curl in soft little ringlets, Emily found consolation. The bang was tacitly permitted, and she felt that her looks were greatly improved thereby. Of course, as soon as it grew long enough she knew Aunt Elizabeth would make her brush it back. But for the time being she took comfort in her added beauty.
The bang was just about at its best when the letter came from Great-Aunt Nancy.
It was written to Aunt Laura﻿—Great-Aunt Nancy and Aunt Elizabeth were not overfond of each other﻿—and in it Great-Aunt Nancy said, “If you have a photograph of that child Emily send it along. I don’t want to see *her*; she’s stupid﻿—I know she’s stupid. But I want to see what Juliet’s child looks like. Also the child of that fascinating young man, Douglas Starr. He *was* fascinating. What fools you all were to make such a fuss about Juliet running away with him. If you and Elizabeth had *both* run away with somebody in your running days it would have been better for you.”
This letter was not shown to Emily.

===Chinese===

我这就吩咐伯恩利大夫把书柜锁上。”
“哦，别这样，伊丽莎白姨妈，”艾米丽喊。“里头没有别的小说了。可我正在那儿读一本顶有意思的书。讲的全是人身体里头的东西。我已经读到肝和肝的病了。那些图多有意思呀。求您让我读完吧。”
这比小说还糟。伊丽莎白姨妈当真吓着了。人身体里头的东西，是不许读的。
“你不知羞吗，艾米丽·斯塔尔？你要是不知，我替你羞。小姑娘家不读那样的书。”
“可是，伊丽莎白姨妈，为什么不能读？我有肝，不是吗——还有心，有肺——有胃——还有——”
“够了，艾米丽。不许再说一个字。”
艾米丽不痛快地睡着了。她巴望方才一个字没提《埃斯蒙德》。她也知道，那另外一本迷人的书，再没机会读完了。果然没有。伯恩利大夫的书柜从此上了锁，大夫还粗声粗气地下令，不许她和伊尔丝再进他的诊室。他为此很不痛快——为这事同伊丽莎白·默里吵了一场。
刘海的事也没人让她忘掉。学校里人人拿它打趣；伊丽莎白姨妈呢，看艾米丽一眼就瞟刘海一眼，眼中的轻蔑像火苗一样燎着艾米丽。然而，随着受虐待的头发长出来、渐渐鬈成一个个柔软的小卷，艾米丽找到了安慰。刘海被默许留下了，她觉得自己的模样因此大大改善。当然，她知道等它长长了，伊丽莎白姨妈就会逼她刷回去。可眼下，她在这新添的美貌里自得其乐。
刘海正当最好的时候，南希姨婆的信来了。
信是写给劳拉姨妈的——南希姨婆和伊丽莎白姨妈彼此不怎么亲——信里南希姨婆说：“你们要是有那孩子艾米丽的照片，寄一张来。我不想见她本人；她蠢——我知道她蠢。可我想看看朱丽叶的孩子长什么样。也想看看那个迷人的年轻人道格拉斯·斯塔尔（Douglas Starr）的孩子长什么样。他可真迷人。朱丽叶跟他私奔，你们大惊小怪，全是些傻瓜。当年你和伊丽莎白要是也各自跟人私奔一回，如今倒好些。”
这封信没给艾米丽看。

===Original===

Aunt Elizabeth and Aunt Laura had a long secret consultation and then Emily was told that she was to be taken to Shrewsbury to have her picture taken for Aunt Nancy. Emily was much excited over this. She was dressed in her blue cashmere and Aunt Laura put a point lace collar on it and hung her Venetian beads over it. And new buttoned boots were got for the occasion.
“I’m so glad this has happened while I still have my bang,” thought Emily happily.
But in the photographer’s dressing-room, Aunt Elizabeth grimly proceeded to brush back her bang and pin it with hairpins.
“Oh, please, Aunt Elizabeth, let me have it down,” Emily begged. “Just for the picture. After this I’ll brush it back.”
Aunt Elizabeth was inexorable. The bang was brushed back and the photograph taken. When Aunt Elizabeth saw the finished result she was satisfied.
“She looks sulky; but she is neat; and there is a resemblance to the Murrays I never noticed before,” she told Aunt Laura. “That will please Aunt Nancy. She is very clannish under all her oddness.”
Emily would have liked to throw every one of the photographs in the fire. She hated them. They made her look hideous. Her face seemed to be *all* forehead. If they sent Aunt Nancy that Aunt Nancy would think her stupider than ever. When Aunt Elizabeth did the photograph up in cardboard and told Emily to take it to the office Emily already knew what she meant to do. She went straight to the garret and took out of her box the watercolour Teddy had made of her. It was just the same size as the photograph. Emily removed the latter from its wrappings, spurning it aside with her foot.
“That isn’t *me*,” she said. “I looked sulky because I felt sulky about the bang. But I hardly ever look sulky, so it isn’t fair.”
She wrapped Teddy’s sketch up in the cardboard and then sat down and wrote a letter.

> “Dear Great-Aunt Nancy:
> “Aunt Elizabeth had my picture taken to send you but I don’t like it because it makes me look too ugly and I am putting another picture in instead. An *artist friend* made it for me. It is just like me when I’m smiling and have a bang. I am only *lending* it to you, not *giving* it, because I valew it very highly.
> “Your obedient grand niece,

**“Emily Byrd Starr.**
> “P.S. I am not so stupid as you think.
> “E. B. S.
> “P.S. No. 2. I am not stupid *at all*.”

===Chinese===

伊丽莎白姨妈和劳拉姨妈悄悄商量了好半天，随后告诉艾米丽：要带她去什鲁斯伯里（Shrewsbury）照相，寄给南希姨婆。艾米丽兴奋极了。她穿上蓝羊呢裙，劳拉姨妈给她钉上一条针绣花边领，又给她挂上威尼斯珠链。为这桩大事，还新添了系扣的新靴子。
“真高兴这事赶在我还有刘海的时候来，”艾米丽快活地想。
可到了照相馆的化妆间，伊丽莎白姨妈冷着脸，动手把她的刘海刷回去，用发卡别住。
“哦，求求你，伊丽莎白姨妈，让我留着吧，”艾米丽哀求。“就照相这一回。往后我都刷回去。”
伊丽莎白姨妈毫不动摇。刘海刷了回去，相照了。伊丽莎白姨妈一见成品，满意了。
“样子倔；可是齐整；还有几分默里家的相，我从前没看出来过，”她对劳拉姨妈说。“这正合南希姨妈的意。她古怪归古怪，心向着本家。”
艾米丽恨不得把照片一张张全扔进火里。她恨它们。它们把她照得奇丑。她的脸好像光剩脑门了。这个寄去，南希姨婆要当她比原先更蠢。伊丽莎白姨妈把照片用硬纸板包好，吩咐艾米丽送到邮局去，艾米丽那时已经打定了主意。她径直上阁楼，从盒子里取出泰迪给她画的那张水彩。同照片一般大小。艾米丽把照片从纸板里抽出来，用脚把它踢到一边。
“这不是我，”她说。“我照得倔，是因为为刘海心里正倔着。可我难得倔，所以这不公道。”
她把泰迪的画包进纸板，坐下写信。

> “亲爱的南希姨婆：
> “伊丽莎白姨妈带我去照相，要寄给您，可我不喜欢它，它把我照得太丑了，我另放了一张画进去。是一位画家朋友给我画的。我微笑、有刘海的时候，就是那个样子。这张画我只是借给您，不是送给您，因为我顶珍昔它。
> “您听话的侄孙女，

**“艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔（Emily Byrd Starr）。**
> “又及：我没有您想的那么蠢。
> “E. B. S.
> “又及之二：我一点儿都不蠢。”

===Original===

Emily put her letter in with the picture﻿—thereby unconsciously cheating the post-office﻿—and slipped out of the house to mail it. Once it was safely in the post-office she drew a breath of relief. She found the walk home very enjoyable. It was a bland day in early April and spring was looking at you round the corners. The Wind Woman was laughing and whistling over the wet sweet fields; freebooting crows held conferences in the tree tops; little pools of sunshine lay in the mossy hollows; the sea was a blaze of sapphire beyond the golden dunes; the maples in Lofty John’s bush were talking about red buds. Everything Emily had ever read of dream and myth and legend seemed a part of the charm of that bush. She was filled to her fingertips with a rapture of living.
“Oh, I smell spring!” she cried as she danced along the brook path.
Then she began to compose a poem on it. Everybody who has ever lived in the world and could string two rhymes together has written a poem on spring. It is the most be-rhymed subject in the world﻿—and always will be, because it is poetry incarnate itself. You can never be a real poet if you haven’t made at least one poem about spring.
Emily was wondering whether she would have elves dancing on the brookside by moonlight, or pixies sleeping in a bed of ferns in her poem, when something confronted her at a bend in the path which was neither elf nor pixy, but seemed odd and weird enough to belong to some of the tribes of Little People. Was it a witch? Or an elderly fay of evil intentions﻿—the bad fairy of all christening tales?
“I’m the b’y’s Aunt Tom,” said the appearance, seeing that Emily was too amazed to do anything but stand and stare.
“Oh!” Emily gasped in relief. She was no longer frightened. But what a *very* peculiar looking lady Perry’s Aunt Tom was. Old﻿—so old that it seemed quite impossible that she could ever have been young; a bright red hood over crone-like, fluttering grey locks; a little face seamed by a thousand fine, crisscross wrinkles; a long nose with a knob on the end of it; little twinkling, eager, grey eyes under bristly brows; a ragged man’s coat covering her from neck to feet; a basket in one hand and a black knobby stick in the other.
“Staring wasn’t thought good breeding in my time,” said Aunt Tom.
“Oh!” said Emily again. “Excuse me﻿—How do you do!” she added, with a vague grasp after her manners.

===Chinese===

艾米丽把信同画装在一起——不知不觉占了邮局一个小便宜——溜出宅子去寄。信一安全落进邮局，她长舒一口气。回家的路走得好不惬意。这是四月上旬一个温和的日子，春天正从各个角落里探头看你。风之女在湿漉漉、甜丝丝的田野上又笑又吹；打劫的乌鸦在树顶开会；阳光一小汪一小汪积在生苔的洼地里；金色沙丘外，海是一片蓝宝石的烈焰；高个子约翰（Lofty John）林子里的枫树正议论着红芽。艾米丽读过的所有梦、神话和传说，仿佛都成了那片林子魅力的一部分。生的狂喜一直灌到她的指尖。
“哦，我闻见春天了！”她沿着溪边小路蹦跳着喊。
随后她动起手来，要为春天作一首诗。凡是活在世上、能把两个韵脚串起来的人，个个都写过咏春的诗。它是天下被吟咏得最多的题目——也永远会是，因为它本身就是诗的化身。一生里连一首咏春的诗都没写过，你就永远成不了真正的诗人。
艾米丽正盘算，她的诗里是让精灵在月光下的溪畔跳舞呢，还是让小妖精睡在蕨草铺的床上——小路一拐弯，有个东西迎面而来：不是精灵，也不是小妖精，可那古怪劲儿、邪乎劲儿，够得上小小部族的哪一支。是巫婆吗？还是个没安好心的老仙子——一切受洗故事里都有的那种坏仙女？
“我是那小子的汤姆婶婆（Aunt Tom），”那影子说，它看出艾米丽惊得只会站着瞪眼。
“哦！”艾米丽松了口气。她不怕了。可佩里的这位汤姆婶婆，长相也真叫特别。老——老得叫人没法相信她年轻过；老太太风的红兜帽底下，灰白的乱发簌簌地飘；一张小脸让一千道细细的皱纹缝满；长鼻子，鼻尖还带个疙瘩；扎煞的眉毛底下，一双灰色的小眼睛滴溜溜地又急又亮；一件男人的破外套从头罩到脚；一手提篮子，一手拄一根疙瘩棒。
“我年轻那会儿，瞪着人看可不算是好教养，”汤姆婶婆说。
“哦！”艾米丽又说了一声。“对不起——您好！”她加了一句，慌慌张张抓回一点礼数。

===Original===

“Polite﻿—and not too proud,” said Aunt Tom, peering curiously at her. “I’ve been up to the big house with a pair of socks for the b’y but ’twas yourself I wanted to see.”
“Me?” said Emily blankly.
“Yis. The b’y has been talking a bit of you and a plan kem into my head. Thinks I to myself it’s no bad notion. But I’ll make sure before I waste my bit o’ money. Emily Byrd Starr is your name and Murray is your nature. If I give the b’y an eddication will ye marry him when ye grow up?”
“Me!” said Emily again. It seemed to be all she could say. Was she dreaming? She *must* be.
“Yis﻿—you. You’re half Murray and it’ll be a great step up f’r the b’y. He’s smart and he’ll be a rich man some day and boss the country. But divil a cent will I spend on him unless you promise.”
“Aunt Elizabeth wouldn’t let me,” cried Emily, too frightened of this odd old body to refuse on her own account.
“If you’ve got any Murray in you you’ll do your own choosing,” said Aunt Tom, thrusting her face so close to Emily’s that her bushy eyebrows tickled Emily’s nose. “Say you’ll marry the b’y and to college he goes.”
Emily seemed to be rendered speechless. She could think of nothing to say﻿—oh, if she could *only* wake up! She could not even run.
“Say it!” insisted Aunt Tom, thumping her stick sharply on a stone in the path.
Emily was so horrified that she might have said something﻿—anything﻿—to escape. But at this moment Perry bounded out of the spruce copse, his face white with rage, and seized his Aunt Tom most disrespectfully by the shoulder.
“You go home!” he said furiously.
“Now, b’y dear,” quavered Aunt Tom deprecatingly. “I was only trying to do you a good turn. I was asking her to marry ye after a bit an﻿—”
“I’ll do my own asking!” Perry was angrier than ever. “You’ve likely spoiled everything. Go home﻿—go home, I say!”
Aunt Tom hobbled off muttering, “Then I’ll know better than to waste me bit o’ money. No Murray, no money, me b’y.”
When she had disappeared down the brook path Perry turned to Emily. From white he had gone very red.
“Don’t mind her﻿—she’s cracked,” he said. “Of course, when I grow up I mean to ask you to marry me but﻿—”
“I couldn’t﻿—Aunt Elizabeth﻿—”
“Oh, she will then. I’m going to be premier of Canada some day.”
“But I wouldn’t want﻿—I’m sure I wouldn’t﻿—”
“You will when you grow up. Ilse is better looking of course, and I don’t know why I like you best but I do.”

===Chinese===

“客气——又不拿大，”汤姆婶婆说，好奇地打量她。“我上大宅给那小子送双袜子来着，可我想见的原来是你。”
“我？”艾米丽发懵。
“对。那小子念叨过你几句，我脑子里就来了个主意。我自个儿琢磨：这主意不赖。可我不能白扔我那点子钱，得先问个准。你叫艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔（Emily Byrd Starr），骨子里是默里家的性子。我要是供那小子念书，你长大了肯嫁他不？”
“我！”艾米丽又说一遍。她好像只会说这个了。是在做梦吗？一定是在做梦。
“对——就是你。你有半个默里，对那小子就是高高的抬举。他机灵，早晚要发财，要掌这个国家的舵。可你不答应，我一个子儿也不为他花。”
“伊丽莎白姨妈不会答应的，”艾米丽喊，这个古怪老太太吓得她不敢自己做主回绝。
“你骨头里要有默里的血，婚事就自己拿主意，”汤姆婶婆说，把脸凑得离艾米丽那么近，扎煞的眉毛直蹭艾米丽的鼻子。“说一句肯嫁那小子，他就上大学去。”
艾米丽像被夺了话。她一句话也想不出——哦，要是能醒过来多好！她连跑都不会跑了。
“说呀！”汤姆婶婆不依，把棒子在小路的石头上笃笃地敲。
艾米丽吓成那样，为了脱身，多半什么都说得出来。可正在这时，佩里从云杉矮林里蹿出来，脸气得煞白，大不敬地一把抓住他汤姆婶婆的肩膀。
“你回家去！”他怒吼。
“哎哟，亲爱的小子，”汤姆婶婆抖抖索索地讨饶，“我不过想帮你个忙。我正求她过些年嫁给你，还有——”
“求婚的事我自己来！”佩里火更大了。“八成全让你搅黄了。回家去——我说回家去！”
汤姆婶婆一步一挪地走了，嘴里嘀咕：“那我往后可知道，不糟蹋我那点子钱了。没有默里，没有钱，我的小子。”
她顺着溪边小路走没了影，佩里转向艾米丽。他的脸从煞白转成了通红。
“别理她——她脑子有毛病，”他说。“当然喽，我长大了是要自己向你求婚的，可是——”
“我不能——伊丽莎白姨妈——”
“哦，她到时候会肯的。我早晚要当加拿大总理。”
“可我不想——我拿准我不想——”
“你长大了就想了。伊尔丝当然更好看，我也不懂为什么我偏偏最喜欢你，可我就是最喜欢你。”

===Original===

“Don’t you ever talk to me like this again!” commanded Emily, beginning to recover her dignity.
“Oh, I won’t﻿—not till we grow up. I’m as ashamed of it as you are,” said Perry with a sheepish grin. “Only I had to say something after Aunt Tom butted in like that.
