They might never know what had become of her﻿—never find her. The crows or the gulls would pick her eyes out. She dramatized the thing so vividly that she almost screamed with the horror of it. She would just disappear from the world as Ilse’s mother had disappeared.
*What had become of Ilse’s mother?* Even in her own desperate plight Emily asked herself that question. And she would never see dear New Moon again and Teddy and the dairy and the Tansy Patch and Lofty John’s bush and the mossy old sundial and her precious little heap of manuscripts on the sofa shelf in the garret.
“I must be very brave and patient,” she thought. “My only chance is to lie still. And I can pray in my mind﻿—I’m sure God can hear thoughts as well as words. It is nice to think He can hear me if nobody else can. O God﻿—Father’s God﻿—please work a miracle and save my life, because I don’t think I’m fit to die yet. Excuse my not being on my knees﻿—You can see I can’t move. And if I die please don’t let Aunt Elizabeth find my letter-bills ever. Please let Aunt Laura find them. And please don’t let Caroline move out the wardrobe when she housecleans because then she would find my Jimmy-book and read what I wrote about her. Please forgive all my sins, especially not being grateful enough and cutting a bang, and please don’t let Father be very far away. Amen.”
Then, characteristically she thought of a postscript. “And oh, *please* let somebody find out that Ilse’s mother didn’t do *that*.”
She lay very still. The light on the water began to turn warm gold and rose. A great pine on a bluff in front of her overflowed in a crest of dark boughs against the amber splendour behind it﻿—a part of the beauty of the beautiful world that was slipping away from her. The chill of the evening gulf breeze began to creep over her. Once a bit of earth broke off at her side and went down﻿—Emily heard the thud of the little pebbles in it on the boulders below. The portion upon which one of her legs lay was quite loose and pendent also. She knew it might break off, too, at any moment. It would be very dreadful to be there when it got dark.

===Chinese===

从前她跟死亡照过一回面——至少自己以为照过——在高个子约翰（Lofty John）说她吃了毒苹果的那晚——可这回更难。死在这儿，孤零零，离家这么远！他们也许永远不知道她的下落——永远找不着她。乌鸦、海鸥要把她的眼睛啄掉。她把这一切想得活灵活现，几乎要恐怖得喊出声。她就要像伊尔丝的妈妈那样，从这世上消失了。
伊尔丝的妈妈到底怎么样了？纵然身陷绝境，艾米丽还在自问。她再也见不着亲爱的新月庄园了，见不着泰迪、奶房、艾菊丛（the Tansy Patch）、高个子约翰的林子、长苔的老日晷，还有阁楼沙发搁板上她那堆珍贵的小手稿。
“我必须非常勇敢、非常忍耐，”她想。“我唯一的活路是趴着不动。我可以在心里祷告——我拿准，话语上帝听得见，心思他也听得见。想到谁都听不见我、唯他听得见，心里真好。上帝啊——爸爸的上帝啊——求你显个神迹，救我一命，因为我想我还不配死。恕我不能跪着——你也看得见，我动不了。还有，万一我死了，求你千万别让伊丽莎白姨妈发现我的信单。求你让劳拉姨妈（Aunt Laura）发现。还有，求你别让卡罗琳大扫除时挪那口衣柜，不然她会翻出我的吉米本子，读到写她的那些话。求你饶恕我一切的罪，尤其是感恩不够和偷剪刘海；还有，求你别让爸爸离得太远。阿们。”
随后，她照例又想起一条附笔。“哦，还求你叫什么人查明：伊尔丝的妈妈没干过那件事。”
她纹丝不动地趴着。水面上的光开始转成暖金和玫瑰色。她面前一座岬顶上的大松树，泼洒出一冠黑黢黢的枝桠，衬在背后琥珀色的壮丽上——正从她身边溜走的美妙世界的美的一部分。海湾傍晚的凉风开始往她身上爬。她身侧有一小块土剥落下去——艾米丽听见里头的小石子在底下巨石上磕响。她一条腿压着的那一块也松了、也悬着。她知道它也随时可能断落。天黑以后还在这儿，就太可怕了。

===Original===

She could see the big spray of farewell-summer that had lured her to her doom, waving unplucked above her, wonderfully purple and lovely.
Then, beside it, she saw a man’s face looking down at her!
She heard him say, “My God!” softly to himself. She saw that he was slight and that one shoulder was a trifle higher than the other. This must be Dean Priest﻿—Jarback Priest. Emily dared not call to him. She lay still and her great, grey-purple eyes said, “Save me.”
“How can I help you?” said Dean Priest hoarsely, as if to himself. “I cannot reach you﻿—and it looks as if the slightest touch or jar would send that broken earth over the brink. I must go for a rope﻿—and to leave you here alone﻿—like this. Can you wait, child?”
“Yes,” breathed Emily. She smiled at him to encourage him﻿—the little soft smile that began at the corners of her mouth and spread over her face. Dean Priest never forgot that smile﻿—and the steadfast child-eyes looking out through it from the little face that seemed so perilously near the brink.
“I’ll be as quick as I can,” he said. “I can’t go very fast﻿—I’m a bit lame, you see. But don’t be frightened﻿—I’ll save you. I’ll leave my dog to keep you company. Here, Tweed.”
He whistled﻿—a great, tawny-gold dog came in sight.
“Sit right there, Tweed, till I come back. Don’t stir a paw﻿—don’t wag a tail﻿—talk to her only with your eyes.”
Tweed sat down obediently and Dean Priest disappeared.
Emily lay there and dramatized the whole incident for her Jimmy-book. She was a little frightened still, but not too frightened to see herself writing it all out the next day. It would be quite a thrilling bit.
She liked to know the big dog was there. She was not so learned in lore of dogs as in lore of cats. But he looked very human and trusty watching her with great kindly eyes. A grey kitten was an adorable thing﻿—but a grey kitten would not have sat there and encouraged her. “I believe,” thought Emily, “that a dog is better than a cat when you’re in trouble.”
It was half an hour before Dean Priest returned.
“Thank God you haven’t gone over,” he muttered. “I hadn’t to go as far as I feared﻿—I found a rope in an empty boat up-shore and took it. And now﻿—if I drop the rope down to you, are you strong enough to hold it while the earth goes and then hang on while I pull you up?”
“I’ll try,” said Emily.

===Chinese===

她望得见那枝诱她至此的告别夏，在她头顶无人采撷地摇曳，紫得奇妙，可爱极了。
随后，在花枝旁边，她看见一张男人的脸正低头望她！
她听见他轻轻自语了一声：“我的天！”她看出他身子单薄，一边肩膀比另一边略高。这准是迪恩·普里斯特——歪背·普里斯特。艾米丽不敢喊他。她静静趴着，一双灰紫的大眼睛说：“救救我。”
“我怎么救你？”迪恩·普里斯特喑哑地说，像是自言自语。“我够不着你——而且看样子，轻轻一碰一震，那块裂土就要翻下崖去。我得去找条绳子——可要把你一个人攞在这儿——这样攞着。你能等吗，孩子？”
“能，”艾米丽轻轻说。她朝他嫣然一笑，好给他鼓劲——那个从嘴角漾起、漫上整张脸的柔柔的笑。迪恩·普里斯特一辈子忘不了那一笑——还有那双从险险悬在崖边的小脸里望出来的、坚毅的孩子眼睛。
“我尽快去，”他说。“我快不了——你看，我腿有点瘸。可别怕——我会救你。我把狗留给你作伴。过来，特威德（Tweed）。”
他吹了声口哨——一条金褐色的大狗出现了。
“就坐在这儿，特威德，等我回来。爪子别动——尾巴别摇——只许拿眼睛同她说话。”
特威德乖乖坐下，迪恩·普里斯特走了。
艾米丽趴在那儿，把整桩事在脑子里排演成吉米本子的一篇。她还有点儿怕，可没怕到看不见明天的自己伏案把它写出来。那将是顶惊心动魄的一章。
她喜欢知道那条大狗在。论狗的学问她不如论猫。可它拿一双大大的、和善的眼睛守着她，看着通人性、靠得住。灰狸猫是可疼的东西——可灰狸猫不会蹲在那儿给她鼓劲。“我相信，”艾米丽想，“遭了难的时候，狗比猫强。”
半个钟头后，迪恩·普里斯特回来了。
“谢天谢地，你没掉下去，”他喃喃道。“我不必跑我担心的那么远——上滩一条空船里有根绳子，我拿了。这就来——我把绳子放下去，你有力气攢住它熬过塌方、再吊着让我拽你上来吗？”
“我试试，”艾米丽说。

===Original===

Dean Priest knotted a loop at the end and slid it down to her. Then he wound the rope around the trunk of a heavy fir.
“Now,” he said.
Emily said inwardly, “Dear God, *please*﻿—” and caught the swaying loop. The next moment the full weight of her body swung from it, for at her first movement the broken soil beneath her slipped down﻿—slipped over. Dean Priest sickened and shivered. Could she cling to the rope while he drew her up?
Then he saw she had got a little knee-hold on the narrow shelf. Carefully he drew on the rope. Emily, full of pluck, helped him by digging her toes into the crumbling bank. In a moment she was within his reach. He grasped her arms and pulled her up beside him into safety. As he lifted her past the farewell-summer Emily reached out her hand and broke off the spray.
“I’ve got it, anyhow,” she said jubilantly. Then she remembered her manners. “I’m much obliged to you. You saved my life. And﻿—and﻿—I think I’ll sit down a moment. My legs feel funny and trembly.”
Emily sat down, all at once more shaky than she had been through all the danger. Dean Priest leaned against the gnarled old fir. He seemed “trembly” too. He wiped his forehead with his handkerchief. Emily looked curiously at him. She had learned a good deal about him from Aunt Nancy’s casual remarks﻿—not always good-natured remarks, for Aunt Nancy did not wholly like him, it seemed. She always called him “Jarback” rather contemptuously, while Caroline scrupulously called him Dean. Emily knew he had been to college, that he was thirty-six years old﻿—which to Emily seemed a venerable age﻿—and well-off; that he had a malformed shoulder and limped slightly; that he cared for nothing save books nor ever had; that he lived with an older brother and travelled a great deal; and that the whole Priest clan stood somewhat in awe of his ironic tongue. Aunt Nancy had called him a “cynic.” Emily did not know what a cynic was but it sounded interesting. She looked him over carefully and saw that he had delicate, pale features and tawny-brown hair. His lips were thin and sensitive, with a whimsical curve. She liked his mouth. Had she been older she would have known why﻿—because it connoted strength and tenderness and humour.

===Chinese===

迪恩·普里斯特在绳头打了个套，顺下去给她。随后把绳子绕上一棵粗壮的冷杉树干。
“来，”他说。
艾米丽心里说：“亲爱的上帝，求你——”一把攢住晃晃悠悠的绳套。下一霎，她全身的重量都吊在了上头，因为她一动，脚下的裂土就滑了下去——滑没了。迪恩·普里斯特一阵目眩心惊。他往上拽的工夫，她抓得住吗？
随后他看见她在窄石棱上蹭出了一点膝头的支点。他小心地拽绳。艾米丽浑身是胆，脚趾抠着酥松的岸坡帮他使劲。一霎她就够得着他的手了。他攢住她的胳膊，把她拉上来，拉到自己身边安全的地方。经过那枝告别夏时，艾米丽伸手折下了花枝。
“好歹我摘着了，”她欢天喜地说。随后她想起了礼数。“多谢您。您救了我的命。还——还——我想我得坐一会儿。腿觉着怪怪的，直打颤。”
艾米丽坐下了，此刻反而比历险全程抖得都厉害。迪恩·普里斯特倚在虬结的老冷杉上。他看上去也在“打颤”。他掏出手帕擦额头。艾米丽好奇地打量他。南希姑婆的闲谈里，她听来不少他的事——那话可不总是好话，南希姑婆看来不大喜欢他。她叫他“歪背”，口气颇为轻蔑；卡罗琳则一丝不苟地叫他“迪恩”。艾米丽知道他上过大学，三十六岁——在艾米丽眼里这已是高龄——家境殷实；一个肩膀畸形，微跛；除书以外什么都不爱，从来如此；同一位兄长同住，常出门旅行；普里斯特全族都有点怕他那张反唇相讥的嘴。南希姑婆叫过他“愤世者”。艾米丽不知道愤世者是什么，可听着有意思。她细细把他端详了一遍，见他眉目清秀苍白，头发棕黄。嘴唇薄而敏感，带看一点乖觉的弯。她喜欢他的嘴。她要是大几岁，就会知道为什么——因为那嘴唇含着力量、温柔和幽默。

In spite of his twisted shoulder there was about him a certain aloof dignity of presence which was characteristic of many of the Priests and which was often mistaken for pride. The green Priest eyes, that were peering and uncanny in Caroline’s face and impudent in Jim Priest’s, were remarkably dreamy and attractive in his.
“Well, do you think me handsome?” he said, sitting down on another stone and smiling at her. His voice was beautiful﻿—musical and caressing.
Emily blushed. She knew staring was not etiquette, and she did not think him at all handsome, so she was very thankful that he did not press his question, but asked another.
“Do you know who your knightly rescuer is?”
“I think you must be Jar﻿—Mr. Dean Priest.” Emily flushed again with vexation. She had come so near to making another terrible hole in her manners.
“Yes, Jarback Priest. You needn’t mind the nickname. I’ve heard it often enough. It’s a Priest idea of humour.” He laughed rather unpleasantly. “The reason for it is obvious enough, isn’t it? I never got anything else at school. How came you to slide over that cliff?”
“I wanted this,” said Emily, waving her farewell-summer.
“And you have it! Do you always get what you go after, even with death slipping a thin wedge between? I think you’re born lucky. I see the signs. If that big aster lured you into danger it saved you as well, for it was through stepping over to investigate it that I saw you. Its size and colour caught my eye. Otherwise I should have gone on and you﻿—what would have become of you? Whom do you belong to that you are let risk your life on these dangerous banks? What is your name﻿—if you have a name! I begin to doubt you﻿—I see you have pointed ears. Have I been tricked into meddling with fairies, and will I discover presently that twenty years have passed and that I am an old man long since lost to the living world with nothing but the skeleton of my dog for company?”
“I am Emily Byrd Starr of New Moon,” said Emily, rather coldly. She was beginning to be sensitive about her ears. Father Cassidy had remarked on them﻿—and now Jarback Priest. Was there really something uncanny about them?
And yet there was a flavour about the said Jarback that she liked﻿—liked decidedly. Emily never was long in doubt about anyone she met. In a few minutes she always knew whether she liked, disliked, or was indifferent to them.

艾米丽脸红了。她知道瞪人看失礼，再说她根本不觉得他俊，所以她谢天谢地，他没有追问，另起了话头。

“对，歪背·普里斯特。别把这诨名放心上。我听得多了。这是普里斯特式的幽默。”他笑了一声，不大好听。“缘由也明摆着，不是吗？上学那会儿我就得过这一个外号。你怎么滑下那崖的？”
“我想要这个，”艾米丽说着，晃晃那枝告别夏。
“还真叫你得着了！凡是你追的，你回回都得着？连死神楔进来一道细缝也不放？我看你是生成的好运气。我瞧得出征候。这大紫菀诱你历险，也救了你——正是跨过去端详它，我才看见你。它那块头、那颜色抓了我的眼。不然我就走过去了，那你——你就怎么样了？你是哪家的，竟放你到这些险岸上来拿命玩？你叫什么——要是你有名字的话！我开始疑心你了——我看你有尖耳朵。莫非我上了当，招惹了精灵？回头一发现，二十年已经过去，我成了早被阳世除名的老头，身边只剩我那条狗的骨架？”
“我是新月庄园（New Moon）的艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔（Emily Byrd Starr），”艾米丽说，口气很冷。她开始为自己的耳朵敏感了。卡西迪神父（Father Cassidy）议论过——如今又是歪背·普里斯特。它们真有什么邪性的地方吗？
可这位歪背身上自有一种味道，她喜欢——分明喜欢。对遇到的人，艾米丽从来用不了多久就能拿定主意。几分钟内，她总能知道自己是喜欢、不喜欢，还是无所谓。

She had a queer feeling that she had known Jarback Priest for years﻿—perhaps because it had seemed so long when she was lying on that crumbling earth waiting for him to return. He was not handsome but she liked that lean, clever face of his with its magnetic green eyes.
“So you’re the young lady visitor at the Grange!” said Dean Priest, in some astonishment. “Then my dear Aunt Nancy should look after you better﻿—my *very* dear Aunt Nancy.”
“You don’t like Aunt Nancy, I see,” said Emily coolly.
“What is the use of liking a lady who won’t like me? You have probably discovered by this time that my Lady Aunt detests me.”
“Oh, I don’t think it’s as bad as that,” said Emily. “She must have some good opinions about you﻿—she says you’re the only Priest who will ever go to heaven.”
“She doesn’t mean that as a compliment, whatever you in your innocence believe it to be. And you are Douglas Starr’s daughter? I knew your father. We were boys together at Queen’s Academy﻿—we drifted apart after we left it﻿—he went into journalism, I to McGill. But he was the only friend I had at school﻿—the only boy who would bother himself about Jarback Priest, who was lame and hunchbacked and couldn’t play football or hockey. Emily Byrd Starr﻿—Starr should be your first name. You look like a star﻿—you have a radiant sort of personality shining through you﻿—your proper habitat should be the evening sky just after sunset﻿—or the morning sky just before sunrise. Yes. You’d be more at home in the morning sky. I think I shall call you Star.”
“Do you mean that you think me pretty?” asked Emily directly.
“Why, it hadn’t occurred to me to wonder whether you were pretty or not. Do you think a star should be pretty?”
Emily reflected.
“No,” she said finally, “the word doesn’t suit a star.”
“I perceive you are an artist in words. Of course it doesn’t. Stars are prismatic﻿—palpitating﻿—elusive. It is not often we find one made flesh and blood. I think I’ll wait for you.”
“Oh, I’m ready to go now,” said Emily, standing up.
“H’m. That wasn’t what I meant. Never mind. Come along, Star﻿—if you don’t mind walking a bit slowly. I’ll take you back from the wilderness at least﻿—I don’t know that I’ll venture to Wyther Grange tonight. I don’t want Aunt Nancy to take the edge off *you*. And so you don’t think me handsome?”
“I didn’t say so,” cried Emily.
“Not in words.

“不管你们天真地当那是什么好话，她可不是那个意思。这么说，你是道格拉斯·斯塔尔（Douglas Starr）的女儿？我认得你父亲。我们当年一起在女王学院念书——毕业以后就疏远了——他进了报界，我上了麦吉尔。可在学校，他是我唯一的朋友——唯一肯为一个又瘸又驼、踢不了足球打不了冰球的歪背·普里斯特操心的男生。艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔——斯塔尔该当你的名才对。你看着就像一颗星——你浑身透出一种光芒四射的性灵——你合适的家园，该是日落刚过的夜空——要么日出之前的晨空。对，晨空更配你。我看我就叫你‘星儿’吧。”

“嘴上没有。

===Original===

But I can read your thoughts, Star﻿—it won’t ever do to think anything you don’t want me to know. The gods gave me that gift﻿—when they kept back everything else I wanted. You don’t think me handsome but you think me nice. Do you think you are pretty yourself?”
“A little﻿—since Aunt Nancy lets me wear my bang,” said Emily frankly.
Jarback Priest made a grimace.
“Don’t call it by such a name. It’s a worse name even than bustle. Bangs and bustles﻿—they hurt me. I like that black wave breaking on your white brows﻿—but don’t call it a bang﻿—ever again.”
“It *is* a very ugly word. I never use it in my poetry, of course.”
Whereby Dean Priest discovered that Emily wrote poetry. He also discovered pretty nearly everything else about her in that charming walk back to Priest Pond in the fir-scented dusk, with Tweed walking between them, his nose touching his master’s hand softly every now and then, while the robins in the trees above them whistled blithely in the afterlight.
With nine out of ten people Emily was secretive and reserved, but Dean Priest was sealed of her tribe and she divined it instantly. He had a right to the inner sanctuary and she yielded it unquestioningly. She talked to him freely.
Besides, she felt *alive* again﻿—she felt the wonderful thrill of living again, after that dreadful space when she had seemed to hang between life and death. She felt, as she wrote to her father afterwards, “as if a little bird was singing in my heart.” And oh, how good the green sod felt under her feet!
She told him all about herself and her doings and beings. Only one thing she did not tell him﻿—her worry over Ilse’s mother. *That* she could not speak of to anyone. Aunt Nancy need not have been frightened that she would carry tales to New Moon.
“I wrote a whole poem yesterday when it rained and I couldn’t get out,” she said. “It began,

> I sit by the western window

That looks on Malvern Bay﻿—”

“Am I not to hear the whole of it?” asked Dean, who knew perfectly well that Emily was hoping that he would ask it.
Emily delightedly repeated the whole poem. When she came to the two lines she liked best in it,

> Perhaps in those wooded islands

That gem the proud bay’s breast﻿—

“别拿那个名目叫它。那名字比裙撑还糟。刘海、裙撑——它们伤我。我喜欢你白眉上拍岸的那道黑浪——可别再叫它刘海——永远别。”
“这词儿确实难听。写诗我当然从来不用。”
由此迪恩·普里斯特发现了艾米丽写诗。回普里斯特池塘（Priest Pond）那一路，在冷杉清香的暮色里，他又差不多发现了她的其他一切：特威德走在两人中间，鼻子时不时轻轻碰碰主人的手，头顶树上的知更鸟在余光里欢快地吹哨。
十个人里有九个，艾米丽对之都缄默矜持；可迪恩·普里斯特是她族类里的人，她当下就觉出来了。他有资格进她内殿，她毫不设防地让了进去。她对他无话不谈。
再说，她又活过来了——经历了那阵仿佛悬在生死之间可怕的时辰，她又感到了活着的奇妙震颤。她感到——如她后来写信给爸爸的——“好像有只小鸟在我心里唱歌”。哦，脚底下绿草的感觉多好！
她把自己的事、自己的行止，统统讲给他听。只有一样没讲——她对伊尔丝妈妈的忧心。那件事她对谁也开不了口。南希姑婆本不必担心她把闲话捎去新月庄园。
“昨天下雨出不去，我写了一整首诗，”她说。“开头是——

> 我坐在西窗旁边，

> 望着马尔文湾——”

“整首我听不着吗？”迪恩问。他明明白白知道，艾米丽正巴着他问。
艾米丽欢欢喜喜把整首背了。背到她顶喜欢的两行——

But I’ve got Father’s forehead, and Grandma Starr’s hair and eyes, and Great-Uncle George’s nose, and Aunt Nancy’s hands, and Cousin Susan’s elbows, and Great-great-Grandmother Murray’s ankles, and Grandfather Murray’s eyebrows.”
Dean Priest laughed.
“A ragbag﻿—as we all are,” he said. “But your soul is your own, and fire-new, I’ll swear to that.”
“Oh, I’m so glad I like you,” said Emily impulsively. “It would be *hateful* to think anyone I didn’t like had saved my life. I don’t mind *your* saving it a bit.”
“That’s good. Because you see your life belongs to me henceforth. Since I saved it it’s mine. Never forget that.”
Emily felt an odd sensation of rebellion. She didn’t fancy the idea of her life belonging to anybody but herself﻿—not even to anybody she liked as much as she liked Dean Priest. Dean, watching her, saw it and smiled his whimsical smile that always seemed to have so much more in it than mere smiling.
“That doesn’t quite suit you? Ah, you see one pays a penalty when one reaches out for something beyond the ordinary. One pays for it in bondage of some kind or other. Take your wonderful aster home and keep it as long as you can. It has cost you your freedom.”
He was laughing﻿—he was only joking, of course﻿—yet Emily felt as if a cobweb fetter had been flung round her. Yielding to a sudden impulse she flung the big aster on the ground and set her foot on it.
Dean Priest looked on amusedly. His strange eyes were very kindly as he met hers.
“You rare thing﻿—you vivid thing﻿—you starry thing! We are going to be good friends﻿—we *are* good friends. I’m coming up to Wyther Grange tomorrow to see those descriptions you’ve written of Caroline and my venerable Aunt in your Jimmy-book. I feel sure they’re delicious. Here’s your path﻿—don’t go roaming again so far from civilization. Good night, my Star of the Morning.”
He stood at the crossroad and watched her out of sight.
“What a child!” he muttered. “I’ll never forget her eyes as she lay there on the edge of death﻿—the dauntless little soul﻿—and I’ve never seen a creature who seemed so full of sheer joy in existence. She is Douglas Starr’s child﻿—*he* never called me Jarback.”
He stooped and picked up the broken aster. Emily’s heel had met it squarely and it was badly crushed. But he put it away that night between the leaves of an old volume of *Jane Eyre*, where he had marked a verse﻿—

迪恩·普里斯特大笑。
“一床百衲被——人人都这样，”他说。“可你的灵魂是你自己的，崭新出炉，这个我敢起誓。”
“哦，我真高兴我喜欢你，”艾米丽冲口而出。“要是救我命的是个我不喜欢的人，那多可恨。你的救法我一点儿不嫌。”
“这就好。因为你看，打今儿起，你的命归我了。我救的，就是我的。永远别忘了。”
艾米丽心头掠过一阵古怪的反抗。她不喜欢自己的命属于任何人——哪怕属于她这么喜欢的迪恩·普里斯特。迪恩瞧着她，看出来了，露出他那乖觉的笑——那笑里永远像装着比笑多得多的东西。
“这不合你意？啊，你看，谁伸手去够超乎寻常的东西，谁就要付代价。用这种或那种形式的束缚来付。把你这支奇妙的紫菀带回家，能留多久留多久。它花掉了你的自由。”
他在笑——当然只是玩笑——可艾米丽只觉一副蛛丝的镣铐朝她甩来。她一时冲动，把那大紫菀摔在地上，一脚踏了上去。
迪恩·普里斯特饶有兴味地看着。他遇上她的眼光时，那双奇怪的眼睛和善极了。
“你这稀罕物——你这鲜活物——你这星星物！我们要做好朋友了——我们已经是好朋友了。明天我上威瑟农庄，去看你吉米本子里写卡罗琳和我那位老尊姑的描写。我拿准写得有味儿极了。喏，你的路到了——别再逛到离人烟这么远的地方去。晚安，我的晨星。”
他站在岔路口，望她走远，望不见了。
“多好的一个孩子！”他喃喃道。“我一辈子忘不了她趴在死亡边缘的那双眼睛——无所畏惧的小灵魂——我还从没见过谁，浑身洋溢着这么纯粹的生之欢喜。她是道格拉斯·斯塔尔的孩子——他从来不叫我歪背。”
他俯身拾起那枝踩断的紫菀。艾米丽的鞋跟正正踩中，它已零落不堪。可当夜他把它夹进一册旧书里——《简·爱》——他在里头标过一行诗——

===Original===

> All glorious rose upon my sight

That child of shower and gleam.

===Chinese===

> 那雨和阳光的孩子，

> 辉辉煌煌升上我的眼帘。

## XXVII The Vow of Emily / 二十七 艾米丽的誓言
===Original===

In Dean Priest Emily found, for the first time since her father had died, a companion who could fully sympathize. She was always at her best with him, with a delightful feeling of being understood. To love is easy and therefore common﻿—but to *understand*﻿—how rare it is! They roamed wonderlands of fancy together in the magic August days that followed upon Emily’s adventure on the bay shore, talked together of exquisite, immortal things, and were at home with “nature’s old felicities” of which Wordsworth so happily speaks.
Emily showed him all the poetry and “descriptions” in her “Jimmy-book” and he read them gravely, and, exactly as Father had done, made little criticisms that did not hurt her because she knew they were just. As for Dean Priest, a certain secret wellspring of fancy that had long seemed dry bubbled up in him sparklingly again.
“You make me believe in fairies, whether I will or no,” he told her, “and that means youth. As long as you believe in fairies you can’t grow old.”
“But I can’t believe in fairies myself,” protested Emily sorrowfully. “I wish I could.”
“But *you* are a fairy yourself﻿—or you wouldn’t be able to find fairyland. You can’t buy a ticket there, you know. Either the fairies themselves give you your passport at your christening﻿—or they don’t. That is all there is to it.”
“Isn’t ’Fairyland’ the *loveliest* word?” said Emily dreamily.
“Because it means everything the human heart desires,” said Dean.
When he talked to her Emily felt as if she were looking into some enchanted mirror where her own dreams and secret hopes were reflected back to her with added charm. If Dean Priest were a cynic he showed no cynicism to Emily. But in her company he was not a cynic; he had shed his years and became a boy again with a boy’s untainted visions. She loved him for the world he opened to her view.
There was such fun in him, too﻿—such sly, surprising fun. He told her jokes﻿—he made her laugh. He told her strange old tales of forgotten gods who were very beautiful﻿—of court festivals and the bridals of kings. He seemed to have the history of the whole world at his fingers’ ends. He described things to her in unforgettable phrases as they walked by the bay shore or sat in the overgrown, shadowy old garden of Wyther Grange.

===Chinese===

在迪安·普里斯特（Dean Priest）身上，艾米丽自父亲去世以来头一回找到了一位能全然贴心的伴侣。同他在一起，她总是处在自己最好的状态，怀着一种被理解的愉快。爱是容易的，因而寻常——可*理解*——多么难得！在海湾岸边那场历险之后的那些魔幻的八月日子里，他们一同漫游想象的奇境，一同谈论精微的不朽之物，安然亲昵于华兹华斯（Wordsworth）欢然吟咏的“自然的老欢愉”。
她把“吉米簿”里所有的诗和“描写”全给他看，他郑重地读，又同爸爸当年一模一样，提些小小的批评，伤不着她，因为她知道那些批评是公道的。至于迪安·普里斯特，他那眼久已干涸的想象的秘泉，又在他心里晶亮亮地涌起来了。
“你叫我不由得不信仙子。”他对她说，“这就意味着年轻。只要还信仙子，人就老不了。”
“可我自己没法信仙子。”艾米丽难过地分辩。“我要能信就好了。”
“可*你*自己就是仙子呀——不然你根本找不到仙界。仙界的票是买不来的，你知道。要么仙子们在你受洗那天亲自给你护照——要么不给。就这么回事。”
“‘仙界’难道不是*顶顶可爱*的词吗？”艾米丽悠悠地说。
“因为它意味着人心渴望的一切。”迪安说。
他同她说话时，艾米丽觉得自己仿佛在照一面魔镜，自己的梦和隐秘的盼望映还给她，还添了光彩。迪安·普里斯特若是个愤世者，他至少没对艾米丽愤世。同她做伴时他不愤世；他卸下了年纪，重新变回一个男孩，怀着男孩未经玷污的幻梦。她爱他，为他向她眼前打开的世界。
他身上还有那么多趣味——那种促狭的、出其不意的趣味。他给她讲笑话——逗她笑。他给她讲被遗忘的诸神的古老传说，他们美极了——讲宫廷的庆典和君王的婚典。全世界的信史仿佛都挂在他指尖上。他们沿着海湾岸边散步，或坐在威瑟庄园草木蔓生的幽暗老园子里时，他用一句句忘不掉的话向她描孷万物。

===Original===

When he spoke of Athens as “the City of the Violet Crown” Emily realized afresh what magic is made when the right words are wedded; and she loved to think of Rome as “the City of the Seven Hills.” Dean had been in Rome and Athens﻿—and almost everywhere else.
“I didn’t know anyone ever talked as you do except in books,” she told him.
Dean laughed﻿—with a little note of bitterness that was so often present in his laughter﻿—though less often with Emily than with other people. It was really his laughter that had won Dean his reputation for cynicism. People so often felt that he was laughing *at* them instead of *with* them.
“I’ve had only books for companions most of my life,” he said. “Is it any wonder I talk like them?”
“I’m sure I’ll like studying history after this,” said Emily; “except Canadian History. I’ll never like *it*﻿—it’s so dull. Not just at the first, when we belonged to France and there was plenty of fighting, but after that it’s nothing but politics.”
“The happiest countries, like the happiest women, have no history,” said Dean.
“I hope *I’ll* have a history,” cried Emily. “I want a *thrilling* career.”
“We all do, foolish one. Do you know what makes history? Pain﻿—and shame﻿—and rebellion﻿—and bloodshed and heartache. Star, ask yourself how many hearts ached﻿—and broke﻿—to make those crimson and purple pages in history that you find so enthralling. I told you the story of Leonidas and his Spartans the other day. They had mothers, sisters and sweethearts. If they could have fought a bloodless battle at the polls wouldn’t it have been better﻿—if not so dramatic.”
“I﻿—can’t﻿—*feel*﻿—that way,” said Emily confusedly. She was not old enough to think or say, as she would say ten years later, “The heroes of Thermopylæ have been an inspiration to humanity for centuries. What squabble around a ballot-box will ever be that?”
“And, like all female creatures, you form your opinions by your feelings. Well, hope for your thrilling career﻿—but remember that if there is to be drama in your life *somebody* must pay the piper in the coin of suffering. If not you﻿—then someone else.”
“Oh, no, I wouldn’t like *that*.”
“Then be content with fewer thrills. What about your tumble over the bank down there? That came near being a tragedy. What if I hadn’t found you?”
“But you *did* find me,” cried Emily. “I like near escapes﻿—after they’re over,” she added.

===Chinese===

他把雅典说成“紫冠之城”时，艾米丽重新明白了恰当的词一成婚，就生出怎样的魔法；她也爱把罗马想成“七丘之城”。罗马和雅典迪安都去过——几乎哪儿都去过。
“我从前不知道书以外真有人像你这样说话。”她告诉他。
迪安笑了——笑声里带着一丝苦味，他的笑里常有的那种——只是同艾米丽在一处时比同旁人少些。其实，正是他的笑给迪安挣来了愤世的名声。人们时常觉得，他是在笑*他们*，而不是同*他们*一起笑。
“我大半辈子只有书做伴。”他说。“我说话像书，还奇怪吗？”
“往后我准会喜欢学历史了。”艾米丽说，“只除了加拿大史。*它*我永远喜欢不上——太乏味。倒不全是开头，我们归属法国、仗打得热闹那阵，打那以后就净是政治了。”
“最幸福的国家，同最幸福的女人一样，没有历史。”迪安说。
“我希望*我*能有历史。”艾米丽喊道。“我要一段*惊心动魄*的生涯。”
“谁不要呢，傻孩子。你知道历史是什么做成的吗？是痛苦——是耻辱——是反叛——是流血和心碎。星星，你问问自己，史册上那些你读得入迷的猩红紫暗的书页，是多少颗心疼过——碎过——换来的。前几天我给你讲了列奥尼达和他的斯巴达人的故事。他们也有母亲、姐妹和爱人。倚若他们能在投票箱前打一场不流血的仗，岂不更好——只是不那么悲壮。”
“我——没法——*那么*——想。”艾米丽迷乱地说。她还太年轻，想不到也说不出十年后她会说的那句话：“温泉关的英雄们千百年来一直鼓舞着人类。哪一回投票箱边的争执能比得上？”
“而且，同一切雌性动物一样，你凭感情下断语。好，盼你的惊心动魄去吧——只是记住：你的生活里若要有戏，*总得有人*拿受苦的钱币付乐师的钱。不是你——就是别人。”
“哦，不，我可不要*那样*。”
“那就安于少些惊心动魄。你那天从坡上撑下去怎么样？差一点就是悲剧。我要是没找着你呢？”
“可你*就是*找着了呀。”艾米丽喊道。“死里逃生——逃完之后——我喜欢。”她补了一句。
“要是人人一向都快活，就没有什么可读的了。”
特威德（Tweed）给他们俩的漫游做了第三个，艾米丽喜欢上了它，对猫族的忠心却一分未减。
“我拿这部分心思喜欢猫，拿那部分心思喜欢狗。”她说。

===Original===

“If everybody had always been happy there’d be nothing to read about.”
Tweed made a third in their rambles and Emily grew very fond of him, without losing any of her loyalty to the pussy folk.
“I like cats with one part of my mind and dogs with another part,” she said.
“I like cats but I never keep one,” Dean said. “They’re too exacting﻿—they ask too much. Dogs want only love but cats demand worship. They have never got over the Bubastis habit of godship.”
Emily understood this﻿—he had told her all about old Egypt and the goddess Pasht﻿—but she did not quite agree with him.
“Kittens don’t want to be worshipped,” she said. “They just want to be cuddled.”
“By their priestesses﻿—yes. If you had been born on the banks of the Nile five thousand years ago, Emily, you would have been a priestess of Pasht﻿—an adorable, slim, brown creature with a fillet of gold around your black hair and bands of silver on those ankles Aunt Nancy admires, with dozens of sacred little godlings frisking around you under the palms of the temple courts.”
“Oh,” gasped Emily rapturously, “that gave me *the flash*. And,” she added wonderingly, “just for a moment it made me *homesick*, too. Why?”
“Why? Because I haven’t a doubt you *were* just such a priestess in a former incarnation and my words reminded your soul of it. Do you believe in the doctrine of the transmigration of souls, Star? But of course not﻿—brought up by the true-blue Calvinists of New Moon.”
“What does it mean?” asked Emily, and when Dean explained it to her she thought it a very delightful belief but was quite sure Aunt Elizabeth would not approve of it.
“So I won’t believe it﻿—yet,” she said gravely.
Then it all came to an end quite suddenly. It had been taken for granted by all concerned that Emily was to stay at Wyther Grange until the end of August. But in mid-August Aunt Nancy said suddenly to her one day,
“Go home, Emily. I’m tired of you. I like you very well﻿—you’re not stupid and you’re passably pretty and you’ve behaved exceedingly well﻿—tell Elizabeth you do the Murrays credit﻿—but I’m tired of you. Go home.”
Emily’s feelings were mixed. It hurt her to be told Aunt Nancy was tired of her﻿—it would hurt anyone. It rankled in her for several days until she thought of a sharp answer she might have made Aunt Nancy and wrote it down in her Jimmy-book. She felt quite as relieved then as if she had really said it.

===Chinese===

“猫我喜欢，可从来只养。”迪安说。“它们太苛——索求太多。狗只要爱，猫却要人拜。它们到底没改掉布巴斯提斯（Bubastis）那套做神的积习。”
这层艾米丽懂——古埃及和帕什特（Pasht）女神的事他都讲给她听过——可她不大服他。
“小猫崽子并不要人拜。”她说。“它们只要人搂。”
“要她们的女祭司搂——那是。艾米丽，你若是五千年前生在尼罗河边，准是帕什特的一位女祭司——一个可爱的、纤瘦的棕色人儿，黑发上箍一道金发带，姨婆夸的那对脚踝上套着银环，庙院棕榈树下，几十只神圣的小神猫绕着你欢蹦。”
“哦，”艾米丽喜得倒抽一口气，“*灵光*来了。而且，”她诧异地补充，“有一瞬间它还让我*想家*了呢。为什么？”
“为什么？因为我毫不怀疑，你前生就是这样一位女祭司，我的话让你的魂灵想起了它。信不信灵魂转世之说，星星？当然不信——新月庄园铁杆加尔文宗把你带大的。”
“那是什么意思？”艾米丽问。迪安讲给她听之后，她觉得那是顶可爱的信仰，可又拿得准伊丽莎白姨妈不会赞成。
“所以我先不——信。”她郑重地说。
随后这一切戛然而止。众人都想当然地以为，艾米丽要在威瑟庄园住到八月底。可八月中的一天，姨婆忽然对她说：
“回家去吧，艾米丽。我腻味你了。我挺喜欢你——你不蠢，模样也过得去，行止无可挑剔——告诉伊丽莎白，你没给默里家丢脸——可我腻味你了。回家去吧。”
艾米丽百感交集。听姨婆说腻味了她，她受了伤——谁都得受伤。这口气在心里烂了好几天，直到她想起一句本可以回敬姨婆的尖刻话，把它写进了吉米簿。写完，她痛快得同真说了出来一样。

===Original===

And she was sorry to leave Wyther Grange; she had grown to love the old beautiful house, with its flavour of hidden secrets﻿—a flavour that was wholly a trick of its architecture, for there had never been anything in it but the simple tale of births and deaths and marriages and everyday living that most houses have. She was sorry to leave the bay shore and the quaint garden and the gazing-ball and the chessy-cat and the Pink Room bed of freedom; and most of all she was sorry to leave Dean Priest. But on the other hand it was delightful to think of going back to New Moon and all the loved ones there﻿—Teddy and his dear whistle, Ilse and her stimulating comradeship, Perry with his determined reaching up for higher things, Saucy Sal and the new kitten that must be needing proper training now, and the fairy world of the *Midsummer Night’s Dream*. Cousin Jimmy’s garden would be in its prime of splendour, the August apples would be ripe. Suddenly, Emily was very ready to go. She packed her little black box jubilantly and found it an excellent chance to work in neatly a certain line from a poem Dean had recently read to her which had captured her fancy.
“ ‘Goodbye, proud world, I’m going home,’ ” she declaimed feelingly, standing at the top of the long, dark, shining staircase and apostrophizing the row of grim Priest photographs hanging on the wall.
But she was much annoyed over one thing. Aunt Nancy would not give her back the picture Teddy had painted.
“I’m going to keep it,” Aunt Nancy said, grinning and shaking her gold tassels. “Some day that picture will be worth something as the early effort of a famous artist.”
“I only lent it to you﻿—I told you I only lent it to you,” said Emily indignantly.
“I’m an unscrupulous old demon,” said Aunt Nancy coolly. “That is what the Priests all call me behind my back. Don’t they, Caroline? May as well have the game as the name. I happen to have a fancy for that picture, that’s all. I’m going to frame it and hang it here in my parlour. But I’ll leave it to you in my will﻿—that and the chessy-cat and the gazing-ball and my gold earrings. Nothing else﻿—I’m not going to leave you a cent of my money﻿—never count on that.”
“I don’t want it,” said Emily loftily. “I’m going to earn heaps of money for myself. But it isn’t fair of you to keep my picture. It was given to me.”
“I never was fair,” said Aunt Nancy. “Was I, Caroline?”

===Chinese===

离开威瑟庄园她也舍不得；她已爱上这座古老华美的宅子，爱它那股藏满秘密的气味——其实全然是建筑玩的花样，它里面从来只有大多数房子都有的那种简单故事：生老病死、婚嫁、日常起居。她舍不得海湾岸边那座古雅的园子、观景球、柴郡猫门环，和粉红房间里那张自由的床；最舍不得的是迪安·普里斯特。可另一方面，一想到回新月庄园、回到那儿所有亲爱的一切——泰迪同他那亲爱的口哨，伊尔丝同她那叫人振奋的情谊，佩里同他一个劲往上够的高处，调皮的萨尔同那只如今准亟需正规管教的小猫崽，还有《仲夏夜之梦》（*Midsummer Night's Dream*）的仙界——又叫人心花怒放。吉米表哥的园子正当极盛，八月的苹果该熟了。忽然间，艾米丽归心似箭。她欢天喜地收拾她的小黑箱，还觉着正好借机漂亮地用上一句诗——迪安新近念给她听的，正抓着她的心思。
“‘别了，骄傲的世界，我回家去了。’”她站在长长、幽暗、亮堂的楼梯顶上，感情充沛地朗诵，冲着墙上那排神情冷峻的普里斯特家族相片致意。
可有一件事叫她气恼极了。姨婆不肯还她泰迪画的那张画。
“我要留下它。”姨婆呗嘴笑，金穗子直晃。“有朝一日，这张画作为某位名画家早年的手笔，会值钱的。”
“我只是借给您——我说过只是借给您的。”艾米丽愤愤地说。
“我是个没心没肺的老恶魔。”姨婆凉凉地说。“普里斯特家人背后都这么叫我。是不是，卡罗琳？有名就该有实。我碰巧稀罕那张画，就这么回事。我要给它配上框，挂在我这客厅里。不过我遗嘱里会把它留给你——它，还有柴郡猫门环、观景球、我的金耳环。别的没有——我的钱一个子儿也不留给你——死了这条心。”
“我不要。”艾米丽傲然说。“我自己要挣大把的钱呢。可您扣我的画不公平。那是人家送我的。”
“我从来不公平。”姨婆说。“是不是，卡罗琳？

===Original===

“No,” said Caroline shrewishly.
“You see. Now don’t make a fuss, Emily. You’ve been a very good child but I feel that I’ve done my duty by you for this year. Go back to New Moon and when Elizabeth won’t let you do things tell her *I* always let you. I don’t know if it will do any good but try it. Elizabeth, like everyone else related to me, is always wondering what I’m going to do with my money.”
Cousin Jimmy came over for Emily. How glad she was to see his kind face with its gentle, elfish eyes and forked beard again! But she felt very badly when she turned to Dean.
“If you like I’ll kiss you goodbye,” she said chokily.
Emily did not like kissing people. She did not really want to kiss Dean but she liked him so much she thought she ought to extend all the courtesies to him.
Dean looked down smiling into her face, so young, so pure, so softly curved.
“No, I don’t want you to kiss me﻿—yet. And our first kiss mustn’t have the flavour of goodbye. It would be a bad omen. Star O’ Morning, I’m sorry you’re going. But I’ll see you again before long. My oldest sister lives in Blair Water, you know, and I feel a sudden access of brotherly affection towards her. I seem to see myself visiting her very often henceforth. In the meantime remember you have promised to write me every week. And I’ll write you.”
“Nice fat letters,” coaxed Emily. “I love fat letters.”
“Fat! They’ll be positively corpulent, Star. Now, I’m not even going to *say* goodbye. Let’s make a pact, Star. We’ll never *say* goodbye to each other. We’ll just smile and go.”
Emily made a gallant effort﻿—smiled﻿—and went. Aunt Nancy and Caroline returned to the back parlour and their cribbage. Dean Priest whistled for Tweed and went to the bay shore. He was so lonely that he laughed at himself.
Emily and Cousin Jimmy had so much to talk of that the drive home seemed very short.
New Moon was white in the evening sunshine which also lay with exceeding mellowness on the grey old barns. The Three Princesses, shooting up against the silvery sky, were as remote and princessly as ever. The old gulf was singing away down over the fields.
Aunt Laura came running out to meet them, her lovely blue eyes shining with pleasure.

“不。”卡罗琳泼辣地说。
“听见了吧。行了，别闹了，艾米丽。你一直是个顶乖的孩子，可我觉着，今年的本分我算尽到了。回新月庄园去吧，往后伊丽莎白不让你做什么，就告诉她*我*从来都让。管不管用我不知道，试试吧。伊丽莎白，同所有跟我沾亲的人一样，一天到晚琢麽着我的钱打算怎么处置呢。”
吉米表哥过来接艾米丽。再见到他那张和善的脸、那双温柔的精灵眼和分叉的胡须，她多高兴啊！可转向迪安时，她心里很不好受。
“你若愿意，我可以吻你道别。”她哑着嗓说。
艾米丽不喜欢吻人。她并不是真想吻迪安，可她太喜欢他了，觉得该把一切礼数都给他。
迪安低头笑吟吟地望着她的脸，那么年轻，那么纯净，线条那么柔和。
“不，先不要你吻我——还不到时候。我们的第一个吻，不该带着道别的味道。那是个凶兆。晨星啊，你走了我真不舍。可要不了多久我就再见得着你。我最老的姐姐就住在布莱尔沃特，你知道吧，我对她忽然涌起一股手足之情。我仿佛看见，往后我要常去看她。这期间记住，你答应过每礼拜给我写信。我也写给你。”
“肥肥的厚信。”艾米丽央告。“我爱肥肥的信。”
“肥！准是肥得臃肿，星星。这样吧，‘再见’两个字我都*不*说。咱们订个约，星星。我们永远不对彼此*说*再见。我们只微笑，然后走。”
艾米丽拿出全副勇气——笑了——走了。姨婆同卡罗琳回到后客厅玩她们的克里比奇。迪安·普里斯特吹哨唤上特威德，往海湾岸边去了。他孤单得很，只好笑自己。
艾米丽同吉米表哥一路上话多得很，回家的路显得短短的。
夕照里的新月庄园一片洁白，阳光又极其醇厚地铺在灰色的老谷仓上。三公主拔地直上银亮的天空，依旧那么高远，那么公主。古老的海湾在田野尽头远远地唱着。
劳拉姨妈跑出来迎他们，可爱的蓝眼睛欢喜得发亮。

Aunt Elizabeth was in the cookhouse preparing supper and only shook hands with Emily, but looked a trifle less grim and stately than usual, and she had made Emily’s favourite cream-puffs for supper. Perry was hanging about, barefooted and sunburned, to tell her all the gossip of kittens and calves and little pigs and the new foal. Ilse came swooping over, and Emily discovered she had forgotten how vivid Ilse was﻿—how brilliant her amber eyes, how golden her mane of spun-silk hair, looking more golden than ever under the bright blue silk tam Mrs. Simms had bought her in Shrewsbury. As an article of dress, that loud tam made Laura Murray’s eyes and sensibilities ache, but its colour certainly did set off Ilse’s wonderful hair. She engulfed Emily in a rapturous embrace and quarrelled bitterly with her ten minutes later over the fact that Emily refused to give her Saucy Sal’s sole surviving kitten.
“I ought to have it, you doddering hyena,” stormed Ilse. “It’s as much mine as yours, pig! Our old barn cat is its father.”
“Such talk is not decent,” said Aunt Elizabeth, pale with horror. “And if you two children are going to quarrel over that kitten I’ll have it drowned﻿—remember that.”
Ilse was finally appeased by Emily’s offering to let her name the kitten and have a half interest in it. Ilse named it Daffodil. Emily did not think this suitable, since, from the fact of Cousin Jimmy referring to it as Little Tommy, she suspected it was of the sterner sex. But rather than again provoke Aunt Elizabeth’s wrath by discussing tabooed subjects, she agreed.
“I can call it Daff,” she thought. “That sounds more *masculine*.”
The kitten was a delicate bit of striped greyness that reminded Emily of her dear lost Mikes. And it smelled so nice﻿—of warmth and clean furriness, with whiffs of the clover hay where Saucy Sal had made her mother-nest.
After supper she heard Teddy’s whistle in the old orchard﻿—the same enchanting call. Emily flew out to greet him﻿—after all, there was nobody just like Teddy in the world. They had an ecstatic scamper up to the Tansy Patch to see a new puppy that Dr. Burnley had given Teddy. Mrs.

伊丽莎白姨妈在户外灶屋备晚饭，只同艾米丽握了握手，可脸色比平日少了三分冷峻威严，晚饭还做了艾米丽最爱的奶油泡芙。佩里光着脚、晒得黑红，在旁边转来转去，要给她讲小猫、牛犊、小猪和新马驹的全部新闻。伊尔丝一阵风卷过来，艾米丽才发觉自己忘了伊尔丝有多鲜活——琥珀色的眼睛多亮，丝缎般的鬃发多金——在西姆斯太太从什鲁斯伯里给她买的那顶亮蓝丝绸贝雷帽底下，头发显得更金了。论穿戴，那顶扎眼的帽子看得劳拉·默里眼睛和神经都疼，可那颜色确实衬得伊尔丝一头美发格外出彩。她把艾米丽裹进一阵狂喜的拥抱，十分钟后又同她狠狠吵了一架，为的是艾米丽不肯把调皮的萨尔唯一存活的小猫给她。
“该归我，你这个老糊涂的鬣狗。”伊尔丝大发雷霆。“它同你的一样有一半是我的，猪！我们家的老谷仓猫就是它爹。”
“这种话不像样。”伊丽莎白姨妈说，吓得脸都白了。“你们两个要是为那只小猫崽吵架，我就叫人把它淹了——记住。”
最后艾米丽提出让伊尔丝给小猫起名、并享有一半产权，伊尔丝才算消了气。伊尔丝给它起名黄水仙（Daffodil）。艾米丽觉得不合适，因为从吉米表哥管它叫小汤米这一点推断，她疑心它属于雄的那一边。可为了不再因谈论犯忌的话题触怒伊丽莎白姨妈，她依了。
“我可以叫它阿黄。”她想。“听着*雄*些。”
小猫是一小团娇弱的灰条纹，让艾米丽想起她失去的亲爱的迈克们。它气味好闻极了——暖烘烘、干净的绒毛味，还飘着三叶草干草的气息——调皮的萨尔就在那儿筑的产巢。

Kent did not seem very glad to see Emily﻿—she was colder and more remote than ever, and she sat and watched the two children playing with the chubby little pup with a smouldering fire in her dark eyes that made Emily vaguely uncomfortable whenever she happened to glance up and encounter it. Never before had she sensed Mrs. Kent’s dislike for her so keenly as that night.
“Why doesn’t your mother like me?” she asked Teddy bluntly, when they carried little Leo to the barn for the night.
“Because *I* do,” said Teddy briefly. “She doesn’t like *anything* I like. I’m afraid she’ll poison Leo very soon. I﻿—I wish she wasn’t so fond of me,” he burst out, in the beginning of a revolt against this abnormal jealousy of love, which he felt rather than understood to be a fetter that was becoming galling. “She says she won’t let me take up Latin and Algebra this year﻿—you know Miss Brownell said I might﻿—because I’m not to go to college. She says she can’t bear to part from me﻿—ever. I don’t care about the Latin and stuff﻿—but I want to learn to be an artist﻿—I want to go away some day to the schools where they teach that. She won’t let me﻿—she hates my pictures now because she thinks I like them better than her. I *don’t*﻿—I love Mother﻿—she’s awful sweet and good to me every other way. But she thinks I do﻿—and she’s burned some of them. I know she has. They’re missing from the barn wall and I can’t find them anywhere. If she does anything to Leo﻿—I’ll﻿—I’ll *hate* her.”
“Tell her that,” said Emily coolly, with some of the Murray shrewdness coming uppermost in her. “She doesn’t know that *you* know she poisoned Smoke and Buttercup. Tell her you do know it and that if she does anything to Leo you won’t love her any more. She’ll be so frightened of your *not* loving her that she won’t meddle with Leo﻿—I *know*. Tell her gently﻿—don’t hurt her feelings﻿—but *tell* her. It will,” concluded Emily, with a killing imitation of Aunt Elizabeth delivering an ultimatum, “be better for all concerned.”
“I believe I will,” said Teddy, much impressed. “I *can’t* have Leo disappear like my cats did﻿—he’s the only dog I’ve ever had and I’ve always wanted a dog. Oh, Emily, I’m glad you’re back!”
It was very nice to be told this﻿—especially by Teddy. Emily went home to New Moon happily.

太太见了艾米丽似乎并不大高兴——她比先前更冷淡、更疏远，坐在那儿看两个孩子逗那只胖乎乎的小狗，黑眼睛里闷着一团火，艾米丽偶尔抬头撞上它，便没来由地不自在。她对肯特太太的厌憎之感，从来没有哪晚像那晚体会得那么真切。
“你母亲为什么不喜欢我？”他们把小利奥（Leo）抱进谷仓过夜时，她直截了当地问泰迪。
“因为*我*喜欢。”泰迪简短地说。“*我*喜欢的她一概不喜欢。我怕她早晚会毒死利奥。我——我巴不得她别这么疼我。”他脱口而出，开始反抗这份变态的爱的独占——他还只是感觉到、并不真正懂得，这是副越勒越疼的镣铐。“她说今年不让我学拉丁文和代数了——你知道布朗内尔小姐都说我可以学——因为我不上大学。她说她受不了同我分开——永远受不了。拉丁文那些我倒不在乎——可我要学画画——我想有朝一日上教画的学堂去。她不让——她如今恨我的画，因为她以为我爱画胜过爱她。我才*没有*——我爱妈妈——别的样样她都甜、都好。可她以为我有——还烧掉了几张。我知道她烧了。谷仓墙上少了，我哪儿也找不着。她要是再对利奥下手——我就——我就*恨*她。”
“就这么告诉她。”艾米丽冷静地说，默里家的精明占了上风。“她不知道*你*已经知道烟儿和毛茛是她毒的。告诉她你知道，告诉她她若再对利奥下手，你就再不爱她了。她怕*失去*你的爱，怕得厉害，就不敢动利奥——我*敢保*。说的时候轻着点——别伤她的心——可一定*要*说。这样一来，”艾米丽结尾道，把伊丽莎白姨妈下最后通牒的架势模仿得入木三分，“对各有关方面都更为妥当。”
“我看我说。”泰迪大为折服。“我可*不能*让利奥像我的猫那样没了——它是我头一条狗，我一直想要条狗。哦，艾米丽，你回来真好！”
听人说这话真好——尤其出自泰迪之口。艾米丽快快活活地回了新月庄园。

===Original===

In the old kitchen the candles were lighted and their flames were dancing in the winds of the August night blowing through door and window.
“I suppose you’ll not like candles very well, Emily, after being used to lamps at Wyther Grange,” said Aunt Laura with a little sigh. It was one of the bitter, small things in Laura Murray’s life that Elizabeth’s tyranny extended to candles.
Emily looked around her thoughtfully. One candle sputtered and bobbed at her as if greeting her. One, with a long wick, glowed and smouldered like a sulky little demon. One had a tiny flame﻿—a sly, meditative candle. One swayed with a queer fiery grace in the draught from the door. One burned with a steady upright flame like a faithful soul.
“I﻿—don’t know﻿—Aunt Laura,” she answered slowly. “You can be﻿—friends﻿—with candles. I believe I like the candles best after all.”
Aunt Elizabeth, coming in from the cookhouse, heard her. Something like pleasure gleamed in her gulf-blue eyes.
“You have some sense in you,” she said.
“That’s the second compliment she has paid me,” thought Emily.
“I think Emily has grown taller since she went to Wyther Grange,” Aunt Laura said, looking at her rather wistfully.
Aunt Elizabeth, snuffing the candles, glanced sharply over her glasses.
“I can’t see it,” she said. “Her dress is just the same length on her.”
“I’m sure she has,” persisted Laura.
Cousin Jimmy, to settle the dispute, measured Emily by the sitting-room door. She just touched the former mark.
“You see,” said Aunt Elizabeth triumphantly, liking to be right even in this small matter.
“She looks﻿—different,” said Laura with a sigh.
Laura, after all, was right. Emily *had* grown, taller and older, in soul, if not in body. It was this change which Laura felt, as close and tender affection swiftly feels. The Emily who returned from Wyther Grange was not the Emily who had gone there. She was no longer wholly the child. Aunt Nancy’s family histories over which she had pondered, her enduring anguish over the story of Ilse’s mother, that terrible hour when she had lain cheek by jowl with death on the cliffs of the bay shore, her association with Dean Priest, all had combined to mature her intelligence and her emotions.

老厨房里点起了蜡烛，八月的夜风从门窗灌进来，烛焰纷纷起舞。
“在威瑟庄园点惯了油灯，艾米丽，我想你该不大喜欢蜡烛了吧。”劳拉姨妈轻轻叹了口气说。伊丽莎白的专横连蜡烛也要管，这是劳拉·默里生活里一桩小小的苦事。
艾米丽若有所思地环顾四周。一支烛噼啪一跳，朝她点点头，像在招呼她。一支烛芯长长的，阴燃着，闷闷地发亮，像个小怄气的魔鬼。一支只有一小簇火苗——一支狡黠的、沉思的烛。一支在门口的穿堂风里摇曳，摇出一种火辣辣的古怪风致。一支烧得笔直平稳，像一个忠实的灵魂。
“我——说不好——劳拉姨妈，”她慢慢回答。“蜡烛是——可以——做朋友的。我想来想去还是最喜欢蜡烛。”
伊丽莎白姨妈从灶屋进来，听见了。她海湾蓝的眼睛里掠过一丝近似欢喜的东西。
“你倒还有几分见识。”她说。
“这是她第二次夸我了。”艾米丽想。
“我看艾米丽去威瑟庄园以后长高了。”劳拉姨妈打量着她，不无怅然地说。
伊丽莎白姨妈正剪着烛芯，从眼镜上方狠狠瞥了一眼。
“我看不出来。”她说。“她裙子穿着还是原来那个长短。”
“我准知道她高了。”劳拉坚持。
为平息争端，吉米表哥让艾米丽靠起居室门框量了量。她刚好够到原来的记号。
“你看。”伊丽莎白姨妈得意地说，连这样的小事她也要占个对。
“她看着——不一样了。”劳拉叹口气说。
到底还是劳拉对。艾米丽确实长了——纵然身子没长，灵魂却长了高了。劳拉觉出的正是这个变化，亲近而温柔的深情总是敏于觉察。从威瑟庄园回来的艾米丽，已不是去时的那个艾米丽。她不再全然是个孩子。她反复啻嚼过的姨婆的家族旧事，她为伊尔丝母亲的故事熬过的长痛，海湾岸边悬崖上她同死亡脸贴脸躺过的那个可怕的时辰，她同迪安·普里斯特的交往——这一切合在一起，催熟了她的心智和情感。

When she went to the garret next morning and pulled out her precious little bundle of manuscripts to read them lovingly over she was amazed and rather grieved to find that they were not half so good as she had believed they were. Some of them were positively silly, she thought; she was ashamed of them﻿—so ashamed that she smuggled them down to the cookhouse stove and burned them, much to Aunt Elizabeth’s annoyance when she came to prepare dinner and found the firebox all choked up with charred paper.
Emily no longer wondered that Miss Brownell had made fun of them﻿—though this did not mellow her bitterness of remembrance in regard to that lady in any degree. The rest she put back on the sofa shelf, including “The Child of the Sea,” which still impressed her as fairly good, though not just the wonderful composition she had once deemed it. She felt that many passages could be rewritten to their advantage. Then she immediately began writing a new poem, “On Returning Home After Weeks’ Absence.” As everything and everybody connected with New Moon had to be mentioned in this poem it promised to be quite long and to furnish agreeable occupation for spare minutes in many weeks to come. It was very good to be home again.
“There is no place just like dear New Moon,” thought Emily.
One thing that marked her return﻿—one of those little household “epochs” that make a keener impression on the memory and imagination than perhaps their real importance warrants﻿—was the fact that she was given a room of her own. Aunt Elizabeth had found her unshared slumber too sweet a thing to be again surrendered. She decided that she could not put up any longer with a squirming bedfellow who asked unearthly questions at any hour of the night she took it into her head to do so.
So, after a long conference with Laura, it was settled that Emily was to have her mother’s room﻿—the “lookout” as it was called, though it was not really a lookout. But it occupied the place in New Moon, looking over the front door to the garden, that the real lookouts did in other Blair Water houses, so it went by that name. It had been prepared for Emily’s occupancy in her absence and when bedtime came on the first evening of her return Aunt Elizabeth curtly told her that henceforth she was to have her mother’s room.
“All to myself?” exclaimed Emily.
“Yes.
