“太像华兹华斯一声淡淡的回声了。《九月的海》——‘蓝而峻亮’——‘峻亮’——孩子，你怎么把形容词配得这么准的？《晨》——‘种种纠缠夜的秘密恐惧’——夜里纠缠人的恐惧，*你*懂什么？”
“我懂一些。”艾米丽斩钉截铁地说，想起在威瑟庄园的头一夜。
“《致死去的一天》——

> “她眉际凝着清凉的静，
> 唯有死者才能佩戴”——

“死者的眉际那清凉的静，你*见过*吗，艾米丽？”
“见过。”艾米丽轻声说，想起谷中老屋里那个灰蒙蒙的黎明。
“我就知道——不然你写不出*那个*——饶是这样——你几岁了，丫头？”
“十三，五月里刚满的。”
“哼！《致乔治·欧文夫人的幼子》——标题的学问你该研究研究，艾米丽——标题同万物一样有时兴。你的标题同新月庄园的蜡烛一样过时——

> “他睡得香甜，红唇紧贴，
> 像一朵美丽的花偎在她胸前”——

“余下的不值一读。《九月》——还有哪个月你没写过？——‘风吹的草场深及收成’——好句。《月光下的布莱尔沃特》——蛛丝，艾米丽，净是蛛丝。《新月庄园的园子》——

> “逗人的笑语和古老的歌，
> 属于快活的少女和男人”——

“好句——我猜新月庄园*确实*满是亡魂。‘死神的凶恶爪牙功德圆满’——这句子在艾迪生的年月兴许过得去，如今不行——如今不行了，艾米丽——

> “你天蓝的笑靥是坟茔，
> 百万枚埋葬的阳光在里面嬉戏”——

“糟透了，姑娘——糟透了。坟茔不是游乐场。你要是埋了，还嬉戏得动吗？”
艾米丽又扭又红。*为什么*她自己就看不见呢？*随便哪只*呆鹅都看得见啊。

===Original===

> “ ‘Sail onward, ships﻿—white wings, sail on,

Till past the horizon’s purple bar

You drift from sight.﻿—In flush of dawn

Sail on, and ’neath the evening star’﻿—

Trash﻿—trash﻿—and yet there’s a picture in it﻿—

> ‘Lap softly, purple waves. I dream,

And dreams are sweet﻿—I’ll wake no more’﻿—

Ah, but you’ll have to wake if you want to accomplish anything. Girl, you’ve used *purple* twice in the same poem.

> ‘Buttercups in a golden frenzy’﻿—

‘a golden frenzy’﻿—girl, I *see* the wind shaking the buttercups.

> ‘From the purple gates of the west I come’﻿—

You’re too fond of purple, Emily.”
“It’s such a lovely word,” said Emily.

> “ ‘Dreams that seem too bright to die’﻿—

*Seem* but never *are*, Emily﻿—

> ‘The luring voice of the echo, fame’﻿—

===Chinese===

> “‘扬帆向前吧，船——白色的翼，航行，
> 直到越过天际紫色的门闩，
> 你们淡出视野。——在黎明的红晕里
> 航行，在黄昏的星下”——

“垃圾——垃圾——不过里头有幅画——

> “轻拍吧，紫色的浪。我在做梦，
> 梦是甜的——我将不再醒来”——

“啊，可你要想成就点什么，就非醒不可。姑娘，同一首诗里你用了两回*紫色*。

> “‘金色狂热中的毛茛”——

“‘金色狂热’——姑娘，我*看得见*风在摇那些毛茛。

> “‘我从西天紫色的门里来”——

“你太贪恋紫色了，艾米丽。”
“这个词太可爱了嘛。”艾米丽说。

> “‘亮得仿佛不会死的梦”——

“*仿佛*而已，从来*不是*，艾米丽——

> “‘那诱惑的声音，名声的回声”——

===Original===

So you’ve heard it, too? It *is* a lure and for most of us only an echo. And that’s the last of the lot.”
Mr. Carpenter swept the little sheets aside, folded his arms on the desk, and looked over his glasses at Emily.
Emily looked back at him mutely, nervelessly. All the life seemed to have been drained out of her body and concentrated in her eyes.
“Ten good lines out of four hundred, Emily﻿—comparatively good, that is﻿—and all the rest balderdash﻿—balderdash, Emily.”
“I﻿—suppose so,” said Emily faintly.
Her eyes brimmed with tears﻿—her lips quivered. She could not help it. Pride was hopelessly submerged in the bitterness of her disappointment. She felt exactly like a candle that somebody had blown out.
“What are you crying for?” demanded Mr. Carpenter.
Emily blinked away the tears and tried to laugh.
“I﻿—I’m sorry﻿—you think it’s no good﻿—” she said.
Mr. Carpenter gave the desk a mighty thump.
“No good! Didn’t I tell you there were ten good lines? Jade, for ten righteous men Sodom had been spared.”
“Do you mean﻿—that﻿—after all﻿—” The candle was being relighted again.
“Of course, I mean. If at thirteen you can write ten good lines, at twenty you’ll write ten times ten﻿—if the gods are kind. Stop messing over months, though﻿—and don’t imagine you’re a genius either, if you *have* written ten decent lines. I think there’s *something* trying to speak through you﻿—but you’ll have to make yourself a fit instrument for it. You’ve got to work hard and sacrifice﻿—by gad, girl, you’ve chosen a jealous goddess. And she never lets her votaries go﻿—not even when she shuts her ears forever to their plea. What have you there?”
Emily, her heart thrilling, handed him her Jimmy-book. She was so happy that it shone through her whole being with a positive radiance. She saw her future, wonderful, brilliant﻿—oh, her goddess would listen to *her*﻿—“Emily B. Starr, the distinguished poet”﻿—“E. Byrd Starr, the rising young novelist”﻿—
She was recalled from her enchanting reverie by a chuckle from Mr. Carpenter. Emily wondered a little uneasily what he was laughing at. She didn’t think there was anything funny in *that* book.

===Chinese===

“这么说你也听见了？它*确是*诱惑，而对我们大多数人只是回声。这堆里最后一首了。”
卡彭特先生把那沓小纸片扫到一边，双臂往桌上一抱，从眼镜上方看着艾米丽。
艾米丽无言地、脱力地望回去。她全身的生气仿佛都被抽干了，全凝到眼睛里。
“四百行里十行好句，艾米丽——而且是相对的好——其余全是昏话——昏话，艾米丽。”
“我——想是吧。”艾米丽轻声说。
她眼里涌满了泪——嘴唇发颤。她忍不住。骄傲彻底淹没在失望的苦味里。她觉得自己活像一支叫人吹灭了的蜡烛。
“哭什么？”卡彭特先生喝问。
艾米丽眨掉眼泪，想笑一笑。
“我——对不起——您觉得它不行——”她说。
卡彭特先生擂了桌子一拳。
“不行！我没告诉你有十行好句吗？丫头，为了十个义人，所多玛都免于毁灭呢。”
“您是说——那——到底——”蜡烛重新点亮了。
“当然是这个意思。你十三岁能写十行好句，二十岁就能写十个十行——如果诸神仁慈。不过别在月份上瞎纠缠——也别自以为是天才，哪怕你*当真*写出过十行像样的。我看*有什么东西*正想借你说话——可你得把自己先打造成配得上它的器。你得苦干，得牺牲——老天，姑娘，你挑了位善妒的女神。她从不放她的信徒走——哪怕她永远掩耳不听他们的祈求。你那儿是什么？”
艾米丽心弦颤动着，把吉米簿递过去。她幸福得整个人放着光。她看见了自己的未来，美妙，辉煌——哦，她的女神会垂听*她*的——“艾米丽·伯·斯塔尔，卓越的诗人”——“艾·伯德·斯塔尔，冉冉升起的年轻小说家”——
卡彭特先生一声轻笑，把她从醉人的白日梦里拽了回来。艾米丽有点不安地纳闷他笑什么。她可不觉得*那本*簿子里有什么好笑的。

===Original===

It contained only three or four of her latest stories﻿—“The Butterfly Queen,” a little fairy tale; “The Disappointed House,” wherein she had woven a pretty dream of hopes come true after long years; “The Secret of the Glen,” which, in spite of its title, was a fanciful little dialogue between the Spirit of the Snow, the Spirit of the Grey Rain, the Spirit of Mist, and the Spirit of Moonshine.
“So you think I am not beautiful when I say my prayers?” said Mr. Carpenter.
Emily gasped﻿—realized what had happened﻿—made a frantic grab at her Jimmy-book﻿—missed it. Mr. Carpenter held it up beyond her reach and mocked at her.
She had given him the wrong Jimmy-book! And this one, oh, horrors, what was in it? Or rather, what wasn’t in it? Sketches of everyone in Blair Water﻿—and a full﻿—a very full﻿—description of Mr. Carpenter himself. Intent on describing him exactly, she had been as mercilessly lucid as she always was, especially in regard to the odd faces he made on mornings when he opened the school day with a prayer. Thanks to her dramatic knack of word painting, Mr. Carpenter *lived* in that sketch. Emily did not know it, but *he* did﻿—he saw himself as in a glass and the artistry of it pleased him so that he cared for nothing else. Besides, she had drawn his good points quite as clearly as his bad ones. And there were some sentences in it﻿—“He looks as if he knew a great deal that can never be any use to him”﻿—“I think he wears the black coat Mondays because it makes him feel that he hasn’t been drunk at all.” Who or what had taught the little jade these things? Oh, her goddess would not pass Emily by!
“I’m﻿—sorry,” said Emily, crimson with shame all over her dainty paleness.
“Why, I wouldn’t have missed this for all the poetry you’ve written or ever will write! By gad, it’s literature﻿—*literature*﻿—and you’re only thirteen. But you don’t know what’s ahead of you﻿—the stony hills﻿—the steep ascents﻿—the buffets﻿—the discouragements. Stay in the valley if you’re wise. Emily, *why* do you want to write? Give me your reason.”
“I want to be famous and rich,” said Emily coolly.
“Everybody does. Is that all?”
“No. I just *love* to write.”
“A better reason﻿—but not enough﻿—not enough. Tell me this﻿—if you knew you would be poor as a church mouse all your life﻿—if you knew you’d never have a line published﻿—would you still go on writing﻿—*would* you?”

===Chinese===

那本里只收了她最近的三四个故事——《蝴蝶女王》，一篇小童话；《失望屋》，她在里面织了一个多年之后心愿成真的美梦；《幽谷之谜》，名目虽如此，其实是雪之精魂、灰雨之精魂、雾之精魂和月光之精魂之间一段小小的奇想对答。
“这么说，你认为我祷告的时候不好看？”卡彭特先生说。
艾米丽倒抽一口气——明白出了什么事——发疯似的去抢那本吉米簿——没抢着。卡彭特先生把它举到她够不着的高处，朝她直乐。
她拿错吉米簿了！而这本里，哦，天哪，装着什么？不如说，没装着什么？布莱尔沃特人人的速写——还有一段对卡彭特先生本人的完整的——极其完整的——描写。她一心要描得分毫不差，下笔便一如既往地毫不留情地清楚，尤其是他那每早领祷开学时的种种怪相。亏得她那一手戏剧性的白描功夫，卡彭特先生在那篇速写里*活*了。艾米丽不知道，*他*知道——他像照镜子一样看见了自己，那笔艺术功力让他欢喜得别的全顾不上了。何况，他的好处她也画得同毛病一样清楚。里头还有几句——“他那样子，好像知道得很多，多到永远派不上用场”——“我想他礼拜一总穿黑外套，是因为那让他觉得自己根本没醉过”。是谁、是什么教给了这小丫头这些？哦，女神不会从艾米丽身边走过去了！
“我——对不起。”艾米丽说，白净的小脸从上到下红透了。
“啐，你就是把你写过、将写的诗全给我，也不换我错过这一篇！老天，这是文学——*文学*——而你才十三岁。可你不知道前头有什么——石山——陡坡——拳脚——灰心。识相就待在谷里。艾米丽，你*为什么*要写？把你的理由给我。”
“我想出名，想有钱。”艾米丽干干脆脆地说。
“谁不想。就这些？”
“不。我就是*爱*写。”
“好些的理由——可不够——不够。再答我——倘若你知道自己一辈子穷得像教堂里的老鼠——倘若你知道自己一行也发表不了——你还写不写——*写不写*？”

===Original===

“Of course I would,” said Emily disdainfully. “Why, I *have* to write﻿—I can’t help it by times﻿—I’ve just *got* to.”
“Oh﻿—then I’d waste my breath giving advice at all. If it’s *in* you to climb you must﻿—there are those who *must* lift their eyes to the hills﻿—they can’t breathe properly in the valleys. God help them if there’s some weakness in them that prevents their climbing. You don’t understand a word I’m saying﻿—yet. But go on﻿—climb! There, take your book and go home. Thirty years from now I will have a claim to distinction in the fact that Emily Byrd Starr was once a pupil of mine. Go﻿—go﻿—before I remember what a disrespectful baggage you are to write such stuff about me and be properly enraged.”
Emily went, still a bit scared but oddly exultant behind her fright. She was so happy that her happiness seemed to irradiate the world with its own splendour. All the sweet sounds of nature around her seemed like the broken words of her own delight. Mr. Carpenter watched her out of sight from the old worn threshold.
“Wind﻿—and flame﻿—and sea!” he muttered. “Nature is always taking us by surprise. This child has﻿—what I have never had and would have made any sacrifice to have. But ‘the gods don’t allow us to be in their debt’﻿—she will pay for it﻿—she will pay.”
At sunset Emily sat in the lookout room. It was flooded with soft splendour. Outside, in sky and trees, were delicate tintings and aerial sounds. Down in the garden Daffy was chasing dead leaves along the red walks. The sight of his sleek, striped sides, the grace of his movements, gave her pleasure﻿—as did the beautiful, even, glossy furrows of the ploughed fields beyond the lane, and the first faint white star in the crystal-green sky.
The wind of the autumn night was blowing trumpets of fairyland on the hills; and over in Lofty John’s bush was laughter﻿—like the laughter of fauns. Ilse and Perry and Teddy were waiting there for her﻿—they had made a tryst for a twilight romp. She would go to them﻿—presently﻿—not yet. She was so full of rapture that she must write it out before she went back from her world of dreams to the world of reality. Once she would have poured it into a letter to her father. She could no longer do that. But on the table before her lay a brand-new Jimmy-book. She pulled it towards her, took up her pen, and on its first virgin page she wrote.

===Chinese===

“当然写。”艾米丽不屑地说。“啐，我*非*写不可——时不时忍不住——就是*非*写不可。”
“哦——那我劝也白劝了。攀爬若*长*在你身上，你就必须爬——世上有一种人，*非*要举目望山不可——谷里他们喘不匀气。他们身上若偏偏带着爬不动的弱处，就求上帝保佑他们吧。我一个字你也没听懂——眼下。可去吧——爬吧！行了，拿上你的簿子回家去。三十年后，凭着艾米丽·伯德·斯塔尔做过我的学生这一条，我也就算有份名气了。去吧——去吧——趁我还没想起你是个多没大没小的东西、竟敢写我这种东西，还没正经动怒。”
艾米丽走了，还有点怕，怕底下却奇异地翻腾着狂喜。她幸福得那幸福仿佛用自己的光辉照亮了世界。四围大自然的种种甜美声响，都像她自己的欢喜摔碎的言语。卡彭特先生立在旧门槛上，望她走得不见了。
“风——还有火——还有海！”他咕哝。“大自然总爱叫人吃惊。这孩子身上有——我从来没有、又肯拿一切去换的东西。可是‘诸神不许我们欠他们的债’——她会偿的——她会的。”
日落时分，艾米丽坐在瞭望屋里。满屋淌着柔光。窗外，天空和树影是精致的淡彩，空中有轻音。下面园子里，阿黄正沿着红砖小径追枯叶。它油光的条纹身子和灵巧的步态叫她看了欢喜——巷外犁过的田垄匀净发亮，水晶绿的天上第一颗淡淡的白星，也叫她欢喜。
秋夜的风在山上吹着仙界的号角；高个儿约翰的林子里一片笑声——像农牧神的笑。伊尔丝、佩里和泰迪在那儿等她——他们约好了一场暮色里的嬉闹。她会去赴约的——这就去——先不。她满腔的狂喜必须先写出来，再从梦的世界回到现实的世界。从前她会把它倒进给爸爸的信里。如今不能再那样了。可面前的桌上摊着一本崭新的吉米簿。她把它拉到跟前，提起笔，在头一页洁白的纸上写下——

===Original===

I am going to write a diary, that it may be published when I die.

===Chinese===

我要开始写日记了，好等我死后拿去出版。
